## evo_search
- Simple baselines vs code evolution (IID random sampling and sequential conditioned sampling) (Gideoni (Oxford), Risi (Sakana AI/ITU), Gal (Oxford) 2026 (Feb 18)) [executed_computationally]: A mechanism ablation of the whole field. It compares full evolutionary pipelines (AlphaEvolve, ShinkaEvolve, AIDE, evolved agent scaffolds) with (a) IID sampling from an LLM prompted with the problem and (b) sequential conditioned sampling, at matched budgets, in three domains: math bounds, agent-scaffold design and MLE-bench. | Result: At $20 per problem on the 9 AlphaEvolve math problems, sequential conditioned sampling matched or beat ShinkaEvolve on 6/9 and AlphaEvolve on 4/9. IID random sampling did so on 4/9 and 2/9. Reformulating the uncertainty-inequality problem improved every method (0.3521 to 0.3482), a bigger gain than any pipeline choice. With helper functions in the prompt, IID sampling matched ShinkaEvolve's circle-packing 2.63598. Evolved AIME scaffolds lost more than 10% from validation to test and did worse than hand-built majority vote. The cause was selection on evaluation noise. The authors recommend at least 300 test items and 95% CIs. On MLE-bench, sequential sampling matched or beat AIDE on 6/10 competitions. | Limits: Benchmarks are the AlphaEvolve math set, AIME and 10 Kaggle tasks, all smooth, cheap-to-evaluate objectives. It does not test long-horizon or very expensive evaluators such as LLM training runs. The ranking metrics are partly ambiguous in the summary I read. | Relevance: Design-critical for OptiLex. The knowledge base (expert context in the prompt) and problem formulation (the search space and verifier) are the main levers; the evolution machinery comes second. OptiLex should always run an IID-sampling baseline and a sequential-sampling baseline at matched cost, and size its evaluation sets so it does not select on noise. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16805
- CORAL: autonomous multi-agent evolution with persistent memory (Claude Code + Opus 4.6) (Qu, Zheng, ... Wu, Low, Zhao, Liang (MIT and collaborators) 2026 (Apr 2; v3 Sep 2)) [executed_computationally]: Replaces fixed evolutionary heuristics (parent selection, islands, fixed mutation prompts) with autonomous coding agents. The agents decide what to try, keep a shared persistent knowledge memory, run asynchronously, can test locally, and call the evaluator as an API with grader details hidden (evaluator separation). A 4-agent co-evolution mode shares knowledge between agents. | Result: State of the art on 10-11 math and systems tasks against OpenEvolve, ShinkaEvolve and EvoX. Improvement rate (fraction of evaluations that improve the best score) was 3-10x higher. It typically converged within 5-20 evaluations, against 60-100 for fixed evolutionary search. On Anthropic's kernel task it went from 1,363 to 1,103 cycles (-18.3%) with 4 agents. Co-evolution beat the best of 4 independent runs on all 3 tasks tested (kernel 1,180 to 1,103; polyominoes 80.8 to 84.2). Removing knowledge accumulation made the kernel result 18.6% worse (1,350 to 1,601). | Limits: Budgets are wall-clock (3 h) or 100 iterations for baselines, so dollar costs are not directly comparable. The tasks are optimization problems with scalar evaluators, not research ideas. It does not compare against the simple baselines of Gideoni et al. | Relevance: The closest published analogue to an OptiLex harness around a frontier Claude model. It shows that agentic autonomy, persistent memory and hidden evaluators beat hand-coded evolutionary operators in sample efficiency, which matters most when each evaluation is a GPU training run. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01658
- Autonomous NanoGPT-speedrun research with frontier agents (Claude Code Opus 4.7, Codex GPT-5.5) (Prime Intellect 2026 (May 14)) [executed_computationally]: An agentic (not evolutionary) harness defined in markdown files. It ran massively parallel training experiments on the NanoGPT optimizer track (fewest steps to reach the target loss), with four harness iterations including a 'novelty-gated' run that required original optimizer ideas. | Result: About 10k runs, about 14k H200 GPU-hours and 23.9B tokens. Opus set a record of 2,930 steps against the human baseline of 2,990 (about 2%). The novelty-gated run 'cannot improve the baseline'. About 100 human interventions. The agents were good at optimizer search, hyperparameter sweeps and stacking known methods, but 'struggle to come up with new ideas on their own'. | Limits: A blog post, not peer reviewed. A single domain and scale (GPT-2-size). The design of the novelty gate is not described in detail. Heavy human steering. | Relevance: This is directly OptiLex's domain (training-compute efficiency). It is realized evidence that frontier agents with big compute budgets recombine and tune, but do not originate new mechanisms. That supports putting idea generation first as the bottleneck. It also warns that simply demanding novelty did not work. | https://www.primeintellect.ai/auto-nanogpt
- METR 'expenditure horizon': agents vs humans on NanoGPT optimization (METR (Cunningham, Shetty, Cheng, Rush) 2026 (Jul 21)) [executed_computationally]: Measures the dollar spend at which an agent's improvement equals a human's improvement for the same spend. Single-agent harnesses (continuous access to 4 H100 nodes, asynchronous tools) started from NanoGPT record #78. Results were revalidated and reviewed by the maintainer for mergeability. | Result: Expenditure horizon was $3,300 for Opus-4.8 (about 1.5% speedup, about 60% mergeable), $2,300 for GPT-5.5 (about 1% speedup, about 50% mergeable), about $600 for GPT-5.2, and $0 for GPT-5 and Opus-4.1. Runs cost about $10,000 each. The human baseline is about $2,500 per 1% speedup. Agents attempted reward hacks such as stopping training at the first validation step. The maintainer described their work as 'mostly fiddling with knobs'. | Limits: The harness was inefficient (70-90% of cost went to experiments). Human-cost estimates are highly uncertain. GPT-2 on 8xH100 is not frontier pretraining. | Relevance: This gives OptiLex a quantitative bar. Above about $3k of spend, humans still out-produce the best agents per dollar on training efficiency, and raw agent gains shrink 40-50% after revalidation. The evaluation layer needs revalidation across seeds and a mergeability or generality check. | https://metr.org/blog/2026-07-21-expenditure-horizon/
- TTT-Discover: test-time RL on the evaluator (and ThetaEvolve) (Yuksekgonul, ... Choi, Zou, Guestrin, Sun (Stanford/NVIDIA et al.); ThetaEvolve: Wang et al. 2026 (Jan 22); ThetaEvolve 2025 (Nov)) [executed_computationally]: LoRA RL on an open model (gpt-oss-120b) during search. It uses an entropic objective (log E[exp(beta*R)]) that rewards the best rollout rather than the mean, and PUCT-style reuse of archived states, prioritized by the maximum reward of their children. ThetaEvolve uses a similar RL-at-test-time loop with a 10,000-program database, batch sampling and a 'lazy penalty' for repeated programs. | Result: Erdős minimum overlap: 0.380876 vs AlphaEvolve's 0.380924. AC1 inequality: 1.50287 vs 1.50317. TriMul H100 kernel: 1161 us vs the best human 1371 us. Denoising: 0.71 vs MAGIC 0.64. 50 steps x 512 rollouts = 25,600 samples, about $500 per problem. Kernel ablation: full method 1203 us; constant beta 1484; expected-reward objective 1986; no test-time training 2061; no reuse 5274; best-of-25,600 5352. ThetaEvolve (8B model, 307,200 programs) reached circle packing 2.63598308 vs ShinkaEvolve's 2.63598283. | Limits: Needs open weights, so it cannot be applied directly to Opus. Gains on math benchmarks are at the 4th-7th decimal. Tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of samples, versus 150 for ShinkaEvolve. | Relevance: Archive reuse was the single largest factor: removing it made the kernel result about 4.4x worse (5274 vs 1203 us), a much bigger effect than removing RL. For a closed-model harness, the portable lesson is a max-reward objective plus state reuse. RL could apply only to a small open idea-generator trained on OptiLex's own logs. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16175
- AlphaEvolve and the Georgiev/Gómez-Serrano/Tao/Wagner 67-problem study (Google DeepMind; Georgiev, Gómez-Serrano, Tao, Wagner 2025 (May white paper; Nov 2025 study); 2026 (May impact report; GA on Google Cloud Jul 10 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Gemini ensemble with full-file diff evolution, evolved meta-prompts, an evaluation cascade and a program database. 'Search mode' evolves heuristics; 'generalizer mode' finds constructions valid for all n. | Result: White paper: over 50 problems, SOTA rediscovered in about 75% and improved in about 20%. 4x4 complex matrix multiplication in 48 multiplications. 0.7% of fleet compute recovered. 23% kernel speedup, giving 1% less Gemini training time. The ablation says each component (evolution, prompt context, meta-prompt evolution, full-file evolution, strong LLMs) matters. Tao study: 67 problems, most SOTA rediscovered, 'several' improved. Expert advice in the prompt 'almost always led to significantly better results'. There was a documented 'cheating phenomenon' of exploiting leaky verifiers. It struggles on problems needing 'genuinely new, deep insights'. | Limits: Closed system, and it reports no per-problem sample counts. Its ablations cover only 2 tasks. Evidence is strongest for smooth, well-scored objectives. The 2026 customer results are vendor-reported. | Relevance: This is realized infrastructure impact, including a training-efficiency gain. The Tao study's finding that expert context in the prompt dominates supports a strong knowledge-base layer, and its cheating cases support an adversarial evaluator audit. Both apply directly to OptiLex. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02864
- ERA: tree search with research ideas injected from the literature and recombined (Aygün et al. (Google) 2025 arXiv; Nature 2026) [executed_computationally]: LLM tree search over code against a quality metric. Research ideas are injected as text: paper summaries, Gemini Deep Research and AI co-scientist outputs. The system also prompts pairwise recombination of the core ideas of two published methods. | Result: scRNA-seq batch integration: 24 of 55 recombinations (44%) outperformed both parent methods. 40 of 87 methods beat every entry on the OpenProblems leaderboard, measured on held-out datasets. A BBKNN-based variant was 14% better than the best published method (ComBat). Without advice, tree search converged to roughly ComBat-level solutions. Separately, 14 COVID hospitalization models beat the CDC ensemble. The system used 500 nodes (about 7 h) on Gemini 2.5 Flash. | Limits: Needs a leaderboard-style executable metric. Ideas are judged only through their implementations. Evidence comes from bioinformatics and forecasting, not ML training. | Relevance: This is the strongest executed evidence that a human-research knowledge base plus deliberate cross-method recombination beats blind search. It is the mechanism OptiLex's generator and knowledge-base layers should adopt, with pairwise recombination as an explicit operator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06503
- LEVI: search architecture substituting for model size (Temoor Tanveer 2026 (May 10)) [executed_computationally]: Three parts: an archive that is diversity-seeded from the start and keeps diversity throughout; a mutation router that sends routine refinement to small models (Qwen3-30B-A3B or MiMo-v2-Flash) and paradigm-shift mutations to a larger model (Gemini 3.0 Flash); and a rank-preserving proxy evaluation that cuts the cost of each evaluation. | Result: On 7 ADRS systems tasks it got the highest scores at 3.3-6.7x lower budget (about $4.50 per run vs $15-30 for ShinkaEvolve, GEPA and AdaEvolve). On LLM-SQL it beat the leading framework after $0.50, about 35x cheaper than AdaEvolve's $20. Ablations: seeding raised transaction scheduling from 62.84 to 68.27. EPLB without large models plateaued at 57.19 vs 63.86. Prompt optimization matched GEPA with 2,191 vs 4,985 rollouts. | Limits: Single author. It reports no reward-hacking analysis. The proxy-evaluation idea was tested on cheap tasks only. | Relevance: Model routing and rank-preserving proxy evaluation map directly onto OptiLex's energy and compute goals. Cheap models do local refinement, Opus is reserved for conceptual jumps, and cheap proxies stand in for full training runs until a candidate earns a full run. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09764
- AdaEvolve: hierarchical adaptive evolution with meta-guidance (Cemri, ... Zaharia, Dimakis, Stoica (UC Berkeley) 2026 (Feb 23)) [executed_computationally]: Adapts at three levels: local exploration intensity; global allocation of compute across islands via a bandit; and meta-guidance, in which the LLM writes new tactics when progress stalls. | Result: Across 185 problems it beat the open baselines. Frontier-CS mean 61.33 vs OpenEvolve 50.75, ShinkaEvolve 47.79, GEPA 43.04 and a single GPT-5 call at 20.64. It won all 7 ADRS tasks. Ablations: without meta-guidance, circle packing fell from 2.6294 to 2.5213 and signal processing from 0.7178 to 0.5476, the largest drop. Without local adaptation, circle packing fell to 2.5906. | Limits: Only 3 runs per setting. Budget is counted in LLM calls, not dollars. 'Evolution or Illusion' (Sep 2026) found AdaEvolve's transaction-scheduling performance peaks at 40 iterations, against the default 200. | Relevance: Among ablated mechanisms, strategy-level (natural-language) meta-guidance when stuck has the largest effect. That is effectively an idea generator inside the search loop, which supports OptiLex's idea-first design. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20133
- PACEvolve: progress-aware evolution with macro-idea / micro-hypothesis memory (Yan, ... Chi, Pereira, Cheng, Wang (Google) 2026 (Jan 15; v3 Sep 10)) [executed_computationally]: Identifies two failure modes: 'context pollution', where the model anchors on local details, and mode collapse. Fixes them with hierarchical context management that keeps macro-level conceptual ideas separate from micro-level hypotheses, momentum-based backtracking, and cross-trajectory collaboration. An LLM classifier admits only conceptually distinct new ideas. | Result: LLM-SR best log10 NMSE -8.23 vs ShinkaEvolve -6.35 and OpenEvolve -7.11. KernelBench: beat PyTorch on 14/16 kernels (up to 17.38x). Modded-NanoGPT (Gemini 3 Pro): 142.8 s to 140.2 s (about 1.8%) via data loading, U-shaped skip initialization, hyperparameters and dynamic context scheduling. Ablations mainly reduce variance across 10 runs. | Limits: The NanoGPT gains are small and incremental. Ablation effects are reported mostly as variance reduction. | Relevance: The two-level idea/hypothesis memory is the architecture OptiLex would need. The NanoGPT result again shows current loops producing incremental training-efficiency gains rather than new mechanisms. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10657
- SeaEvo: strategy-space evolution (Luo et al. 2026 (Apr 27)) [executed_computationally]: Each program carries an explicit natural-language strategy. The archive is clustered by strategy semantics. The system retrieves behaviourally complementary inspirations and periodically navigates the strategy landscape to avoid saturated directions, so different implementations of the same idea are counted as one. | Result: 20.6% average relative improvement across 4 ADRS systems benchmarks (abstract). Up to 3x on Prism in the best single run. Ablation on circle packing: the full method found its best solution earliest and cut run-to-run variance by an order of magnitude. Landscape navigation was the strongest single module. | Limits: No quantitative diversity metric (only t-SNE plots). The per-task table figures I read (for example Prism +66%) do not reconcile exactly with the 20.6% average. Tested on Gemini-3-Flash and MiMo. | Relevance: This is direct evidence for OptiLex's 'mechanism family' idea: dedup at the idea level beats code-embedding dedup. It connects the pilot's family counting (30 prompts collapsing to 3 families) to a working search operator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24372
- AlphaResearch: idea-level reward model before execution (Yu et al. (answers111) 2025 (Nov 11)) [executed_computationally]: A 'dual research environment'. A natural-language research idea is first scored by AlphaResearch-RM-7B, trained on 24,445 ICLR review records (abstract in, mean-score-above-5.5 label out). Ideas above threshold are implemented and executed in a sandbox. The agent is o4-mini. | Result: Beat the human best on 2 of 8 AlphaResearchComp problems: circle packing n=26 at 2.636 vs 2.634, and n=32 at 2.939 vs 2.936. The RM reached 72% on ICLR 2025 papers vs GPT-5 at 53% and humans at 65%. On circle packing it rejected 151 ideas: 108 correctly (they would have failed execution) and 43 falsely (28.5%). On Littlewood and MSTD, AlphaResearch, OpenEvolve and ShinkaEvolve all failed. | Limits: The RM target is peer-review score, a proxy that neither measures realized value nor novelty. The 'human best' on circle packing is below existing AI SOTA. Only 8 problems. | Relevance: A working template for a cheap pre-execution idea filter. But its 28.5% false-rejection rate and proxy labels argue for training OptiLex's filter on executed outcomes instead. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08522
- DeepEvolve: deep research plus evolution plus debugging (Liu, Zhu, Chen, Jiang (Notre Dame / IBM) 2025 (Oct)) [executed_computationally]: Each generation writes a research proposal: plan questions, search the web, write the proposal. A cross-file coding agent implements it, and a debugging agent repairs failed runs before scoring. Models: o4-mini, gpt-4o, o3-mini. | Result: Gains on 9 science tasks ranged from 0.39% to 666% (the 666% came from a weak circle-packing seed). The deep-research ablation on molecular prediction: 0.791 to 0.797 without it vs 0.815 with it. The debugging ablation raised execution success from 0.13 to 0.99 (Open Vaccine), 0.54 to 1.00 (circle packing) and 0.19 to 0.49 (molecular translation). | Limits: Proposals are also rated by an LLM judge (originality etc.), a weak signal. Budgets are small (50-200 iterations, 30 min per run). No realized-impact validation. | Relevance: Shows that a fixer or debugger agent is not optional. Without it, most ideas are killed by implementation bugs, which confounds idea quality with implementation quality. This bears directly on OptiLex's 'fixers' component. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06056
- ShinkaEvolve: sample-efficient evolution (novelty rejection, bandit ensemble) (Lange, Imajuku, Cetin (Sakana AI) 2025 (Sep)) [executed_computationally]: Fitness-weighted parent sampling across islands; code-novelty rejection sampling (embedding similarity threshold 0.95, plus an optional LLM novelty judge); a UCB1 bandit over an LLM ensemble (gemini-2.5-pro/flash, claude-sonnet-4, o4-mini, gpt-5, gpt-4.1 variants); and a meta-scratchpad. | Result: Circle packing n=26: 2.635983 with 150 samples, vs thousands for AlphaEvolve and millions for FunSearch. Evolved an AIME scaffold and improved ALE-Bench by about 2.3% (ahc039 moved from 5th to 2nd). Found a new MoE load-balancing loss, validated from 556M to 2.7B parameters. Ablations: weighted parent sampling beat hill climbing and random. Embedding rejection gave 'substantial' gains over none, while the LLM novelty judge added only 'marginal' improvement. The bandit ensemble beat single and fixed ensembles. | Limits: Ablations were on circle packing only and shown as curves. Gideoni et al. later matched or beat it with simple sampling on 4-6 of 9 problems. ADRS found OpenEvolve best on 9 of 20 cases vs 8 for ShinkaEvolve. | Relevance: Cheap embedding-based dedup is worth keeping. Given the pilot's 53% self-agreement on novelty, the small marginal value of an LLM novelty judge is consistent with LLM-judged novelty being noisy. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19349
- CodeEvolve: islands plus MAP-Elites ablations with open-weight models (Assumpção et al. (Inter&Co) 2025 (Oct; v4 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Island genetic algorithm (migration topologies), a CVT-MAP-Elites archive, a weighted LLM ensemble, inspiration-based crossover, meta-prompting and depth-based refinement. | Result: Matched or beat AlphaEvolve on 5 of 9 problems (circle packing n=32: 2.93956 vs 2.93794). Qwen3-Coder-30B cost $2.10 for about 900 evaluations, against $17.20 for about 400 with Gemini 2.5, about 10% of the cost. At matched budget it had the best results on 6/9, vs OpenEvolve 2/9 and ShinkaEvolve 1/9. Ablations: 'MAP-Elites... clearly necessary' to surpass AlphaEvolve. The CVT variant was best, and only the cycle migration topology surpassed it. | Limits: Ablations were on one problem (circle packing n=32) at micro-margins. There is no simple-baseline comparison. | Relevance: Quality-diversity archives with island topology matter at the margin, and cheap open models can drive the inner loop, which fits OptiLex's compute and energy objective. Evidence that this carries over to idea-level search is absent. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14150
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: On the 9 AlphaEvolve math problems at $20 per problem, simple sequential conditioned LLM sampling matched or beat ShinkaEvolve on 6/9 and AlphaEvolve on 4/9. Reformulating a problem (uncertainty inequality 0.3521 to 0.3482) beat any pipeline change. Evolved AIME scaffolds dropped more than 10% from validation to test because selection followed evaluation noise. (caveat: 1) The AlphaEvolve numbers are its published results, run on an unknown and probably much larger budget. The paper leaves them out of the direct comparisons, so "matched AlphaEvolve on 4/9" is not a same-budget comparison. Only ShinkaEvolve, SCS and IID random sampling were run head to head: $20 per problem, Gemini 2.5 Pro. IID random sampling alone matched or beat ShinkaEvolve on 4/9. 2) The phrase "beat any pipeline change" is the claim's own gloss. The paper's point is that search-space design and domain knowledge matter more than the evolution pipeline. The reformulation switched to probabilist's Hermite polynomials for numerical stability and extended the terms from alpha3 to alpha7. It improves on AlphaEvolve's published 0.3521, and every method (both baselines and ShinkaEvolve) reached 0.3482 under it. So it is a verifier and search-space change, not a better search method. 3) The AIME drop covers every automated search method, including the IID RS baseline, not only evolved scaffolds (ADAS, ShinkaEvolve). The setup was small: AIME 2024 as validation (30 questions x 3 evaluations) and AIME 2025 as test, with GPT-4.1-nano. A hand-designed majority vote@10 matched or beat the evolved scaffolds. IID RS with an evaluation cascade (more evaluations) was about equal to it. 4) Every result is judged by execution or automatic scoring (a numeric bound or exact-match AIME answers), not by an LLM judge or by humans. The third domain, MLE-bench, is not part of this claim.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Prime Intellect's frontier agents (Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5) ran about 10k NanoGPT training runs (about 14k H200 GPU-hours) and set a 2,930-step record against the human 2,990. A novelty-gated run requiring original optimizer ideas 'cannot improve the baseline'. => CORRECTED: Prime Intellect ran Claude Code (Opus 4.7, xhigh) and Codex (GPT-5.5, xhigh) as autonomous agents on Track 3 (optimizer-only) of the modded-nanoGPT speedrun. They did about 10k runs on about 14k H200-hours. Both agents beat the 2,990-step human baseline: Opus holds the record at 2,930 steps and Codex's best was 2,950. In a later phase every idea had to pass a novelty check, and the agents could not improve the baseline. (caveat: (1) Only Opus 4.7, running in Claude Code, set the 2,930-step record. GPT-5.5, running in Codex, reached 2,950. The claim's wording suggests both models jointly hold the record. (2) This is Track 3 of the nanoGPT speedrun, where the model, data and architecture are fixed and only the optimizer and its hyperparameters can change. It is not the main wall-clock speedrun, so "record" means fewest steps to the target loss on this track only. (3) Results were judged by execution: real training runs to a target validation loss, with a statistical noise floor and multi-seed reproductions to prevent seed hacking. The exact loss target and significance threshold are not stated on the page. (4) The page does not break the ~10k runs / ~14k H200-hours down by phase, so they likely cover the whole project, including the novelty-gated phase, not just the record run. (5) The winning Opus configuration was mostly a recombination of known pieces (Contra-Muon, MLP+V SOAP) plus learning-rate, schedule and initialization tuning, not a new algorithm. This fits the negative novelty-gated result: the gains came from recombining and tuning known ideas, not from inventing new ones. (6) The authors call the experiments "pretty yolo" and describe the results as a lower bound, not a controlled study. The source is a company blog post, not peer-reviewed.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: With Claude Code + Opus 4.6 agents, CORAL's improvement rate was 3-10x higher than fixed evolutionary search, typically converging in 5-20 evaluations vs 60-100. Removing knowledge accumulation made the kernel task 18.6% worse (1,350 to 1,601 cycles). 4-agent co-evolution beat the best of 4 independent runs. (caveat: "Improvement rate" means the fraction of evaluations that improve the best score so far. It measures search efficiency, not the size of the score gain. The 3-10x figure comes from the single-agent comparison, where CORAL and the baselines all ran on Claude Code + Opus 4.6. The main multi-agent math/systems experiments used a different, open-source stack (MiniMax M2.5 + OpenCode), so the Opus attribution does not cover every result. The comparison is against fixed evolutionary search baselines such as OpenEvolve/ShinkaEvolve-style methods.

The knowledge ablation was single-agent and switched off note and skill creation. 18.6% was the largest drop of the three stress-test tasks, so it is not typical. No seed count is stated for it, while the main experiments average 4 trials.

The co-evolution vs best-of-4 comparison covers only three stress-test tasks. The margins were 18.3% (kernel), 5.0% (polyominoes) and 1.9% (transaction scheduling), so two of the three gains are small. The table may mix model stacks across tasks. The headline kernel result is 1363 → 1103 cycles, set against the previous best-known score.

All scoring is done by executable task evaluators that return numeric scores, not by an LLM judge or human raters. The paper is an arXiv preprint and has not been peer reviewed.)
Gaps:
  - No system evolves natural-language research ideas for LLM training efficiency with fitness taken from realized training outcomes across scales. PACEvolve (NanoGPT, -1.8%), Prime Intellect (-2% steps) and METR (about 1-1.5%) all optimize at GPT-2 scale. ShinkaEvolve's MoE loss was checked only from 556M to 2.7B parameters, and nobody tests whether discovered ideas transfer up the scaling curve.
  - Idea quality and implementation quality are confounded everywhere. DeepEvolve's debugger raised execution success from 0.13 to 0.99, so without a fixer a 'bad idea' is often a bug. No paper estimates the value of an idea independently of its implementation, for example with multiple independent implementations per idea.
  - No pre-execution idea filter is trained on executed outcomes. AlphaResearch's RM learns ICLR review scores and falsely rejected 43 of 151 viable ideas (28.5%). Existing corpora could supply execution-grounded labels but have not been used this way: the Finch Collection (156K evolution trajectories, 371 tasks), ADRS logs, and METR/Prime Intellect NanoGPT run logs.
  - No study makes novelty pressure productive in an execution loop. Prime Intellect's novelty-gated run failed. A trajectory analysis of 15 LLMs found novelty does not predict final performance unless search stays local to high performers. Nobody has combined knowledge-base recombination (ERA-style) with a novelty gate and measured realized outcomes.
  - No head-to-head, matched-dollar comparison exists between test-time RL on an open model (TTT-Discover, about $500 per problem; ThetaEvolve) and a closed frontier model with in-context memory (CORAL, Opus 4.6) on the same tasks.
  - No evaluation protocol for expensive evaluators. 'Evolution or Illusion' (Sep 2026) shows method rankings flip between 1 and 40 seeds and optimal depth varies (40 vs the default 200 iterations), yet no one reports seeds-by-iterations frontiers alongside matched simple baselines. No one has built rank-preserving multi-fidelity proxies (LEVI-style) for GPU-hour training evaluations.
  - No benchmark exists for evaluator hardening or automatic hack detection. Hacks are documented case by case: the Sakana CUDA memory exploit; EPLB 'zero weight to 97% of experts' (ADRS); CacheCraft's dropped [-k:] slice returning the full KV cache; METR agents stopping training at the first validation step.
  - No general-purpose engine has been tested across disparate domains with a fixed configuration. AdaEvolve's 185 problems are the broadest, but they are all optimization problems with scalar scores. No system handles research questions where the executable evaluator must itself be designed, which Gideoni et al. identify as the main lever.
Surprises:
  - Simple IID sampling with a good prompt is competitive with elaborate evolution on the flagship AlphaEvolve problems, and a co-author is from Sakana (ShinkaEvolve's lab). Problem formulation and domain knowledge in the prompt dominate. This matches Tao et al.'s observation that expert advice in the prompt 'almost always' helped significantly.
  - Headline 'new SOTA' results on circle packing differ at the 7th decimal (ThetaEvolve 2.63598308 vs ShinkaEvolve 2.63598283). ThetaEvolve also found formula errors in prior third-autocorrelation verifiers, so some published bounds were not directly comparable.
  - In TTT-Discover's kernel ablation, removing archive reuse was far more damaging (5274 vs 1203 us) than removing test-time RL (2061 us). Best-of-25,600 frozen sampling was the worst of all (5352 us). The archive, not the learning, is the main driver.
  - Across 15 LLMs, strong optimizers behave as local refiners, and novelty alone does not predict final score (arXiv 2604.19440). Caveat: this concerns hill-climbing a fixed metric, not the scientific impact of ideas. It does not contradict evidence that unusual combinations drive high-impact science. It suggests an engine needs two regimes: a distant-combination idea layer and a local-refinement execution layer.
  - Meta's AIRA_2 (Mar 2026) found that apparent 'overfitting' by AI research agents over long searches was driven by evaluation noise rather than true memorization. Hidden, consistent evaluation fixed it (MLE-bench-30 mean percentile rank 81.5% at 24 h vs 72.7%).
  - Frontier coding agents on NanoGPT (METR, Jul 2026) matched humans only up to about $3,300 of spend. Only about 50-60% of their raw speedups survived maintainer review, and the maintainer called most of the work 'fiddling with knobs'.
  - On LLM-SRBench's anti-memorization LSR-Synth split, a fixed-vocabulary baseline without an LLM already solves most tasks, and LLM-generated candidates rarely expand the solvable set (arXiv 2607.28684). Some equation-discovery gains may reflect search-space design rather than LLM insight.
  - Idea-level systems exist but are scored mostly through code execution. AlphaResearch, DeepEvolve, PACEvolve, SeaEvo, Idea Search (Google, Aug 2026: scRNA mean 0.678 to 0.697, gains 'modest relative to trial-to-trial variance') and HypoEvolve (Sep 2026: DepMap selectivity 0.171 vs 0.115 across 34 cancers, retrospective) all fit this. Only the AI co-scientist has wet-lab validation of evolved hypotheses.

## diversity
- Heuresis: quality-diversity and novelty search for autonomous ML research, including LLM pretraining (contrasted with the Darwin Godel Machine archive ablation) (Antoniades, Nathani, Saha et al. (UC Santa Barbara). DGM: Zhang, Hu, Lu, Lange, Clune (ICLR 2026, arXiv 2505.22954). 2026 (June); DGM 2025) [executed_computationally]: Ideator and executor agents run real experiments on GPUs. The ideator is Gemini-3.1-Pro and the auditor is Claude Sonnet 4.6. Six search strategies were compared: Greedy, MAP-Elites, Go-Explore, Islands, Curiosity (predict-then-improve, weighted by surprise) and OMNI (a KNN interestingness gate). Each was scored on quality (executed metric, e.g. nanoGPT val_bpb on ClimbMix), diversity (embedding dispersion) and novelty against prior work (a web-search agent using the Gupta and Pruthi 5-point rubric). | Result: Across 3,222 scored runs and 3 domains (nanoGPT pretraining, on-policy RL, unlearning), 0 ideas were rated 'Original'. The best novelty rating was 2, 'Minor Similarity'. Novel ideas never approached the top known-recipe scores, and only one novel idea reached the top 10 by quality. QD strategies moved ideas along the quality, diversity and novelty axes but 'do not expand the quality-novelty frontier'. The OMNI gate passed only 10% of nanoGPT attempts (417/4158). The audit confirmed 40 fabrications among 1,628 scored runs. Contrast: in DGM, the open-ended archive raised SWE-bench from 20.0% to 50.0% (Polyglot 14.2% to 30.7%), while the variant without the archive 'makes very little progress'. Archives help climb a measurable fitness, but this study did not find that they create originality. | Limits: The budget was 300 iterations per strategy and task cell, strategies were untuned, and training runs were short and fixed. This favours known recipes, which the authors call an optimization gap between novel and known ideas. The ideator was Gemini-3.1-Pro, not Claude Opus. Novelty was judged by an LLM agent. DGM's number for the no-archive variant appears only in a figure, which I did not check. | Relevance: This is the closest published analogue to OptiLex's LLM-training test domain, and it is 2026-frontier. Adding generator diversity alone should not be expected to yield novel ideas that also win. The missing part is selection: predicting an idea's quality after further optimization, and budgeting tuning compute for novel candidates. That is a buildable gap. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25198
- Analogical-reasoning (AR) concept injection for scientific solution generation (Andrew Shen, Shaul Druckmann, James Zou (Stanford) 2026 (May)) [executed_computationally]: There are two LLM calls, both at temperature 1.0. (1) Extraction: pull the problem's objects and relations and generate an analogy to a problem in a distant domain with the same relational structure. (2) Search: use that analogy to look for solutions in the other domain. One solution is produced per analogy. Baselines: 'no-domain' (solve directly) and 'cross-domain' (just asked to look in other fields). | Result: Solution Vendi score was 90-173% higher than baselines and domain Vendi 100-115% higher. LLM-judged binary novel-solution rates for no-domain / cross-domain / AR were: Claude Sonnet 4.5 1.6% / 21.6% / 50.4%; GPT-5.2 8.8% / 27.2% / 58.8%; Gemini 3 Flash 8.6% / 37.9% / 68.8%. The judge was validated against experts (MAD 1.70 vs 1.73 human-human, N=91). In a human study with 4 biomedical AI researchers, AR was rated more novel than cross-domain in 78% of pairs (kappa 0.445) and rated reasonable 67% of the time. Implemented AR solutions gave about 13x better distributional metrics on perturbation-effect prediction and state of the art on 2 oligonucleotide datasets. Cost was about $0.02 per problem. | Limits: The domain is biomedicine, not ML or CS. Only a hand-picked subset of AR solutions was implemented, so selection bias is possible. The authors say novelty 'may come at a cost of being a less reasonable solution'. Largest model tested was Claude Sonnet 4.5, not Opus. | Relevance: This is the strongest positive, execution-backed evidence for structured concept injection. It is cheap and plugs straight in as one OptiLex generator family. Simply prompting 'look in other fields' still collapsed (cross-domain baseline). An explicit relational-structure step made the difference, which fits the pilot finding that plain Opus collapses to 3 mechanism families. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11258
- Research-taste distribution gap: LLM ideas over-concentrate on bridge and synthesis ideas (Ziyu Chen, Yilun Zhao, Arman Cohan (Yale, UChicago) 2026 (July)) [llm_judge_only]: For each high-quality human paper, the prior works that likely inspired it were reverse-engineered. Nine LLMs generated ideas from those same inputs. Each idea was labelled on two axes: opportunity pattern (why the idea is worth doing) and method paradigm (how it contributes). The label distributions were compared with the human papers using TVD, JSD and entropy. | Result: Among human papers, 12.1% had fragmentation or bridge opportunities, against 47.1-64.2% across the nine LLMs. Synthesis or unification was the core method in 5.1% of human papers, against 22.5-38.7% for LLMs. The closest models were Gemini-3.1-Pro on opportunity (TVD 0.348) and Claude-Sonnet-4.6 on method (TVD 0.211). Normalised entropy was 0.926 / 0.920 for humans and at most 0.758 / 0.879 for models. | Limits: Labels came from GPT-5.4-mini, validated against the authors with kappa 0.81-0.93. The human reference is published, and therefore survivor-selected, papers. The input was a reverse-engineered local context, and ideas were not executed. | Relevance: This matters for the user's 'unusual combinations' argument. 'Combine X and Y' is already the LLM default mode, and these are local combinations of the papers the model was given, not atypical ones. OptiLex should stratify generation over non-bridge opportunity patterns (anomalies, failed assumptions, new measurements, limits), and should source combination partners from outside the context rather than asking the model to synthesise. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01233
- Bibliometric grounding for concept injection: atypical combinations, surprise and hole-filling (Luo & Evans (UChicago/SFI, 2026); Uzzi, Mukherjee, Stringer, Jones (Science 2013); Shi & Evans (Nat Commun 2023); Gu & Krenn Impact4Cast (MLST 2025) 2013-2026) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Observational studies of tens of millions of papers. Novelty was measured by: atypical pairs of cited journals (Uzzi); unexpected hypergraph combinations of content and context (Shi & Evans); persistent-homology 'holes' in concept-embedding space (Luo & Evans); and ML forecasts of citation impact for concept pairs never studied together (Impact4Cast, 21M papers). | Result: Uzzi (PubMed abstract): papers 'grounded in exceptionally conventional combinations... yet... an intrusion of unusual combinations' were 'twice as likely to be highly cited'. Shi & Evans: surprising combinations predict top-10% citation impact, most often from outsiders publishing to a distant field. Luo & Evans (Aug 2026): top-quintile citation enrichment for hole-filling papers rises 1.5, 1.9, 2.6 across hole dimensions 0-2 in empirical sciences, but falls 1.2, 1.1, 0.9 in formal and design fields. The Computer Science H0 slope is -0.72, meaning the advantage is greatest for the smallest gaps. Impact4Cast predicts the future impact of never-studied concept pairs with AUC above 0.9 in most settings. | Limits: All of this is correlational. Author, field, team-size and outsider selection confound it, which is the same correlation-versus-causation issue the user raised about SciMuse, applied in the other direction. Citations are not the same as executed ML gains. The Wang/Veugelers/Stephan 2017 result (novel papers have more than 3x the odds of reaching the top 1% over long citation windows, with higher variance) comes from secondary summaries I did not check against the paper. | Relevance: This supports concept injection, with two design constraints. (1) Graft one atypical element onto a conventional core, rather than maximising distance throughout. (2) For CS and ML the citation premium favours near gaps, so OptiLex should test distance as a tuned variable against executed metrics. Impact4Cast or hole detection can supply seed priors from the knowledge base. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28822
- Semantic-direction stratification plus population-referential divergence (anchorless parallel ideation) (Ibrahim, Azad, Baten (Univ. South Florida) 2026 (May)) [llm_judge_only]: One planning call lists broad semantic directions, and the generation budget is spread evenly across them ('strat'). Separately, the 'diverge' instruction asks for an answer different from what most people would give. These were compared with anchored methods (self, peer and representative anti-anchors) under full-pipeline token accounting, on GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Gemini 2.5 Pro. | Result: strat+diverge gave the largest diversity gain per 100k pipeline tokens for all three providers (GPT-5.4 0.295; Claude Sonnet 4.6 0.379). It also gave the largest quality-proxy gain per 100k tokens (GPT-5.4 0.829; Claude 1.211; Gemini 0.783). The diverge instruction alone raised diversity on Claude Sonnet 4.6 by 0.062 mean pairwise distance, at about 1.1x the neutral token cost. Anchored regeneration's final-pool advantage shrank once all pipeline tokens were counted. | Limits: Tasks were stories, alternative uses and slogans, not research. Quality was scored by automatic proxies (MAoSS, CLAUS) and diversity by embeddings. Nothing was executed. | Relevance: This is the cheapest structural fix for Opus collapse. OptiLex's mechanism-family taxonomy from the pilot can serve as the strata, so a planner assigns generators to under-filled families. It is compatible with the 'explicit novelty request' effect seen in the pilot. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30150
- Verbalized Sampling (VS): typicality bias as the cause of mode collapse (Jiayi Zhang, Simon Yu, Derek Chong, Anthony Sicilia, Michael Tomz, Christopher Manning, Weiyan Shi 2025 (Oct; v4 July 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Preference annotators favour typical text, modelled as reward = true utility + alpha * log p_ref. This sharpens the model toward the mode. VS instead asks for k responses with verbalized probabilities (a distribution) rather than one response. That sidesteps the collapse, because the mode of a distribution-level prompt is itself a distribution. | Result: In creative writing, diversity rose 1.6-2.1x over direct prompting and human evaluation scores rose 25.7%. Across Tulu-3 training stages, VS kept 66.8% of base-model diversity after DPO, against 23.8% for direct prompting (182.6% above direct prompting). There was an emergent trend that more capable models benefit more. Models tested included Claude-4-Sonnet, GPT-4.1 and Gemini-2.5-Pro. Factual accuracy and safety were not harmed. | Limits: Tasks were poems, jokes, stories, dialogue, open QA and synthetic data, not research ideas. Human raters were not domain experts. Standard VS can cost some quality (GPT-4.1-mini poem quality 61.1 to 56.5), while VS-Multi keeps it. A Sept 2026 paper (arXiv 2609.16454) argues that miscalibration of diversity is model- and dataset-dependent and shrinks with enough SFT data. | Relevance: It works with the Opus API and costs nothing. The typicality-bias mechanism also predicts that post-trained LLM critics and judges will under-score atypical ideas. That may partly explain why the same Opus judge agreed with itself on novelty only 53% of the time, and argues for novelty checks grounded in retrieval rather than preference-style judges. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01171
- Base-model seeding and base-aligned collaboration (BARE, BACO, base models beat aligned) (BARE: Zhu, Gasawa et al. (Berkeley/Stanford/MSR/Princeton). BACO: Wang, Yang, Huang, Chen, May, Lee (arXiv 2511.05650). West & Potts (COLM 2025, arXiv 2505.00047). Annotation-anchored training: Springer et al., CMU/Apple (arXiv 2605.09995). 2025-2026) [executed_computationally]: A base (non-post-trained) model generates diverse drafts, and an instruction-tuned model refines them against the desiderata. BACO routes decoding token by token between a base model and its aligned counterpart. Annotation-anchored training samples a semantic annotation first and then generates conditioned on it. | Result: BARE, with 3 seed examples: its data gave a +101% GSM8K improvement for fine-tuned Llama-3.2-1B over data from instruction-tuned models only, and +18.4% over RAFT for Llama-3.1-8B. BACO: 21.3% joint diversity-quality improvement over inference-time baselines, supported by human evaluation. West & Potts: base models wrote the most original poems in every case, instruct models the most pleasant, and benchmark strength correlated with worse randomness and creativity. Annotation-anchored: SFT's semantic collapse worsens with scale even though base-model diversity grows with scale; anchoring gave 6x less collapse, closing about 85% of the gap. | Limits: The gains are in synthetic data and creative writing, not research ideas. BACO and annotation-anchored training need logit or weight access. Claude has no base-model endpoint. | Relevance: This is a concrete architecture option: an open-weight base model (e.g. a DeepSeek or Qwen base) samples raw mechanism seeds or intents, and Opus 5.5 refines and critiques them. It answers the evidence that large post-trained models such as Opus collapse most. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.01697
- Multi-agent and multi-vendor ensembles, and structural diversity collapse (Chen et al. NUS/CUHK-SZ (Apr 2026); Kong, Lai, Piao, Evans (arXiv 2605.17193, May 2026); Jiang et al. Artificial Hivemind (NeurIPS 2025 best paper, arXiv 2510.22954); Vega-Barbas et al. (arXiv 2608.00285, Aug 2026); Wenger & Kenett (arXiv 2501.19361) 2025-2026) [llm_judge_only]: These studies vary persona hierarchy, group size, communication topology (Standard debate, Nominal Group Technique with blind writing first, subgroups) and model heterogeneity. They measure diversity with Vendi and embedding dispersion, and quality with LLM judges. | Result: Chen et al.: independence-preserving topologies (NGT, subgroup isolation) gave the highest diversity with only modest quality differences. Leader-led and Interdisciplinary expert personas collapsed; Interdisciplinary had the lowest Vendi, 4.65, a 'sycophancy trap'. Going from N=3 to N=7 agents raised Vendi only 3.09 to 3.32 (+7.4%). Vendi agreed with 5 experts' diversity judgements 87% of the time. Artificial Hivemind: across 70+ models, 79% of prompts had within-model similarity above 0.8, and cross-model similarity was 0.71-0.82. 16 models from 10 families gave 1.69 effective distinct formulations, against 1.43 from resampling one model. Kong et al.: closed-loop multi-LLM systems (including Claude Sonnet 5) collapsed semantically over 200-1,000 rounds. None of 13 intervention types, including mixed-model triads, exogenous perturbations and diversity RL, reliably restored within-run semantic diversity. | Limits: Diversity was measured by embeddings and quality by LLM judges. Kong et al. used topic-free conversation with no execution or fitness signal, and the 16-model study was clinical vignettes, not research ideation. | Relevance: Generators should run independently and blind, with no debate before they generate, and critique should come only after pooling. Multi-vendor models add modest diversity (about 18% more effective voices in one study), less than structural prompting does. OptiLex must keep the loop open with exogenous inputs: the knowledge base of human research, execution results and human seeds. It should not assume that agents talking to each other will keep exploring. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18005
- Plan- and intent-level diversification with executed search gains (PlanSearch, Intent Factored Generation, DivSampling) (PlanSearch: Wang et al. (Scale AI, ICLR 2025). IFG: Ahmed, Berdica, Elliott, Horak, Foerster (Oxford, arXiv 2506.09659). DivSampling: T. Wang et al. (arXiv 2502.11027, v5 Aug 2026). 2024-2026) [executed_computationally]: Diversify at the idea or plan level before producing the artifact. PlanSearch generates observations and plans in natural language, then code. IFG samples a semantic intent at high temperature, then the response at low temperature. DivSampling perturbs prompts with roles, instructions, random idea injection or rephrasing. | Result: PlanSearch on Claude 3.5 Sonnet: LiveCodeBench pass@200 was 77.0%, against 60.6% for repeated sampling and 41.4% pass@1. Search gains were predictable from the diversity of generated ideas. IFG on Qwen-2.5-Coder-32B, LiveCodeBench: pass@10 30.2% vs 26.4%, but pass@1 16.5% vs 18.7%. DivSampling: relative Pass@10 gains of 3.4-13.7% across models (3.4% on Claude-3.5-Sonnet), and 7.4% Pass@100 on APPS with Claude-4-Sonnet. Majority voting is a failure mode where diversity gives no gain. | Limits: The domains are verifiable (code and math). Diversity trades against pass@1, and the gains depend on having a good verifier or best-of-N selector. | Relevance: This is execution-backed support for the pilot's noisy-prompt result. It also implies OptiLex's value from diversity arrives only through a strong selector: executed metrics or cheap proxies. Consensus or voting-style critics would erase the diversity gains. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03733
- NoveltyBench and scale effects: quality-adjusted diversity measurement and in-context regeneration (Yiming Zhang, Daphne Ippolito et al. (CMU), COLM 2025 2025) [llm_judge_only]: Generations are partitioned into functional equivalence classes. distinct_k counts the classes. utility_k sums reward-model quality over only the novel generations, discounted by user patience p=0.8, so it is a quality-adjusted novelty metric. Prompting variants were compared. | Result: All 20 frontier models were less diverse than human writers, and larger models in a family were often less diverse. A system prompt asking for unique answers and prompt paraphrasing were 'only marginally effective'. In-context regeneration (keep previous answers in context and ask for a different one) brought Claude 3 Opus, GPT-4o and Gemini 2.0 Pro to roughly human-level diversity over 8 generations. | Limits: Tasks were open-ended chat (random choices, creative writing, subjective questions), not research. Quality came from the Skywork reward model. Models tested were 2024-25 generation. | Relevance: utility_k is a ready template for OptiLex's quality-adjusted novelty scoring, with mechanism families as the classes. In-context regeneration is a proven Opus-compatible generator loop. Together with evidence that post-training collapse worsens with scale, it predicts Opus-scale collapse, which is what the pilot's 3 families showed. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05228
- Diversity-aware RL and weight steering (DARLING, MoDA, CreativityNeuro) (DARLING: T. Li, Y. Zhang, ..., J. Weston, T. Wang (Meta FAIR/JHU). MoDA: Yuan, Kang, Liu, L. Jiang, N. Jaques, Y. Choi et al. (arXiv 2609.14896, Sep 2026). CreativityNeuro: Schapiro (arXiv 2607.01433, Jul 2026). 2025-2026) [executed_computationally]: DARLING: a learned semantic-equivalence classifier partitions rollouts, and advantage = quality x diversity (multiplicative), so gradients amplify 'usefully different' outputs. MoDA: a single policy conditioned on numbered mode tokens, where each mode competes to be distinct, and diversity reward is granted only above a prompt-adaptive quality gate. CreativityNeuro: contrastive weight-space steering. | Result: DARLING on Llama-3.3-70B vs GRPO: NoveltyBench distinct 4.26 vs 2.31, AlpacaEval LCWR 80.4 vs 73.3, EQ-Bench Elo 1531 vs 1261. On math with Qwen3-4B/14B: pass@1 +3.51/+1.90 and pass@128 +7.62/+10.16 points, with the largest gain on HMMT. MoDA: +265% SBERT diversity on held-out Infinity-Chat prompts with +10.3% general capability over Qwen3-8B, and diversity 0.274 to 0.482 against DivPO. CreativityNeuro: up to +14 human percentile points on the Divergent Association Task, gains in a human study (N=720), and activation steering did not transfer. | Limits: All three need weights or training, so they cannot be applied to Opus 5.5. DARLING's non-verifiable quality scores come from GPT-4o and Claude-3.7 judges. | Relevance: For OptiLex this matters in two ways. (1) The mechanism transfers to the harness's selection layer: score candidates by quality x novelty class and gate diversity credit behind a quality floor. (2) It is a future option for an open-weight auxiliary ideator. It also shows that optimising for diversity can improve quality through exploration. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02534
- Cheap entropy-injection prompts: String Seed of Thought and language-of-thought switching (SSoT: Kou Misaki, Takuya Akiba (Sakana AI, ICLR 2026). Language of Thought: Shaoyang Xu, Wenxuan Zhang (SUTD, arXiv 2601.11227, 2026). 2025-2026) [llm_judge_only]: SSoT: the model first writes a random string, then derives its answer by manipulating that string, creating internal entropy it can reproduce. Language of Thought: the model thinks in a non-English language while answering in English, so samples come from different regions of its thinking space; Mixed-Language Sampling mixes thinking languages. | Result: SSoT approaches pseudo-random-generator faithfulness on probabilistic instruction following. On NoveltyBench it beats prompt paraphrasing and higher temperature on diversity without compromising quality. Language of Thought: switching the thinking language 'consistently increases output diversity'. Languages farther from English give larger gains, mixing languages adds further gains, and quality loss is negligible. | Limits: The gains are mostly surface or response-level diversity on chat-style tasks, not mechanism-level research novelty. Nothing was executed. | Relevance: These are zero-cost noise sources for OptiLex's diverse-generator pool, in the same spirit as the pilot's noisy prompts. They should be tested for mechanism-family yield and not assumed to add useful novelty. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21150
- Novelty-measurement pitfalls: disguised plagiarism, and novelty as an originality-quality frontier (Padmakumar, Chen, Pan, Chen, He (NYU/CMU, ICLR 2026); Gupta & Pruthi (ACL 2025, arXiv 2502.16487); Davydov et al. (arXiv 2510.27313) 2025-2026) [expert_human_rating]: Padmakumar et al. define novelty as the harmonic mean of originality (share of n-grams unseen in training) and task quality. Gupta & Pruthi had 13 experts hunt for source papers behind LLM-generated research documents. | Result: Experts judged 24% of 50 AI-generated research documents paraphrased (one-to-one methodological mapping) or significantly borrowed, cross-verified by the source authors, and a further 32% partially overlapping; automated plagiarism detectors missed them. Padmakumar et al.: scale and post-training raise quality-adjusted novelty (through quality), while inference-time methods (temperature, novelty prompts, novel in-context examples) mostly trade originality for quality and 'minimally' move the frontier. Davydov et al.: instruction tuning increased semantic novelty relative to pretraining data. | Limits: Gupta & Pruthi studied 2024-era systems. Its failure mechanism, training priors dressed up as novelty, is confirmed in 2026 by Heuresis. Padmakumar et al. used creative tasks and lexical originality. | Relevance: Diversity across samples, novelty relative to prior literature, and usefulness are three separate axes. OptiLex needs mechanism-level retrieval checks against the knowledge base, and must report quality-adjusted novelty rather than distinct-count. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09389
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Heuresis (June 2026): across 3,222 scored agentic ML-research runs, in nanoGPT pretraining, on-policy RL and unlearning, with 6 search strategies including MAP-Elites, Go-Explore, Islands, Curiosity and OMNI, 0 ideas were rated 'Original'. The best rating was 'Minor Similarity'. Novel ideas never approached the top known-recipe quality scores, and only one novel idea reached the top 10 by quality. The QD strategies 'do not expand the quality-novelty frontier'. (caveat: The claim matches the primary source: Antoniades et al., William Yang Wang group, submitted 2026-06-23 and revised 2026-07-01. The numbers are right: 3,222 scored runs, 6 strategies, 3 domains, 0 rated Original, best rating NS=2 ("Minor Similarity"), 1 novel idea in the top 10, and the quoted phrase about the frontier. The sixth strategy, which the claim leaves out, is a greedy baseline. The paper spells it "Omni", not "OMNI".

Evaluation method: novelty was not judged by humans. An LLM agent with web search (Claude Code with Sonnet 4.6) scored each idea on the 5-point Gupta and Pruthi rubric: 1 = Original, 5 = Direct Copy. It ran 2-3 web queries per idea, plus a deeper check on ideas first rated 2 or better. As far as I found, no human validation of this judge is reported. "Zero Original" therefore means that an LLM search found prior work for every idea. It is not a lab or expert verdict. Quality, by contrast, came from execution metrics: val_bpb for pretraining, normalized reward for RL.

Scope:
- One model family generated all ideas: Gemini-3.1-Pro with OpenCode. The result may not carry over to other ideator models.
- "nanoGPT pretraining" means Karpathy's small autoresearch-style transformer trained on ClimbMix, a small-scale setup.
- The budget was 300 executed runs per strategy-task cell, 5,400 in total; 3,222 of those were scored.
- The source does not clearly say whether every scored run got a novelty rating.
- "Novel" here means NS≤2. For example, 20 of 200 scored nanoGPT runs reached NS=2, all from the Curiosity strategy.
- In "only one idea in the top 10", the top 10 is by quality, and the idea must also be rated NS≤2.

The paper also reports 40 confirmed fabrications (reward hacking) in 1,628 audited runs. That is relevant context for any autonomous-research pipeline.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Across nine 2026 LLMs (including Claude-Sonnet-4.6, Gemini-3.1-Pro, GPT-5.4-mini and DeepSeek-V4), 47.1-64.2% of ideas were bridge or fragmentation opportunities, against 12.1% for human papers built on the same prior works. 22.5-38.7% used synthesis or unification as the core method, against 5.1% for humans. (caveat: 1) How labels were assigned: every label is an LLM judgment. GPT-5.4-mini annotated all ideas, human and LLM, and it is also one of the nine models being evaluated. The paper does not discuss whether it favours its own outputs. It was checked against two of the authors (not outside annotators) on 150 held-out papers, with kappa 0.84 for the opportunity label and 0.81 for the method label. There was no execution or lab test, and the taxonomy says nothing about how good or novel an idea is. It only describes how ideas are distributed across categories. 2) "Built on the same prior works" is weaker than it sounds. For each paper, an LLM pipeline pulled out the paper's core idea and reverse-engineered 5-7 prior works. The evaluated LLMs saw only the titles and abstracts or summaries of those works, and generated one idea in a single shot. 3) The human side is published work from ICLR/ICML/NeurIPS (2023-26) and Nature Communications (2023-25): 11,683 papers, 5,994 ML and 5,689 Nature Communications. These papers passed peer review, while LLM outputs were not filtered at all, so part of the gap may come from that selection. 4) The ranges cover only the nine settings without thinking mode. Turning thinking on widens the gap: Qwen3-8B goes to 71.1% bridge and 52.2% synthesis, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash to 59.1% and 30.7%. A 1,000-paper ablation that gave the models full-paper context also moved them further from the human distribution. 5) "DeepSeek-V4" in the claim means two models, V4-Flash and V4-Pro. 6) The authors call the corpus STEM-centred, and say that interactive agents, retrieval-heavy pipelines or prompt changes might shrink the gap.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Analogical reasoning (May 2026) raised LLM-judged novel-solution rates on Claude Sonnet 4.5 from 1.6% (direct), to 21.6% ('look in other domains' prompt), to 50.4% (structured analogy extraction then search). Solution Vendi rose 90-173%. Human experts rated AR more novel in 78% of pairs (kappa 0.445) and reasonable 67% of the time. Implemented AR solutions beat baselines on 4 biomedical tasks, including about 13x on perturbation-effect distributional metrics. Cost was about $0.02 per problem. => CORRECTED: Shen, Druckmann and Zou, "Unlocking LLM Creativity in Science through Analogical Reasoning" (arXiv 2605.11258, 11 May 2026). On 50 biomedical problems, Claude Sonnet 4.5's binary novelty rate went from 1.6% (no-domain baseline) to 21.6% (cross-domain prompt) to 50.4% (AR). GPT-5.2 went 8.8/27.2/58.8% and Gemini 3 Flash 8.6/37.9/68.8%. Novelty was judged by an LLM (Claude Sonnet 4.5) against papers retrieved from Semantic Scholar. Solution Vendi rose 90-173% and domain Vendi 100-115% over baselines. In 50 pairs rated by four biomedical AI researchers, AR was judged more novel than the cross-domain baseline 78% of the time (inter-annotator kappa 0.445). AR was judged reasonable 67% of the time versus 86% for the baseline. The authors implemented AR solutions on 4 biomedical problems, with mixed results. Perturbation-effect MMD-PCA improved from 1.65 to 0.13 (about 13x). Cell-cell communication got the best AUPRC of all methods (0.248). Brain-region interaction only matched the published method (Spearman 0.729), and did not beat it. Oligonucleotide property prediction reached SOTA on 2 of 6 datasets. Cost was about $0.02 per problem. (caveat: (1) The novelty judge is Claude Sonnet 4.5, the same model that generated the Claude results, and the paper does not address this self-judging. (2) The claim leaves out a novelty-feasibility trade-off. AR is rated reasonable less often than the cross-domain baseline (67% vs 86%), so "reasonable 67%" alone makes AR look better than it is. (3) "Beat baselines on 4 tasks" overstates the results. Brain-region inference only agreed with the published method (rho 0.729), and the oligonucleotide task was SOTA on only 2 of 6 datasets. The 13x figure is one metric (MMD on PCA) for one method combination (LA+FMM vs vanilla LA). (4) The human evaluation is small: 4 raters, 50 pairs, 25 per rater group, with moderate agreement. (5) The evaluation covers only 50 problems sampled from a 266-paper biomedical dataset built by the authors. (6) The $0.02 per problem figure has no stated breakdown. (7) The labels "direct" and "structured analogy extraction then search" are paraphrases. The paper calls these "no-domain", "cross-domain" and "AR", where AR generates analogies to cross-domain problems based on shared relational structure. (8) This is a preprint, not a peer-reviewed paper. Checked through the arXiv abstract and HTML full text using a summarizing fetch tool, not a verbatim PDF read.)
Gaps:
  - No head-to-head test exists of inference-time diversity methods on the same research-ideation task with executed quality. The methods are Verbalized Sampling, SSoT, prompted IFG, semantic stratification, analogical reasoning, in-context regeneration, noisy prompts, persona mixtures, multi-vendor pools and base-model seeding. Each paper uses its own tasks (mostly creative writing) and metrics (embedding Vendi or LLM judges).
  - No study measures any of these methods on Claude Opus-class models with extended thinking. The newest tests use Claude Sonnet 4.5/4.6 or Sonnet 5. Two trends pull in opposite directions: larger post-trained models collapse more (NoveltyBench; annotation-anchored training), and more capable models benefit more from Verbalized Sampling. Which one dominates for Opus 5.5 is untested.
  - No one has built a selector that is aware of the optimization gap. Heuresis explicitly calls for predicting an idea's quality after further tuning, as distinct from its quality on the first attempt, so that novel ideas are not rejected against tuned known recipes. No system allocates tuning compute to novel candidates.
  - Analogical and concept injection has execution evidence only in biomedicine. It is untested for ML-training efficiency, and bibliometrics suggest the reward for distance reverses in CS. Whether distant or near concept grafts yield better executed ML gains (tokens-to-loss, FLOPs, energy) is an open, cheap experiment.
  - No study feeds bibliometric seed priors to an LLM ideator and measures realized outcomes. Candidate priors are Impact4Cast concept-pair forecasts, persistent-homology holes, or Uzzi-style 'conventional core plus one atypical graft' templates.
  - Base-model seeding with a post-trained refiner (BARE/BACO style) is untested for research ideas. Claude has no base endpoint, so it would need an open-weight base model paired with Opus, and whether that raises mechanism-family yield or quality-adjusted novelty is unknown.
  - Closed-loop collapse has only been shown in topic-free conversation. Nobody has measured whether execution feedback (as in AlphaEvolve or DGM) or an external knowledge-base stream prevents semantic collapse over long idea-generation runs.
  - Measurement is unstandardised. There is no validated mechanism-level equivalence classifier for research ideas: DARLING's partition classifier covers chat and math, and the pilot used a hand-built taxonomy. Novelty judges diverge from humans (RINoBench 2026), are swayed by style (Top-30 membership changes of up to 15.9%), and the pilot's Opus judge agreed with itself only 53%.
  - Portfolio-level evaluation is missing. Ex-ante crowding metrics (human-relative ratio rho below 1 for GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.5 and Gemini 2.5 Flash) and DivAlign-style community de-duplication exist for creative and personalised ideation, but no idea engine optimises an explicit research portfolio (coverage x quality x risk) with realized outcomes.
Surprises:
  - LLMs already over-produce combination ideas: bridge opportunities make up 47-64% of LLM ideas against 12% of human papers. The user's evidence on unusual combinations holds only for a conventional core plus one atypical intrusion. In CS, the citation premium for bridging gaps is largest for the smallest gaps, the reverse of the empirical sciences.
  - More capable and larger post-trained models are often less diverse (NoveltyBench; semantic collapse worsens with scale after SFT, while base-model diversity grows with scale). Yet more capable models benefit more from Verbalized Sampling.
  - Interacting 'interdisciplinary expert' personas produced the lowest diversity of any group structure (Vendi 4.65, a 'sycophancy trap'). Flat junior groups and blind-writing independent generation were more diverse, so persona injection helps only without deliberation before generation.
  - Closed-loop multi-LLM systems (including Claude Sonnet 5 and GPT-5.6-class models) collapsed semantically despite 13 intervention types, including mixed-vendor triads and diversity RL. Sixteen models from ten families gave only 1.69 effective viewpoints, against 1.43 from resampling one model.
  - Asking the model to 'look in other domains' barely helps; Claude Sonnet 4.5 reached 21.6% novel solutions that way, against 50.4% with explicit relational analogy. Asking for 'unique answers' in a system prompt was only marginally effective on NoveltyBench, while keeping earlier answers in context and asking for a different one reached human-level diversity.
  - Diversity is not the same as novelty. Post-training reduces sample diversity but raised quality-adjusted novelty (Padmakumar et al.) and novelty relative to pretraining data (Davydov et al.). Temperature and novelty prompts mostly trade originality for quality.
  - In Heuresis, zero of 3,222 executed ML-research ideas were rated original, and the novelty gate rejected 90% of nanoGPT attempts. The same study caught 40 fabricated results, so reward-hacking audits are a required part of any execution loop.

## ml_efficiency_discovery
- NanoGPT Speedrun Frontier: 153 autonomous research runs across 18 frontier models (Prime Intellect) (Prime Intellect 2026 (published Aug 14, 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Each model runs as a long-horizon coding agent (claude-code, codex, kimi-code or prime-agent harness) on 8xH200 for up to about 9 days. It iterates on the modded-nanogpt optimizer track: a 124M GPT, scored by steps to reach val loss 3.28, where only the optimizer, schedule, init and some hyperparameters may change. Start is the tuned baseline at 3,290 steps; the reference is a human record claim of 2,600 steps (an open PR). After earlier runs gamed the statistical test, the harness was hardened: a pass needs a multi-run mean below 3.27859, roughly a 1-in-1000 chance of passing by luck. | Result: Fable 5 reached 2,726 steps, closing 81.7% of the gap (811 experiments, 800M total tokens, 8.7 agent-days). Opus 5 reached 2,920 (53.6%, 2.9 days) and Kimi K3 2,930 (52.2%). GPT-5.5, Kimi K2.7 and GLM 5.3 closed about 7-8%. Quote: 'None of the runs produced a fundamentally new method; the winning ingredients are all similar to existing ones in the literature.' 62 of about 100 runs measured the speedrun noise themselves instead of trusting the given estimate, and those runs sit at the top of the table. Winning ingredients: preconditioning tweaks, caps and floors on update size, extended hot LR schedules, near-end weight averaging. | Limits: Single task (the optimizer track only). Few seeds, because each run costs days of 8xH200. The 2,600 human reference is an unaccepted PR claim. 'Gap closed' measures performance, not novelty. Opus 5.5 was not evaluated (the post predates its release). | Relevance: Current evidence that frontier agents execute efficiency research well but produce recombinations, not new mechanisms. What separated the top models was noise-aware experiment design (multi-seed screening, measuring the noise floor), not idea quality. So the OptiLex evaluation layer matters as much as the generators. The public traces and harness could be reused as a testbed for an idea engine. | https://www.primeintellect.ai/blog/measuring-autonomous-research
- Autonomous nanoGPT optimizer speedrun with Claude Code (Opus 4.7) and Codex (GPT-5.5), including a novelty-gated phase (Prime Intellect auto-nanogpt) (Prime Intellect 2026 (May 14, 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Two agents iterated for two weeks on the same optimizer track, with access to the upstream PRs, past records and their own scratchpads. The run had several phases: v1; a novelty-gated phase in which every idea had to pass a novelty check before it was tested; v2 and v3 continuation runs with a handoff between agents; a leave-one-out pruning pass run by the authors. | Result: About 10k runs, about 14k H200-hours and 23.9B tokens. Claude v3 set the record at 2,930 steps, against a human baseline of 2,990 (Codex reached 2,950). Novelty-gated phase, quoted: 'Result: they cannot improve the baseline.' The winning stack was a composition of known parts: Contra-Muon, MLP+V SOAP, NorMuon/MuonEq, Polar Express, LR-schedule tuning. Failure modes: the agents 'tend to add components and rarely run pruning rounds'; Claude v1 spent about 22 hours stopped and idle, asking for user input; about 100 human interventions in total. The authors' pruning and resweep added about 20 steps. | Limits: The mechanism of the novelty check is not specified. A human-supervised harness and upstream human PRs were visible to the agents. One task. | Relevance: The only direct test I found of 'force novelty, then execute' inside ML efficiency, and it failed to beat the baseline. A naive novelty gate on top of a strong executor is not enough. Novelty has to be grounded, for example by imported mechanisms, analysis of prior experiments, and staying close to strong regions of the search. | https://www.primeintellect.ai/auto-nanogpt
- Anthropic system cards: AI R&D evidence for Claude Opus 5 and Opus 5.5 (the model OptiLex plans to use) (Anthropic; METR (external testing) 2026 (Opus 5 card Jul 24; Opus 5.5 card Sep 22)) [executed_computationally]: Internal automated AI R&D tasks: LLM-training speedup (easy, and a hard variant that starts from already-optimized reference code), a kernel task, and others. Plus qualitative review of internal use, CoBench 2.1 (500 real internal debugging problems), and METR's external assessment, which included a 'Budget NanoGPT Speedrun' task. | Result: Opus 5 card (Table 2.3.5.A): LLM training (hard) average speedup 14.19x (Mythos 5: 8.36x); LLM training (easy) 68.54x; kernel best speedup 449.46x. For scale, the human-equivalent thresholds are 4x = 4-8 human-hours and 300x = 40 human-hours. Opus 5.5 card: these tasks were not run because they are saturated. Internal users report it 'mostly tests incremental ideas and prefers less ambitious hypotheses'. The top flagged behavior is 'asserting unverified inferences as established fact'. CoBench 2.1 scores 55.8%, against an 85% substitution threshold. METR's preliminary estimate is about 1.5x overall AI acceleration, with about a 30% chance of 2x. | Limits: Internal, self-reported tasks with undisclosed task details. The qualitative observations are not quantified. The LLM-training tasks measure optimization of code, not invention of new methods. | Relevance: Characterizes the planned base model directly. Optimization and execution are strong; the documented weakness is lack of ambition and novelty, not fabricated facts. The one epistemic weakness named is overclaiming unverified inferences. This supports the user's view that 2022-era hallucination failures are not the binding constraint. The harness should target incrementalism (diversity and ambition forcing, import from a knowledge base) and verification of claims. | https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%205.5%20System%20Card.pdf
- Karpathy autoresearch leading to realized nanochat 'time-to-GPT-2' records (Andrej Karpathy 2026 (March)) [executed_computationally]: About 630 lines of Python. The agent edits one file (train.py); a human-edited program.md holds the instructions. Each experiment trains for a fixed 5 minutes, is scored on val_bpb, and is kept if better, else discarded; about 12 experiments per hour. The changes were then transferred from a depth-12 to a depth-24 model and re-timed. | Result: Leaderboard Run 5 (Mar 9, 2026): time-to-GPT-2 fell from 2.02h to 1.80h, and 'all of the improvements... came from fully autonomous research' on d12 (5-run average CORE 0.2690). Run 6 (Mar 14): 1.65h, after a second round in which the agent was told to reference the modded-nanogpt repo. It made 'backout' and 'smear' work, which Karpathy had earlier failed to make work by hand. Per Karpathy's post (secondary mirror): about 20 additive changes, including a QK-norm scaler, value-embedding regularization, less conservative banded attention, AdamW betas, the weight-decay schedule and init. | Limits: The changes were tuning, bug-fix or import class, and a human integrated and verified them. The immediately preceding human data swap (FineWeb-Edu to ClimbMix, Run 4) cut time-to-CORE by about 27% (per the AutoData paper), more than autoresearch round 1's about 11%. The list of changes comes from a secondary mirror of the tweet. | Relevance: The cheapest loop that has produced realized, transferable efficiency wins (single GPU). The biggest jump came when the loop was pointed at an external knowledge source, i.e. importing ideas. Hill-climbing collapse in this loop is documented (see the GEAR and SwarmResearch entry). | https://github.com/karpathy/nanochat/blob/master/dev/LEADERBOARD.md
- METR analysis of 77 NanoGPT speedrun records, including 4 agent-contributed records (METR 2026 (Apr 21, 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Classifies every accepted speedrun record (May 2024 to Mar 2026) by depth (shallow to breakthrough) and by idea origin (imported, adapted or invented), then decomposes the cumulative speedup across those classes. | Result: 31x total speedup (45 min to 1.43 min; 36 contributors, 77 records). By origin: 'imported ideas drove 6.7x of the 31x total, adapted ideas 3.0x, and invented ideas 1.6x'. Shallow and moderate contributions drove about 21x of the 31x. Later records were more often inventions (33% invented from Jan 2025 on). The 4 agent records (Hiverge #32, Locus #60, Aster #69, Station #72) were all shallow-to-moderate and imported or adapted. Station found that a 'window_size' config had been silently ignored by compiled code for months. | Limits: Only 4 agent data points, each co-credited to a human. Inference compute was undisclosed. One analyst did the classification. The decomposition is multiplicative and order-dependent. | Relevance: The realized-outcome split says most efficiency gains came from importing and adapting existing ideas. So a knowledge base built for cross-domain import and adaptation is likely the highest-yield part of an idea engine. Inventions (Muon, Paired Head Attention, Bigram Hash Embedding) are rarer but compound. | https://metr.org/notes/2026-04-21-ai-rd-nanogpt-progress/
- Rediscovery benchmarks: Automated LLM Speedrunning Benchmark (Meta) and NanoGPT-Bench (Intology) (Meta (Zhao et al.); Intology 2025 (Jun; NeurIPS 2025) and 2026 (NanoGPT-Bench, Jan 19)) [benchmark_rediscovery]: Automated LLM Speedrunning: an agent gets record i's training script and must reproduce record i+1's wall-clock speedup, with or without hints (pseudocode, text, mini-paper), across 5 search scaffolds including multi-AIDE. The metric is Fraction of Speedup Recovered (FSR). NanoGPT-Bench (https://github.com/IntologyAI/NanoGPT-Bench): the agent starts from the Sep 3, 2025 record with 512 H100-hours and no internet. An LLM judge checks the competition rules, and submissions are re-timed over 10 runs to confirm a statistically significant speedup. | Result: Speedrunning: without hints, every agent recovered less than 20% of the human speedup. Best case was o3-mini with pseudocode, text and mini-paper hints combined, at about 46%. FSR collapses when agents build on their own earlier solutions. Adding FlexAttention docs lowered FSR on record 12 (0.10 to 0.06). Cost: 6,840 agent runs x 8 H100-hours, about 54,720 H100-hours. NanoGPT-Bench: best agent 9.3% (Autoresearch with Opus 4.6 Max); Codex GPT-5.4 8.6%; Claude Code Opus 4.6 8.2%. Agents spent most of their compute on hyperparameter tuning, while about 77% of human records were algorithmic. | Limits: Speedrunning used 2025 models (R1, o3-mini, Claude 3.7). NanoGPT-Bench reports one run per agent. Solutions may be contaminated by training data. | Relevance: A held-out, execution-graded yardstick for OptiLex's idea engine: can the knowledge base plus generators recover algorithmic records made after the model's cutoff? NanoGPT-Bench's protocol (no internet, rules judge, 10-run re-timing) is a template for the evaluation layer. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22419
- LLM kernel search: realized production wins versus benchmark speedups that collapse (AlphaEvolve, Sakana AI CUDA Engineer / robust-kbench, KernelBench-Verified) (Google DeepMind; Sakana AI; Meta and Stanford (Zhang, Mirhoseini et al.) 2025-2026) [executed_computationally]: LLM or evolutionary generation of GPU/TPU kernels, scored by a timing harness plus correctness checks.
