CS only. The closed context limits how well raters can judge novelty. | Relevance: The best protocol available if OptiLex needs human ratings: pairwise, blinded, shared context, aggregated by Elo. Use it as a secondary signal and as a calibration set for judges, not as ground truth. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29696
- SoundnessBench: testing critics against expert soundness labels (Ho, Liu, Nghiem, Huang (University of Maryland) 2026 (28 May)) [expert_human_rating]: 1,099 ML research proposals reconstructed from ICLR submissions and labeled by reviewers' soundness sub-scores: 458 low (score 2 or below) and 641 high (3 or above), kept only where reviewers agreed. 12 frontier LLMs classify each proposal as sound or not, under default and strict prompting. | Result: Across models, mean recall on low-soundness proposals was 26.0%, meaning 74% of unsound proposals were accepted; 9 of 12 models exceeded 70% false positives. Claude-Opus-4.6 caught 28.2% (71.8% false positives), with 97.2% recall on sound proposals and macro-F1 60.5%. GPT-5.4 had the best balance: 64.6% / 74.6%, F1 69.7%. Strict prompting cut false positives to 19.9%, but recall on sound proposals fell to 36.1% (F1 from 54.9 to 49.3). Larger Qwen3.5 models were more permissive, not less. The bias held on ICLR 2026-only data (77.47% of low-soundness proposals accepted). | Limits: Labels are reviewer opinions, not execution outcomes. Reconstructing a proposal from a paper can drop details. ML only. | Relevance: With default prompting, soundness critics built on Claude will pass most flawed ideas. OptiLex's critics need calibration on labeled sets and threshold tuning, and cannot be the only first gate. This evidence comes from 2026 frontier models, so the objection that the failures come from outdated models does not apply. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30329
- LLM-judge-scored idea benchmarks and audits of novelty judges (Sinhahajari, Majumder, Poria (RQ-Bench); Schopf et al. (RINoBench, arXiv 2603.10303); Tang et al. (Scientist-Bench, 2505.18705); LiveIdeaBench (2412.17596, Nature Communications 2026); AI Idea Bench 2025 (2504.14191); IdeaBench (KDD 2025) 2025-2026) [llm_judge_only]: The first-generation benchmarks (IdeaBench, AI Idea Bench 2025, LiveIdeaBench, Scientist-Bench) score ideas by LLM-judged similarity to a target paper, by LLM judge panels, or by LLM reviewers comparing the output with a human paper. The audits test those judges against experts. RQ-Bench asks LLM judges and domain experts to compare model-generated research questions with the questions of recent arXiv papers. RINoBench provides 1,381 ideas with ICLR 2022-23 reviewer novelty scores binned to a 1-5 scale. | Result: Scientist-Bench: depending on which LLM reviewer was used, the share of AI papers rated comparable to the human paper ranged from 13.64% to 81.82% (GPT-4o 78.95% vs Claude-3.7 21.05%). LiveIdeaBench: more than 40 models, 1,180 keywords, 22 domains; ideation scores are poorly predicted by general-intelligence metrics. RQ-Bench: LLM judges rate model-generated questions as highly novel (a 'novelty mirage'), even more so in head-to-head comparisons, while experts prefer the authors' own questions. RINoBench: best model GPT-5 reached macro-F1 17.2 with MAE of about 1.0, even though its justifications aligned 0.93-0.99 with human rationales. TPR (arXiv 2608.25660) found a bias toward 'medium novel' and a 22.30% gain from probing hidden states. | Limits: The benchmarks are circular: an LLM scores the work of LLMs. Reviewer novelty labels are entangled with quality (Liu & Zhai, arXiv 2604.15145). ML only. IdeaBench's figures (2,374 papers, 29,408 references) come from a search snippet and were not confirmed in the primary source. | Relevance: Independent support for OptiLex's own pilot, in which the judge agreed with itself on novelty only 53% of the time. LLM novelty scores should not drive selection; use distance to a frozen corpus plus an audited collision check instead. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12071
- MLRC-Bench: objective competition metrics vs LLM-judged innovation (Zhang et al. (University of Michigan / LG AI Research) 2025 (NeurIPS 2025 Datasets & Benchmarks)) [executed_computationally]: 7 recent ML research competitions, each with a baseline score and a top-human score. The agent proposes and implements a method, and its score is the fraction of the baseline-to-top-human gap it closes. LLM judges separately rate how innovative each method is. | Result: The best agent (gemini-exp-1206 under MLAB) closed 9.3% of the gap. LLM-judged innovativeness correlated -0.06 with actual effectiveness. | Limits: Only 7 tasks, run with 2024-era agents. Competition solution reports are public even when code is not, so contamination is partial. | Relevance: Direct evidence that the LLM-judged novelty axis carries almost no information about performance. OptiLex should not weight judged novelty when selecting efficiency ideas. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09702
- DeepScientist: the realized funnel of a generator that executes at scale (Weng et al. (Westlake University) 2025 (ICLR 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Discovery framed as Bayesian optimization. An LLM reviewer scores each idea for utility, quality and exploration value (0-100 each). An upper-confidence-bound (UCB) rule picks which ideas to implement, at roughly 1 GPU-hour per experiment, and a cumulative findings memory feeds later rounds. | Result: More than 5,000 ideas led to ~1,100 implementations and 21 results that counted as progress. About 60% of implementations were terminated early by errors. Total compute exceeded 20,000 GPU-hours on 16 H800s. The system beat 2025 state of the art on 3 tasks: +183.7% (agent failure attribution), +1.9% (LLM inference acceleration), +7.9% (AI text detection). | Limits: Never analyzes whether the pre-execution scores predicted success. Tasks were chosen by the authors, and the gains are on narrow metrics. | Relevance: Gives OptiLex a base rate for sizing execution budgets: fewer than 0.5% of generated ideas, and about 2% of implemented ones, paid off. It also shows why a validated pre-execution filter matters. OptiLex could supply the missing analysis simply by logging pre-execution scores against outcomes. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
- Blind rediscovery of unpublished lab results (AI Co-Scientist, Kosmos) (Gottweis et al. (Google), with Penades lab (Cell 2025; Nature 2026); Kosmos: FutureHouse/Edison (arXiv 2511.02824) 2025-2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: Ask a question whose answer exists only in a lab's unpublished experiments, then check whether the system's top-ranked hypothesis matches it. Separately, test new candidates in the wet lab. For Kosmos, independent scientists graded report statements, and discoveries were checked against preprints and unpublished manuscripts the system did not see at runtime. | Result: Co-Scientist's top hypothesis matched the unpublished cf-PICI mechanism (the islands hijack diverse phage tails). Its AML repurposing candidates inhibited tumor cells in vitro. Top-1 accuracy on GPQA diamond was 78.4%, with higher Elo tracking higher accuracy, but the authors caution that 'the Elo metric is auto-evaluated and not based on independent ground truth'. Kosmos: 79.4% of 102 statements were judged accurate (data analysis 85.5%, literature 82.1%, interpretation 57.9%); 3 of its 7 discoveries reproduced unpublished or preprinted findings it never accessed. | Limits: Few cases, chosen as showcases. Rediscovery is not discovery. The matches were judged by the authors themselves. | Relevance: Shows that the unpublished-result design works beyond ML, which matters for a general-purpose engine. It also shows that interpretive claims are the weak point for critics to target. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18864
- How reliable small-scale runs are as evidence for efficiency ideas (DataDecide; Fantastic Optimizers) (Kaiyue Wen et al. (Stanford); Magnusson et al. (Ai2, DataDecide, arXiv 2504.11393, ICML 2025) 2025) [executed_computationally]: DataDecide pretrains 1,050 models on 25 corpora, up to 1B parameters, to test whether the ranking of data recipes at small scale predicts the winner at 1B. Fantastic Optimizers tunes the AdamW baseline as carefully as each new optimizer, then compares them across scales up to 1.2B. | Result: Ranking at 150M picks the 1B pairwise winner about 80% of the time, and continuous likelihood proxies make benchmarks more than 80% predictable at 1B using 0.01% of the compute. Speedups of matrix optimizers over AdamW shrink from 1.4x at 0.1B to 1.1x at 1.2B once the baseline is properly tuned. Earlier claims were inflated by '(i) unequal hyperparameter tuning and (ii) limited or misleading evaluation setups'. | Limits: Evidence goes only up to 1-1.2B parameters, and only for data-recipe and optimizer ideas. | Relevance: Sets the error bars on OptiLex's cheapest non-circular evaluator: about 20% of small-scale pairwise calls are wrong, and untuned baselines inflate gains. The evaluation layer must tune baselines equally and test at 2 or more scales before crediting an idea. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02046
- Rediscovery benchmarks with planted or known ground truth (HypoBench, LLM-SRBench, ResearchBench, DiscoveryBench/DiscoveryWorld) (HypoBench (Liu et al., U Chicago); LLM-SRBench (Shojaee et al., ICML 2025); ResearchBench (Liu et al., 2503.21248); DiscoveryBench/World (Ai2); library-reachability audit (Yao et al., 2607.28684); Dictionaries, Not Darwin (Pan Li, 2607.04108); Correct Answer, Wrong Mechanism (Eulig, 2606.23175) 2024-2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: The hypotheses to recover are either planted in synthetic data or taken from recent papers, so recovery can be checked without a judge. HypoBench has 7 real and 5 synthetic tasks over 194 datasets. LLM-SRBench makes equations harder to memorize by transforming or synthesizing them. ResearchBench decomposes 1,386 papers from 2024 onward into background, inspirations and hypothesis. DiscoveryBench has 264 real tasks plus 903 synthetic ones; DiscoveryWorld is a simulated environment with 120 tasks. | Result: HypoBench: the best models recovered only 38.8% of ground-truth hypotheses as task difficulty rose. DiscoveryBench: the best system scored 25%. ResearchBench: GPT-4o placed the true inspiration in its top 4% of candidates 45.65% of the time. The audits undercut these benchmarks: in LSR-Synth, a fixed operator library with no semantics already solves most of the anti-memorization tasks. In Dictionaries, Not Darwin, LLM evolution conditioned on parent solutions matched independent sampling (median OOD NMSE 0.045 vs 0.049), while set-level selection solved 165-169 of 717 cells against 74-78, using one-tenth of the call budget. In a rediscovery task, 7 of 28 agent episodes gave the correct answer with the wrong mechanism. | Limits: Synthetic is not real science. Tasks can be solved by recombining known pieces. Rediscovery is not discovery. LLM-SRBench's ~31% symbolic accuracy comes from a search snippet and was not confirmed in the primary source. | Relevance: Cheap, non-circular unit tests for generators and critics, useful for regression-testing the harness. They cannot certify novelty, and Dictionaries, Not Darwin suggests that selection over candidate pools, rather than evolutionary loops, may be where AlphaEvolve-style gains come from. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11524
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Date-filtered web search leaks post-cutoff information in backtests. Major leakage appeared for 71% of questions on Google and 81% on DuckDuckGo, and the answer was revealed outright for 41% and 55%. Forecast Brier score improved artificially to 0.10, versus 0.24 with leak-free documents. (caveat: 1) Leakage was graded by an LLM (gpt-oss-120b as judge, on a 0-4 scale), not by humans. It was checked against only 134 human-labeled documents: 76.1% exact agreement, QWK 0.85, F1 0.82 for score 4. "Major" means score 3 or higher. The same model family judged the leakage and made the forecasts.
2) The percentages count questions where at least one of about 100 retrieved URLs leaked. That is 393 Google and 389 DuckDuckGo questions resolving 2021-2025, with 10-20 generated queries per question. It is not a per-document leakage rate.
3) The Brier comparison is a deliberately curated setup, not a natural retrieval pipeline. It used 93 binary questions opened in 2025, all chosen because they had at least one score-4 document. It used Google results only and one model (gpt-oss-120b). Documents were grouped by the judge's leakage score. The exact value is 0.108, and feeding only score-4 documents gave 0.129.
4) The 0.24 leak-free result is the same as the no-retrieval baseline (0.244). So retrieval without leakage added nothing here, and the whole gain came from leaked documents.
5) Only Google and DuckDuckGo date filters were audited. The authors recommend frozen, time-stamped web snapshots instead.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In HindSight (arXiv 2603.15164, Mar 2026), an LLM judge found no difference between retrieval-augmented and plain idea generation (p=0.584). The time-split, impact-weighted metric found 2.5x higher scores for retrieval (p<0.001). The time-split metric correlated negatively with LLM-judged novelty (rho=-0.29, p<0.01). (caveat: Single-author preprint (Bo Jiang), submitted 16 Mar 2026, not peer-reviewed. The sample is small: 200 ideas in total, 100 from ResearchAgent (retrieval-augmented) and 100 vanilla, 10 per topic across 10 AI/ML topics. Groups were compared with Mann-Whitney U tests. Both systems generate with Llama-3.3-70B. The time cutoff is June 2023, and the answer key is 27,589 Semantic Scholar papers from June 2023 to December 2025. The generator's own knowledge cutoff is December 2023, which overlaps the scoring window, so some leakage is possible. Ideas are matched to papers by SPECTER2 embedding cosine similarity (threshold 0.96) rather than by an LLM, so the metric avoids LLM judging but depends on embedding similarity and on a threshold choice. Score = 0.6 x normalized citations + 0.4 x top-venue indicator, taking the best-matching paper. It therefore rewards ideas that match what the field went on to publish (the authors call this conformity bias), not ideas that are novel or correct. The authors also note false-zero scores from gaps in the answer key and reliance on a single judge model (Qwen3-32B, 1-10 scale, 3 trials averaged). The rho=-0.29 correlation was computed only on the 100 ResearchAgent ideas, not on all 200. No human or experimental validation was done: the ground truth is later publications and their citations.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Prompted frontier models such as o3 predict which of two AI research ideas performs better at no better than chance, even with retrieval. A fine-tuned GPT-4.1 system reaches 77% on 1,585 post-cutoff pairs, 64.4% versus 48.9% for experts on 45 NLP pairs, and 63.6% on 33 pairs of unpublished executed ideas. Follow-up arXiv 2605.21491: an 8B model after supervised fine-tuning reaches 77.1% versus GPT-5 at 61.1%. (caveat: 1) Ground truth is not decided by an LLM judge. In the main benchmark, labels come from benchmark results reported in published conference papers. For the 33 unpublished pairs, labels come from expert researchers who actually implemented the ideas (about 103 hours per idea), taken from Si et al.'s execution study. Half of those ideas were written by an LM agent and half by NLP experts. 2) The samples are small. 63.6% on 33 pairs is 21 of 33 correct, with a 95% confidence interval of roughly 45-79%, so the result is not clearly above chance. The expert comparison uses only 45 pairs, and each expert spent about 9 minutes per label. The 48.9% is a majority vote of 5 experts, not the accuracy of an individual expert. 3) Scoring: each pair is predicted twice with the two ideas swapped, to control for order bias. Pairs come from papers comparing ideas on at least 3 shared benchmarks. The tasks are mostly "which method wins on benchmarks", which is a narrow, benchmark-driven outcome and not a judgment of idea quality in general. 4) The claim does not say which configuration produced 77%. The paper attributes the jump from 51.9% to fine-tuning. The pages I fetched were ambiguous on whether that figure also includes the retrieval agent (the full "system"). The abstract gives no exact o3 number; figures show about 51-52%. 5) The follow-up is not comparable to the 77%. It uses a different dataset: 11,488 pairs from PapersWithCode, with 869 test pairs split into 1/2/3-sigma difficulty levels. It also uses a strict consistency metric where a prediction that flips when the order is swapped counts as wrong, which is why the base 8B model scores about 25-30%, below chance. The 8B model is Qwen3-8B. Its RLVR variant reaches 71.35%. GPT-5's 61.1% is measured under the same strict metric. 6) Both papers measure how well a model forecasts benchmark wins between similar ideas in the same area of ML. That supports using them to rank ideas without circular LLM judging only within this narrow empirical-ML setting, not for ideas that are new or different in kind.)
Gaps:
  - Nobody has combined the pieces of the most trustworthy design available into one evaluation ladder. That design is: execution on a contamination-free target (unpublished results, as in shadow evaluations, or live-leaderboard records set after a fixed start date), a statistical noise-floor gate, equally tuned baselines at 2 or more scales, and a separate audit of whether the winning ingredients already exist in the literature. Its known weaknesses: high cost (DeepScientist used >20,000 GPU-hours for 21 wins); tiny samples (shadow evaluation n=2); mostly one domain (ML with objective metrics); the metric is not scientific value; seed hacking; contamination once records become public; and small-scale proxies that are wrong on about 20% of pairwise calls (DataDecide).
  - The false-zero rate of time-split metrics has never been measured. No one has executed a random sample of AI ideas that matched no future paper to see how many were actually good. HindSight admits false zeros, and FAS admits a bias toward the mainstream, so how much time-split metrics penalize genuine novelty is unknown.
  - No prospective, pre-registered idea ledger exists for a frontier generator: ideas committed now with timestamps or hashes, then scored 6-24 months later against both the literature and executions. ForeSci's prospective mode and RAP's rolling episodes forecast research directions only, not idea quality.
  - Outcome predictors have been validated on AI-generated ideas at n=33 only (63.6%). There is no large test on raw, unfiltered AI ideas, many of which fail to implement (about 60% in DeepScientist). Nobody has tested Goodhart effects when a generator is optimized against such a predictor, which is the use Wen et al. propose. Debate-as-Reward (arXiv 2604.16723) showed a single LLM judge can be tricked by placeholder text into giving a false reward of 1.0, but no comparable audit exists for learned outcome predictors.
  - The largest execution funnels never report whether pre-execution scores predicted outcomes: DeepScientist (5,000 ideas, 1,100 implemented, 21 wins) and Prime Intellect (153 runs) both publish none. OptiLex could produce this dataset at no extra cost by logging every judge and critic score before execution.
  - There is no idea-level dataset of which efficiency ideas survive scale-up. Speedrun benchmarks run at 124M parameters, and optimizer speedups shrink from 1.4x to 1.1x by 1.2B (Fantastic Optimizers), yet no labeled small-to-large transfer set exists to train or validate a predictor.
  - No validated metric exists for diversity at the level of mechanism families. Current metrics use embedding distance or LLM judges, and Liu & Zhai (arXiv 2604.15145) find conceptual redundancy unsolved. No benchmark reports family-level yield: the number of distinct mechanism families that pass execution per dollar. Budgeted Subset Refinement (arXiv 2607.14118) measures portfolio yield on proxy ratings only.
  - No published contamination audit covers Claude Opus 5.x in backtests; Zhang & Stadie audited GPT-5.5, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V3.1 and Kimi-K2.6. With Opus 5.5's training cutoff around June 2026 (per the model information available to this agent), only about 3 months of clean literature exist as of 25 Sep 2026. Time-split evaluation of the production generator itself is therefore starved of data, and older-cutoff surrogate models or unpublished-result sets are needed.
  - Outside ML and equation discovery, execution-grounded evaluation of idea generators is still anecdotal: Co-Scientist and Kosmos contribute a handful of cases. A general-purpose engine has no shared, cheap, objective testbed in other sciences.
Surprises:
  - LLM-judged novelty does not just fail to predict success; it anti-predicts whether an idea is later realized. HindSight found rho=-0.29, and MLRC-Bench found a correlation of -0.06 between judged innovativeness and actual performance. In HindSight the LLM judge could not tell retrieval-augmented from plain generation (p=0.584), while realized matching showed a 2.5x gap.
  - Small specialist verifiers beat frontier generalists at predicting outcomes: a fine-tuned 8B model scored 77.1% against GPT-5's 61.1%, and prompted o3 was at chance.
  - Claude-Opus-4.6 accepted 71.8% of proposals that ICLR reviewers rated low in soundness. Within the Qwen3.5 family, larger models were more permissive toward weak proposals, not less.
  - Date-filtered Google search revealed the answer outright for 41% of backtest questions. In a separate audit, the standard before/after-cutoff contamination check wrongly flagged 4 of 5 flagship models because of recency effects alone.
  - In OptiLex's own domain, frontier agents beat the human nanoGPT record (Fable 5 closed 81.7% of the gap), yet none produced a fundamentally new method. When every idea had to pass a novelty check first, the agents could not improve the baseline at all.
  - A trivial exponentially weighted moving average beat most LLM agents at forecasting where research attention would go over the next 6 months (RAP, Sep 2026). In another forecasting benchmark, a small Qwen2.5 model outscored GPT-4.1, partly because its forecasts were broader and so matched more papers.
  - Parent-conditioned LLM evolution performed no better than independent sampling in equation discovery (median OOD NMSE 0.045 vs 0.049). Set-level selection over the sampled terms roughly doubled the number of solved cells using one-tenth of the budget, which suggests the value lies in selection rather than in evolutionary loops.
  - In Scientist-Bench, the choice of LLM reviewer alone moved the share of AI papers rated comparable to the human paper from 13.64% to 81.82%. At Agents4Science 2025, the three LLM reviewers correlated with each other at only Pearson 0.48, and Gemini 2.5 Pro's mean absolute difference from human scores was 2.73, against 0.91 for GPT-5.
  - The strongest recent negative evidence comes from 2026 frontier models: Opus 4.6-4.8, Opus 5, GPT-5.4/5.5/5.6 and Fable 5 in SoundnessBench, NanoGPT-Bench, the Prime Intellect runs and the shadow evaluations. The objection that failures came from outdated technology therefore does not apply to the evaluation-reliability findings.

## lit_hypothesis
- The Alien Space of Science: an availability-aware idea-atom sampler (Artiles, Weiss, Brinkmann, Rahwan, Schoelkopf, Pal, Larochelle, Goyal, Rahaman 2026 (v1 1 Mar, v2 15 May)) [expert_human_rating]: Gemini 3 Flash breaks 16,068 LLM papers (NeurIPS/ICLR/ICML/NLP venues, 2017-2025) into 82,255 conceptual units. UMAP+HDBSCAN clusters them into a vocabulary of 273 'idea atoms'. Two models are learned over this vocabulary. (1) A coherence model: a causal transformer over atom sets that scores whether a combination is a viable research direction. (2) An availability model: an InfoNCE dual encoder over the atom repertoires of 46,329 authors that scores whether any existing author community could produce that combination. The sampler ranks combinations by coherence minus beta times availability. Claude Opus 4.7 then writes the chosen combinations up as ideas. The approach operationalises Sourati & Evans' human-aware 'alien hypotheses' for LLM-era ideation. | Result: Diversity: with direct prompting, Claude Opus 4.7's ten most-used atoms appear in 95.7% of generated combinations (effective atom coverage 10.5%); Gemini 3.1 Pro is at 76.7% and 20.6%; the Alien sampler is at 34.3% and 72.5%. Human raters (40 blind reviews from 10 raters with ML/CS postgraduate degrees) gave Alien and Claude the same overall quality (4.03 each); Alien was higher on novelty (3.90 vs 3.70) and feasibility (3.90 vs 3.70), while Claude was higher on promise (4.40). In the GPT-5.5 forced-choice judge's top-10 pool, Alien won 69.4% of comparisons and Claude 30.6%. Temporal backtest (models trained on papers up to 2024): coherence-only ranking puts 23 of 2,477 held-out 2025 three-atom sets in its top 1,000, a 31.1x enrichment over random. Autoresearch: 10 ideas per method were run on A100 GPUs and the reports judged by a blind LLM. Average rank percentiles were Alien 0.633, Claude 0.574, Gemini 0.495, Random 0.295. During refinement the Claude reports drifted toward interpretability (2/10 before, 8/10 after); Alien stayed at 0/10. | Limits: Only 10 ideas per method were executed, and the reports were judged by an LLM with no objective metric (loss or accuracy). The human sample is small and the raters were not senior experts. The atoms only recombine existing primitives, so new primitives would need the vocabulary to expand. Availability is inferred from publications only. Temporal recall is highest at beta=0; exact recall falls as the availability weight rises. | Relevance: This directly replicates our pilot result (Opus collapsing to about 3 mechanism families) with a 2026 frontier Claude, and shows a non-prompt fix: an explicit concept vocabulary plus coherence and availability scorers built from the knowledge base, with no loss of human-rated quality. The corpus is LLM research, so the method transfers directly to OptiLex's LLM-efficiency test domain. Collapse also appeared inside refinement loops, so OptiLex's fixers and critics need diversity constraints as well as its generators. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01092
- Analogical Reasoning (AR) against mode collapse in scientific solution generation (Andrew Shen, Shaul Druckmann, James Zou (Stanford) 2026 (11 May)) [executed_computationally]: The system extracts the objects and relations in a research problem. The LLM then proposes cross-domain problems that share the same relational structure, and a search step finds solutions in those domains and maps them back. Novelty is judged by Claude Sonnet 4.5 against literature retrieved from Semantic Scholar (3 queries, top 15 each, re-ranked with SPECTER to the top 10). The analogies are generated by the LLM, not retrieved. | Result: Share of solutions judged novel, in the order no-domain < cross-domain < AR: Claude Sonnet 4.5 1.6% < 21.6% < 50.4%; GPT-5.2 9.0% < 28.2% < 58.8%; Gemini 3 Flash 8.9% < 37.8% < 68.9%. Solution Vendi diversity rose 90-173%. In a human pairwise study, AR solutions were judged more novel than the cross-domain baseline 78% of the time (kappa 0.445), and 67% were judged reasonable. Executed case studies: perturbation-effect prediction MMD-PCA fell from 1.65 to 0.13 (about 13-fold); cell-cell communication AUPRC 0.248 beat 14 baselines; state of the art on 2 of 6 oligonucleotide datasets. | Limits: AR makes 2 LLM calls per generation against 1 for the no-domain baseline (the cross-domain baseline also makes 2). Novelty labels are mainly LLM-judged. The case studies are selected and implemented with humans in the loop. The domain is biomedicine, not ML efficiency. | Relevance: This is the cheapest evidenced fix for generator collapse: abstract the problem to its relational structure and search analogous domains, rather than retrieving by topic. It complements our noisy-prompt and novelty-request finding (3-5x more families) and is a candidate 'diverse generator' module. The case studies show gains that hold up under execution. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11258
- MOOSE-Star: tractable training of P(hypothesis|background) via inspiration decomposition (with GIANTS as a related RL approach) (Zonglin Yang, Lidong Bing (ICML 2026). Related: GIANTS, He-Yueya, ..., Finn, Brunskill, Goodman, arXiv 2604.09793 2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: MOOSE-Star trains the discovery process as separate subtasks: inspiration retrieval (IR) and hypothesis composition (HC). A motivation-guided hierarchical search over a clustered corpus makes retrieval logarithmic in corpus size, and bounded composition is trained on noisy proxy inspirations. The TOMATO-Star dataset holds 108,717 decomposed papers (38,400 A800 GPU-hours) in biology, chemistry, medicine, psychology and cognitive science, split by time: training Jan 2020-Sep 2025, test Oct 2025 (1,658 papers). GIANTS instead trains a 4B model with RL to predict a downstream paper's core insight from its parent papers (17k examples, 8 domains), using LM-judge similarity as the reward. | Result: Brute-force end-to-end sampling (R1-Distilled-Qwen-32B, GPT-4o M3 rubric, pass at >=8/12) collapses as more inspirations are needed: 9.46% (k=1), 1.17% (k=2), 0.23% (k=3), about 41x. Decomposed HC per step stays at 18.88%, 22.53% and 25.75%. IR accuracy (1 positive among 15, about 35% hard negatives, random 6.70%): trained MS-IR-7B 54.37% vs base 28.42%; Gemini-3-Pro 54.89%; GPT-5.4 51.50%; GPT-4o 48.37%; Claude-Sonnet-4.6 45.02%. With all-random negatives accuracy rises to 78-91%, so hard negatives drive the difficulty. Hierarchical search cuts IR calls from 218.00 to 67.78. On composition, frontier models still lead (GPT-5.4 M3 score 7.82 vs MS-7B 5.02). GIANTS-4B scores 34% higher (relative) than gemini-3-pro on similarity, and SciJudge-30B prefers its insights in 68% of pairs. | Limits: Success means similarity to the held-out paper's hypothesis as judged by an LLM rubric; rediscovery of what was published is not a measure of value. The domains are natural sciences, not ML. GIANTS optimises a judge-similarity proxy, which risks reward hacking. | Relevance: This is the strongest quantitative evidence that ideas needing several inspirations are almost unreachable by sampling alone. It supports a harness that retrieves one inspiration at a time and composes, rather than one bigger prompt. Even 2026 frontier models only reach about 50% on hard-negative retrieval, so retrieval is the bottleneck. The time-sliced decomposition recipe could turn OptiLex's knowledge base into a training and evaluation set for ML efficiency. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03756
- Reconstruction: blind recovery of research ideas from pre-publication bibliographies (Shaolong Chen, ..., Qingqing Mao, Ritankar Das 2026 (17 Aug; v3 24 Aug)) [benchmark_rediscovery]: Models see only a seed paper's frozen pre-publication bibliography (temporal citation cutoff, anonymised reference IDs, no web search) and propose hypotheses. An independent LLM judge scores a binary Match against the held-out idea. The multi-agent variant has 4 frontier models write 5 hypotheses each, aligns them by slot, runs cross-model review, and then a Swiss tournament with conflict-of-interest recusal and order debiasing to pick 5 of the 20. | Result: 643 papers across 6 domains. Seven frontier single models reach Match rates of about 3-15%; the best is Claude-Opus-4.8 at 13.3% +/- 2.3%. The top-4 pipeline (Claude-Opus-4.8, GPT-5.6-Sol-Pro, Kimi-K3, GLM-5.2) reaches ML 22.9%, astronomy 36.5%, chemistry 38.4%, materials 40.1%, medicine 41.6%, physics 36.4%; mean 36.0% +/- 6.1%, about 2.4x the best single model. | Limits: The authors state they do not yet report judge-human agreement. The multi-agent pipeline selects 5 of 20 hypotheses and is not token-normalised, so collaboration is confounded with inference-time scaling. Rediscovery rewards the consensus next step. | Relevance: This is current-generation evidence (Claude Opus 4.8) that bibliography-grounded single-model ideation rarely recovers the actual next idea, and ML is the hardest domain at 22.9%. The protocol is ready for OptiLex to reuse as its leakage-controlled rediscovery evaluation. Cross-model review plus a tournament is a harness pattern worth testing, but only on a compute-matched basis. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16645
- Link-prediction ideation: Science4Cast, then Impact4Cast, then SciMuse (v4, 24 Sep 2026) (Xuemei Gu, Mario Krenn et al. (MPL Erlangen) 2022-2026) [expert_human_rating]: Semantic networks are built from concept co-occurrence in papers. Science4Cast used 143,000 arXiv AI papers, 64,719 concepts and 17,892,352 edges; Impact4Cast used 21M papers plus a citation-impact network. ML link prediction then forecasts which concept pairs will connect or become high-impact. SciMuse gives GPT-4 knowledge-graph-selected concept pairs (random, or predicted high-citation) plus the researcher's recent paper titles, and asks for personalised project ideas. | Result: Link prediction works as forecasting: new-link AUC up to 0.93, where hand-crafted network features beat end-to-end ML (Science4Cast); impact AUC above 0.9 (Impact4Cast, MLST 2025). As an idea source it did not help. In SciMuse v4, 110 research-group leaders gave 4,451 ratings: mean interest 2.350 +/- 0.034 for high-citation-predicted pairs, 2.446 +/- 0.035 for random pairs and 2.414 +/- 0.036 for the titles-only baseline; ideas rated 4-5 were 23.3%, 25.2% and 26.2%. The authors conclude the concept pairs 'do not improve expert-rated interest over a titles-only GPT baseline'. High-citation-predicted pairs trended lower (1.94 sigma). Predicting interest: GPT-4o zero-shot AUC 67.44% (top-5 precision 45.76%); a graph-feature neural network AUC 63.20%. In a supplement, 3 leaders rating 180 pairs preferred GPT-4o ideas over GPT-4 ideas 60.56% vs 31.1%. | Limits: The main study used GPT-4 (2023-24). Interest in a personalised idea is not realised value. The v4 authors state that semantic distance is inherently coupled to the rater's self-relevance, so the negative distance-interest correlation (rho = -0.223) is confounded. | Relevance: This is the largest direct expert test of graph-selected concept pairs as the knowledge base's interface to the generator: no gain over titles only, and a model upgrade moved interest more than graph grounding did. Graphs look more useful for forecasting, controlling or sampling combinations than as generator context. The v4 text also supports the user's confound critique of the earlier 'distant fields rated lower' reading. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.17044
- Learned inspiration retrieval: TCA-SIR, MUSES, RATIO, HyBIRD (Suzuki & Banaei-Kashani (TCA-SIR); Pandey, Kwon, Yu (MUSES, 2609.00313); Sharon & Hope (RATIO, 2608.27394); Yang et al. (HyBIRD, 2606.28336) 2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: These systems represent how a candidate paper transfers to the target problem, not just topical similarity. TCA-SIR LoRA-tunes Llama-3.1-8B to write a target-conditioned abstract principle and reasoning trace for each (target, candidate) pair and to predict graded transferability. RATIO types retrieval by ideation move (Address, Broaden, Specify), using discourse-marker distant supervision over millions of CS full texts with a 2026 test split. MUSES is a prospective benchmark for retrieving author-confirmed 'intellectual roots' over 2.33M papers. HyBIRD adds hyperbolic 'bridge' structure over a frozen dense retriever for methodology inspiration. | Result: TCA-SIR on the ResearchBench test240 split: HitRate@top4% 0.481 +/- 0.045 vs 0.371 for direct LLM retrieval (the abstract reports more than 10 points over MOOSE-Chem); @top20% 0.850 vs 0.750. Untrained abstraction prompts do worse than direct LLM retrieval. MUSES: the best retriever (SPECTER2 multi-centroid) falls from Hit@100 0.534 on CiteNext to 0.424 on CiteNew and 0.171 on author-endorsed roots of new work, a 3.1x decline; about half of broad-tier instances are still unsolved at K=1,000. HyBIRD: 59.034 mAP; hyperbolic geometry helps as structure over a dense anchor, not as a replacement. RATIO: operation-specific fine-tuning boosts retrievers substantially (numbers not verified). | Limits: All labels are the inspirations authors actually cited or endorsed (retrospective). TCA-SIR needs one LLM generation per target-candidate pair, which is expensive at scale. None of these tests downstream execution. | Relevance: Retrieving useful inspiration is a learnable problem of its own. Off-the-shelf embeddings and LLM re-ranking favour popular, topically close papers and miss generative roots. OptiLex's knowledge base should store mechanism-level transferable abstractions and support typed moves (broaden, specify, address). | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28498
- Outcome-trained idea scorers: HARPA (testability-driven literature grounding) and Wen et al. outcome prediction (Vasu, Jansen, Siangliulue, Sarasua, Bernstein, Clark, Dalvi Mishra (HARPA). Wen et al., NeurIPS 2025, arXiv 2506.00794 2025) [executed_computationally]: HARPA progressively generalises a seed hypothesis and searches S2ORC with each generalisation, extracts the space of key variables, and converges on a testable, under-studied hypothesis (GPT-4o backbone). HARPA-Scorer (Qwen-7B) is distilled from Claude-Sonnet-4 rubric judgements and RLVR-trained on 3,459 success/failure pairs from 1,222 proposals executed by CodeScientist. Wen et al. fine-tune GPT-4.1 together with a paper-retrieval agent to predict which of two ideas wins on benchmarks. | Result: HARPA vs AI-Researcher (40 proposals each, 12 experts): feasibility +0.78 (p<0.05), grounding +0.85 (p<0.01). CodeScientist execution: 20 vs 11 successes and 16 vs 21 failures out of 40. HARPA-Scorer accuracy about 0.80 vs 0.52 for an untrained scorer (+0.28 absolute, 53% relative) on 186 success-failure pairs; agreement with the oracle is 0.80 on execution success but only 0.55 on interestingness. Wen et al.: 1,585 idea pairs published after the base model's cutoff; the system reaches 77% on the full test set and 64.4% vs 48.9% for 25 human experts in NLP. Off-the-shelf o3 does no better than random even with the same retrieval, and the system reaches 63.6% on unpublished projects. | Limits: CodeScientist runs are small code experiments, and 'success' means a faithful run with a clear verdict, not impact. Wen et al. compare pairs of ideas that come from the same paper. | Relevance: This is the best evidence that literature context alone does not make a judge predictive, while training on outcomes does. OptiLex's evaluation layer should log every cheap executed pilot (small LM runs) as training data from day one; that is the path around our 53% self-agreement. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00620
- ResearchBench and the MOOSE-Chem family (MOOSE-Chem, Chem2, Chem3) (Zonglin Yang, Wanhao Liu, ... (Shanghai AI Lab / NTU). ResearchBench: Findings of ACL 2026. MOOSE-Chem: ICLR 2025. Chem2: NeurIPS 2025. Chem3: arXiv 2505.17873 2024-2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: The framework assumes a hypothesis is a research background plus a few inspirations, and splits discovery into three tasks: retrieve inspirations (the LLM screens titles and abstracts in rounds), compose (evolutionary mutate, refine and recombine), and rank (pairwise comparison with positions swapped). ResearchBench has 1,386 papers from 2024 onward across 12 disciplines. Each paper has 75 candidates: 2-3 ground-truth inspirations plus 25 negatives at each of 3 distances (citation or semantic neighbours, same discipline, other discipline). Chem2 adds hierarchical search for fine-grained hypotheses; Chem3 adds experiment-guided ranking against a simulator. | Result: Retrieval (ResearchBench): keeping the top 4% (3 of 75; random 4%), GPT-4o finds 45.65% of ground-truth inspirations; at 20% it finds 83.43%. Of the errors, 56% came from judging by title or abstract overlap and 23% from missing other-discipline papers that use different terms. Ranking (1 ground truth vs 15 negatives): Claude 3.5 Sonnet 81.59%, DeepSeek-V3 80.99%, GPT-4o 59.60%. Under order swap Llama-3.1-8B contradicts itself 91.67% of the time; Claude 3.5 Sonnet 19.17%. MOOSE-Chem (51 chemistry papers, 3,000-paper corpus): GPT-4o-judged Top MS 4.020 vs SciMON 2.549, MOOSE 2.882 and Qi et al. 2.686. In the expert-rated copilot setting, top MS was 5 for 0/51 papers, 4 for 2, and 3 for 19; GPT-4o scores ran 1-2 points above experts. Chem2: hierarchical search soft recall 40.4% vs 16.6% for greedy. Three identical judges beat one (40.4 vs 32.4), and repeated strongest-model judges beat a mixed committee. Chem3: 15.2 simulated trials to hit the target vs 28.6 for pre-experiment ranking and 32.5 for random. | Limits: Ground-truth inspirations are the authors' own path, not the only valid one. LLM-judged match scores are inflated relative to experts. Chem3's feedback is simulated, not a real lab. Candidate pools (75) are much smaller than a real knowledge base. | Relevance: This is the canonical retrieve-compose-rank decomposition and the only large cross-discipline test of it. It shows LLM rankers need position swapping and ensembles of identical judges, which is directly relevant to our 53% judge self-agreement. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21248
- Scideator plus the Idea Novelty Checker (purpose/mechanism/evaluation facets) (Radensky, Shahid, Fok, Siangliulue, Weld, Hope (AI2 / UW). ACM CAIS '26. Novelty checker: arXiv 2506.22026 2024-2026 (v7 May 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Scideator extracts purpose, mechanism and evaluation facets from the user's papers and from analogous papers retrieved at controlled distances (very near, near, far, very far), and users recombine the facets. The novelty checker runs keyword and snippet retrieval, filters by embedding, re-ranks with facet-based RankGPT, and classifies with o3-mini using expert-labelled in-context examples. | Result: Within-subjects study with 22 CS researchers: Creativity Support Index median 70.5 vs 61.0 for the same LLM without facets (Wilcoxon p<.01). Retrieval ablation (58 ideas with known overlapping work, found in the top 10): full system 89.66%, general-relevance re-ranking 13.79%, embedding only 10.34%, snippet 8.62%, keyword 5.17%. Classifier (26 novel / 17 not novel): expert examples with reasoning 81% accuracy (F1 0.79), zero-shot 68%, the AI Scientist novelty prompt 47%. Participants used near facets far more than far facets. The companion paper reports about 13% higher agreement than existing novelty checkers. | Limits: Creativity support is self-reported, not an outcome of the ideas. Test sets are tiny. The domains are HCI and NLP only. | Relevance: Facets are the best-evidenced knowledge-base schema for novelty checking, and generic novelty prompts are near chance. OptiLex's novelty critic should retrieve and re-rank at the facet level with calibrated examples. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14634
- Literature meets data: HypoGeniC, HypoRefine and HypoBench (Haokun Liu, Yangqiaoyu Zhou, ..., Chenhao Tan (UChicago) 2024-2026) [executed_computationally]: HypoGeniC generates hypotheses from labelled examples and updates a hypothesis bank with a UCB-style bandit reward. HypoRefine alternates refinement rounds between literature summaries (up to 10 papers per task) and data. Each hypothesis is scored by using it to predict held-out data, including out-of-distribution sets. | Result: Across 5 datasets (GPT-4o-mini, Llama-3.1-70B), literature plus data beats few-shot by 8.97%, literature only by 15.75% and data only by 3.37%. In a 60-participant human decision study, the hypotheses raised human accuracy by 7.44% on deception detection and 14.19% on AI-generated-content detection. HypoGeniC alone: +31.7% on synthetic data and +13.9, 3.3 and 24.9% on three real datasets vs few-shot. HypoBench (194 datasets): the best methods recover only 38.8% of ground-truth hypotheses on hard synthetic tasks. | Limits: The tasks are classification-style social science. The hypotheses are predictive rules, not mechanisms or methods, and the models are older. | Relevance: This is clean executed evidence that literature helps most when paired with data-driven verification. The OptiLex analogue is to pair the knowledge base with cheap empirical signals (small-run logs, scaling-law fits) and treat them as 'data' when refining hypotheses. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.17309
- Robin (FutureHouse), with Google Co-Scientist as context (FutureHouse (Robin; Nature 2026, s41586-026-10652-y). Google (Co-Scientist; Nature 2026, s41586-026-10644-y) 2025-2026) [lab_validated]: Robin's literature agents, Crow (concise) and Falcon (deep), both built on PaperQA2, read about 151 papers on disease mechanisms and about 400 on RPE phagocytosis and AMD therapeutics. They proposed mechanisms and 30 drug candidates, which were ranked by an LLM-judged pairwise tournament (Bradley-Terry) before wet-lab tests. The Finch agent analysed the lab data and proposed the next round. | Result: Robin identified ripasudil, a ROCK inhibitor, as a novel dry-AMD candidate. On average 7.25 of the LLM judge's top 10 overlapped with the experts' top 10. Robin's automated analysis reported that ripasudil raised phagocytosis 7.5-fold vs DMSO; human re-analysis of the same data gave 1.75-fold. Co-Scientist (Nature, May 2026) reports lab-validated AML drug repurposing, liver-fibrosis targets in organoids and a rediscovery of the cf-PICI mechanism; those details were not verified here. | Limits: Few candidates were tested (5 in round 1, 10 in round 2), and there was no controlled comparison against generation without literature grounding. The automated analysis overstated the effect size. | Relevance: This shows real-world payoff from literature-agent grounding plus tournament ranking. But the error that slipped through came from the analysis and evaluation layer, not from ideation, so OptiLex's evaluation layer needs independent re-analysis. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13400
- Knowledge-graph-grounded verification: KG-CoI, TruthHypo/KnowHD, HypoKG (Guangzhi Xiong, Aidong Zhang et al. (UVA). HypoKG: Okonkwo, Okunoye, Arpinar (arXiv 2609.12260) 2024-2026) [benchmark_rediscovery]: KG-CoI retrieves relations from a knowledge graph (PubTator3) plus literature, generates a chain of ideas, and checks each reasoning step against the graph to flag hallucinations. The benchmark removes real graph edges and asks models to predict the relation (stimulate, inhibit or none). | Result: On 300 masked-edge instances, GPT-4o scored 73.33% direct, 74.33% with CoT, 75.67% with RAG and 86.00% with KG-CoI; Llama-3.1-8B went from 47.00% direct to 68.67% with RAG and 70.33% with KG-CoI. TruthHypo (IJCAI 2025): the KnowHD groundedness score filters out untruthful hypotheses. HypoKG (Sep 2026; 13,200 hypotheses from 6 LLMs): prompts giving only the source and disease endpoint got the highest rubric scores but were the least grounded, and shuffling the steps of the given path cut grounding (delta = -0.793, p<0.001). | Limits: Masked-link prediction measures recovery of known facts, not novelty. The domain is biomedicine and the benchmarks are small. | Relevance: This is where graphs clearly pay off: as verifiers of factual and mechanistic steps in a soundness critic, not as a source of novelty. HypoKG also shows rubric judges reward ideas that are compelling but under-grounded. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.02382
- Structured retrieval context: Nova (planned retrieval) and Chain-of-Ideas (progression chains) (Hu et al. (Nova). Li et al., DAMO Academy (Chain-of-Ideas, arXiv 2410.13185) 2024) [expert_human_rating]: Nova has the LLM plan what outside knowledge to retrieve for each seed idea, and iterates: 15 seeds, tripled each round, reach about 405, which are clustered to 100. Chain-of-Ideas orders retrieved papers into chains of progressive development, predicts future trends, and is evaluated with Idea Arena, a pairwise Elo scheme. | Result: Nova produced 3.4x more unique novel ideas than without planning (deduplicated at MiniLM cosine 0.8) and at least 2.5x more top-rated ideas in a Claude-3.5-Sonnet Swiss tournament over 170 seed papers; a panel of 10 experts also rated them. Chain-of-Ideas beat the second-best baselines by 108 Elo (GPT-4o judge) and 56 Elo (human judges); GPT-4o and humans agreed 70.8% of the time. Removing the chain structure dropped the average ablation score from 50 to 42.4 (GPT-4o-judged). Cost is at least $0.50 per idea plus experiment design. | Limits: Evaluation is mostly by LLM judge with small human panels, uses 2024 models, and includes no execution. | Relevance: Planning retrieval and ordering context by progression look cheap and helpful by judge ratings, but they sit in a lower evidence tier than execution- or expert-validated representations. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.14255
- Human-aware AI and 'alien' hypotheses (Sourati & Evans) (Jamshid Sourati, James A. Evans (Nature Human Behaviour 7:1682-1696) 2023) [retrospective_prediction]: A hypergraph links papers, authors, materials or drugs, and properties. Random walks through author nodes simulate which inferences are cognitively accessible to experts, and the resulting embeddings predict future discoveries. Inverting the availability term produces 'alien' hypotheses that are plausible but unlikely to be pursued soon. | Result: Modelling the distribution of human expertise improved prediction of future discoveries by up to 400% over content-only models, especially where literature is sparse (abstract). Secondary sources give per-domain gains (about 100% for energy-materials properties, 43% for drug repurposing, 260% for COVID-19 therapies); those were not verified in the primary. | Limits: The model is pre-LLM. It predicts what humans will discover, not what is valuable. The quality of alien hypotheses is judged through proxies. | Relevance: This is the foundation for modelling who is already working on what, and the Alien Space paper operationalises it for LLM research ideas. OptiLex could add an availability map to its knowledge base to target underexplored but coherent combinations. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.01495
- Graph and academic-graph context generators: ResearchAgent, SciPIP, SciAgents, Graph2Idea, Graphs of Research, GYWI, Idea-Catalyst (Baek et al. (ResearchAgent, NAACL 2025); Wang et al. (SciPIP); Ghafarollahi & Buehler (SciAgents); Li et al. (Graph2Idea, 2606.09105); Gao et al. (GoR, 2605.14790); Xie & Liang (GYWI, 2602.22215); Kargupta et al. (Idea-Catalyst, 2603.12226) 2024-2026) [llm_judge_only]: Each system gives the generator a different graph-based context. ResearchAgent: citation-graph neighbours plus an entity store. SciPIP: semantic, entity and citation retrieval at multiple granularities. SciAgents: ontological graph paths. Graph2Idea: a target-centred graph of triples. GoR: a citation-evolution DAG used as fine-tuning supervision. GYWI: a co-author graph with GraphRAG. Idea-Catalyst: reformulates the problem in domain-agnostic terms for interdisciplinary retrieval. | Result: ResearchAgent (GPT-4, judged by GPT-4) scored problem/method/experiment 4.52/4.28/4.18 in full, 4.35/4.13/4.02 without entities and 4.26/4.08/3.97 without references; human-model Spearman was 0.49-0.64. SciPIP (12 human raters): 27.22% (NLP) and 22.41% (CV) of ideas scored novelty >=4, and it won 80% of novelty comparisons vs AI Scientist. Graph2Idea: novelty 0.45 to 0.52 and feasibility 0.22 to 0.28 under an automatic protocol. Idea-Catalyst: +21.38% novelty and +16.22% insightfulness vs Guided Dual (gpt-oss-120b judge); 6 PhD users rated novelty 3.22/5 and usefulness 3.00/5. SciAgents: qualitative case studies only. | Limits: Nearly all gains are LLM-judged, baselines differ across papers, effects are small, and nothing is executed. | Relevance: The evidence that graph context beats good retrieval as generator input is weak, which matches SciMuse v4. These should be lower priority for OptiLex than facets, idea atoms, analogies and outcome-trained judges. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.07738
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: SciMuse v4 (posted 2026-09-24): 110 research-group leaders gave 4,451 ratings. Knowledge-graph-selected concept pairs did not beat a titles-only GPT baseline on interest: high-citation-predicted 2.350, random 2.446, titles-only 2.414. High-citation-predicted pairs trended lower (1.94 sigma). The authors state the distance-interest correlation is coupled to rater self-relevance. (caveat: 1. The 1.94σ gap compares high-citation-predicted pairs with random pairs, not with the titles-only baseline. It is below the usual 2σ significance threshold, so it is only a weak trend. The finding is a null result: knowledge-graph pairs did not improve interest. It does not show that the graph hurt interest.
2. Humans did the judging. Each leader rated ideas personalized to their own research (48 per person, 16 per condition) on a 1-5 interest scale. Execution, lab work and LLM judges played no role, and only interest was rated, not feasibility or eventual impact.
3. GPT-4 generated the ideas, and the ratings were collected around January 2024. v4 is a 2026 revision of an old experiment, so it does not say how current models perform. v4 also mentions a small GPT-5.2 comparison with human-written ideas.
4. The "distance" is the semantic distance between the evaluator and the proposed collaborator (k=-0.309, ρ=-0.223, a negative relationship with interest). The authors say this is confounded with self-relevance: the design cannot separate the two. They do not measure how strongly the two are linked, so "coupled" should be read as "confounded", not as a measured result.
5. Overall ratings were low: mean 2.40, the most common rating was 1, and 24.9% of ideas got a 4 or 5. The paper does report that graph features and zero-shot LLM ranking can predict which ideas will be rated interesting, so the graph still has value for picking ideas after they are generated.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: When asked directly for LLM-research ideas, Claude Opus 4.7's ten most-used idea atoms appear in 95.7% of its generated combinations (effective coverage 10.5%). An availability-aware atom sampler gets 34.3% and 72.5%. Blind human raters (40 reviews, 10 raters) give both the same overall quality (4.03 each). In an autoresearch loop, Claude's reports drift from 2/10 to 8/10 on interpretability. => CORRECTED: In Artiles et al., "The Alien Space of Science" (arXiv 2603.01092, v2 May 2026), Claude Opus 4.7 was queried 300 times. Each time it saw the full vocabulary of 273 pre-extracted idea atoms and was asked to pick a novel but feasible 3-atom combination. It was not asked for free-form research ideas. Its ten most-used atoms appeared in 95.7% of combinations, with effective atom coverage exp(H)/|A| of 10.5%. The Alien sampler (top-300 triples ranked by F-beta at beta=0.7 over coherence and unavailability) scored 34.3% and 72.5%. Gemini 3.1 Pro scored 76.7% and 20.6%, and uniform random triples reached 86.6% effective coverage. In a human survey (40 blinded reviews from 10 raters with ML/CS postgraduate degrees, 10 ideas per method, 5-point scales), Alien and Claude tied on overall quality: 4.03 +/- 0.11 vs 4.03 +/- 0.23. Each method then had 10 ideas run through a fixed autoresearch pipeline (Claude Code with claude-sonnet-4-6 for all methods). Among Claude-sourced ideas, the count in the SAE / feature-level mechanistic-interpretability cluster went from 2/10 original titles to 8/10 final reports. Gemini went 4/10 to 5/10, Random 0/10 to 1/10, and Alien stayed at 0/10. (caveat: (1) Task framing: the LLM baselines were not doing open-ended ideation. They picked 3 atoms from a supplied list of 273 atoms (order shuffled each query), and Claude Opus 4.7 then turned every method's combinations into prose. The diversity numbers therefore measure atom selection within the authors' own vocabulary, built from 16,068 LLM papers. (2) The same 34.3% for Alien shows up as a different metric in Appendix Table 13 ("Top-10%": share of selections taken by the top 10% of atoms). In that table Claude's figure is 88.7%, not 95.7%, so the metrics are easy to mix up. Coverage there is Claude 31.1% of atoms vs Alien 91.9%. (3) The human evaluation is very small: 10 ideas per method, and each rater saw 4 ideas. A tie at 4.03 with SE of 0.11 and 0.23 means "not distinguishable", not "equal". Claude scored higher on promise (4.40 vs 4.10) and coherence. The autoresearch reports themselves were judged by a blind LLM (Bradley-Terry over 224 pairwise comparisons: Alien 0.633, Claude 0.574 average rank percentile), not by humans. The idea-level pairwise tournament also used an LLM judge (GPT-5.5). (4) "Claude's reports" is misleading. Every autoresearch run used Claude Code with Sonnet 4.6. The 2/10 to 8/10 drift is for ideas that Opus 4.7 selected, counted by coding reports into a narrow SAE / feature-level mech-interp cluster (generic "interpretable" wording did not count), with n=10. The paper does not say who did the coding. No wet-lab or real-world validation beyond bounded A100 experiments. (5) Alien is really a deterministic top-300 ranking, not stochastic sampling.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Brute-force end-to-end hypothesis sampling pass rate falls from 9.46% to 1.17% to 0.23% as the number of required inspirations rises from 1 to 3; decomposed per-step composition stays at 18.9-25.8%. Picking the 1 true inspiration from 15 candidates with hard negatives: Gemini-3-Pro 54.89%, GPT-5.4 51.50%, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 45.02%, a trained 7B 54.37%. (caveat: 1) Everything is judged by an LLM. No human or lab validation. In Table 5, R1-Distilled-Qwen-32B both generates the samples and judges them with the M3 rubric (pass means at least 8 of 12, a recall-style score for motivation, mechanism and methodology against the paper's ground-truth hypothesis deltas). Tables 2 and 3 use GPT-4o as the judge. So "pass" means "reconstructs the published paper's hypothesis according to an LLM judge". It does not mean the hypothesis is novel or valid.
2) BF and HC are not like-for-like. A BF sample has to satisfy all k target deltas at once with a single hypothesis. HC is scored per step, against only its own delta. Part of the ~41x collapse is built into how the metric is defined. The table exists to compare rejection-sampling yield for building RFT training data. It is not a benchmark of discovery quality.
3) HC does not "stay" flat. It rises with k (18.88, then 22.53, then 25.75), and the paper attributes this to context drift, which it analyzes in an appendix. IR per step is 27.84 / 25.48 / 24.22.
4) The IR pool mixes easy random negatives with hard negatives (keyword or embedding similar). It is not all hard negatives. Under the "All Hard" ablation (Table 6) the ranking changes: Claude-Sonnet-4.6 58.69, Gemini-3-Pro 54.66, GPT-5.4 51.34, MS-IR-7B 53.42. With all-random negatives Claude scores 91.44. The paper also admits some negatives could be valid alternative inspirations.
5) The trained 7B (MS-IR-7B) is R1-Distilled-Qwen-7B (base score 28.42%) fine-tuned on about 152k examples that were rejection-sampled from R1-Distilled-Qwen-32B. It is evaluated on the same in-distribution TOMATO-Star task format. The multitask MS-7B scores 54.34%.
6) The 9.46/1.17/0.23 figures are per-attempt yields of a 32B teacher. They are not success rates of frontier models.)
Gaps:
  - No head-to-head, compute-matched comparison of knowledge representations exists: flat abstracts vs facets vs knowledge-graph triples vs idea atoms vs structural analogies vs inspiration papers, with the same generator, the same budget and realised (executed) outcomes. Every paper uses a different baseline and judge, from GPT-4o scores to CSI self-reports.
  - Nobody reports cost or energy per validated idea. There are scattered compute facts (MOOSE-Star IR calls 218 to 68, Chain-of-Ideas at least $0.50 per idea, Reconstruction not token-normalised, AR 2 calls vs 1), but no 'novel-and-validated ideas per GPU-hour or kWh' frontier. That frontier is exactly OptiLex's core metric.
  - Inspiration retrieval is only trained and evaluated against the inspirations authors cited or endorsed (ResearchBench, TCA-SIR, MUSES, MOOSE-Star). No benchmark labels inspirations by counterfactual downstream utility, i.e. whether retrieving X led to an idea that executed better.
  - No system feeds executed outcomes back into the knowledge representation itself. HARPA and Wen et al. train scorers on outcomes, and MOOSE-Chem3 uses only simulated feedback. Nobody updates the atom coherence/availability models, facet index or retriever from execution results.
  - No one has tested diversity-preserving refinement. Alien Space shows collapse appears during autoresearch loops (Claude 2/10 to 8/10 interpretability), but no one has tried atom- or mechanism-locked fixers and critics and measured executed outcomes.
  - There is no time-sliced rediscovery-plus-execution benchmark for ML efficiency. None asks whether a system limited to pre-T literature proposes data-, compute- or energy-efficiency techniques that later proved out, and whether it reproduces their gains in small runs. Reconstruction's ML domain is the hardest at 22.9% match, and it has no execution.
  - Negative-results knowledge bases are nearly untested for ideation. The only 2026 study is a single-author paper on shared failure memory for AutoResearch, on PDE tasks with no verified numbers. Nobody mines published non-replications or failed ablations in LLM efficiency to steer generation away from dead ends.
  - Analogical and interdisciplinary retrieval has been tested in biomedicine (AR), HCI (Idea-Catalyst) and materials (SciAgents), but never for LLM training efficiency with executed outcomes (e.g., analogies from compression, statistical physics or neural energy efficiency).
  - Judge calibration in this domain is missing. Novelty classifiers range from 47% (AI Scientist prompt) to 81-90% (facet-based) on test sets of 43-58 items, and LLM rediscovery scores run 1-2 points above experts. No calibrated novelty or soundness judge exists for ML efficiency. Fixes such as identical-judge ensembles (MOOSE-Chem2) and position swapping (ResearchBench) have not been tested on Opus-class judges against a 53% self-agreement baseline.
  - Availability maps have never been exposed to a frontier model interactively. In Alien Space, Claude only decodes the sampled atoms. No one has tested letting an Opus-class model reason over explicit coherence/availability maps or facet graphs as tools, and measured whether that beats either component alone.
Surprises:
  - SciMuse v4 was posted on 2026-09-24, the day before this search. It now says graph-selected concept pairs do not beat a titles-only GPT baseline in 4,451 expert ratings, that high-impact-predicted pairs trend slightly lower, and that the distance-interest correlation is confounded with rater self-relevance, which supports the user's critique. In its supplement, a model upgrade (GPT-4o over GPT-4, preferred 60.56% vs 31.1%) moved interest more than graph grounding did.
  - The collapse failures come from current 2026 models, not outdated ones, and the mechanism persists. Claude Opus 4.7's top-10 atom concentration is 95.7%. Unprompted Claude Sonnet 4.5 finds novel solutions only 1.6% of the time vs 50.4% with analogical reasoning. Collapse gets worse inside refinement loops.
  - Frontier models are no better than a fine-tuned 7B model at inspiration retrieval with hard negatives: Gemini-3-Pro 54.89%, GPT-5.4 51.50%, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 45.02%, trained MS-IR-7B 54.37%. In ResearchBench, retrieval plateaus around 70B parameters while ranking keeps improving with scale.
  - Retrieval does not rescue judges. Off-the-shelf o3 with the same paper-retrieval augmentation is no better than random at predicting which idea wins empirically, while a fine-tuned GPT-4.1 reaches 77% and beats 25 experts in NLP (64.4% vs 48.9%).
  - In MOOSE-Chem2, a committee of repeated instances of the strongest model gave a better search signal than a mixed-model committee. That runs against the 'diverse judges' intuition. Reconstruction's cross-model gain points the other way, but it is not compute-matched.
  - LLM judges systematically over-credit rediscovery and ungrounded appeal. GPT-4o's matched scores ran 1-2 points above chemistry experts (0/51 full rediscoveries by expert rating). HypoKG found endpoint-only prompts earn the highest rubric scores while being the least evidence-grounded.
  - In lab-validated Robin, the error came from the automated analysis layer, not from ideation: it reported 7.5-fold for ripasudil vs 1.75-fold on human re-analysis of the same data.
  - Scideator's human users mostly ignored far-distance facets as less relevant. Machine-driven far analogy (AR: humans judged it more novel 78% of the time) may add value precisely where human researchers do not go.

## knowledge_memory
- Theorizer: literature-grounded theory synthesis as <LAW, SCOPE, EVIDENCE> tuples, tested against parametric-only generation (Jansen, Clark, Downey, Weld (Ai2 / U. Arizona / UW) 2026 (arXiv 22 Jan 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: PaperFinder retrieves up to ~100 papers per query. A cheap model (GPT-5-mini) fills a query-specific extraction schema per paper. GPT-4.1 then writes laws, each with an explicit scope and evidence. Evaluation is by backtesting: the knowledge cutoff is fixed, and each law's predictions are checked against the full text of 4,554 papers published in the following 6 months (16,713 law-paper evaluations). Precision is supported/(supported+contradicted); recall is the share of laws that any later paper tests. | Result: Accuracy-focused mode: literature-grounded and parametric laws are about equally precise (0.90 vs 0.88), with recall 0.51 vs 0.45. Novelty-focused mode: 61% of literature-grounded predictions were supported by later papers vs 34% for parametric; recall was 16% vs 4%. Parametric generation 'quickly saturate[s] to generating only duplicates'. Comparing the two conditions, 32% of statements were non-duplicate after 40 theories. The literature-grounded method is about 7x more expensive ($0.26 vs $0.04 per theory). Literature-grounded laws were judged LESS novel, which the authors attribute to anchoring. Under novelty mode the model's self-assessed belief overestimates accuracy (67-76% predicted vs 34-61% observed). | Limits: Only the AI/NLP domain. Generator is GPT-4.1, not a current frontier model. Temperature differs between modes (0 vs 1). Support and contradiction are judged by an LLM (GPT-5-mini) against real later papers. Novelty is judged by an LLM (Sonnet 4.5) and only relative to the retrieved papers. Backtesting cannot credit predictions that nobody has tested yet. | Relevance: This is the strongest outcome-anchored evidence that a knowledge base of human research is worth paying for, and specifically in the novelty regime OptiLex cares about: grounding nearly doubles how often novel predictions later hold up. Two things are directly reusable: the law/scope/evidence representation as a scope-preserving layer, and backtesting as a cheap, judge-free way to score the engine. It also reproduces our pilot's collapse finding (parametric saturates to duplicates). Budget the roughly 7x compute cost against the efficiency frontier. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16282
- Fidelity Before Structure: verbatim chunks vs LLM-extracted typed artifacts in agent memory (Tao An 2025-12 (v4 revised 22 Jul 2026)) [executed_computationally]: A controlled swap. The memory store holds either verbatim source chunks or LLM-extracted typed artifacts (facts, summaries, or a semantic graph). Model, retriever, reranker and judge are held fixed. | Result: Verbatim chunks scored 43.9% vs 28.0% for extracted artifacts on LoCoMo, and 67.4% vs 45.4% on LongMemEval-S. A semantic-graph variant did not close the gap. Accuracy tracks how much original text survives. Adding artifacts alongside chunks keeps accuracy; substituting them forfeits the gap. The author attributes the loss to 'lossy distillation, not structure per se'. | Limits: Single author. Conversational-memory benchmarks, not scientific corpora. QA accuracy is scored by an LLM judge against gold answers. Not yet peer-reviewed. | Relevance: This is the core nuance-preservation rule for the polymath knowledge base: always store and retrieve original spans, and use claims, graphs or laws only as an additive index over those spans, never as a replacement. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00821
- ACE (Agentic Context Engineering): incremental delta-updates to an itemized playbook instead of monolithic rewriting (Stanford / SambaNova / UC Berkeley (Zhang et al.) 2025-10 (ICLR 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Three roles: a Generator, a Reflector and a Curator. Rather than rewriting the whole context, the Curator makes small, localized itemized edits to the context. The paper names two failure modes this avoids: 'brevity bias' and 'context collapse'. | Result: Context collapse under monolithic LLM rewriting (AppWorld): at step 60 the context held 18,282 tokens and scored 66.7% accuracy. One step later it had collapsed to 122 tokens and 57.1%, below the 63.7% no-adaptation baseline. ACE averages +10.6% on agent benchmarks and +8.6% on finance. It cuts adaptation latency by 82.3% and rollouts by 75.1% vs GEPA, and token cost by 83.6% vs Dynamic Cheatsheet. | Limits: Domains are agent tasks and finance, not research ideation. Improvements are relative to prompt-optimizers, not to budget-matched plain actors. | Relevance: The compaction policy for the engine's long-running memory (critic lessons, fixer patterns, findings) should be append/patch-only, with no whole-document rewrite summaries. Collapse is invisible until performance falls below having no memory at all. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04618
- Kosmos structured world model (shared state between parallel literature and data-analysis agents) (Edison Scientific / FutureHouse (Mitchener et al.) 2025-11) [expert_human_rating]: Each cycle runs up to 10 parallel literature-search or analysis tasks. Their output summaries are written into a structured world model, which is then queried to propose the next cycle's tasks. Every report claim links to a notebook or a paper. The internal schema of the world model is not published. | Result: Per run: an average of 42,000 lines of code over 166 data-analysis rollouts, and 1,500 papers over 36 literature rollouts (9.8x the code of Robin). Experts judged 79.4% of 102 statements from 3 reports accurate: data-analysis statements 85.5%, literature statements 82.1%, synthesis/interpretation statements 57.9%. Collaborators estimated a 20-cycle run at 6.14 months of their own work (n=7). There are 7 discoveries: 3 reproduce preprinted or unpublished findings and 4 are novel. | Limits: The world model is never ablated, so its causal contribution is unmeasured. Only 102 statements were graded. Discovery value relies on expert time. Runs are stochastic and sensitive to the phrasing of the objective. The paper notes a tendency to invent obscure metrics and to conflate statistical significance with scientific value. | Relevance: This is the reference architecture for a research-agent memory at scale, and it shows where error concentrates: synthesis across sources, which is exactly where cross-discipline analogy lives. Put OptiLex's critics and fixers on the synthesis layer, not on retrieval. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02824
- Domain-stripped computational fingerprints for cross-field solution import (Eryk Kulikowski (JCDL 2026) 2026-09 (v2 15 Sep 2026)) [benchmark_rediscovery]: Each paper is distilled once, by a cached LLM call, into a mechanism skeleton with domain and method names removed, plus controlled computational facets. A cheap embedder then retrieves with a facet-selectable similarity. Example: Kalman filter, Bayesian forecasting and data assimilation all map to one skeleton. | Result: Benchmark of 18 method families across 109 papers. Average precision: abstracts 0.222, skeleton alone 0.513, full fingerprint 0.557. All four trained scientific embedders scored below abstract+TF-IDF; per the paper they encode topical and citation similarity, 'the wrong signal'. On a 501-paper wild corpus, blind LLM judges rated 3 of the top 5 and 8 of the top 30 candidates genuine (0 of 30 random pairs). Four imports were executed, including an open solver reproducing a bespoke clinical dosing engine. | Limits: Small benchmark from a single author. Wild-corpus validation is judged by an LLM. The task is retrieval of known same-mechanism pairs, not whether the imported ideas advance a frontier. | Relevance: This is the most concrete recipe found for the 'polymath' index. Cross-discipline analogy needs a representation keyed on computational structure with domain vocabulary removed. Off-the-shelf scientific embeddings (and probably GraphRAG built on topical entities) retrieve the wrong neighbours. It is cheap because distillation is a one-time cost per paper. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07595
- Analogical Reasoning (AR): parametric cross-domain analogies over shared relational structure (Shen, Druckmann, Zou (Stanford) 2026-05) [executed_computationally]: The model generates an analogy to a problem in a distant domain via explicit object mappings and shared relations, finds solutions in that domain, and transfers them back. No curated knowledge base is used; novelty is checked by retrieving from Semantic Scholar and reranking. | Result: Solution Vendi diversity +90-173%. Binary novelty for Claude Sonnet 4.5: 1.6% with no domain, 21.6% with a plain cross-domain prompt, 50.4% with AR. GPT-5.2: 9.0% to 58.8%. Gemini 3 Flash: about 8.9% to 68.9%. AR solutions were implemented with Claude Code (Opus 4.6) on 4 biomedical tasks, giving about 13x better perturbation-prediction distributional metrics, ρ=0.729 on brain-region inference, and SOTA on 2 oligonucleotide datasets. | Limits: Novelty and analogy quality are scored by an LLM judge (Sonnet 4.5). Only 4 executed tasks, all biomedical. The gains are relative to weak baselines. | Relevance: Much of the 'polymath' capability can be elicited from the model's own weights by forcing relational mapping. The knowledge base may be more valuable for verification and novelty-checking than as an analogy source. AR is a candidate generator strategy for the diversity problem our pilot found. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11258
- PaperGym: targeted cross-domain seed retrieval vs a random diverse-seed control (Yunju Choi, Min Song 2026-05) [llm_judge_only]: Three stages: (1) tool-augmented seed extraction (read/grep/bash over a paper environment); (2) cross-domain seed retrieval by paraphrasing across 7 ML domains; (3) method synthesis from the seeds. The key control arm samples random diverse seeds. | Result: Cross-domain retrieval won more pairwise novelty comparisons than no retrieval or same-domain retrieval. However, it 'shows no significant difference from a random diverse-seed control'. Ideation systems benefit from diverse exposure but do not exploit why a seed was retrieved. | Limits: Rubric-based LLM judges only; no execution or human rating. ML-only. | Relevance: This is the essential control for OptiLex's knowledge-base A/B test: any polymath-KB gain must beat random diverse seeds, not just the no-KB arm. It is consistent with our pilot, where noisy prompts gave 3-5x more mechanism families. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11532
- AInstein: research problem-solving from parametric memory alone (the 'parametric knowledge boundary') (Mishra, Sahu, Pedersoli, Charlin, Dolz, Pal (ÉTS / Mila / ServiceNow) 2025-10 (v2 28 Apr 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Solution-free problem statements are distilled from ICLR papers. Models solve them without retrieval, using iterative critique loops. Solutions are scored for Success and for strict Rediscovery of the published method by 20 experts (96 held-out ICLR 2026 problems) and at scale by an LLM judge (1,214 ICLR 2025 papers). | Result: Success on over 70% of problems, but strict rediscovery at most 19%. Experts rated the LLM solutions more original than the published ones (p=.006). Models 'fail when solutions require cross-domain analogical transfer'. Examples: they miss the spiking/predictive-coding route to GNN calibration and CPU speculative execution as a route to agent acceleration. | Limits: Solvers are GPT-OSS-120B, Qwen-235B and Mistral-24B, not frontier models. The cross-domain boundary is argued from case studies and clustering, not a controlled measurement. Large-scale scoring uses an LLM judge. | Relevance: Motivates the cross-discipline layer. Within-domain proposals come cheaply from the weights; cross-domain bridges are where external structure (fingerprints, AR prompting) should help. Whether the boundary still holds for Opus 5.5 needs replication. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.05432
- Budget-matched evaluation of memory/skill modules (Agent Workflow Memory, ASI, ReasoningBank) (Hajimiri, Aminbeidokhti, Dolz, Ben Ayed, Laradji, Gella, Gontier 2026-06 (rev. 30 Aug 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Counts the test-time tokens consumed by the memory, workflow or skill modules themselves, then compares against a plain actor given the same total token budget. | Result: The vanilla budget-matched actor 'matches or surpasses all three augmentation methods in aggregate success rate while often using fewer total tokens'. Tested on 4 WebArena domains and WorkArena-L1 with Gemini 3 Flash, GPT-5.4-mini and Qwen 3.6-27B. Related result (arXiv 2605.18854): on 60 DiscoveryBench tasks with GPT-4o, none of 8 memory condensers significantly improved hypothesis quality; LLM-based condensers raised token cost 24-94%, while masking tool outputs saved a net 8.6%. | Limits: Web-navigation tasks and mid-size models, not research ideation with a frontier model. The DiscoveryBench study used GPT-4o. | Relevance: This is central to OptiLex's data+compute+energy goal. Headline gains for memory and knowledge bases (ReasoningBank, AWM's +24.6%/+51.1%) largely disappear once memory overhead is charged. Every knowledge-base or memory arm in OptiLex needs a token- and energy-matched baseline. The cheapest compaction (masking stale tool output) is also the one that works. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15017
- PaperQA2 re-ranking + contextual summarization (RCS) and citation traversal; base of Edison Literature/PaperQA3, Robin and Kosmos (Skarlinski et al. (FutureHouse; now Edison Scientific) 2024-09 (PaperQA3 Feb 2026; Robin in Nature 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Evidence is summarized per query, not stored pre-summarized. Each retrieved chunk is summarized and scored for relevance to the specific question; only the top summaries, with source metadata, enter the answer context. A citation-graph traversal tool expands recall. | Result: LitQA2 (248 questions): precision 85.2% and accuracy 66.0%, at or above PhD experts. Removing RCS significantly reduced accuracy. Weaker models used for RCS (GPT-3.5, Llama3-70B) did worse than no summarizer, implying a 'comprehension threshold'. ContraCrow found 2.34 ± 1.99 contradictions per paper; experts agreed with 70%. Downstream, in Robin (https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13400), literature reports over ~400 papers fed 30 candidates, which an LLM tournament ranked.
