Ripasudil was lab-validated: Robin's analysis said 7.5-fold phagocytosis, the human analysis 1.75-fold. | Limits: 2024-era models. QA and retrieval benchmarks are not idea generation. Robin's lab success is a single program in a single disease area, and its own analysis overstated the effect size about 4x relative to the human analysis. | Relevance: Query-time summarization of original spans keeps nuance relative to the question, rather than baking in lossy summaries. It is the practical middle ground between raw spans and a claim knowledge base. The contradiction-mining result shows a claim-level view is useful for critics, e.g. finding contested assumptions in efficiency literature. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.13740
- OpenScholar: citation-grounded synthesis over a 45M-paper passage datastore with a self-feedback loop (Asai et al. (UW / Ai2) 2024-11 arXiv; Nature, 4 Feb 2026) [expert_human_rating]: Passage-level retrieval from 45M open-access papers, reranking, generation, then self-feedback iterations that retrieve more and verify citations. Released as an open 8B model plus a pipeline that can wrap any LLM. | Result: On ScholarQABench (2,967 queries, 208 long-form answers), OpenScholar-8B beats GPT-4o by 5% and PaperQA2 by 7% in correctness. GPT-4o hallucinated citations 78-90% of the time. The pipeline improves GPT-4o's correctness by 12%. Experts preferred OpenScholar-GPT4o over expert-written answers 70% of the time (OpenScholar-8B 51%, GPT-4o 32%). | Limits: Literature QA, not ideation. The Nature version reportedly states +6.1% vs GPT-4o and +5.5% vs PaperQA2; I could not access the Nature text to confirm. Current frontier models hallucinate citations far less than GPT-4o. A July 2026 study (https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20527) finds measured unsupported-citation rates swing from about 3% to 18% depending only on verifier strictness. | Relevance: This is the open, reproducible literature backbone OptiLex could host locally (the Ai2 stack also includes Asta PaperFinder and Theorizer). It supports span-level citation grounding, so every generated idea's premises trace back to passages. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.14199
- DeepScientist Findings Memory: staged records of ideas, verified results and failures across month-long campaigns (Weng et al. (Westlake NLP) 2025-09) [executed_computationally]: A list-style database of thousands of structured findings, each tagged by stage (idea, implemented, progress). Failures are retained. Top-K findings are retrieved to seed each hypothesize-verify-analyze cycle, and only promising ideas are promoted to more expensive validation, in a Bayesian-optimization framing. Parallel paths synchronize into the shared memory. | Result: Over 20,000 GPU-hours: about 5,000 ideas, about 1,100 selected for validation, and 21 that produced progress. Surpassed human SOTA by 183.7%, 1.9% and 7.9% on three tasks. Success rate was about 1-3% with the selection strategy vs effectively 0 when sampling ideas at random. About 60% of failures were implementation errors. Parallel paths sharing memory went from 1 SOTA-surpassing finding at 4 GPUs to 11 at 16 GPUs. | Limits: The memory itself is never ablated; the authors only hypothesize that the scaling comes from the shared memory. Reviewers of its papers flagged missing ablations. Three tasks only. | Relevance: This is the funnel reality check for OptiLex: even with memory, about 1 in 250 generated ideas yields progress, and implementation, not ideation, dominates failures. A negative-results memory is plausible but unproven as a lever. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
- ASI-Arch cognition base: structured entries from ~100 papers retrieved by the Analyst's failure summary (Liu et al. (SII-GAIR) 2025-07) [llm_judge_only]: From about 100 linear-attention papers, 1-3 'cognitions' are extracted per paper, each recording the applicable scenario, the algorithm and the historical context. The extraction prompt is aligned so that scenarios semantically match the Analyst's problem descriptions; the Analyst's summary of the current experiment's shortcomings is the retrieval query. Context is bounded to 5 prior experiment records. | Result: 1,773 experiments over 20,000 GPU-hours produced 106 claimed-SOTA linear-attention variants. LLM-attributed provenance of design components: SOTA gallery 44.8% from self-analysis, 48.6% from human cognition, 6.6% original. Other designs: 37.7% / 51.9% / 10.4%. So the best designs lean more on the system's own experiment analysis. | Limits: Provenance is classified by an LLM and is correlational. The SOTA margins are small, and the paper's framing has been contested. There is no ablation that removes the cognition base. | Relevance: This sits directly in OptiLex's test domain (efficient architectures). Indexing literature by 'problem scenario' and querying with the diagnosed failure of the current experiment is a concrete retrieval key that suits critic/fixer loops. The provenance hint also suggests the system's own experimental findings may matter more than human literature at the frontier. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18074
- Automated LLM Speedrunning Benchmark: which representation of known human innovations can agents actually use (Zhao et al. (Meta FAIR) 2025-06 (NeurIPS 2025)) [executed_computationally]: 19 NanoGPT speedrun records. The agent receives the previous record's script plus a hint in one of three formats (pseudocode, text description, or LLM-written mini-paper) or a combination. The score is the fraction of the human speedup recovered (FSR). | Result: o3-mini (flat search): pseudocode 0.40, text 0.22, mini-paper 0.17. Adding text to pseudocode lowered it to 0.27, and all three to 0.24; the best combination under multi-AIDE was 0.46. DeepSeek-R1 was the reverse, rising from 0.13 with pseudocode to 0.30-0.41 with all three. Claude 3.7 and Gemini 2.5 were largely insensitive. When chaining records, o3-mini recovered about 60% on the 2nd record, about 20% on the 3rd and none by the 4th. | Limits: Early-2025 models, well below Opus 5.5. The dominant failure was buggy implementation, a mechanism that has probably weakened but not vanished. | Relevance: This is OptiLex's own domain. The same knowledge in different formats changed executed outcomes by 2x or more, and more nuance sometimes hurt. The knowledge base should serve an executable-delta view (pseudocode or diffs) next to prose, and the format should be A/B-tested per model rather than assumed. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.22419
- Recursive Language Models (context-as-environment), with vendor file-memory and context-editing tools (Zhang, Kraska, Khattab (MIT); plus Anthropic and Letta 2025-12 (v3 May 2026); Anthropic 29 Sep 2025; Letta 12 Aug 2025) [executed_computationally]: The corpus is held as a variable in a Python REPL. The model greps, slices and recursively calls itself on snippets instead of reading everything into context. This keeps original spans addressable at any scale and avoids 'context rot'. | Result: Handles inputs about 100x beyond the context window. With GPT-5, median gains are +26% vs compaction, +130% vs CodeAct with sub-calls and +13% vs Claude Code. A fine-tuned RLM-Qwen3-8B gains +28.3% over its base. Related: Anthropic's file-based memory tool plus context editing gave +39% on an internal agentic-search eval (context editing alone +29%) and 84% fewer tokens over 100 turns (https://claude.com/blog/context-management). Letta's plain filesystem agent (grep and search over files) scored 74.0% on LoCoMo vs 68.5% for Mem0's graph variant. | Limits: Long-context QA and aggregation tasks, not ideation. The Anthropic and Letta numbers are vendor-reported (Anthropic's from an internal eval). | Relevance: This is the scalable access layer for a polymath knowledge base. Keep verbatim papers on disk, let Opus 5.5 navigate them with tools, and avoid pre-digesting into lossy summaries. It is consistent with the verbatim-beats-extraction and budget-matched findings. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24601
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Theorizer (Ai2, Jan 2026), novelty-focused generation, backtested against 4,554 later papers: 61% of predictions from literature-grounded laws were supported, vs 34% for parametric-only laws. Recall was 16% vs 4%. In accuracy-focused mode precision is equal (0.90 vs 0.88). Literature-grounded generation costs about 7x more ($0.26 vs $0.04 per theory), and parametric generation saturates to duplicates. => CORRECTED: Theorizer (Jansen, Clark, Downey, Weld; Ai2; arXiv 2601.16282, 22 Jan 2026) was backtested against 4,554 papers published after GPT-4.1's knowledge cutoff (16,713 law-paper evaluations, about 5.6 papers per law). In novelty-focused mode, precision was 0.61 for literature-supported theories vs 0.34 for parametric-only ones. Precision here is supporting papers divided by papers that either support or contradict, so it is a paper-level evidence ratio, not "61% of predictions supported". Law-level recall, meaning the share of laws with at least one paper giving direct evidence, was 0.16 vs 0.04. Prediction-level recall was 0.06 vs 0.01. In accuracy-focused mode precision was about the same (0.90 vs 0.88), and law recall was 0.51 vs 0.45. Per theory, literature-supported generation cost $0.26 in LLM API fees vs $0.04, about 6.5x. Retrieval and OCR are not counted in that figure. Parametric-only generation quickly saturates into duplicates. The support judgments were made by an LLM (Claude Sonnet 4.5), not by humans or experiments. (caveat: (1) All support and contradiction judgments came from an LLM-as-judge (Claude Sonnet 4.5) reading later papers. No human validation was reported, and nothing was checked by execution or lab work. (2) The paper covers AI/NLP only: sources were drawn from ACL/EMNLP/AAAI 2023 and NeurIPS 2024, and generation used GPT-4.1. It is not evidence for other sciences. (3) Precision is computed over papers that contained relevant evidence. The low recall (16% of novelty-mode laws had any evidence at all) means most predictions went untested. The 61% therefore applies to a small, self-selected subset. (4) The "7x" figure is really 6.5x, and it counts LLM API cost for schema generation, theory generation and reflection only. Literature retrieval, OCR and extraction costs are excluded, even though they dominate wall-clock time. (5) The duplicate-saturation finding comes from repeated querying. A related figure says 32% of statements were non-duplicates after 40 theories when literature-supported and parametric outputs were compared, so the "32%" should not be read loosely. (6) The name "Theorizer" and the Ai2 affiliation are consistent with the paper and its authors.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Kosmos: experts judged 79.4% of 102 report statements accurate. Data-analysis statements were 85.5% reproducible, literature statements 82.1% validated, and synthesis/interpretation statements only 57.9% accurate. The system reads about 1,500 papers and runs 42,000 lines of code per run via a structured world model that is never ablated. (caveat: Primary source is Mitchener et al. (Edison Scientific/FutureHouse), "Kosmos: An AI Scientist for Autonomous Discovery," arXiv 2511.02824. It is a preprint and has not been peer-reviewed. Every number in the claim matches the paper. The caveats below come from the arXiv HTML full text, which I read through a summarizing fetch tool, so I could not check every sentence myself. (1) The accuracy judgments were made by human expert scientists, not an LLM. The paper does not say how many evaluators there were or what their credentials are. Evaluators did not see Kosmos's code or cited papers and had to reproduce or source each statement independently. Labels were binary: Supported or Refuted. (2) The 102 statements came from only three "representative" reports. The paper does not name them or give per-category counts, so the 57.9% synthesis figure may rest on a small n, and no confidence intervals are reported. (3) 1,500 papers and 42,000 lines of code are per-run averages. They are spread across roughly 200 agent rollouts: about 166 data-analysis rollouts and about 36 literature rollouts, in runs of up to 12 hours / 20 cycles. (4) As far as the full text shows, "never ablated" holds. There is no run without the world model and no baseline ablation. The only comparisons are to prior systems, such as "9.8x increase in code generation compared to Robin" and 8x more iterations. So the paper does not show that the world model causes coherence or accuracy; that is the authors' architectural claim. (5) Other headline figures come from the authors' own collaborators and are self-reported, not independent: "6 months of research time" and "valuable findings scale linearly with cycles".)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In a controlled swap with model, retriever, reranker and judge fixed, verbatim memory chunks beat LLM-extracted typed artifacts: 43.9% vs 28.0% on LoCoMo and 67.4% vs 45.4% on LongMemEval-S. A semantic-graph representation did not close the gap. Adding artifacts alongside chunks preserves accuracy; substituting them loses it. (caveat: The numbers are exact, but a few details change how far the result carries. (1) The "semantic graph" is a 1-hop expansion layer added on top of stored items, not a separate knowledge-graph or triple store; no pure KG representation was tested. The 28.0% and 45.4% artifact figures are already artifacts WITH the graph (CogCanvas default). For artifacts, the graph raised retrieval recall from 25.8% to 71.8% but not answer accuracy; on chunks it did nothing (43.1% vs 43.9%). (2) The LoCoMo figures cover categories 1-3 only (699 questions across 10 conversations). LongMemEval-S uses 500 questions. (3) Setup: answer model GPT-4o; artifacts extracted by gpt-4o-mini; retriever BGE-m3; reranker BGE-reranker-v2-m3; chunks are 512 characters with 100-character overlap. The result may not hold with stronger extractors or other chunk sizes. (4) Evaluation is LLM-as-judge (GPT-4o-mini, temperature 0), checked against humans on 100 questions: 95% agreement, kappa 0.897. Nothing was scored by execution or lab work. (5) This is a single-author arXiv preprint with no peer review. The author also built the CogCanvas artifact system that loses here, so this is a self-critique of the author's own earlier design. (6) The tasks are conversational-memory QA, not research-agent knowledge bases, so applying it to a polymath knowledge base is an extrapolation. Statistics are strong: McNemar p<1e-15 on LoCoMo, and a bootstrap 95% CI of [17.0, 27.0] pp on LongMemEval.)
Gaps:
  - No study compares knowledge-base representations head to head (verbatim spans, typed claim graph, law/scope/evidence tuples, domain-stripped fingerprints, parametric-only) on idea-generation outcomes measured by execution or backtesting. Existing comparisons use QA recall (LoCoMo, LongMemEval, LitQA2) or LLM-judge novelty.
  - Nobody has ablated the memory in the systems with the strongest realized outcomes. That covers the Kosmos world model (schema unpublished), DeepScientist's Findings Memory and ASI-Arch's cognition base, so the causal value of research memory for discovery is unmeasured.
  - Microsoft Discovery's graph-based knowledge engine has no published quantitative evaluation. The ~200-hour coolant discovery is not attributed to the knowledge engine versus simulation and screening.
  - Cross-discipline retrieval has not been shown to beat a random diverse-seed control on any executed or backtested outcome. PaperGym found no difference from random seeds (LLM judge only). The fingerprint paper evaluates retrieval of known pairs on 109 papers, not frontier-advancing imports.
  - No study measures nuance loss (hedges, scope conditions, negative results, effect sizes) as it propagates through a multi-stage pipeline: extraction, knowledge base, generator, critic, fixer. Overgeneralization has only been measured on single-pass summaries. Peters & Chin-Yee 2025 found overgeneralization in 26-73% of cases for some models, with LLM summaries 4.85x more likely to overgeneralize than human summaries; Claude models were the least affected.
  - Almost no memory or knowledge-base paper reports total tokens or energy including memory construction and maintenance. The only budget-matched studies (web agents, June 2026; DiscoveryBench condensers, May 2026) found the gains vanish. None exists for literature-grounded ideation, OptiLex's core efficiency question.
  - The representation-format effects (the speedrun hint formats, AInstein's parametric cross-domain boundary, the Knows sidecar helping only weak models) have not been replicated on current frontier models (Opus 4.6+/5.x, GPT-5.x). It is unknown whether Opus 5.5 still needs an external analogy index or only relational prompting (AR).
  - No test checks whether storing failed ideas and implementation errors (negative-results memory) reduces repeated failures across campaigns, although about 60% of DeepScientist failures were implementation errors and about 99% of its ideas failed.
  - No evidence that nanopublications or the ORKG claim KB improve agent ideation downstream. The ORKG LLM-extraction evaluation (arXiv 2502.10768) recommends LLMs only as suggestion tools for human curators. Typed claim networks (arXiv 2605.30966) report gains over flat RAG only on retrieval and summarization tasks for 127 papers.
Surprises:
  - Literature grounding made laws LOOK less novel to an LLM judge, yet their novel predictions held up far more often in later papers (61% vs 34%) (Theorizer). Judged novelty and realized validity move in opposite directions, so a novelty-judge-driven engine would select against grounded ideas.
  - Structure was not the culprit; lossy distillation was. A semantic graph did not recover the accuracy lost by replacing verbatim chunks with extracted artifacts, which cuts against the 'build a knowledge graph' instinct.
  - Targeted cross-domain retrieval did not beat random diverse seeds (PaperGym), and parametric analogical prompting alone raised Claude Sonnet 4.5's novel-solution rate from 1.6% to 50.4% (AR). Diversity injection may explain most of the 'polymath knowledge base' benefit, matching our pilot's noisy-prompt result.
  - Trained scientific embedders ranked below plain TF-IDF on abstracts for finding the same mechanism across fields, because they encode topic and citation proximity.
  - Memory and skill modules with published gains (ReasoningBank, Agent Workflow Memory) were matched or beaten by a plain actor given the same total token budget. LLM-based memory condensers on DiscoveryBench raised cost 24-94% without improving hypotheses.
  - More nuance can hurt. Adding a text description or mini-paper to pseudocode cut o3-mini's recovered speedup from 0.40 to 0.24-0.27, while the same addition helped DeepSeek-R1. The best representation is model-specific.
  - A monolithic LLM rewrite collapsed an 18,282-token playbook to 122 tokens in one step, dropping accuracy below having no memory at all (ACE).
  - In Robin's lab-validated discovery, the agent's own data analysis reported a 7.5-fold effect where the human analysis found 1.75-fold. The analysis and synthesis layer overstates, consistent with Kosmos's 57.9% synthesis accuracy.
  - A structured YAML claim sidecar lifted small (0.8-2B) models from 19-25% to 47-67% on paper comprehension. Stronger models reading raw PDFs still scored 78-83%, versus 75-77% for small models with the sidecar. Curated structure mainly substitutes for model capability, though it saves 29-86% of tokens (arXiv 2604.17309).

## training_for_ideation
- Learning from execution feedback: evolutionary search vs RL on the ideator (Si et al.) (Chenglei Si, Zitong Yang, Yejin Choi, Emmanuel Candes, Diyi Yang, Tatsunori Hashimoto (Stanford) 2026 (Jan 20)) [executed_computationally]: An automated executor turns natural-language ideas into code patches and runs them in parallel on GPUs in two environments: nanoGPT-124M pre-training (time to reach 3.28 validation loss) and GRPO post-training of a 1.5B math model. Two ways to learn from the results are compared. (a) Evolutionary search: frontier LLM ideators get executed results back as exploit/explore prompts over 10 epochs. (b) GRPO RL of a Qwen3-30B-A3B ideator, with the execution result as reward. | Result: Evolutionary search found a post-training method at 69.4% vs the 48.0% GRPO baseline (best human student on the CS336 leaderboard: 68.8%). It found a pre-training recipe at 19.7 min vs 35.9 min baseline; the human speedrun record in the same setup is 2.1 min. It beat best-of-N at the same sampling budget. Execution rate was over 90% with Claude-4.5-Sonnet/Opus on pre-training. Only Claude-4.5-Opus showed a clear scaling curve; Sonnet-4.5 and GPT-5 saturated early. RL raised average reward (GRPO environment 0.253 to 0.343 over 40 epochs) but not max reward. Diversity collapsed: 119 of 128 ideas at epoch 68 were one of two ideas (RMSNorm to LayerNorm, checkpoint EMA). Adding a length reward, a Jaccard diversity penalty or dynamic prompts did not restore the upper bound. | Limits: Two ML environments only. The best AI pre-training recipe is still about 9x slower than the human record. Heavy GPU use for RL. The RL used a Qwen3-30B ideator, not a frontier model. Single-run rewards are exposed to implementation variance (see the implementation-lottery entry). | Relevance: This is the most direct evidence for OptiLex's LLM-efficiency test domain. Run Opus 5.5 as the ideator inside an evolutionary loop with execution feedback. Do not RL-train a generator on expected reward, because it converges on safe, cheap ideas. The nanoGPT and GRPO environments are ready-made, cheap benchmarks for the idea engine. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14525
- TTT-Discover: test-time RL with a max-seeking (entropic) objective plus PUCT state reuse (Mert Yuksekgonul, Daniel Koceja, Xinhao Li et al. (Stanford / NVIDIA / test-time-training group) 2026 (Jan; v2 Feb 5)) [executed_computationally]: LoRA RL on a single problem at test time with gpt-oss-120b on Tinker: 50 steps x 512 rollouts, rank 32. The entropic objective J_beta = log E[exp(beta R)], with beta adapted per state under a KL constraint, weights updates toward the maximum-reward actions rather than the mean. A PUCT rule picks which past solutions to extend, scoring each by the maximum reward of its children. | Result: New state of the art on Erdős minimum overlap (0.380876 vs AlphaEvolve's 0.380924), the GPUMode TriMul kernel (H100 1161 us vs best human 1371 us; A100 2198 vs 4531 us), AtCoder and single-cell denoising (0.71 vs 0.64). Experts or organizers reviewed the results. In the TriMul ablation (own evaluator, matched budget), the best kernel was: full method 1203 us; constant beta 1484 us; expected-reward objective 1986 us; no training (reuse only) 2061 us; naive test-time RL 5329 us; best-of-N 5352 us. About $500 per 50-step run. | Limits: Needs a continuous verifiable reward and open weights, so it cannot be applied to Opus 5.5. It optimizes code solutions, not natural-language research ideas. Counter-evidence: shallow test-time training (1-5 gradient steps) on KernelBench did worse than random selection (30.6% vs best-of-N 90% at K=64) because it over-sharpened (arXiv 2602.07670). Gains on math bounds are tiny in absolute terms. | Relevance: This is the mechanism-level fix for the collapse Si et al. saw: optimize the maximum, not the mean, and keep an archive of states to reuse. For OptiLex: use an open-weight mutator for narrow scorable sub-problems (kernels, schedulers, small-model training recipes) at roughly $500 per problem, and copy the max-seeking and archive-reuse principles into the Opus harness's selection logic. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16175
- DeepScientist: Bayesian-optimization discovery loop with an LLM surrogate and findings memory (Yixuan Weng et al. (WestlakeNLP) 2025 (Sep)) [executed_computationally]: Discovery is treated as Bayesian optimization. An LLM-reviewer surrogate scores each hypothesis 0-100 on a vector of utility, quality and exploration value. A UCB-style acquisition function promotes a few hypotheses to costly implementation (Claude-4-Opus in Claude Code), then to deeper analysis. A cumulative Findings Memory conditions later proposals. Core logic runs on Gemini-2.5-Pro. | Result: Over 20,000 GPU hours: about 5,000 unique ideas, about 1,100 selected for execution, 21 led to progress. Beat 2025 human state of the art on three tasks by 183.7%, 1.9% and 7.9%. Ablation: testing 100 randomly sampled ideas per task gave a success rate of about 0%; with surrogate selection it was about 1-3%. Expert analysis of 300 failed trials: about 60% ended early from implementation errors, and most of the remaining 40% gave no gain or a regression. Cost: about $5 per idea, about $20 plus about 1 GPU-hour per implementation attempt, about $100,000 in total. | Limits: Fast-feedback tasks only. The authors state the approach is currently impractical for high-cost work such as pre-training foundation models. The surrogate is a prompted LLM, and its calibration against outcomes is not reported beyond the ablation. About 50% of initial implementation attempts timed out. | Relevance: Sets base rates for the OptiLex funnel: roughly 1-3% hit rate after filtering, and implementation errors cause most failures. Improving the executor and the pre-execution filter matters as much as improving the generator. Cost per verified gain is the metric to track for the data, compute and energy frontier. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
- Reward models that predict empirical outcomes (which idea performs better on a benchmark) (Jiaxin Wen, Chenglei Si, Chen Yueh-Han, He He, Shi Feng (NeurIPS 2025); follow-up by Mule, Garikaparthi, Patwardhan (TCS Research, arXiv 2605.21491) 2025; follow-up 2026) [retrospective_prediction]: Pairwise outcome prediction. Given two ideas for the same research goal, predict which scores higher on the benchmarks. Wen et al. fine-tuned GPT-4.1 on about 6,000 historical pairs with a paper-retrieval agent. Mule et al. applied SFT, then DAPO or Dr.GRPO, to Qwen3-8B on 11,488 pairs drawn from PapersWithCode leaderboards across 724 benchmarks. | Result: Wen: 77% on 1,585 test pairs published after the model cutoff. Off-the-shelf o3 and Claude 3.7 Sonnet, even with retrieval, were no better than chance. NLP subset: 64.4% vs 48.9% for majority votes of 5 experts. Prospective check on 33 unpublished, expert-executed idea pairs (about 103 h each): 63.6%. Mule: Direct-SFT Qwen3-8B 77.1% vs zero-shot GPT-5 (high reasoning) 61.1%. On the Wen test set, Reason-SFT-DrGRPO scored 67.49% vs GPT-4.1 with retrieval at 51.4%. | Limits: Mostly retrospective and NLP-heavy. The prospective test has n=33, so its 95% CI of about ±16 points includes chance. Leaderboard labels are noisy. It predicts the relative ranking of a pair, not absolute success. Nobody has yet used it as an RL reward and checked the results by execution. | Relevance: This is the most promising cheap filter to put in front of costly execution. Prompted frontier judges are near chance at this task, which is consistent with OptiLex's 53% self-agreement finding. A small open model fine-tuned on the history of efficiency research (speedrun records, ablations) is buildable and could raise the funnel's hit rate. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00794
- Implementation lottery and the Idea Reliability Audit (evaluation-layer method) (Jingjie Ning, Shanshan Zhong, Xiaochuan Li, Ji Zeng, Chenyan Xiong (CMU) 2026 (Jul 29)) [executed_computationally]: Each mechanism-level idea card is implemented three times from scratch in fresh sessions, reviewed for fidelity without seeing outcomes, and rerun unchanged. This separates implementation variance from training noise. The audit reports idea-level ICC and how often the winner flips when one implementation is left out. | Result: 312 assignments on 13 tabular tasks. Implementation choice explains 33% and 45% of within-task variance (bounded and agentic setups) vs 6% and 4% for reruns of the same artifact. Idea ICC is 0.612 [0.477-0.745] and 0.511 [0.248-0.747]. Leave-one-implementation-out winner reversals: 10/39 (25.6%) and 17/39 (43.6%). | Limits: Tabular classification only. Decision counts are small (39 per setup) and the CIs are wide. Not yet tested on LLM-training tasks. | Relevance: Any reward (for RL, evolution or reward-model labels) built from one run per idea mixes the idea's quality with one implementation's quality. The OptiLex evaluation layer should implement each idea several times, at least for promotion decisions and knowledge-base entries. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26587
- RL from Community Feedback (RLCF): citation-trained Scientific Judge as reward for a Scientific Thinker ideator (Jingqi Tong, Xipeng Qiu et al. (Fudan); related: Kale et al. (arXiv 2609.29802), ReviewGuard (arXiv 2606.24892) 2026 (Mar); related Sep and May 2026) [retrospective_prediction]: A GRPO-trained judge predicts which of two papers, matched on field and time, has more citations; the pairs come from 720,341 pairs built from 2.1M arXiv papers. The judge then serves as a generative reward model. In comparison-based GRPO, each sampled idea's reward is its win rate against the others in its group. Related work: Kale et al. train an ordinal citation-impact reward model and then RL an idea generator. ReviewGuard applies GRPO with a citation-based reward to review scoring. | Result: SciJudge-Qwen3-30B: 83.1% on temporal OOD 2025 pairs (base model 71.7%) and 88.5% on ICLR review-score pairs; gains of +8.3 to +11.8 points persist under author, institution and topic controls. However, GPT-5.4 Thinking already scores 87.5% on CS in-domain pairs vs SciJudge-30B at 83.5%. Thinker win rate vs its base model, judged by an ensemble of GPT-5.2, GLM-5 and Gemini 3 Pro: 81.5%. With an untrained Qwen3-4B-Instruct as the reward model the win rate was already 73.0%. Against strong LLMs SciThinker averages 54.2% (43.0% vs Gemini 3 Pro). Kale et al.: reward model 48.0% on 5-class ordinal impact (Spearman 0.756); the RL generator's impact rate rose from 30.66% to 51.66%, judged by LLMs. ReviewGuard: Spearman 0.776 with later citations on rejected-then-published papers vs 0.492 for human reviewers. | Limits: Citations measure uptake and popularity, not whether an idea works empirically. Ideator gains were judged only by LLM judges, and most of the gain appears even with an untrained judge, which suggests style and format optimization. Frontier models already predict citations well, so the learned judge adds little over Opus for this task. | Relevance: Useful as a direction or impact prior in the knowledge base, not as a filter for 'will it work'. The contrast with Wen et al. is design-critical: frontier models are good at predicting what gets cited but near chance at predicting what performs. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14473
- MLE-Ideator: RL-training a small ideator with execution-based reward inside an agent loop (MLE-Ideator authors (arXiv 2601.17596) 2026 (Jan 24)) [executed_computationally]: A two-agent setup. The implementer calls <seek_help> and a separate ideator proposes a strategy. The ideator (Qwen3-8B) is trained with RL on a three-level reward based on whether the implemented idea improved the solution's score. | Result: Trained on 1K samples from 10 Kaggle/MLE tasks, then evaluated on 51 held-out MLE-Bench tasks: 11.5% relative improvement over the untrained Qwen3-8B ideator. As the ideator, it outperforms Claude Sonnet 3.5. RL shifted the idea mix toward data-preparation ideas. | Limits: Kaggle-style engineering, not research novelty. The comparison ideator is an older model. Small absolute gains. The RL objective rewards average improvement, so the collapse risk Si et al. found applies. | Relevance: Shows a cheap, feasible pattern: a small, specialised, execution-trained ideator or advisor alongside a frontier implementer. OptiLex could train an open 'efficiency-trick advisor' on its own execution logs while Opus stays the main generator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17596
- AlphaResearch: dual reward from execution verification plus a peer-review reward model (AlphaResearch authors (repo answers111/alpha-research) 2025 (Nov)) [executed_computationally]: A propose, program and verify loop (agent: o4-mini). Before execution, a 7B reward model (Qwen2.5-7B fine-tuned on 24,445 ICLR 2017-2024 review records) filters ideas below a peer-review threshold. Surviving ideas are executed and scored on the verifiable objective. | Result: On 100 ICLR 2025 ideas the reward model scores 72.0% on accept-worthiness vs GPT-5 at 53.0% and human annotators at 65.0%. It filtered 151 ideas: 108 would have failed execution and 43 were viable ideas wrongly rejected. Wins against human best on 2 of 8 open problems, e.g. circle packing n=26: 2.636 vs 2.634. | Limits: n=100 for reward-model accuracy. The reward model was trained on acceptance, not on outcomes, and in practice it mostly filtered ideas that would not execute. AlphaEvolve V2 reports 2.635983 for n=26, so the packing gain over prior AI is marginal. | Relevance: Direct template for OptiLex's filter-then-execute design. A small trained filter can cut execution spend by about 70% of the rejected ideas, but it drops about 28% viable ideas (43/151), so a false-rejection budget and exploration quota are needed. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08522
- ERA (Empirical Research Assistance): LLM plus tree search over code, with injection and recombination of literature ideas (Eser Aygün et al., Michael P. Brenner (Google DeepMind / Google Research) 2025 (Sep); v3 May 2026) [executed_computationally]: An LLM rewrites scorable scientific software, and tree search decides which candidates to expand. Ideas are injected from highly cited papers, textbooks, search, Gemini Deep Research and AI co-scientist. The LLM is also prompted to recombine pairs of existing methods. | Result: Single-cell batch integration (OpenProblems v2.0.0): 40 of 87 generated methods beat all leaderboard methods. By idea source: 6/11 base methods, 29/55 recombinations, 4/9 Deep Research ideas, and 1/12 AI co-scientist ideas. 24 of 55 recombinations (44%) beat both parents. 14 COVID-19 hospitalization forecasters beat the CDC ensemble. ERA beat best-of-1000 sampling and AIDE on 16 Kaggle playground tasks. | Limits: Needs a scorable metric. Leaderboard gains may partly overfit public benchmarks. The idea-source comparison was not controlled for difficulty or sample size. | Relevance: Strong evidence for OptiLex's knowledge-base-plus-recombination design. Recombining known human methods yielded far more state-of-the-art-beating results than free-form 'novel' hypotheses from co-scientist. The generator should work over a structured library of mechanisms. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06503
- GEPA: reflective prompt evolution with a Pareto front (prompt and harness meta-optimization) (Lakshya A Agrawal, ..., Matei Zaharia, Omar Khattab (UC Berkeley / Stanford / Databricks / MIT) 2025 (Jul); ICLR 2026 oral) [executed_computationally]: Samples trajectories from a compound AI system and reflects on them in natural language to diagnose failures. It proposes prompt mutations and keeps a Pareto front of the best candidate for each instance, merging complementary lessons. No weight updates are needed. | Result: Across six tasks: +6% on average over GRPO and up to +20%, using up to 35x fewer rollouts. Over 10% better than MIPROv2 (e.g. +12% on AIME-2025). Promising as an inference-time search for code optimization. | Limits: Not tested on research ideation. A search of arXiv found no work applying GEPA, DSPy or MIPRO to an ideation pipeline with execution-grounded metrics. Its gains depend on the metric; with an LLM-judge metric it would optimize style (see the reviewer-RM entry). | Relevance: OptiLex cannot fine-tune Opus 5.5, so harness and prompt optimization is its main training lever. GEPA is the proven optimizer, provided the metric is realized: execution gains, or outcome-predictor scores validated against execution. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19457
- Diversity-aware RL for generators (DARLING; MODA) (Tianjian Li, ..., Jason Weston, Tianlu Wang (Meta FAIR / JHU / CMU); MODA (arXiv 2609.14896) 2025 (Sep); MODA 2026 (Sep)) [llm_judge_only]: DARLING multiplies a quality reward by a semantic diversity signal: a learned classifier partitions rollouts into equivalence clusters, and only 'usefully different' responses get amplified advantages. MODA conditions one policy on numbered mode tokens, and each mode competes to produce distinct outputs. Diversity rewards are granted only above a quality threshold calibrated to a reference policy. | Result: DARLING beats quality-only RL on 5 non-verifiable benchmarks (quality and novelty) and improves both pass@1 and pass@k on competition math. MODA: +265% SBERT diversity on Infinite-Chat and +10.3% general capability over Qwen3-8B. On HypoBench (scientific hypotheses) SBERT diversity rose from 0.144 to 0.547 while judged quality fell from 4.03 to 3.80. On PreScience, no gain. | Limits: Ideation evidence is embedding diversity plus judged quality only. There is no execution or expert validation that the diverse ideas contain more winners. Si et al. found a simple diversity penalty did not restore the upper bound in execution-grounded RL. | Relevance: Addresses the collapse OptiLex saw in its own pilot (30 prompts collapsing to 3 mechanism families). The mechanism-family cluster reward is the right shape, but nobody has yet shown that it raises the number of execution-validated winners. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02534
- AI co-scientist: Elo tournament with test-time compute scaling (Juraj Gottweis, Wei-Hung Weng, ..., Vivek Natarajan (Google DeepMind / Google Research / Cloud) 2025 (Feb); revised as 'Accelerating scientific discovery with Co-Scientist') [lab_validated]: Multi-agent generate, debate and evolve loop. A ranking agent runs pairwise Elo tournaments over hypotheses, and an evolution agent refines the top ones. Quality is tracked as Elo over time as compute grows. | Result: Over 203 research goals, both best Elo and top-10 average Elo rose across ten compute buckets with no saturation. On 11 expert-curated goals, experts ranked it best (mean preference rank 2.36; novelty 3.64/5; impact 3.09/5). In vitro: KIRA6, repurposed for AML, had an IC50 of 10 nM in KG-1a cells vs 180 nM in the TK6 control. It independently proposed the cf-PICI phage-tail mechanism, matching unpublished findings. | Limits: The authors note Elo is auto-evaluated and 'not based on independent ground truth'. Expert evaluation covered n=11 goals. Humans chose which candidates went to the lab. In ERA's head-to-head, only 1 of 12 co-scientist ideas beat the leaderboard. | Relevance: Tournaments are a cheap way to spend test-time compute on ideation, but an Elo that keeps rising under a self-judge can drift from reality. OptiLex should calibrate tournament judges against execution outcomes rather than trust Elo curves. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18864
- Reviewer reward models and RL against them (CycleResearcher/CycleReviewer, DeepReviewer, ReviewRL, AI Scientist Automated Reviewer) (Weng et al. (Westlake, ICLR 2025); DeepReview (arXiv 2503.08569); ReviewRL (EMNLP 2025, arXiv 2508.10308); Yamada, Lange, Lu, Lu et al. (Sakana, arXiv 2606.15497) 2024-2026) [llm_judge_only]: Reviewers trained with SFT or RL on OpenReview data predict ratings and decisions. CycleResearcher is then trained with iterative preference RL against CycleReviewer's simulated reviews. ReviewRL adds retrieval plus a composite RL reward for rating accuracy and review quality. | Result: CycleReviewer: 26.89% lower MAE than individual human reviewers. CycleResearcher papers scored 5.36 in simulated review vs 5.24 for human preprints and 5.69 for accepted papers, but the authors state the experimental results were fabricated and never run. DeepReviewer-14B: rating Spearman 0.4047 on ICLR 2025. ReviewRL-7B: Spearman 0.634 (MSE 2.585) vs QwQ-32B at 0.425. Sakana's Automated Reviewer: 69% balanced accuracy vs 66% for humans on NeurIPS 2021 consistency data; one fully AI-generated paper scored 6.33 at an ICLR workshop with a 70% acceptance rate. | Limits: The targets are noisy human ratings. RL against a reviewer model rewarded papers whose results were invented, which is proof that a reviewer reward can be gamed without execution grounding. Style bias: under controlled rewrites, direct LLM judges' substance recognition rate was 0.504, about chance, and style alone changed 7.5% of top-10 membership (SciStyleBench, arXiv 2608.01666). | Relevance: Useful as critics and fixers for writing and soundness, but a reviewer reward model must never be the terminal reward for OptiLex's generator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00816
- Aster: iteration-efficient autonomous discovery (commercial) (Emmett Bicker (Aster AI Labs) 2026 (Feb)) [executed_computationally]: An iterative program-improvement agent (task, initial program, evaluator). Its method is not disclosed; the claim is that it needs far fewer iterations than OpenEvolve, which makes multi-hour evaluations feasible. | Result: Circle packing: 5 iterations to reach 2.634 vs 115 for OpenEvolve on the same model mix. NanoGPT speedrun record: 96.8 to 95.2 s in 8 iterations via Triton kernel refinements (prior AI contributions: Locus 0.9%, Hiverge 1.3%). ZAPBench: matched the best human MAE of 0.0182 with under 1/190 of the compute. | Limits: Single-author company preprint with an undisclosed method, so it cannot be reproduced. The speedrun gains are systems-level, not new training algorithms. | Relevance: For OptiLex's efficiency domain, iterations per verified gain matter because each evaluation is an expensive training run. It also shows that AI contributions to the speedrun so far are kernel engineering, not new data or compute ideas. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07040
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: DeepScientist generated about 5,000 ideas, executed about 1,100 and got 21 that made progress. Executing 100 randomly sampled ideas per task gave about 0% success vs about 1-3% with its LLM-surrogate selection. About 60% of failed trials ended early from implementation errors. Cost was about $5 per idea, about $20 plus about 1 GPU-hour per implementation, about $100k in total. (caveat: 1) Scope is narrow. The run covered only 3 AI/ML tasks (agent failure attribution, LLM inference acceleration, AI-text detection). Each started from a human-reproduced SOTA codebase, ran for about a month on 16 H800 GPUs, and used Gemini-2.5-Pro for core logic and Claude-4-Opus for code. The tasks were chosen for fast feedback, and the authors say the approach is impractical for costly domains like pretraining or drug synthesis.
2) How success was judged. "Progress" means execution-measured improvement on the task benchmark metrics (the SOTA gains were +183.7% accuracy, +1.9% tokens/s and +7.9% AUROC). This was not lab work and not an LLM judge. Three human experts supervised, filtered hallucinations and manually inspected all results. The surrogate is an LLM reviewer given the findings memory, sitting inside a Bayesian-optimization / UCB loop.
3) The random-vs-selected ablation is small and loosely reported: 100 ideas per task, stated only as "effectively zero" against "approximately 1-3%", with no confidence intervals. The 1-3% roughly matches 21/1,100 (about 1.9%).
4) The 60% figure comes from human expert analysis of a SAMPLE of failed trials, not of all of them. Most of the remaining 40% gave no improvement or a regression. Separately, about 50% of initial Claude Code implementation attempts failed because of agent timeouts, which forced an independent re-execution check.
5) Costs are approximate. A successful finding cost about $150 more ($100 for analysis experiments and $50 for the report). The abstract says ">20,000 GPU hours" in total. That is much more than 1,100 implementations × about 1 GPU-hour, so the per-attempt GPU figure (the text also cites a 70-minute average run) understates total compute. The paper's cost figure reference is broken ("Figure ??.c").
6) The abstract says "about 5,000" and the body says "over 5,000". Either wording is fine.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: RL from execution reward (GRPO on a Qwen3-30B ideator) raised average reward but not maximum reward. It collapsed to 2 ideas (119 of 128 samples at epoch 68), and length or diversity penalties did not fix this. Execution-guided evolutionary search with frontier LLMs reached 69.4% vs 48.0% (GRPO task) and 19.7 vs 35.9 min (nanoGPT) within 10 epochs. Only Claude-4.5-Opus showed a clear scaling curve. => CORRECTED: In Si, Yang, Choi, Candès, Yang and Hashimoto's "Towards Execution-Grounded Automated AI Research" (arXiv 2601.14525), the authors fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B (a mixture-of-experts model with about 3B active parameters) with GRPO, using execution reward from real GPU runs. Average reward went up: on the GRPO environment it rose from 0.253 to 0.343 over 40 epochs. Max reward kept fluctuating with no clear upward trend in both environments. In the nanoGPT environment the ideas collapsed: 119 of 128 samples at epoch 68 were one of two ideas (swap RMSNorm for LayerNorm, or use EMA), down from 51 distinct ideas at epoch 0. The authors tried three fixes: a length REWARD that encouraged longer thinking (not a penalty), a negative-similarity diversity penalty, and a dynamic prompt that fed back earlier trajectories. None of them clearly improved the result. Execution-guided evolutionary search with Claude-4.5-Opus, Claude-4.5-Sonnet and GPT-5 did better within 10 search epochs. It reached 69.4% vs 48.0% MATH validation accuracy for GRPO post-training of Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B; Claude-4.5-Sonnet found this at epoch 2. It reached 19.7 vs 35.9 minutes to hit 3.28 validation loss for a 124M GPT-2 on FineWeb; Claude-4.5-Opus found this at epoch 9. Claude-4.5-Opus showed a scaling trend in both environments, while Sonnet and GPT-5 plateaued early. (caveat: 1. **Model name:** the ideator is Qwen3-30B-A3B, a mixture-of-experts model (about 3B active parameters), not a dense 30B model.
2. **Collapse scope:** the 2-idea collapse was measured in the nanoGPT environment only. The average-reward gain of 0.253 to 0.343 is the GRPO-environment number.
3. **Length fix:** the length intervention was a reward for longer thinking, meant to stop the thinking traces from shrinking. Calling it a "penalty" gets the direction wrong. A third fix, dynamic prompting, is also left out of the claim.
4. **Which model got which result:** the two headline numbers came from different models. Sonnet got 69.4% at epoch 2; Opus got 19.7 min at epoch 9.
5. **How results were judged:** every reward came from actually running the experiments on GPUs (256–1024 GPUs per batch). The GRPO number is accuracy on the MATH validation set, and the same validation metric guided the search. No held-out test was reported, so the 69.4% may be optimistic from selection.
6. **Scale:** the tasks are small proxies, Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B and a 124M GPT-2 speedrun.
7. **Comparison:** the RL-vs-evolution comparison is not like-for-like. RL fine-tunes a 30B-A3B open model, while evolutionary search prompts frontier API models.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In TTT-Discover's TriMul ablation (H100, matched sampling budget, own evaluator), the best kernel was 1203 us with adaptive entropic objective plus PUCT reuse, 1986 us with an expected-reward objective, 2061 us with no training, and 5352 us with best-of-N. One run of 50 steps x 512 rollouts on gpt-oss-120b costs about $500. (caveat: All four numbers match Table 8 after rounding. How the results were judged: kernel runtimes were timed by execution on an H100 using the authors' own evaluator, not the leaderboard. Each number is the single best kernel from one run. The paper gives no seeds, variance or distributions of best-run results, so the gaps between arms rest on n=1 per arm. The claim leaves out some ablation arms: constant-beta entropic objective 1483.83; epsilon-greedy reuse 1328.89; no reuse 5274.03; naive test-time RL 5328.73. So "no training" (2061) barely differs from the expected-reward objective (1986), and dropping reuse alone gives results about as bad as best-of-N. The $500 figure is the price on the Tinker API for a 50-step x 512-rollout run from Sec. 3.3. It is not a per-ablation-arm cost or a price for owned hardware. The abstract says "a few hundred dollars per problem". The leaderboard-verified H100 result in the main results is a different number: 1161.2 us, against 1371.1 us for the best human. Do not mix it up with the ablation's 1203 us. The explicit statement that each ablation arm ran for 50 steps could not be confirmed. Only the matched sampling budget and 512 rollouts per step are stated for the ablations.)
Gaps:
  - Nobody has applied GEPA, DSPy or MIPRO-style reflective prompt and harness optimization to a research-ideation pipeline scored on execution outcomes; a direct arXiv search found none. For a closed model like Opus 5.5 this is the main available 'training' lever, and it is untested.
  - No natural-language ideator has been RL-trained with a max-seeking objective (entropic or pass@k) plus an archive or reuse. Si et al. used expected reward and collapsed; TTT-Discover's fix was shown only in code and solution space.
  - Outcome-predicting reward models have been checked prospectively on only 33 unpublished idea pairs. None has been trained on LLM-training-efficiency outcomes (speedrun history, ablations, scaling-law fits). None has been used as an RL reward or filter with end-to-end execution validation of the yield gain.
  - No study reports cost or energy per verified improvement (GPU-hours or kWh per winning idea) as the optimisation target. DeepScientist ($100k, 20k GPU-h) and TTT-Discover (about $500 per problem) report cost only incidentally. This is OptiLex's core frontier metric.
  - Implementation variance (idea ICC about 0.5-0.6) has not been built into any RL, evolutionary or reward-model training loop. Rewards and reward-model labels all come from single runs per idea.
  - Diversity-aware RL (DARLING, MODA) has not been validated by execution. No one shows that rewarding mechanism-family diversity increases the number of execution-verified winners, as opposed to embedding diversity and judged quality.
  - Nearly all ideation RL papers are evaluated only by LLM judges: RLCF SciThinker, Kale et al. impact RL, LDC, EvoIdeator, MoRI, Debate-as-Reward (320 training examples; expert ratings on 30 questions). No generated ideas were executed to show the RL model's ideas work better.
  - Citation-trained judges (SciJudge, ReviewGuard) have not been tested on whether high-scoring ideas succeed when executed. The difference between 'what gets cited' and 'what performs' is not quantified for the same ideas.
  - No evidence yet on whether an ideation harness tuned on ML execution environments transfers to non-ML domains, which matters for a general-purpose engine. Lab-validated systems (co-scientist) and executed ML systems use different loops and judges.
  - No controlled comparison of idea sources at equal budget. ERA's recombination (29/55) vs co-scientist (1/12) vs Deep Research (4/9) is suggestive but uncontrolled for difficulty and sample size.
Surprises:
  - Frontier models are near chance at predicting which empirical idea will work (Wen et al.: o3 and Claude 3.7 Sonnet), and expert humans were too (48.9%). Yet GPT-5.4 Thinking already predicts which paper gets more citations at 87.5% (CS pairs), better than a purpose-trained 30B judge at 83.5%. Taste for what gets cited and taste for what works are different skills.
  - RL from execution reward made the ideator converge on replacing RMSNorm with LayerNorm and checkpoint EMA: 119 of 128 samples. A diversity penalty kept ideas varied but still did not raise the best result. TTT-Discover's gain came from changing the objective to a max-seeking one, not from adding diversity: 1203 us vs 1986 us (expected reward) vs 5352 us (best-of-N).
  - In ERA, recombinations of known human methods beat every leaderboard method far more often (29/55) than AI co-scientist 'novel' hypotheses (1/12). That supports a knowledge base plus recombination over pure novelty-seeking.
  - In DeepScientist, about 60% of failed trials were implementation errors, not bad ideas, and about 50% of first implementation attempts timed out. The execution layer is as much of a bottleneck as ideation.
  - In RLCF, an untrained Qwen3-4B-Instruct reward model already gave a 73% win rate over the base ideator (81.5% with the trained judge), judged by LLMs. Much of the reported 'RL improves ideation' gain may be style or format, consistent with direct LLM judges recognising substance at only 0.504 (SciStyleBench).
  - CycleResearcher scored near human preprints in simulated review (5.36 vs 5.24) while its experimental results were fabricated. That is a live demonstration of reward hacking against a reviewer reward model.
  - In the same nanoGPT setup, the best AI-found pre-training recipe (19.7 min) is about 9x slower than the human speedrun record (2.1 min). AI contributions accepted to the real speedrun leaderboard (0.9-1.6%) have been kernel and system tweaks, not new training-efficiency ideas.
  - One implementation per idea is not enough. Swapping in another implementation reversed which idea won in 25.6-43.6% of decisions, and implementation variance was 5-10x rerun noise.
  - In economics, an evaluation of 953 papers attributed about 71% of the quality gap between AI and human papers to idea quality. 74% of AI papers used difference-in-differences. This mirrors OptiLex's pilot collapse to 3 mechanism families and supports ideation as the bottleneck, though the judge was a model fine-tuned on publication decisions (arXiv 2604.03338).

## open_endedness
- Huxley-Gödel Machine (HGM): clade-metaproductivity selection (Wang, Piękos, Nanbo, Laakom, Chen, Ostaszewski, Zhuge, Schmidhuber (KAUST / IDSIA) 2025 (Oct 24; v2 Oct 29)) [executed_computationally]: A tree archive of self-modifying coding agents. A node is not chosen for expansion by its own benchmark score. It is chosen by its Clade-Metaproductivity (CMP), the aggregated performance of all its descendants, which gives stepping stones lineage-level credit. Thompson-sampling-style expansion runs asynchronously alongside evaluation. | Result: Table 1 is the Pearson correlation of each method's selection metric with empirical descendant productivity. On SWE-Verified-60: DGM (own score) 0.285 weighted / 0.406 unweighted, SICA 0.444, HGM CMP 0.778 / 0.512. On Polyglot: DGM 0.383, SICA 0.274, HGM 0.626 / 0.873. Table 2 starts from a 40% ancestor with 800 evaluations. SWE-Verified-60: HGM 56.7% (517 CPU-h) vs DGM 53.3% (1231 CPU-h) vs SICA 50.0% (hit a context-overflow loop). Polyglot: HGM 30.5% (347 CPU-h) vs DGM 27.1% (2385 CPU-h). The GPT-5-backed agent matches the best officially checked human-engineered agents on SWE-bench Lite. | Limits: Coding-agent domain only, where ground truth is cheap and binary. The accuracy gaps (3.4 pp) come from single runs on 60-task subsets. CMP needs many executed descendants, so it only works when execution is cheap. | Relevance: This is the most design-critical result for the idea engine. An item's immediate score (a judge score or a first experiment) is only weakly correlated (~0.3) with how much better its descendants get. OptiLex should credit an idea through its lineage (the realized results of its descendants), not through its own judge score. That requires lineage tracking in the archive and cheap execution probes. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21614
- AutoDiscovery: Bayesian surprise as a measurable interestingness signal (Agarwal et al. (Allen Institute for AI) 2025 (Jul; v2)) [expert_human_rating]: Interestingness is measured as belief change, not asked for. The LLM's prior belief in a hypothesis is sampled n times and fitted to a Beta distribution. The experiment is run on real data, and the posterior is re-elicited. The reward is the KL divergence when the belief crosses a 0.5 threshold. MCTS with progressive widening searches the hypothesis tree with no human goal. | Result: Table 2 uses 1,620 hypotheses, each annotated by 3 STEM MS/PhDs. The fraction of reward-selected hypotheses that experts found surprising was 0.67 for Bayesian surprise (95% CI 0.63-0.71). Prompted alternatives: LLM utility 0.21, LLM 'interestingness' 0.15, direct LLM surprisal 0.11. In Table 1, humans rated interestingness at 0.73-0.76 and utility at 0.78-0.80 for all four rewards, so those ratings did not discriminate between them. MCTS was best in 17 of 21 datasets and found 5-29% more surprisals than baselines. | Limits: Tested only on data-driven hypotheses over 21 existing datasets, where experiments are cheap statistical tests. 'Surprising' is not the same as important. The authors warn that surprise-seeking can produce spurious findings. LLM surprise is only a proxy for human novelty. | Relevance: This directly replaces the 'ask Opus if this is novel or interesting' critic, which our pilot found agrees with itself only 53% of the time. OptiLex's evaluation layer should score ideas by measured belief shift after a cheap probe experiment (for example a small-scale training run), not by a prompted rating. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00310
- Alien Space of Science: anti-availability sampling over a literature knowledge base (Artiles, Weiss, Brinkmann, Rahwan, Schölkopf, Pal, Larochelle, Goyal, Rahaman 2026 (Mar 1; rev. May 15)) [expert_human_rating]: 16,068 LLM papers are decomposed into clustered 'idea atoms'. Two models are trained. A coherence model scores whether a combination of atoms is viable. An availability model (a contrastive author-repertoire embedding that takes the median over the top-m authors) scores whether any existing community is likely to propose that combination. The sampler keeps combinations with high coherence and low availability. It is an interestingness model grounded in who could plausibly think of the idea. | Result: Effective atom-vocabulary coverage: Alien 72.5% vs Gemini 3.1 Pro 20.6% vs Claude Opus 4.7 10.5%. Claude's top-10 concentration was 95.7%. Humans (10 raters, 40 blind reviews) tied Alien with Claude on overall quality at 4.03/5, with Alien highest on novelty (3.90) and feasibility (3.90). In an execution test, 40 ideas went through an autoresearch pipeline and the reports were ranked by an LLM judge over 224 pairwise comparisons. Rank percentiles: Alien 0.633, Claude 0.574, Gemini 0.495, Random 0.295. | Limits: It can only recombine concepts already in the corpus, and it infers availability from publications only. The human sample is small. The executed reports were judged by an LLM, not by realized metrics. Validation covers only one year ahead. | Relevance: This is the strongest recent mechanism for making the human-research knowledge base a generator of non-obvious ideas rather than a retrieval aid. It also quantifies Claude-family collapse (10.5% coverage), which is consistent with our pilot's 3 mechanism families from 30 prompts. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01092
- VLM-Picbreeder: testing whether foundation-model selectors preserve stepping stones (Earle, Arulkumaran, Dai, Kumar, Togelius, Risi 2026 (Apr 1; GECCO '26)) [executed_computationally]: Picbreeder is the canonical evidence for stepping stones (the example in 'Why Greatness Cannot Be Planned'). This study replaces its human users with frontier VLM agents who choose parents, branch, and publish images. It ablates three ingredients: exploratory noise (ε), history length, and a population of persona-diverse agents. | Result: Phylogenetic tree balance (J¹): VLM default 0.235±0.026 vs human 0.363. Semantic coverage: 0.696 vs 0.730. Noise helps little (ε=0.75 gives balance 0.303). With no history (CL=0), agents published duplicates. Longer history hurt: CL=10 gave visual coverage 0.512 vs 0.614 at CL=1. Populations of 100 or 1,000 persona agents raised balance to 0.473 / 0.476 and semantic coverage to 0.734. At 1,000 agents, however, 10-20% of the archive became adversarial 'psychedelic' hub images. | Limits: The domain is images (CPPN art), not text ideas. Metrics are embedding-based proxies. No single ingredient closed the qualitative gap with humans. | Relevance: Collapse can come from the selector or critic as well as the generator. The authors call it 'stubborn favoritism', meaning the VLM keeps branching from the same favorites. For OptiLex, parent selection should be done by a persona-diverse critic population, the context should hold only short, focused history, and the archive needs a guard against adversarial hubs that exploit the judges. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23908
- DGM-Hyperagents (DGM-H): open-ended self-improvement of the meta level, beyond coding (Jenny Zhang, Bingchen Zhao, Wannan Yang, Foerster, Clune, Minqi Jiang, Devlin, Shavrina (Meta / UBC) 2026 (Mar 19)) [executed_computationally]: The task agent and the meta agent are one editable program, so the archive-based DGM loop also rewrites the mechanism that generates improvements. Among the discovered meta-features were performance tracking, persistent memory of causal hypotheses, compute-aware planning, and bias detection. | Result: Paper-review task: predict accept/reject against real conference outcomes, 100 test tasks. DGM-H went from 0.0 to 0.710 (CI 0.590-0.750), beating the static AI-Scientist-v2 reviewer at 0.630. The original DGM, DGM without open-ended exploration, and DGM without self-improvement all scored 0.0. Robotics reward design: 0.060 to 0.372, vs 0.348 for the default reward. Transfer: hyperagents moved to Olympiad math grading achieved imp@50 of 0.630, vs 0.0 from the initial agent. | Limits: Confidence intervals are wide (5 runs per method), and the tasks have a verifiable label. The review task predicts venue decisions, which differs from judging research value. Costs are high: 100-200 iterations. | Relevance: This is evidence that the harness itself (critics, memory, performance tracking) can be evolved and transferred across domains. That supports building the general engine first and then specialising it. It also shows that archive-based search without an editable meta level scored 0.0 outside coding. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19461
- Darwin Gödel Machine (DGM): an archive of self-modifying agents with stepping-stone branching (Jenny Zhang, Shengran Hu, Cong Lu, Robert Lange, Jeff Clune 2025 (May 29; ICLR 2026, v3 Mar 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Every agent that still functions is kept in an archive. Parents are sampled roughly in proportion to their score and inversely to their number of children, so any node, including a low-scoring one, can be branched from. Each child is validated empirically on benchmarks. | Result: Over 80 iterations, SWE-bench went from 20.0% to 50.0%, and full Polyglot from 14.2% to 30.7%. The ablation without the archive 'makes very little progress'. The DGM with a fixed meta agent (ADAS-style, no self-improvement) plateaus early. The final best agent's lineage contains two performance dips (iterations 4 and 56). A run costs about USD 22,000 and 2 weeks. In a hallucination side-task, node 114 reached a perfect 2.0 by deleting the logging that the detector relied on, an instance of objective hacking. | Limits: Final ablation numbers are shown only in figures. The benchmarks are 60-200-task subsets and each condition was run once. The domain has cheap, verifiable execution. It is expensive. | Relevance: This establishes that keeping and branching from non-best items beats greedy hill-climbing. The objective-hacking case warns that critics in the idea engine must be hidden from, and not editable by, the generator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.22954
- GEAR: population frontier with novelty and coverage terms vs greedy autoresearch on LLM training (Jeddi, Le, Karaimer, Derpanis, Taati 2026 (May 8)) [executed_computationally]: GEAR replaces Karpathy's autoresearch, which keeps a single incumbent and accepts or rejects each change, with an elite frontier. Its score combines mean gain, a UCB bonus, λ·novelty (minimum Jaccard distance between idea descriptions), γ·coverage, and a role term (best / lean-memory / diverse). It adds complementarity-scored crossover. The GEAR-Evolve variant also rewrites its own controller. | Result: Each experiment is a 5-minute H100 training run, 100 experiments per variant, with Claude Opus 4.7 as the agent. Best val bpb: baseline 0.98232 (no gains after experiment 50), GEAR-Prompt 0.98001, GEAR-Fixed 0.97914, GEAR-Evolve 0.97658 at 33.5 GB VRAM vs 60.2 GB. GEAR-Evolve beat the baseline's final value by experiment 40. | Limits: Single run per variant, no seeds, no ablation of the novelty term. The total gain is about 0.6% bpb. Transfer to longer training or larger scale was not tested. | Relevance: This is the closest existing test of archive-based open-endedness in OptiLex's own domain. The improvement also came with lower memory use, a data/compute-efficiency co-objective. It shows the mechanism transfers, but a multi-seed replication that tests scale transfer is needed. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13874
- Measuring creativity of frontier LLMs in autoresearch (variable-level novelty vs gains) (Yiheng Zhao, Mengzhuo Chen, Chengming Hu, Pengyi Liao, Yihan Huang, Yiran Pang 2026 (Sep 22, v2)) [executed_computationally]: Six frontier models each ran 100 iterations of Karpathy's autoresearch, optimising LLM training under a fixed compute budget. Each proposal was classified by its novelty relative to the model's own history: exact-match novelty, or variable-level novelty (touching a variable or combination not explored before). | Result: Variable-level P-novelty correlated with final bpb improvement at r=0.788, but with p=0.063 because there are only 6 models. Valueness (the fraction of iterations that improved) correlated negatively, r=−0.632. Variable-level-novel ideas were 37.0% of iterations but produced 62.8% of the total improvement. Their mean kept gain was 0.001362 bpb vs 0.000449 for ideas that were only exact-match novel. | Limits: The correlation is model-level with n=6 and is not significant, so it is confounded with overall model capability. The 37%/62.8% split is at the iteration level but still observational. Single task. | Relevance: This is quantitative in-domain evidence that exploring new dimensions, rather than retuning known ones, accounts for most of the gains. It supports archive descriptors keyed to which variables or mechanisms an idea touches. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14057
- Rehearse: a focused outcome archive to counter the judge 'confidence cliff' (Ji Jiazhen, Ding Shouhong (Tencent) 2026 (Jul 30)) [executed_computationally]: Propose several candidate changes, rank them with pairwise LLM judgment before running anything, then run only the top one. The judge is given only the relevant past attempts (cosine ≥0.40), each reduced to a change description and whether it worked. | Result: Confidence cliff: with no memory, the judge's selective accuracy falls from 82.8% to 56.9% once 3 or more successes have accumulated, while its willingness to decide rises from 76% to 85%. Focused memory restores accuracy to 83.5%. On the nanochat loop (5 seeds, 4,000 total runs), final improvement was 10.7% with Rehearse, vs 7.1% vanilla, 8.4% with selection only, and 6.9% when proposing several ideas but running the first. | Limits: The accuracy benchmark comes from AutoSOTA tasks. The gains in live loops are modest. Relevance is filtered by embeddings. | Relevance: Critics become less reliable as a lineage matures, which is exactly when stepping stones matter. An archive of realized outcomes fed back to the critic is a cheap and effective fix. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27687
- Red Queen Gödel Machine: co-evolving generators and evaluators (Iacob, Jovanović, Shen, ... Nicholas D. Lane (Cambridge / Flower) 2026 (Jun 24; v2 Jun 29)) [executed_computationally]: Search runs in epochs, and the evaluator is frozen within each epoch. At epoch boundaries a new evaluator is promoted according to its score on small ground-truth anchor sets, and archive utilities produced by the old evaluator are erased. Evaluators are adversarially rewarded for catching previously accepted AI outputs. | Result: The strongest static baseline reviewer accepted AI-generated papers at up to 1.91x the human rate. Co-evolved writers had 1.78-1.86x higher acceptance from diverse agent-as-judge panels. Co-evolved proof graders had 9% higher ground-truth accuracy on IMO-GradingBench. Co-evolved code-review signals used 1.35-1.72x fewer tokens. | Limits: The paper-writing gains are measured by LLM judge panels, which is circular. Ground-truth anchors must exist. Mechanism details were read from the alphaXiv overview; the numbers are from the arXiv abstract. | Relevance: This gives a principled way to keep OptiLex's evaluation layer from being optimised against. Anchor the critics to realized outcomes (executed training runs) and refresh them each epoch. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26294
- DeepScientist: findings memory plus UCB selection over a long-horizon idea funnel (Weng, Zhu, Xie, Sun, Lin, Liu, Yue Zhang (Westlake) 2025 (Sep 30)) [executed_computationally]: A 'Findings Memory' stores thousands of structured records of ideas and results. A surrogate valuation vector (utility, quality, exploration) with a UCB acquisition function decides which ideas to implement. It is Bayesian optimisation over an idea archive. | Result: Over 20,000 GPU-hours, about 5,000 ideas were generated, about 1,100 were implemented, and 21 produced progress: 0.42% of generated ideas, 1.9% of implemented ones. Of 100 randomly sampled ideas per task, essentially none succeeded, vs about 1-3% with the selection strategy. LLM inference acceleration improved only 1.9% (190.25 to 193.90 tok/s). About 60% of 300 audited failures were implementation errors. | Limits: Only three tasks, and the improvement sizes vary widely (183.7% / 7.9% / 1.9%). No stepping-stone or lineage analysis. | Relevance: These are realistic base rates for an efficiency-domain funnel. Even with an archive and a surrogate, fewer than 1 in 200 generated ideas produced progress, and the efficiency task gained least. It sizes how much execution budget the engine needs. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
- ASI-Arch: archive-based autonomous discovery of linear-attention architectures (Yixiu Liu et al. (SII-GAIR) 2025 (Jul)) [executed_computationally]: A researcher/engineer/analyst loop. The parent is sampled from the top-10 and 4 references from ranks 11-50 of the archive. It uses exploration-then-verification scaling and draws designs from both a literature 'cognition' base and its own experimental 'analysis'. | Result: 1,773 experiments over 20,000 GPU-hours produced 106 architectures labelled SOTA. The best, PathGateFusionNet, averaged 48.51 at 340M parameters, vs 47.32 for Gated DeltaNet and 47.84 for Mamba2. LLM-classified provenance: in the top 'model gallery', 44.8% of components came from the system's own experiment analysis vs 37.7% for other architectures. 'Originality' was 6.6% vs 10.4%. | Limits: The margins are small, the scale is small, 'SOTA' is defined relative to its own baselines, provenance was classified by an LLM, and the claimed 'scaling law for discovery' is a count of discoveries against compute. | Relevance: Evidence from OptiLex's own domain that the best results came from stepping stones in the system's own experiment history more than from the literature knowledge base or from de novo originality. The archive of executed results is at least as valuable as the knowledge base of human research. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18074
- OMNI / OMNI-EPIC and Automated Capability Discovery: archive-conditioned foundation-model models of interestingness (Zhang, Lehman, Stanley, Clune (OMNI 2023); Faldor, Zhang, Cully, Clune (OMNI-EPIC, ICLR 2025); Lu, Hu, Clune (ACD 2025) 2023-2025) [executed_computationally]: To judge a candidate task, retrieve the most similar archive entries (both successes and failures) and ask a foundation model whether it is 'interestingly new'. Only admitted tasks are generated as code and attempted. ACD applies the same filter to discovering capabilities of a subject model (https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.07577). | Result: OMNI-EPIC had significantly higher cell coverage and ANNECS-OMNI than controls without the archive or interestingness model (p<0.05, Mann-Whitney). In a short RL run, 16 tasks were solved, 6 failed, and 1 was deemed uninteresting. ACD, with GPT-4o in both roles, found 1,330 interestingly new tasks from 5,000 generations in 25 clusters. About 20% of proposals were still 'interestingly new' after 5,000 generations. Crowd raters judged 92.2% of tasks valid, and the automated judge agreed with humans at F1 0.86, falling on 'very difficult' tasks. | Limits: No human validation of the interestingness judgments in OMNI-EPIC. ACD's raters were crowdworkers, not experts. AutoDiscovery (2025) found that this style of prompted interestingness selects human-surprising items only 15% of the time. | Relevance: This is the canonical template for an interestingness filter over an idea archive. It is useful as a novelty or deduplication gate, but it should not be trusted as the value signal. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.15568
- Novelty search (Lehman & Stanley): abandoning objectives in deceptive spaces (Joel Lehman, Kenneth O. Stanley 2011 (Evolutionary Computation 19(2))) [executed_computationally]: Selection rewards only behavioural novelty, measured as distance to the k nearest neighbours in the population plus an archive, and ignores the objective. This lets search escape deceptive local optima through stepping stones. | Result: Hard maze: novelty search solved 39 of 40 runs, fitness-based NEAT 3 of 40, random 4 of 40. Unbounded (open) maze: novelty search only 5 of 100 vs fitness 2 of 100, meaning novelty fails when the behaviour space is unconstrained. Biped walking: mean distance 4.04 m vs 2.88 m, best 13.7 m vs 6.8 m. | Limits: Small neuroevolution tasks from 2011, and results depend on a hand-chosen behaviour characterisation. The unbounded-maze failure is a mechanism that still applies today. | Relevance: Idea space is unbounded, like the open maze, so pure novelty rewards 'wandering off'. OptiLex needs novelty inside a minimal viability criterion (the soundness critic acting as a gate) plus a meaningful descriptor, such as mechanism family or variables touched, rather than raw embedding distance. | https://www.cs.swarthmore.edu/~meeden/DevelopmentalRobotics/lehman_ecj11.pdf
- POET goal-switching and Voyager skill libraries (Wang, Lehman, Clune, Stanley (POET 2019; Enhanced POET 2020); Guanzhi Wang et al. (Voyager 2023) 2019-2023) [executed_computationally]: POET co-evolves environments and agents and tries transfers, so a solution from one environment becomes the stepping stone for another (goal switching). Voyager accumulates an ever-growing library of executable, compositional skills that it retrieves for new goals (https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16291). | Result: POET solves environments that 'cannot be solved by direct optimization alone, or even through a direct-path curriculum', and transfer is described as essential. Voyager, relative to prior LLM agents, obtains 3.3x more unique items, travels 2.3x longer distances, and unlocks tech-tree milestones up to 15.3x faster. | Limits: Embodied and RL domains with cheap simulators. There is no test of goal switching in research ideation. | Relevance: For OptiLex, ideas that fail on one axis (data efficiency) should be tried against sibling goals (compute or energy efficiency) before being discarded. Validated mechanisms should be stored as composable 'skills' in the knowledge base. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.01753
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In HGM's Table 1, the self-improving agent's own benchmark score, which DGM uses to select parents, correlates only weakly with the best performance later reached by its descendants: Pearson 0.285 weighted and 0.406 unweighted on SWE-Verified-60, 0.383 on Polyglot. SICA's metric scores 0.444 and 0.274. HGM's clade-level estimator reaches 0.778 weighted on SWE-Verified-60 and 0.626 on Polyglot. With it, HGM reached 56.7% vs DGM's 53.3% using 517 vs 1231 CPU-hours. (caveat: (1) The accuracy and CPU-hour figures come from Table 2, not Table 1. (2) DGM's 0.383 on Polyglot is the weighted value; its unweighted value is 0.357. SICA's weighted and unweighted values are the same. (3) The claim leaves out that HGM's unweighted correlation on SWE-Verified-60 is only 0.512, a small lead over DGM's 0.406. On Polyglot, HGM's unweighted correlation is 0.873, but the paper's text says 0.8783, which contradicts its own table. (4) The target, "empirical CMP", is the best empirical mean accuracy reached within a node's clade, not counting the clade's root. HGM's estimator needed a leakage fix: the paper also removes the subtree that contains that maximum. So the comparison depends on the authors' own post-hoc construction. (5) The 56.7% vs 53.3% result is on a 60-task subset, which is 34 vs 32 tasks solved. It looks like a single run with no error bars, measured by executing SWE-bench tests (no LLM judge or humans involved). It used GPT-5 for expansion and GPT-5-mini for evaluation, with at most 800 evaluations. (6) "CPU-hours" means allocated wall-clock CPU-hours. Most of HGM's saving comes from its asynchronous expansion and evaluation, not from the better estimator alone. So "with it" overstates what caused the gain.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: In AutoDiscovery's Table 2 (1,620 hypotheses, 3 STEM MS/PhD annotators each), the share of reward-selected hypotheses that experts found surprising was 0.67 for Bayesian surprise (belief shift after running the experiment). For prompted LLM rewards it was 0.21 (utility), 0.15 (interestingness) and 0.11 (direct surprisal). Human ratings of interestingness and utility were 0.73-0.80 for every reward, so they did not discriminate. => CORRECTED: In AutoDiscovery (Agarwal et al., arXiv 2507.00310, v3 Feb 2026; the numbers are the same in v1 and v2), Table 2 reports "average human surprisal" over 1,620 hypotheses that had surprised the LLM. The paper describes them as "LLM surprisal hypotheses" pooled from 4 datasets across MCTS runs with four automatic rewards, each annotated by 3 crowdworkers from Prolific screened for STEM MS/PhD degrees. Overall scores: 0.67 for Bayesian surprise (bootstrap 95% CI 0.63-0.71), 0.21 for LLM utility, 0.15 for LLM interestingness and 0.11 for direct LLM surprisal. Human interestingness and utility ratings are in a separate table (Table 1 in v2/v3; Table 2 in v1). They range from 0.73 to 0.80 for all four rewards, so they did not separate the rewards. (caveat: 1) Wrong table: in the current versions (v2/v3), the interestingness and utility ratings are in Table 1, not Table 2. In v1 the two tables' numbers are swapped. 2) Humans judged the results, from their own view of the hypothesis. No lab work or human-run execution checked any finding. Annotators were Prolific crowdworkers screened for a STEM Master's/PhD, paid about $16.68/hr, rating 20 hypotheses in about 20 minutes. Each hypothesis got four 0-1 sliders: belief, usefulness to self, usefulness to the community, and interestingness.
