The paper does not clearly explain how "human surprisal" is computed from these; it is presumably human prior belief versus the experiment's outcome. The authors call 0.67 a "correlation"/"agreement" with human surprisal (abstract: "two-thirds"), and the caption calls the values "average human surprisal ratings". Calling it a "share of hypotheses experts found surprising" is a reasonable reading, but the paper does not state it. 3) The pool covers only hypotheses that were already LLM surprisals. The Bayesian-surprise reward is also the paper's own method, and the same LLM (GPT-4o) is the belief observer. 4) "1,620 hypotheses" is ambiguous. With 20 hypotheses per survey set and 3 annotators each, 1,620 may count annotations (about 540 unique hypotheses) rather than hypotheses. 5) Only 4 datasets were used, and Table 2 is broken out by annotator familiarity (Low/Med/High: 0.64/0.66/0.62 for Bayesian surprise, IAA 0.51-0.71). 6) Bayesian surprise here is the LLM's own belief shift after running a data-analysis experiment on existing datasets (DiscoveryBench etc.), not new wet-lab experiments.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Given the same literature pool, Claude Opus 4.7 prompted to propose research directions covered only 10.5% of the effective idea-atom vocabulary, with 95.7% of its choices in the top 10 atoms. Gemini 3.1 Pro covered 20.6% and a coherence-high, availability-low sampler covered 72.5%, with humans rating that sampler's overall quality equal to Claude's (4.03/5). During iterative autoresearch refinement, Claude's reports drifted from 2/10 to 8/10 interpretability-themed. => CORRECTED: Artiles et al., "The Alien Space of Science" (arXiv 2603.01092, v2, May 2026), split 16,068 LLM papers into a vocabulary of 273 idea atoms. Claude Opus 4.7 and Gemini 3.1 Pro were each queried 300 times, with the full shuffled atom list in context, and asked to pick novel but feasible 3-atom combinations. Claude's effective atom coverage (exp(entropy)/273) was 10.5%, and 95.7% of its combinations contained at least one of its own ten most-used atoms. Gemini scored 20.6% coverage and 76.7% top-10 concentration. The "Alien" coherence-high, availability-low sampler scored 72.5% coverage and 34.3% concentration. Uniform random triples scored 86.6% coverage. In a small blinded human study (10 reviewers, 40 reviews, 10 ideas per method, 5-point scales), Alien and Claude tied on overall quality at 4.03, with standard errors of ±0.11 and ±0.23. Across the autoresearch pipeline, 2 of Claude's 10 original idea titles fell in the SAE/feature-level mechanistic-interpretability cluster, rising to 8 of 10 final reports. Gemini went from 4/10 to 5/10, Alien stayed at 0/10, and Random went from 0/10 to 1/10. (caveat: All the numbers match the paper, but several framings in the claim are off.
(1) The models did not get "the same literature pool". Each received the same 273 pre-clustered atoms and had to choose 3-atom combinations. That is a constrained selection task, not open-ended proposal of research directions.
(2) "95.7% of its choices in the top 10 atoms" is inaccurate. The figure is the fraction of combinations that contain at least one of Claude's ten most-used atoms. Claude put Sparse Autoencoders in 42.67% of its samples.
(3) The human study is tiny: 10 ideas per method and 40 reviews in total. No significance tests are reported. The 4.03 tie is on the "Overall" column, and Claude led on promise (4.40) and coherence (4.30).
(4) Random sampling had higher coverage (86.6%) than Alien, so the diversity metric does not measure quality by itself.
(5) The interpretability drift covers 10 ideas per method. It was coded by the authors using a keyword/cluster rubric (SAE, activation patching, steering, etc.) and was not judged by humans or by an LLM. It counts original idea titles against final reports, so it is not a per-iteration trajectory.
(6) The comparison uses one corpus (LLM/ML papers), one atomization pipeline, and a single prompt per baseline, and Alien's β was tuned with Claude as the reconstructor. The downstream report ranking was done by an LLM judge using Swiss pairwise comparisons.)
Gaps:
  - Nobody has measured the metaproductivity-performance mismatch for research ideas: whether ideas with low immediate judge or experiment scores are the ancestors of the best later ideas. HGM-style clade credit has only been tested on coding agents, never on an idea to execution lineage.
  - There is no head-to-head test, against realized execution outcomes, of the interestingness signals an idea engine could use: prompted LLM interestingness (OMNI), Bayesian surprise (AutoDiscovery), anti-availability (Alien), learning progress (MAGELLAN), and variable-level novelty. Each was validated in isolation and on different proxies.
  - Archive-based open-ended search vs greedy keep/discard in LLM-training efficiency rests on a single run per variant (GEAR), 5-minute budgets, and one task. There is no multi-seed replication, no test of whether discovered improvements transfer to longer training or larger scale, and no joint data+compute+energy objective.
  - Nobody has tested goal switching (POET-style transfer) in research ideation, for example re-evaluating an idea that failed on data efficiency against compute or energy efficiency, or measured how often the best results come from cross-goal transfer.
  - Selector favoritism has been measured for VLMs choosing images (tree balance 0.235 vs human 0.363) but not for LLM critics choosing research ideas. Nobody knows whether an Opus critic population collapses the archive's phylogeny, or whether persona-diverse critic populations fix it without creating judge-exploiting 'adversarial hubs'.
  - There is no validated behaviour descriptor for novelty search in idea space. GEAR uses Jaccard distance over descriptions, IDEAgent an 8-axis pairwise decomposition, Alien idea atoms, and the creativity study variable-level novelty. None has been compared, although the novelty-search literature shows that the descriptor and the space constraint decide success (open maze: 5/100).
  - Stepping-stone value of rejected ideas is untested. IDEAgent and others store rejected patterns only in order to avoid them, and nobody has measured whether reviving archived failures yields later wins, as DGM's lineage dips suggest it might.
  - Evaluator co-evolution anchored to realized outcomes (Red Queen Gödel Machine) has not been tried for research ideation. For OptiLex, the natural anchors are executed small-scale training results, and critics would be refreshed each epoch.
  - There is no operational open-endedness metric for an idea archive, such as Hughes et al.'s novelty plus learnability to an observer, or a research analogue of ANNECS. So 'the engine is still producing new, learnable ideas' is currently unmeasurable.
  - There is no cost-per-realized-discovery accounting that compares open-ended archives with greedy loops. DGM costs about USD 22k per run and DeepScientist about 20,000 GPU-hours for 21 successes, but no study normalises by compute or energy.
Surprises:
  - DGM's own parent-selection signal (the agent's current score) predicts descendant quality weakly (r≈0.29). The archive works mainly because it keeps and occasionally branches from non-best nodes, not because it identifies the right ones (HGM, 2025).
  - Prompted LLM 'interestingness' found human-surprising hypotheses only 15% of the time, vs 67% for measured belief shift. Humans rated every reward's outputs about equally 'interesting' (0.73-0.76), so human interestingness ratings do not discriminate either (AutoDiscovery).
  - Mode collapse got worse during execution and refinement, not only at generation: Claude went from 2/10 to 8/10 interpretability-themed reports inside the autoresearch loop (Alien Space, 2026). Fixers and refiners act as attractors.
  - VLM selectors show 'stubborn favoritism' even among highly noisy candidates. Exploratory noise raised phylogenetic but not genetic diversity. More history made things worse (history length 1 was best), and 1,000-persona populations recovered diversity but produced 10-20% adversarial hub images (VLM-Picbreeder, GECCO 2026).
  - In the autoresearch creativity study, 'valueness' (how often a model's edits improved) correlated negatively with final improvement (r=−0.632). The frequent small winners lost to models that explored new variables.
  - ASI-Arch's best architectures were less 'original' (6.6% vs 10.4%) and drew more on the system's own experiment analysis (44.8% vs 37.7%). The executed-result archive mattered more than de novo invention or the literature base.
  - LLM judges get worse as a lineage matures: selective accuracy fell from 82.8% to 56.9% after ≥3 accumulated successes while their willingness to decide rose (Rehearse, 2026). This is a confidence cliff exactly where stepping stones matter.
  - Outside coding, the original DGM and the archive-free ablation both scored 0.0 on paper-review prediction. Only the version with an editable meta level (DGM-H) reached 0.710, beating the AI-Scientist-v2 reviewer's 0.630 (Hyperagents, 2026).
  - Pure novelty search failed in its own founding paper when the space was unbounded: the open maze was solved 5/100 times vs 2/100 for fitness. This is a direct warning for unconstrained idea-space novelty.
  - The Station (Nov 2025, https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.06309) is an open-world multi-agent setting with a shared paper archive and lineages. Its scRNA-seq SOTA (0.5877 vs 0.5867 with <5k vs 130k-260k evaluations) came from importing 'density-awareness' from clustering, a goal-switching-style cross-domain transfer. It has no ablations, though.
  - A July 2026 shadow evaluation found that frontier agents given six days and thousands of dollars did all the engineering on two unpublished NeurIPS 2026 questions but made no substantial research progress. The named failure modes included 'ineffective backtracking' and uncreative problem-solving, which are what archives and stepping-stone preservation address (https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27191).

## evidence_check
- SciMuse v4 (revised 24 Sep 2026): expert-rated interest of GPT-4 collaboration ideas (Xuemei Gu, Mario Krenn (MPI Science of Light) 2024 (v1), v4 posted 2026-09-24) [expert_human_rating]: What was generated: ideas for pairs of Max Planck group leaders, framed as a collaboration between Researcher A (the rater) and Researcher B (anonymised). GPT-4 (used Sep 2023 to Feb 2024) was given up to 7 recent paper titles per researcher and asked for a 'creative and surprising scientific project', with 3 rounds of self-critique and revision. There were three randomised conditions, 16 ideas each, 48 per rater: (1) a concept pair (one concept from each researcher) ranked by Impact4Cast's predicted citations, where 'high-impact' means at least 10 citations within 3 years; (2) a random concept pair; (3) titles only, with no KG pair. Researcher pairs were sampled from the same institute, the same broad domain, or across domains. Who rated: only Researcher A, on a 1-5 scale from 'not interesting' to 'very interesting'. 'Interest' was defined as early-stage curiosity worth further thought, before feasibility, resources or priority come in. | Result: 110 raters, 4,451 of 5,280 possible ratings. Mean 2.403 ± 0.020. Most common rating was 1 (36.7%); 24.9% rated 4-5. Mean by condition: high-citation-predicted pairs 2.350 ± 0.034, random pairs 2.446 ± 0.035, titles-only 2.414 ± 0.036. High-citation pairs trend lower than random at only 1.94σ. Share rated 4-5: 23.3%, 25.2%, 26.2%. The KG pairs gave no gain over titles-only. Across 2,996 KG ideas, rated interest fell as the rater's own concept was more connected: degree slope −0.213 (idea-level ρ = −0.166), PageRank ρ = −0.176, total citations ρ = −0.153, last-year citations ρ = −0.143. Interest also fell with semantic distance between A and B: slope −0.309, ρ = −0.223, but binned R² = 0.734 is on 50 bin means. Higher concept citations go with higher predicted impact but lower rated interest. Predicting which ideas rated 4-5: neural net AUC 63.2%, GPT-4o Elo ranking AUC 67.4%, GPT-3.5 AUC 60.4%. | Limits: The authors now say the design 'cannot distinguish an effect of interdisciplinarity itself from a preference for ideas closer to the evaluator's expertise', because A defines one end of the distance. No evaluator fixed effects or mixed model; correlations use raw ratings. Degree and PageRank belong to the rater's own concept, so they partly measure the rater (niche vs broad field) and how specific the idea is, not field saturation as such. Binned R² makes the effects look stronger than they are: idea-level |ρ| is at most 0.22. How well GPT-4 bridged distant fields was never measured. Raters judged half an idea in a field they don't know. Other limits: 41.2% participation; 104 of 110 raters from natural sciences; all Max Planck; small order effect (−0.18 points across 48 questions); nothing was executed and no citations were tracked. Earlier versions v1-v3 said only 'no significant difference' between conditions. | Relevance: This measures rated interest, not realized impact. For OptiLex: (a) a KB that picks concept pairs gave no rated gain over a plain prompt, so KB value has to be shown by executed A/B tests, not ratings; (b) the distance penalty is plausibly self-relevance, not a quality signal, so it is weak evidence against cross-domain generation; (c) GPT-4o predicts expert interest only at AUC 0.67, which matches our own finding that LLM judges are unreliable. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.17044v4
- SciMuse v4 human baseline: PhD-written vs GPT-4 vs GPT-5.2 ideas (Gu & Krenn 2026 (added in v4)) [expert_human_rating]: 7 experienced PhD students (at least 3rd year) wrote 148 personalised ideas for 6 physics-related group leaders. They had the same inputs as the LLM (both researchers' articles plus two concepts), 10 minutes per idea, and no internet. GPT-5.2 generated 160 ideas from the same inputs, and 100 matching GPT-4 ideas were drawn from the main study. The 6 leaders rated all of them blind to source. | Result: Share rated 4-5: human 29.1 ± 3.7%, GPT-4 25.0 ± 4.4%, GPT-5.2 32.5 ± 3.7%. The authors call the distributions similar; no significant difference. | Limits: Only 6 raters, all in physics; small sample. Humans were limited to the provided materials while the LLM could draw on its pretraining, which the authors say favours the AI. Rated interest only. | Relevance: The roughly 25-30% 'high-interest' ceiling hardly moves from GPT-4 to GPT-5.2 or to human PhD students. That suggests the ceiling comes from the rater and the format (unsolicited ideas for someone else's agenda), not from generator quality. So ratings of this kind cannot detect generator improvements, and OptiLex should not use them as its objective. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.17044v4
- HindSight: time-split realized-impact evaluation vs LLM-as-judge (Bo Jiang (Temple University) 2026-03) [retrospective_prediction]: Ideas are generated by Llama-3.3-70B (knowledge cutoff Dec 2023) restricted to literature before a June 2023 cutoff T. Each idea is matched by SPECTER2 cosine similarity (threshold 0.96) against 27,589 AI/ML papers published June 2023 to Dec 2025, and scored by the matched papers' citations and venue. The same ideas were also scored by an LLM judge. | Result: 200 ideas, 100 from a ResearchAgent-style retrieval system and 100 from a vanilla baseline, across 10 topics. LLM-judge overall score: 7.44 vs 7.40 (p = 0.584). HindSight score: 0.297 vs 0.119, a 2.5x gap (p < 0.001). 81% vs 42% of ideas matched at least one future paper. The judge rated the baseline higher on novelty and on impact (both p < 0.001). Within the retrieval arm, HindSight correlated with LLM-judged novelty at ρ = −0.291 (p = 0.003), with judged impact at −0.225, and with feasibility at +0.252. None of the baseline correlations was significant. The gap grows from 1.1x to 3.8x as the matching threshold tightens. | Limits: Single author, n = 200, one 70B generator, one embedding model. By construction the metric rewards ideas the community went on to publish, so it penalises genuinely unanticipated ideas. The negative novelty correlation is partly built into the metric, not purely a judge failure. Nothing was executed. | Relevance: This is the direct counterpart to SciMuse. Retrieval grounding was invisible to the LLM judge but produced 2.5x more realized matches, so an LLM judge can miss exactly the benefit a KB provides. OptiLex should score its KB A/B tests on outcomes (execution, or time-split matching), not on judge scores. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15164
- Predicting empirical AI research outcomes (pairwise idea success) (Jiaxin Wen, Chenglei Si, Yueh-han Chen, He He, Shi Feng 2025 (NeurIPS 2025)) [retrospective_prediction]: Given two ideas aimed at the same benchmark, predict which performed better; outcomes are taken from published experiments. A fine-tuned GPT-4.1 with a paper-retrieval agent was compared with 25 human experts and with off-the-shelf frontier LMs. Test set: 1,585 idea pairs published after the model cutoff; 6,000 training pairs. | Result: NLP subset: system 64.4% vs human experts 48.9%, i.e. experts were at chance. Full test set: system 77%. o3 was no better than random, even with the same retrieval. On unpublished ideas, including ideas from an AI ideation agent, the system reached 63.6%. | Limits: Outcomes come from published papers, so there is publication and selection bias. It is pairwise and within a benchmark only, which says nothing about novelty or impact. Expert results cover a subset only. | Relevance: Strong evidence that expert ratings and off-the-shelf LLM judgement do not predict executed outcomes, while a model fine-tuned on outcomes does. OptiLex's evaluation layer should be trained or calibrated on its own executed runs (loss per unit of data, compute and energy), not on prompted judges. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00794
- Comparative empirical forecasting with small fine-tuned models (Srujan P. Mule, Aniketh Garikaparthi, Manasi Patwardhan (TCS Research) 2026-04) [retrospective_prediction]: 11,488 idea pairs built from PapersWithCode benchmark results. Task: given a research goal and two ideas, predict which scored higher. An 8B model was trained with SFT, or with RL from verifiable rewards (RLVR). | Result: Off-the-shelf 8B model 30%. SFT 8B model 77.1%. GPT-5 61.1%. RLVR 71.35% with readable justifications. The authors report robustness to surface heuristics and transfer to a cross-domain time-split test set. | Limits: Outcomes are drawn from published benchmark results (survivorship; leaderboard noise). Pairwise and within-benchmark only. | Relevance: A cheap outcome-trained verifier beats a frontier model's judgement by about 16 points. This supports building OptiLex's evaluation layer as a small learned predictor on executed LLM-training runs rather than relying on Opus as judge. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21491
- Learning to Ideate for Scientific Impact (citation-reward RL for idea generation) (Shubham Kale, Aniketh Garikaparthi, Manasi Patwardhan 2026-09-24) [llm_judge_only]: More than 100K CS papers are converted into (goal, idea) pairs, each with an ordinal, year-normalised citation label. A goal-conditioned reward model is trained to predict that label, and a generator is then tuned by SFT followed by RL against the reward model. Evaluation is held out and reference-grounded: an LLM compares a generated idea with a historical idea for the same goal, weighted by the historical idea's citation label. | Result: Reward model: 48.0% exact ordinal accuracy, more than 20 points above GPT-5; Spearman 0.756, QWK 0.754. Majority-vote 'impact rate': RL generator 51.66% vs base 30.66% vs SFT 26.61%. | Limits: The generated ideas were never executed or cited; the gains are judge-estimated. Citation labels mix idea quality with author, venue and popularity effects. A Spearman of 0.756 from idea text alone is suspiciously high and could reflect the LLM recognising famous papers, or leakage. Citation-optimised generation may learn to favour crowded topics (see the SciMuse and Impact4Cast anti-alignment). | Relevance: The first 'optimise the generator for realized impact' pipeline. For OptiLex, the same template would work better with an executed metric (efficiency frontier) as the reward instead of citations, which avoids both the popularity confound and circularity. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29802
- Atypical combinations and scientific impact (Brian Uzzi, Satyam Mukherjee, Michael Stringer, Benjamin Jones 2013 (Science 342:468, 25 Oct 2013)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: For each of 17.9M Web of Science papers, every pair of referenced journals gets a z-score: observed co-citation frequency against a randomised citation network that keeps in-degree, out-degree and timing. z > 0 means a conventional pairing and z < 0 a novel one. 'Median conventionality' is whether the paper's median z falls in the upper half of all papers. 'Tail novelty' is whether its 10th-percentile z is below 0. A hit paper is one in the top 5% of 8-year citations across the dataset (main analysis: WoS 1990-2000). | Result: Hits per 100 papers: high conventionality + high tail novelty 9.11, nearly twice the 5/100 background; high conventionality + low novelty 5.82; low conventionality + high novelty 5.33; low + low 2.05 (P < 0.00001). The pattern holds for other decades, for top-1% and top-10% hit definitions, and with field controls. Impact rises with median conventionality up to about the 85th-95th percentile, then reverses. Teams are 37.7% more likely than solo authors to include tail novelty. | Limits: Correlational. Measured on published papers only, so atypical attempts that failed or were never published are unseen (survivorship). Novelty is at the journal-pair level, a coarse proxy. The outcome is citation counts, not correctness. | Relevance: This is realized impact, not rated interest, so it does not conflict with SciMuse. It also does not support distance as such: novelty without a conventional core (5.33) is at background. The design rule for the OptiLex generator and KB is a mostly conventional core plus a small tail of atypical, cross-field elements, not fully distant pairings. | https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1240474
- Surprising combinations of research contents and contexts; outsider 'expeditions' (Feng Shi, James Evans 2023 (Nat. Commun. 14:1641, 24 Mar 2023)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A hypergraph embedding model predicts which combinations of contents (MeSH, PACS or USPC codes) and contexts (cited journals) will appear. It separates real from random combinations with AUC 0.95-0.98 for content and 0.83-0.99 for context. A paper's novelty is the surprisal of its combination. The data are 19.9M MEDLINE papers (1865-2009), 541k APS papers and 6.5M US patents. A hit is a top-10% most-cited paper of its year. 'Expedition novelty' is the distance between the authors' publishing backgrounds and the venue of the focal paper. | Result: MEDLINE, compared with random: the most surprising context combinations are about 4x more likely to be hits, content about 2x, joint about 5x. Nearly 50% of papers in the top joint-novelty group are hits, and the effect is stronger for the top 1%. The most surprising patents are about 2x more likely. Papers from the most surprising expeditions are 3.5x more likely to be hits. Nobel-prize papers have high content novelty but below-average context novelty. | Limits: The authors: 'we only evaluate our surprise measure on papers that pass the filter of peer review', so survivorship applies. Correlational, and the causal role of expeditions is untested. Keywords and journals are coarse; a paper is treated as a bag of keywords. | Relevance: Supports cross-field transfer when an outsider brings a problem-solving result to a distant field. Awards, given by disciplinary communities, diverge from citations for context-novel work, so home-field judges undervalue it. OptiLex judges from the home field (e.g. ML-efficiency experts or an ML-framed Opus judge) may systematically under-rate transferred ideas. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-023-36741-4
- Human-aware AI and 'alien' hypotheses (Jamshid Sourati, James Evans 2023 (Nat. Hum. Behav. 7:1682-1696, 13 Jul 2023)) [retrospective_prediction]: Random walks run over a hypergraph of papers, concepts and authors, so predictions account for which experts could plausibly make each inference. A parameter β moves predictions toward (β < 0) or away from (β > 0) the human crowd. Crowd-avoiding ('alien') hypotheses are scored with first-principles or data-driven proxies: DFT power factor for thermoelectrics, symmetry-based polarization for ferroelectrics, protein-interaction proximity for drugs. | Result: Materials: about 100% average precision gain over content-only baselines. Drug repurposing for 100 diseases: 43% higher precision. COVID-19: 36-38% of predictions were chosen for clinical trials within 12 months (42% by July 2021) vs 10-12% for content-only, which is the 'up to 400%' headline. As β rises, discoverability falls much faster than theoretical merit, which only collapses near β ≈ 0.4. The best alien range is β 0.2-0.3, where hypotheses' theoretical scores exceed those of published discoveries. | Limits: The 400% counts which drugs experts chose to trial, i.e. human attention, not whether they worked. The alien hypotheses were checked only against computational proxies, available for about 45% of properties, and never validated in the lab. The data are pre-LLM; the mechanism is graph random walks. | Relevance: Shows why 'interest' and 'impact' can diverge: models that predict human uptake learn crowd behaviour. For OptiLex, a crowd-avoidance knob (penalise ideas many groups would reach) combined with an executable check of merit is the direct analogue, and in the LLM-efficiency domain merit can be measured, not just proxied. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-023-01648-z
- Bias against novelty: high-risk/high-gain and delayed recognition (Jian Wang, Reinhilde Veugelers, Paula Stephan 2017 (Research Policy 46(8):1416-1436); NBER WP 22180 (2016)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A paper's novelty is its first-ever combinations of referenced journals, weighted by how distant the journals are. 'Highly novel' means the top 1% of its field, 'moderately novel' any other positive score. The data are about 1.06M WoS articles from 2001, with citations tracked over 1-14 year windows. The analysis uses negative binomial, multinomial and logistic models with controls. | Result: NBER WP figures: 89% of papers make no new journal combination. Highly novel papers get 12.04% more citations, but with higher variance. Their odds of a top-1% 'big hit' are 39.82% higher, and of landing in the bottom 10% 16.76% higher (vs middle 80%). They are less likely to be top-cited in windows shorter than 3 years and only significantly more likely from year 4. They get no top-cited advantage in their home field but 62.37% higher odds of being top-cited in foreign fields. They are published in journals with about 18.11% lower impact factor. | Limits: Correlational. Published papers only. Figures are from the NBER working paper; the published Research Policy version may differ slightly. A journal-pair novelty proxy. | Relevance: This is the key link from rated interest to realized impact. Novel work's payoff arrives late and outside the home field. A SciMuse-style rater (the home-field Researcher A) or any short-horizon metric will under-rate exactly the ideas with fat-tail upside. OptiLex should treat judge and short-run signals as biased against novelty and keep a deliberate exploration budget. | https://www.nber.org/papers/w22180
- Intellectual distance and novelty in expert evaluation (randomised field experiment) (Kevin Boudreau, Eva Guinan, Karim Lakhani, Christoph Riedl 2016 (Management Science 62(10):2765-2783)) [expert_human_rating]: An internal grant competition at Harvard Medical School (endocrine-related disease). 142 evaluators were each randomly assigned 15 of 150 single-author proposals, giving 2,130 evaluator-proposal pairs, triple blinded, scored on a 10-point scale. Novelty is the percentile share of the proposal's MeSH-term pairs never seen before in PubMed. Intellectual distance is the MeSH overlap between evaluator and proposal. | Result: Evaluators gave lower scores to proposals closer to their own expertise: variation in distance accounts for 1.1 points on the 10-point scale (SD 1.7 after fixed effects), which is causal given the randomisation. Closest-expert scoring would move proposals about 30 rank positions. More novel proposals got lower scores: coefficient on novelty percentile −2.067 (s.e. 0.64) in the 689-pair subsample with controls. The effect is driven by the right tail of novelty; at low novelty, scores rise with novelty. | Limits: The novelty effect is observational at proposal level (with controls), not randomised. One institution and one medical domain. The outcome is a score; later project success was not tracked. | Relevance: Evaluators penalise the most novel ideas, so ratings are not neutral with respect to novelty. The proximity gradient is the reverse of SciMuse: close experts are harsher on quality, while SciMuse raters give close ideas more interest. So 'interest' and 'quality score' respond to proximity in opposite ways. OptiLex's critics need calibration against executed outcomes, and the right tail of novelty needs protected quota rather than critic veto. | https://pubsonline.informs.org/doi/10.1287/mnsc.2015.2285
- ERC Starting Grant selection penalty for novel track records (Reinhilde Veugelers, Jian Wang, Paula Stephan 2022 (NBER WP 30320); 2025 (Economics of Innovation and New Technology)) [expert_human_rating]: Probit models of ERC Starting Grant selection on whether applicants had a top-1% novel publication in the 5 years before applying. The models control for having a top-1% highly-cited paper, publication count, field, country, year and gender. A difference-in-differences design estimates the effect of funding on later novelty. | Result: Applicants with a top-1% novel publication history are significantly less likely to be funded, especially early-career applicants. The penalty holds even among top-cited applicants and still appears with a top-5% novelty cutoff. In the pool, 14.32% had a top-1% novel paper and 26.73% a top-1% cited paper. | Limits: Magnitudes were not extracted here (probit tables only). Observational. Novelty is a journal-combination proxy. | Relevance: Shows expert panels penalising novelty in real decisions with real money. It adds to the case that an OptiLex evaluation layer imitating human expert review would inherit a conservative bias. | https://www.nber.org/papers/w30320
- Negativity bias when evaluators see each other's scores ('Conservatism gets funded?') (Jacqueline Lane, Misha Teplitskiy, Gary Gray, Hardeep Ranu, Michael Menietti, Eva Guinan, Karim Lakhani 2022 (Management Science 68(6):4478-4495)) [expert_human_rating]: Two field experiments with 369 evaluators from 7 universities scoring 97 proposals (761 pairs, more than $250k in awards). After scoring, evaluators were randomly shown fabricated other-reviewer scores that were lower, neutral or higher than their own, and could then update. | Result: Evaluators lowered their scores by 0.759 points after seeing lower scores, but raised them only 0.449 after seeing higher ones. Updated scores differed by 23.5% between the two conditions. In a realistic subset they were about 2.9x more likely to lower than to raise. 40% of the funded set changed after updates. | Limits: Novelty was not measured directly; the link to conservatism is argued, not shown. University seed grants only. | Relevance: This is a mechanism risk for OptiLex's critic→fixer loop. If soundness critics see each other's verdicts, or aggregation lets one negative critique veto, the portfolio drifts toward ideas with the fewest weaknesses rather than the most upside. Keep critics independent, aggregate without veto, and let fixers repair rather than drop ideas. | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9531843/
- Impact4Cast: predicting future citations of never-studied concept pairs (Xuemei Gu, Mario Krenn 2024 (v4 Jun 2025)) [retrospective_prediction]: An evolving knowledge graph built from more than 21M papers, linking concept co-occurrence with citations. 141 graph features (degree, PageRank, Simpson and Dice similarity, citation counts) feed a neural net that predicts whether a concept pair never studied before will pass a citation threshold within 3 years. This is the predictor SciMuse used to pick its 'high-impact' pairs. | Result: AUC 'beyond 0.9 for most experiments' for predicting future citation thresholds of new concept pairs (e.g. trained on 2016-2019, evaluated on pairs from 2019 with outcomes in 2022). | Limits: Predicts citations, which track popularity and crowding, not correctness or value. In SciMuse the pairs it picks trend lower on expert interest (1.94σ), and concept citation counts push predicted impact and rated interest in opposite directions. | Relevance: A realized-impact predictor can be accurate and still steer generation toward crowded topics that experts find dull. This fits Uzzi (a conventional core earns citations). OptiLex should not use predicted citations as its novelty objective; use executed efficiency gains. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.08640
- Convergent validity of bibliometric novelty against expert novelty tags (F1000Prime) (Lutz Bornmann, Alexander Tekles, Helena Zhang, Fred Ye 2019 (J. Informetrics 13(4):100979; corrigendum 2024)) [expert_human_rating; NOT verified from primary]: Tests Uzzi-style (U) and Wang-style (W) reference-combination novelty scores against F1000Prime faculty tags such as 'new finding' and 'hypothesis' on biomedical papers. | Result: For each one-SD increase in the U score, expected assignments of the 'new finding' tag rise by 7.47%. U is mostly consistent with expectations; W shows little convergent validity. | Limits: A 2024 corrigendum exists; its content could not be read (paywalled). Biomedicine only. Tags are expert perceptions, not outcomes. | Relevance: Shows that bibliometric atypicality (which predicts realized citations) and experts' perception of novelty are only weakly linked. It adds to the case for not using one as a stand-in for the other in the OptiLex evaluation layer. | https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.03233
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Uzzi et al. 2013: among WoS papers from 1990-2000, those with high median conventionality AND high tail novelty (10th-percentile journal-pair z < 0) are hits (top 5% of 8-year citations) at 9.11 per 100, against a 5/100 background. High novelty with low conventionality gives 5.33 per 100 and low/low gives 2.05. (caveat: 1) The claim skips the fourth cell, high conventionality with low novelty, which has 5.82/100. That is higher than the 5.33 for high novelty with low conventionality. So novelty alone does not beat the 5% baseline. The effect comes from pairing it with a conventional core.
2) The novelty and conventionality measures are bibliometric. They come from z-scores of co-cited pairs of referenced journals, measured against a randomized citation network. They say nothing about how novel the ideas themselves are.
3) "Impact" means citation counts only. No human or expert judge rated the papers, and nothing was measured by execution or lab work. The study is observational and correlational, so it shows no causal link.
4) Hits are the top 5% across the whole dataset. Field controls, other decades (1950-2000) and 1% or 10% hit thresholds appear only in the supplementary material.
5) The cells differ a lot in size. The WoS shares by bar are 6.7%, 23%, 26% and 44%, but the paper gives them by bar color, so I could not tie each share to a cell from the text alone. The high/high group is probably a small minority.
6) If this is used as evidence about "rated interest vs realized impact", note that it contains no interest ratings. It links a structural property of a paper's reference list to later citations.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: SciMuse v4 (arXiv 2405.17044v4, 24 Sep 2026): 110 Max Planck group leaders each rated 48 GPT-4 ideas framed as a collaboration with an anonymised colleague. Mean interest: high-citation-predicted KG pairs 2.350 ± 0.034, random pairs 2.446 ± 0.035, titles-only (no KG) 2.414 ± 0.036. Share rated 4-5: 23.3%, 25.2%, 26.2%. The authors state that KG concept pairs 'do not improve expert-rated interest over a titles-only GPT baseline'. They also state the negative distance effect (ρ = −0.223) cannot be separated from a preference for ideas close to the rater's own expertise. => CORRECTED: SciMuse v4 (arXiv 2405.17044v4, posted 24 Sep 2026; Gu & Krenn): 110 Max Planck group leaders (104 natural, 6 social sciences; 41.2% of 267 invited) were each offered 48 GPT-4 ideas framed as a collaboration with an anonymised fellow MPG group leader. Only 4,451 of 5,280 possible ratings were recorded (84.3%), so not every leader rated all 48. Mean interest (±SEM): high-citation-predicted KG pairs 2.350 ± 0.034 (n=1516), random KG pairs 2.446 ± 0.035 (n=1480), titles-only baseline with no concept pair 2.414 ± 0.036 (n=1455). Share rated 4-5: 23.3 ± 1.09%, 25.2 ± 1.13%, 26.2 ± 1.15%. The abstract says supplying KG-selected concept pairs 'does not improve expert-rated interest over a titles-only GPT baseline'. The researcher-to-researcher semantic distance correlates with interest at ρ = −0.223 across the 2,996 KG-condition ratings, and the authors say their design cannot separate an interdisciplinarity effect from a preference for ideas close to the evaluator's own expertise. (caveat: 1) 'Each rated 48' overstates things: 48 ideas were offered per leader, but only 84.3% of possible ratings came back. 2) Humans did the judging, but it was one self-rating of subjective interest on a 1-5 scale. Only the evaluating researcher A rated each idea, there were no execution or lab tests, and no realized-impact outcome was measured. So interest is not impact. 3) The paper gives no formal significance tests between conditions, only SEMs. The high-citation vs random gap is called a weak 1.94σ tendency, and all three conditions overlap within about 2-3 SEM. The null is 'no improvement', not 'KG hurts'. 4) ρ = −0.223 covers only the 2,996 KG-condition ratings, not the titles-only ideas. The distance is between the two researchers, not between the concepts. 5) The quoted phrase uses singular 'does not improve' in the abstract, while the claim paraphrases it as 'do not improve'. 6) Other parts of v4 the claim leaves out: a small side study with 6 physics evaluators found similar high-interest shares for 148 PhD-student-written ideas (29.1%), GPT-4 (25.0%) and GPT-5.2 (32.5%). The authors also say graph features can still predict and control properties of ideas. 7) The overall mean is 2.403 ± 0.020, the most common rating is 1 (36.7%), and 24.9% of ideas were rated 4-5.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Wang, Veugelers & Stephan (NBER WP 22180 / Research Policy 2017): highly novel (top-1%) papers have 39.82% higher odds of becoming a top-1% big hit. They are less likely to be top-cited in citation windows under 3 years. They have no top-cited advantage in their home field but 62.37% higher odds of being top-cited in foreign fields. (caveat: (1) Method: these are observational bibliometric results from logistic regressions on Web of Science research articles published in 2001 (773,311 articles; some figure notes say 1,056,936). No LLM, human or lab judgement is involved. Impact means citations, and the effects are associations with controls, not causal. (2) Comparison group: the odds ratios compare against NON-NOVEL papers, meaning papers that make no new journal combinations, not against all other papers. "Highly novel" means the top 1% of novelty score within a WoS subject category, where novelty is first-ever pairings of referenced journals weighted by how hard the pairing is. So this is combinatorial novelty in reference lists, not rated interest. (3) Big hit means top 1% by citations within the same WoS subject category and publication year, using the long window of about 14 years (to 2014). Highly novel papers become significantly more likely to be top-cited only from year 4, and moderately novel papers only from year 9. (4) Foreign-field top-cited means the top 1% by citations coming from other fields, compared with papers in the paper's own category (footnote 7). It does not mean top-cited within the foreign field itself. Moderately novel papers are actually 8.37% LESS likely to be top-cited in their home field. (5) Risk: highly novel papers also have 14.90% higher citation dispersion and 16.76% higher odds of landing in the bottom 10%, so the gain comes with more failures. (6) Version drift: the numbers were checked in the NBER WP only. An earlier conference draft (MPI copy) reports a 36% big-hit odds increase, so the Research Policy 2017 (46(8):1416-1436) figures may differ slightly and were not checked against the journal PDF. Cite the numbers as NBER WP 22180 figures. (7) Relevance: this measures realized citation impact against a structural novelty proxy. It is not direct evidence about how people's rated interest relates to realized impact.)
Gaps:
  - No study has measured, for the same set of AI-generated ideas at scale, expert or LLM rated interest and a realized executed outcome under a fixed compute budget. So there is no calibration curve from rating to outcome, least of all for LLM-training efficiency, where execution is cheap and numeric. Wen et al. 2025 and Mule et al. 2026 use historical published pairs; HindSight uses matching, not execution.
  - Nobody has had both researchers rate the same cross-field idea. SciMuse collected ratings only from A, so self-relevance cannot be separated from quality, and the authors list this as future work. Wang et al. 2017 predicts that B, the foreign-field side, is where the value shows up.
  - No test of Uzzi's 'conventional core + atypical tail' structure as an explicit constraint on LLM idea generation, scored by executed outcomes, against fully distant or fully conventional generation.
  - Every realized-impact study (Uzzi, Shi & Evans, Wang et al.) sees only published papers. The base rate of failed atypical attempts is unknown. An executable harness that runs every generated idea, failures included, would be the first to measure it.
  - The 'alien' crowd-avoiding hypotheses (Sourati & Evans) were checked only against DFT or protein-interaction proxies and discovery wait times. They have never been validated by execution or in the lab, and never tested with frontier LLM generators.
  - Time-split realized metrics (HindSight, IdeaForecastBench 2609.00747, Future-Alignment Score 2603.27146) reward anticipating the crowd, so they structurally penalise correct but unanticipated ideas. Outside direct execution, no metric rewards 'correct and not anticipated'.
  - No study checks whether frontier LLM judges (Opus or GPT-5 class) reproduce the human novelty penalty (Boudreau; ERC) or its reverse (HindSight: judges over-reward novel-sounding ideas), measured against executed outcomes. HindSight used one 70B model and n = 200.
  - No test of whether the negativity bias Lane et al. found in shared human critique also arises among LLM critics in multi-agent critic/fixer pipelines, and whether it pushes idea portfolios toward conservative ones.
  - SciMuse's correlations were not re-run with evaluator fixed effects. It is unknown whether the 'less-connected concept → more interest' effect survives within raters, since degree is a property of the rater's own concept.
Surprises:
  - SciMuse v4 was posted on 24 Sep 2026, the day before this review, and changes the headline. Earlier versions said 'no significant difference' between conditions. v4 says KG-selected pairs give no gain over a titles-only prompt, that high-citation-predicted pairs trend lower (1.94σ), and that the distance penalty cannot be separated from self-relevance. OptiLex's IDEAS_FACTORY.md cites SciMuse from before v4 and should be updated. The confound named in the latest commit ('raters judged fields they did not know') is not the one the authors now emphasise; theirs is self-relevance.
  - In SciMuse, degree and PageRank are measured on the rater's own concept. So 'less-connected concepts are rated more interesting' may mean 'specific, niche raters and ideas rate higher', not 'less-saturated fields are more interesting'.
  - Human PhD students (29.1%), GPT-4 (25.0%) and GPT-5.2 (32.5%) get about the same share of 4-5 ratings from the same 6 physicists. The roughly 25% ceiling looks like a property of the rater and the format, not the generator.
  - Predicted citation impact and rated interest pull in opposite directions on the same graph features: concepts with more citations mean higher predicted impact but lower interest.
  - Boudreau et al. find that evaluators closest to a proposal give lower quality scores, while SciMuse raters give higher interest to ideas closer to their own field. Proximity has opposite effects on 'quality score' and 'interest'.
  - Uzzi's data do not support distance as such. Novelty without a conventional core has a hit rate of 5.33/100, against a 5/100 background. The 2x premium needs both.
  - In Wang et al. 2017, novel papers get no top-cited advantage in their home field; all of the advantage is in foreign fields (62% higher odds), and it only appears after about 3 years. A home-field rater in SciMuse is structurally the wrong audience for novel cross-field ideas.
  - Sourati & Evans' '400%' improvement measures which COVID drugs experts chose to put into trials, i.e. human attention, not whether the drugs worked.
  - Human experts were at chance (48.9%) at picking which of two NLP ideas performed better, and o3 was also near random. Fine-tuned outcome predictors reached 64-77%, and a fine-tuned 8B model beat GPT-5 by 16 points (77.1% vs 61.1%).
  - The LLM judge in HindSight rated the vanilla baseline higher on novelty and impact, yet those ideas matched real future papers 2.5x less often. LLM judges reward ideas that sound novel, while human panels penalise novelty. Both are miscalibrated, in opposite directions.

## judges_critics
- Comparative empirical forecasting with small fine-tuned verifiers (SFT and RLVR on leaderboard idea pairs) (Mule, Garikaparthi, Patwardhan (TCS Research); Findings of ACL 2026 2026) [retrospective_prediction]: Build 11,488 idea pairs from PapersWithCode leaderboards, with results stripped from the idea text. The label is which idea scores higher on a benchmark-specific goal. Qwen3-8B is trained either on direct labels (SFT) or with RLVR (DAPO/DrGRPO) to produce a justification plus a label. Every pair is scored in both A/B orders and counts as correct only if both orders agree. | Result: In-domain: Direct-SFT reaches 77.10%, Reason-SFT-DAPO/DrGRPO 71.35%, GPT-5 (high reasoning) 61.10%, base Qwen3 20.14% (all swap-consistent). Cross-domain test: every approach falls to about 46-49% (Direct-SFT 45.67, DrGRPO 49.08, GPT-5-high 45.96). On a 52-pair 2025-only subset: 57.69% vs GPT-5-high 48.07%. On the independent Wen et al. test set: 67.49% vs 51.4% for GPT-4.1+retrieval. Traces from synthetic-reasoning SFT carry little signal, and RL runs drifted toward degenerate traces (e.g., 'more recent' justifications). | Limits: Labels are single published results, which carry selection and publication bias. Cross-domain accuracy falls to the same level as GPT-5, so the gain is largely in-distribution. The 2025 subset is small (n=52). Tested only on 8B models, not frontier models fine-tuned. | Relevance: This is the current state of the art for 'will idea A beat idea B'. Expect about 70-77% only when the training labels come from the same benchmark family, and near-frontier-zero-shot accuracy otherwise. For OptiLex this means building an in-domain labeled set (LLM-training-efficiency ablations) before trusting any learned critic. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21491
- Implementation lottery: noise ceiling on execution labels (Ning, Zhong, Li, Zeng, Xiong (CMU) 2026) [executed_computationally]: Each idea is frozen as an 'idea card' and implemented three separate times by agents on 13 tabular tasks (312 assignments; Bounded and Agentic setups). The study then splits variance between implementation choice and same-artifact reruns, and checks whether a winner picked from one draw still wins against the mean of the other two draws. | Result: Implementation choice explains 33% (Bounded) and 45% (Agentic) of within-task variation; reruns explain 6% and 4%. The winner from a single implementation draw reverses against the other-two mean in 25.6% (10/39) and 43.6% of decisions. Idea-level ICC is 0.612 [0.477, 0.745] and 0.511 [0.248, 0.747]. | Limits: Tabular and materials tasks, not LLM pretraining. Only three implementations per idea. The paper does not itself estimate a ceiling for predictor accuracy. | Relevance: Critical for design. Any critic trained or validated on one-run outcomes is chasing a label whose own reliability is roughly ICC 0.5-0.6. My inference, not tested in the paper: reported 64-77% pairwise accuracies may already sit near the ceiling that single-realization labels allow. OptiLex should label ideas with at least 3 independent implementations before using outcomes as critic training data. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26587
- Fine-tuned outcome predictor plus retrieval (idea-pair benchmark vs human experts) (Wen, Si, Chen, He, Feng (NeurIPS 2025) 2025) [retrospective_prediction]: GPT-4.1 is fine-tuned on 6,000 historical idea pairs (which of two ideas performed better on benchmarks) with a paper-retrieval agent. It is tested on 1,585 human-verified pairs published after the model's cutoff, scored with swap-consistency. Stress tests probe superficial features such as complexity and recency. | Result: Fine-tuned system 77.0% on the full test set; zero-shot GPT-4.1 51.9%, with o1/o3 'similar or slightly better'. On 45 NLP pairs: 64.4% vs 48.9% for majority votes of 5 experts (222 labels, about 9.1 min per label); best-expert ceiling 60.0%. On 33 unpublished executed ideas (half AI-generated): 63.6%. Fine-tuning on self-generated CoTs gave no gain (48.0/47.8%). | Limits: The expert comparison uses only 45 pairs, and 63.6% on 33 pairs (21/33) is not significantly above 50% (my binomial estimate is p≈0.08). Labels come from single published outcomes. The model tested is GPT-4.1, not a current frontier model. | Relevance: Shows that elicitation through fine-tuning on outcome labels, not prompting, is what moves accuracy, and that expert humans are no better than chance here. Opus prompted as a judge of efficacy should be assumed near chance until measured. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00794
- Community-feedback judges (RLCF): citation-trained pairwise 'taste' models (Tong et al. (Fudan/OpenMOSS), 'AI Can Learn Scientific Taste'; related: Kale et al. 2026 (2609.29802), Rasool et al. ReviewGuard 2026 (2606.24892) 2026) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: GRPO with a binary correctness reward on 720,341 field- and year-matched arXiv pairs (which paper has more citations), using titles and abstracts. The resulting judge then serves as the reward for an idea generator. | Result: In-domain pairwise accuracy: SciJudge-Qwen3-30B 82.7% vs GPT-5.4 Thinking 81.6% zero-shot. Temporal OOD on 2025 papers: 83.1% (base 71.7). ICLR review-score pairs: 88.5%. Altmetric: 86.3%. Topic-controlled pairs (n=245): only 62.9% (base 53.1). Author/institution-controlled (n=541): 79.7%. Related results: an ordinal citation RM gets 48.0% 5-class accuracy vs GPT-5 27.7% (Spearman 0.756). ReviewGuard gets rho=0.776 with future citations vs 0.492 for human reviewers on rejected-then-published papers. | Limits: The labels measure reception, not efficacy. Abstracts include results. Accuracy collapses to about 63% once topic is matched, so much of the signal is topic popularity. Short citation windows are known to penalize novel work (Wang et al. 2017). ReviewGuard's test is drawn from the same cohort as its training data. Generator gains are judged only by LLMs (54.2% win rate). | Relevance: Frontier models already predict community uptake well zero-shot (about 82%) but predict empirical efficacy poorly (51-61%). OptiLex should not use a citation or reception judge as a proxy for 'this improves the data/compute/energy frontier'; that would optimize toward fashionable topics. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14473
- LLM novelty judgment: 'novelty mirage', middle-class collapse, and search grounding (Sinhahajari, Majumder, Poria (NTU) 2606.12071; Schopf & Färber RINoBench 2603.10303; Schopf, Schreieder, Aizawa TPR 2608.25660 (EMNLP 2026 Findings); Gottweis et al. Co-Scientist 2502.18864 2026) [expert_human_rating]: Model-generated research questions are compared with author-anchored questions under standalone LLM scoring, comparative LLM scoring and blinded expert review (50 items). RINoBench (1,381 ideas) scores 5-point novelty. TPR probes the judge's hidden states mid-reasoning. Co-Scientist ablates literature search in its novelty reviewer. | Result: On non-obviousness, gemini-3.1-pro preferred gpt-5.5 research questions 82% of the time, while the two experts preferred the author questions 78% and 56% of the time. Human-LLM agreement fell as low as 22% (expert-expert 60%, LLM-LLM 52%). Comparative judging raised gpt-5.5's strict win rate from 27.2% to 49.1%. An explicit source-boundedness criterion restored 72-76% agreement with one expert. On RINoBench, Claude Opus 4.5 reaches macro-F1 17.1 and GPT-5.4 15.1, with F1=0.0 on novelty classes 1 and 5. TPR probes on 4B-27B open models reach macro-F1 20-25 (+22.3% on average). In Co-Scientist, known non-novel ideas scored 6.14/10 without search and 2.38/10 with search. | Limits: Small expert samples (50 items, 2 experts). RINoBench labels come from reviewer-derived novelty scores of unknown reliability. TPR requires hidden-state access, which rules it out for Opus via API. | Relevance: Direct explanation for the OptiLex pilot (53% self-agreement on novelty). Absolute novelty scores from Opus will cluster at 3-4 and cannot separate outliers. Novelty should be judged relatively against retrieved nearest neighbours, with explicit narrowness and source-boundedness criteria, rather than on a Likert scale. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12071
- Style sensitivity, 'paper laundering' and the AI-reviewer hivemind (Ji et al. SciStyleBench 2608.01666; Baumann, Pei, Koyejo, Hovy 2605.03202 (ICML 2026 position, oral); Hatzel et al. Review Arcade 2605.28897 2026) [executed_computationally]: Controlled style perturbations of fixed-substance ideas (600 ideas × 15 variants). Zero-shot LLM rewrites of papers are then re-scored by GPT-5.1, GPT-5.4 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 reviewers. Review-embedding similarity and score correlations are measured on real ICLR 2026 reviews. | Result: Laundering raises AI review scores by +0.45 on a 1-10 scale across 24 conditions (p<0.001 in nearly all). On ICLR 2026, AI review scores correlate with human scores at r=0.15 but with each other at r=0.49. Human-averaged scores predict decisions at AUC 0.822 vs 0.710 for AI scores (8,015 papers). GPT-5.1 reviews are 37.4% more similar across different papers than human reviews. Style rewrites change top-5 membership by 4.6% and top-30 by 15.9%. Direct judges' substance recognition rate is 0.504, rising to 0.759 with a trained style extractor. Review Arcade finds significant score gains for up to 35% of papers after LLM-guided revision. | Limits: SciStyleBench judges are open models of at most DeepSeek-V3.2 size. Human AUC is inflated by construction, since decisions are based on human scores. Laundering measures gameability, not validity. | Relevance: If OptiLex generators and judges both come from the Opus family, generators will learn judge-pleasing style. All candidates should be canonicalized (rewritten into one fixed template by a separate model) before judging, and judge-vs-outcome agreement should be tracked as optimization pressure rises. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03202
- Pairwise tournaments with Bradley-Terry aggregation vs pointwise scoring (Zhang, Zhang, Ji, Hua, Haber, Cao, Liang 2506.11343; Tripathi, Wadhwa, Durrett, Niekum 2504.14716 (COLM 2025); Gottweis et al. Co-Scientist Elo 2502.18864 2025) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Many cheap LLM agents each compare one random pair of manuscripts, and a Bradley-Terry model recovers a global ranking from under 2% of all pairs. This is contrasted with per-item ratings. A separate study tests the robustness of each protocol to injected distractor features. Co-Scientist uses Elo from debate-style pairwise matches. | Result: GPT-4o-mini pairwise+BT selected ICLR papers averaging 20.00 citations, against 19.36 for human accept decisions, 11.41 for GPT pointwise ratings and 8.56 for random. It overlapped with human accepts on 41.0% (ICLR 2024). Its picks had lower topic novelty and more concentration: 43.8% of top-tier picks came from 10 institutions vs 27.0% for humans (ICLR 2023). Under distractors, pairwise preferences flipped in about 35% of cases vs 9% for absolute scores. In Co-Scientist, the top-Elo answer reached 78.4% top-1 on GPQA-diamond, but correctness-review AUC was only 0.643-0.651. | Limits: The judge is an older model (GPT-4o-mini). Citations are confounded by prestige, and papers were not anonymized for affiliation effects. The GPQA test is not research ideas. | Relevance: Tournaments extract far more signal than absolute scores from the same judge, but they are more gameable and pull selections toward mainstream, prestigious work. OptiLex should use pairwise+BT for ranking, absolute rubric checks for soundness gates, and a diversity or novelty quota so the tournament does not converge on the mainstream. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11343
- Calibration and selective judgment (knowing when an outcome is predictable) (Sehwag et al. (Scale AI) SciPredict 2604.10718; Jung, Brahman, Choi 'Trust or Escalate' 2407.18370 (ICLR 2025); Georgantas AIPR 2606.15887 2026) [retrospective_prediction]: SciPredict has models (including Claude Opus 4.5, GPT-5.2 and Gemini 3 Pro) and experts predict outcomes of 405 post-March-2025 physics, chemistry and biology experiments, and rate their confidence and whether each outcome is predictable. Trust or Escalate uses calibrated confidence (Simulated Annotators) to abstain or escalate with a guaranteed human-agreement level. AIPR runs a pre-registered validation of a structured LLM scoring pipeline. | Result: SciPredict: model accuracy 14-26% vs about 20% for experts. Model accuracy stays around 20% regardless of stated confidence or predictability, whereas expert accuracy rises from about 5% to about 80% as they judge outcomes more predictable. Trust or Escalate: over 80% guaranteed human agreement at almost 80% coverage with cascades starting from Mistral-7B, a level GPT-4 alone almost never reaches. AIPR (GPT-5.4-mini, 300 ICLR 2026 submissions after the cutoff): AUROC 0.82 [0.78-0.87]. The pipeline cuts within-paper SD from 2.8 to 0.7 but adds little discrimination over a one-paragraph prompt. | Limits: SciPredict covers natural sciences, not ML. Trust or Escalate uses chat preferences, not research ideas. AIPR scores full papers after execution. | Relevance: Opus confidence cannot be used as a triage signal without calibration. The highest-value missing component for OptiLex is an abstain-or-route layer: send ideas with low predictability to cheap execution, and reserve the LLM verdict for cases where calibrated agreement has been demonstrated. Repeated-sampling aggregation fixes reliability but not validity. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10718
- Execution-grounded search as the filter (and RL collapse on true execution rewards) (Si, Yang, Choi, Candès, Yang, Hashimoto (Stanford) 2601.14525 2026) [executed_computationally]: An automated executor implements LLM-sampled ideas in nanoGPT pretraining and GRPO post-training environments and launches parallel GPU runs. Two ways of learning from execution feedback are compared: evolutionary search and RL. | Result: Claude-4.5-Opus and Sonnet executed over 90% of their own ideas in the pretraining environment. Execution-guided evolutionary search beat best-of-N from epoch 1: GRPO accuracy went from a 48.0% baseline to 69.4%, and nanoGPT validation loss from 3.255 to 3.1407 (Claude-4.5-Opus, epoch 9). RL on execution reward raised mean reward but not max reward, and diversity collapsed: 119/128 ideas at epoch 68 were one of two tweaks (RMSNorm to LayerNorm, checkpoint EMA), up from 51/128 at epoch 0 (Qwen3-30B). | Limits: Only two environments. Single-run outcomes are subject to the implementation lottery. Execution cost is substantial. | Relevance: This is directly in the OptiLex test domain. Cheap execution, not pre-execution judgment, drove the gains. Even a true execution reward collapses diversity under RL, so a learned judge used as an RL reward will collapse faster. Selection with explicit diversity pressure (evolutionary or quality-diversity) is preferable to RL against critics. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14525
- Cheap small-scale execution as a proxy judge (single-scale ranking) (Magnusson et al. (AI2) DataDecide 2504.11393 (ICML 2025) 2025) [executed_computationally]: 1,050 models across 14 scales (4M-1B), 25 data recipes and 3 seeds. The study measures how often small-scale rankings predict 1B-scale pairwise winners, and compares this against 8 scaling-law extrapolation methods. | Result: Ranking at a single small scale (e.g., 150M) predicts about 80% of 1B pairwise comparisons correctly. None of the 8 scaling-law methods beat the compute-decision frontier of single-scale ranking. With continuous likelihood metrics, benchmarks such as MMLU, ARC and HumanEval are over 80% predictable at 1B using 0.01% of the compute. | Limits: Covers data-recipe choices only, up to 1B scale. Architecture and optimizer ideas may transfer across scale less well. | Relevance: For OptiLex's LLM-efficiency domain, a tiny run gives about 80% pairwise decision accuracy. That already beats the best pre-execution predictors (61-77% in-distribution, about 46-58% OOD), at modest compute and energy. The LLM critic's job should be cheap elimination of unsound, duplicate or infeasible ideas before tiny runs, not ranking of efficacy. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.11393
- Peer-review-trained reward models and reviewers (AlphaResearch-RM, DeepReviewer, ReviewRL) (Yu et al. AlphaResearch 2511.08522; Zhu, Weng, Yang, Zhang DeepReview 2503.08569; Zeng et al. ReviewRL 2508.10308 (EMNLP 2025) 2025) [retrospective_prediction]: SFT on historical OpenReview records (AlphaResearch-RM-7B: 24,445 ICLR 2017-2024 abstracts mapped to mean rating), or SFT plus RL with rating-accuracy rewards for full reviews. Used as a pre-execution idea filter in AlphaResearch. | Result: AlphaResearch-RM-7B scores 72.0% binary (rating above 5.5) on 100 ICLR 2025 abstracts, vs 65.0% for 3 human annotators, 53.0% for GPT-5 (medium) and 37.0% for base Qwen2.5-7B. DeepReviewer-14B: 64.06% decision accuracy on ICLR 2024; rating Spearman 0.3559 (2024) and 0.4047 (2025). ReviewRL-7B: rating Spearman 0.634 on ICLR 2025, vs 0.539 for DeepReviewer-7B. | Limits: n=100 gives CIs of roughly ±9pp. Models learn to imitate reviewer preferences, and human reviewers penalize novelty. A fine-tuned reviewer (OpenReviewer) showed near-zero substance recognition in SciStyleBench. These are acceptance labels, not efficacy labels. | Relevance: Cheap 7B critics can beat GPT-5 zero-shot on 'would reviewers like this abstract'. For OptiLex this is useful only as a presentation and soundness screen, never as an efficacy or novelty oracle. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08522
- Cross-family panels and family-conditioned self-preference (Awuni et al. 'Who Judges Matters' 2609.17857; Bianchi, Queen, Thakkar, Sun, Zou Agents4Science 2511.15534 (Nature Biotechnology) 2026) [executed_computationally]: Measure the score lift judges give to outputs from their own model family, and how much panel composition changes pairwise outcomes. Agents4Science ran three cross-family reviewers (GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, Claude Sonnet 4) on 314 submissions, with human expert review of 79. | Result: Same-family lift of 6.7pp [5.3, 8.4] globally (per family 3.4-8.4pp; Gemma largest). Moving away from a family-balanced panel changes 18.5% [16.4, 20.8] of pairwise outcomes. Controlling for likelihood familiarity cuts the family effect by 61%. In Agents4Science, the three LLM reviewers correlated at mean Pearson 0.48, with mean scores of 2.30 (GPT-5), 3.0 (Claude) and 4.23 (Gemini) on a 1-6 scale. Mean absolute difference from humans was 0.91 (GPT-5), 1.09 (Claude) and 2.73 (Gemini). | Limits: The family-bias study uses only open-weight families (Llama, Qwen, Gemma, Yi). Agents4Science scores are post-execution papers with a single human per paper. | Relevance: An Opus generator judged by an Opus critic invites family preference, driven by likelihood familiarity. Cross-family critics differ hugely in calibration, so OptiLex should use rank-based aggregation across families and per-judge normalization, not raw score averaging. | https://arxiv.org/html/2609.17857
- Debate, verified quotes and single independent critique (Khan et al. 2402.06782 (ICML 2024); Elasky, Nakasako, Goyal 2605.27483; Jiang et al. 2607.01251 (ICML 2026); Salimi et al. Debate-as-Reward 2604.16723 2026) [executed_computationally]: Debaters with privileged access argue opposing positions and the judge sees only verified quotes (Khan). A proposer-critic setup on verifiable code and logic tasks tests rebuttal ablations (Elasky). Collaborative crux-finding replaces adversarial debate (Jiang). A multi-agent deliberative judge serves as the RL reward for ideation (Salimi). | Result: Verified-quote debate: non-expert LLM judges 76% vs 48% baseline, humans 88% vs 60%. Proposer-critic debate beat consultancy significantly in 3 of 5 pairings, only when the critic out-classifies the judge and the judge verifies claims. Removing rebuttal rounds had no measurable effect, since a single independent critique recovers most of the benefit. Disagreement resolution: 62.1% vs 49.2% for standard debate. The Debate-as-Reward judge raised precision on 177 triples labeled by 7 experts from 0.85 (single call) to 0.906 (one analyst) to 1.00 (two debating analysts plus an evaluator), after the generator exploited placeholder text. | Limits: None of these is validated against research-idea execution outcomes. Debate-as-Reward rewards similarity to a future paper, not quality. Debate can reward persuasiveness. | Relevance: The cheapest robust primitive for OptiLex soundness critics is answer, then independent critique that must cite verifiable evidence (retrieved paper spans or code), then judge. Use a critic from a stronger or different model than the judge, and skip multi-round debate. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27483
- Human (and LLM) evaluator bias against novelty (Boudreau, Guinan, Lakhani, Riedl (Management Science 2016); Wang, Veugelers, Stephan (Research Policy 2017); Machado (Scientometrics 2026 / Zenodo) 2016-2026) [expert_human_rating]: Randomized assignment of evaluators to grant proposals (2,130 evaluator-proposal pairs), with novelty measured as new MeSH-term combinations. A bibliometric follow-up of novel papers. Comparison of LLM and human funding decisions on 139 Austrian Science Fund (FWF) proposals. | Result: Evaluators systematically scored highly novel (top-quintile) proposals lower, with a non-monotonic relationship. Evaluators closer to the topic scored about 1 point lower on a 10-point scale. Proposal fixed effects explain only 26% of score variation. Wang et al.: novel papers are more likely to reach the top 1% of citations but get delayed recognition and lower short-window citations. Machado: GenAI matched human funding decisions in 60% of cases; novelty was the only dimension significantly associated with disagreement, and proposals funded by humans but not by AI had higher novelty. | Limits: Boudreau is one medical grant competition. The LLM in Machado is unspecified and n=139. Wang et al. was verified only from its abstract. | Relevance: Any critic trained on human reviews or short-window citations inherits an anti-novelty prior. Combined with the LLM 'novelty mirage' on LLM-written text, OptiLex critics can be simultaneously credulous toward surface novelty and hostile to real novelty. A protected exploration quota and outcome-based validation are required. | https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.2015.2285
- Surrogate-filtered idea funnels at scale (LLM-reviewer surrogate plus UCB) (Weng, Zhu, Xie, Sun, Lin, Liu, Zhang (Westlake) DeepScientist 2509.26603 2025) [executed_computationally]: Bayesian-optimization framing: an LLM reviewer contextualized on a findings memory acts as a cheap surrogate, UCB selects ideas to implement, and results flow back into memory. The study includes an ablation against random selection of ideas. | Result: More than 5,000 unique ideas led to about 1,100 selected for validation and 21 that produced progress (about 0.4% of all ideas). Implementing 100 random ideas per task gave effectively 0 successes; surrogate selection gave about 1-3%. Around 60% of 300 sampled failures were implementation errors. | Limits: Only three tasks, and the random-ablation counts are small. Selection effectiveness is not separated from the memory and UCB components. | Relevance: Gives a realistic base rate. Even a weak filter adds value at a 0.1-1% hit rate, but implementation failure dominates, so OptiLex's 'fixer' and executor reliability may matter as much as critic accuracy. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: A GPT-4.1 predictor fine-tuned on 6,000 idea pairs, plus retrieval, reaches 77.0% on 1,585 post-cutoff pairs, while zero-shot frontier models (GPT-4.1 51.9%, o3) are near chance. On 45 NLP pairs it scores 64.4% against 48.9% for 5-expert majority votes (best-expert ceiling 60.0%). On 33 unpublished executed ideas it scores 63.6%. => CORRECTED: Wen et al. (arXiv 2506.00794, June 2025) fine-tuned GPT-4.1 on 6,000 idea pairs and added a paper-retrieval agent. The system reaches 77% on 1,585 test pairs, each with at least one idea published after GPT-4.1's June 1, 2024 knowledge cutoff. Unfine-tuned frontier models, even with the same retrieval, are near chance: GPT-4.1 scores 51.9% with retrieval and 51.4% plain zero-shot (Table 4), and o1/o3 do only slightly better. On 45 NLP pairs, the system scores 64.4%. A majority vote of 5 experts per topic (25 experts in total) scores 48.9%. A "ceiling" baseline of the best expert per topic, chosen after the fact on the test set, scores 60.0%. On 33 unpublished idea PAIRS (35 pairs minus ties), taken from Si et al.'s ideation study and carried out by human expert researchers, it scores 63.6%. (caveat: (1) The 51.9% figure is GPT-4.1 WITH retrieval. Plain zero-shot is 51.4%, so calling 51.9% the "zero-shot" number blurs two conditions. (2) The 60.0% "ceiling" picks the best expert per topic after the fact, using the test set itself, so it is optimistic. (3) The unit is idea PAIRS, not ideas. 33 labeled pairs remain from 35 after ties were removed. Ground truth comes from human researchers carrying out the ideas (about 103 hours per idea), from Si et al.'s AI-ideation execution study. (4) The main benchmark's ground truth is not new execution. It is benchmark results reported in published papers' result tables, pulled out by an LLM pipeline with majority voting across 3 or more benchmarks. Human checking by 16 students brought the error rate to about 2.5%. Pairs are compared only within the same paper. (5) Predictions count only when they stay the same after the two ideas' order is swapped. (6) No confidence intervals or significance tests are reported for the 45-pair or 33-pair results, and both samples are small (about ±14pp and ±16pp at 95% by a rough binomial estimate). (7) Pairs are drawn from 10 AI/ML/CV conferences (ACL, NeurIPS, ICLR, CVPR, etc.). The expert comparison covers NLP only. (8) The authors ran stress tests on shallow features such as recency, length and lab fame. They report performance stays "non-trivial" on the hard subsets, so shortcut exploitation cannot be fully ruled out. (9) The task is choosing which of two competing ideas wins on benchmarks. It is not scoring the absolute quality of a single idea.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Holding the idea fixed, implementation choice explains 33-45% of within-task outcome variance, 5-10x more than reruns (4-6%). A winner picked from one implementation reverses in 25.6% (Bounded) and 43.6% (Agentic) of decisions, with idea-level ICC of 0.612 and 0.511. (caveat: 1) Scope is narrow. Both Bounded and Agentic are setups on 13 OpenML tabular tasks: 4 ideas x 3 implementations x 2 setups = 312 assignments. They are not two separate domains. Everything was implemented by one model family: DeepSeek v4 Pro wrote the cards and the code, and DeepSeek v4 Flash did the fidelity review. 2) Outcomes are scored by execution: the relative held-out accuracy gain (patched - baseline)/|baseline|. Fixed penalties apply: -0.10 for an evaluation failure or an invalid patch, and -0.02 for drift. These penalties are part of the utility and so add to the "implementation" variance. Fidelity and drift labels come from an LLM. A second LLM (DeepSeek v4 Pro) re-reviewed a 30% subset. There was no human judging. 3) The "reversal" is a leave-one-implementation-out test. It checks whether the winner under one implementation draw still wins against the mean of the other two draws. It covers only 39 decisions per setup, so the confidence intervals are wide: the Agentic ICC CI runs from 0.248 to 0.747. 4) "5-10x" is the ratio of variance shares: 33/6 ≈ 5.5x and 45/4 ≈ 11x. 5) The paper is about the noise in post-execution evaluation of a single implementation. It says nothing directly about how accurate pre-execution critics are. Its relevance to the pre-execution judgment question is that single-run ground truth is itself noisy, which puts a ceiling on how well any critic can be validated.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Across domains, fine-tuned 8B forecasters collapse to about 46-49% swap-consistent accuracy (Direct-SFT 45.67%, DrGRPO 49.08%), matching GPT-5-high's 45.96%, despite 77.10% in-domain. On a 2025-only subset (n=52) they score 57.69% vs 48.07%. => CORRECTED: On a cross-domain test set (705 non-NLP idea pairs from 46 PapersWithCode leaderboards, papers from 2024 onward), fine-tuned Qwen3-8B models score about 46-49% swap-consistent accuracy (Direct-SFT 45.67%, Reason-SFT-DrGRPO 49.08%). GPT-5 with high reasoning scores 45.96%, and a Llama3.1-8B Direct-SFT model drops to 31.20%. In-domain, Direct-SFT scores 77.10% and GPT-5-high 61.10%. On a 2025-only subset of the cross-domain set (52 pairs before augmentation), Reason-SFT-DAPO scores 57.69%, Direct-SFT 53.85% and GPT-5-high 48.07%. (caveat: (1) The 57.69% figure belongs to Reason-SFT-DAPO, not to the 8B models as a group. It is also not DrGRPO, the model named in the first sentence. Direct-SFT scores 53.85% on that subset. (2) "Fine-tuned 8B" means Qwen3-8B. Llama3.1-8B Direct-SFT does much worse across domains (31.20%), so not every 8B model stays near 46-49%. (3) Outcomes are judged by execution: labels come from which of two ideas reached the better PapersWithCode leaderboard score, not from human or LLM judges. (4) Swap-consistent accuracy counts a prediction only if it is correct and stays the same when the two ideas swap positions. Random guessing therefore scores about 25%, not 50%, so 46-49% is well above chance even if it sounds like a coin flip. (5) n=52 is very small: one pair is about 1.9 pp, and the 9.6 pp gap is about 5 pairs, with no significance test reported. (6) The authors present the cross-domain result as reasoning-trained models doing "at par or better than GPT-5". "Collapse" is our framing: it describes the drop from 77% in-domain, not a fall below GPT-5. (7) GPT-5-high also falls from 61.10% to 45.96%, so the drop across domains affects both.)
Gaps:
  - No pre-execution critic has been validated on LLM-training-efficiency ideas using multi-implementation labels (3 or more independent implementations). All outcome predictors (Wen 2025, Mule 2026) use single, publication-selected results, and the implementation-lottery data shows such labels flip 25-44% of the time.
  - No head-to-head, cost-accounted comparison exists on one idea pool between (a) a prompted frontier judge, (b) a fine-tuned outcome predictor, and (c) a tiny-scale execution proxy (DataDecide-style, about 80% decision accuracy), measured as decision accuracy per GPU-hour and per joule. That is exactly OptiLex's data/compute/energy frontier question.
  - There is no calibrated abstain-or-route layer for research-idea judges. SciPredict shows model accuracy does not track stated confidence or predictability. Trust-or-Escalate-style guarantees exist for chat preferences but have not been built or tested for idea efficacy or novelty.
  - Nobody has measured the Goodhart curve for idea critics: how critic-versus-execution agreement decays as a generator is optimized against the critic. Evidence so far is anecdotal reward hacking (Debate-as-Reward) and diversity collapse under true execution rewards (Si et al. 2026: 119/128 ideas converge on two tweaks).
  - Family self-preference and cross-family panels have been quantified only on open-weight families (Llama, Qwen, Gemma, Yi) and on post-execution papers. There is no measurement for Claude-family generator-judge pairs on idea pools with execution ground truth.
  - Debate and critique protocols with verified quotes have been validated on QA, code and logic, but never against executed research-idea outcomes. Evidence-grounded critique (quotes from retrieved papers or code) for idea soundness is untested as a predictor of execution success.
  - No study canonicalizes candidates (a fixed-template rewrite by a separate model) before pairwise tournaments and measures residual style effects for frontier judges on idea pools. SciStyleBench tested open judges of at most DeepSeek-V3.2 size, and its extractor needs training.
  - The anti-novelty bias of LLM judges has not been measured with a randomized, Boudreau-style design and realized long-run outcomes. Machado 2026 uses n=139 with an unspecified model, and the 'novelty mirage' concerns LLM-written text, so the net direction of Opus-class novelty bias on real ideas is unknown.
  - Portfolio-level (diversity-aware) selection has not been validated against realized outcomes. Budgeted Subset Refinement (2607.14118) and pairwise-BT selection with its measured drop in topic novelty are evaluated only by proxy judges or citations.
  - There is no measured reliability (ICC) for frontier judges on idea efficacy comparable to the implementation-lottery ICC. So nobody knows whether judge noise (e.g., the OptiLex pilot's 53% self-agreement) or label noise is the binding constraint.
Surprises:
  - Reception is predictable but efficacy is not. GPT-5.4 zero-shot picks the more-cited paper 81.6% of the time and trained judges reach 88.5% on ICLR score pairs, yet frontier models are near chance (51-61%) at picking which idea actually performs better.
