Once topic is matched, citation prediction drops to 62.9%, so much of the 'taste' is topic popularity.
  - Human expert ensembles did worse than chance at predicting which of two NLP ideas performs better: 5-expert majority 48.9%, best-expert ceiling 60.0% (Wen et al.). The inter-annotator agreement of 75.1% matches NeurIPS reviewer consistency.
  - LLM judges are both novelty-credulous and novelty-averse. They over-rate gap-shaped LLM-written research questions (82% preference vs experts' 78% the other way) while penalizing real novelty: in Machado 2026, novelty was the only dimension driving LLM-human funding disagreement, and pairwise LLM selection lowered topic novelty.
  - Frontier judges, including Claude Opus 4.5 (macro-F1 17.1), never correctly assign the extreme novelty classes (F1=0.0 for 'not novel' and 'highly novel'), while hidden-state probes on 4B models outperform them. The judge 'knows' more than it says.
  - Pairwise tournaments nearly doubled selection quality over pointwise ratings with the same weak judge (20.00 vs 11.41 mean citations, vs 19.36 for human accepts), yet pairwise preferences are about 4x easier to flip with distractor features (35% vs 9%).
  - Rebuttal rounds add nothing in proposer-critic debate: a single independent critique recovers most of the benefit, and only when the critic out-classifies the judge.
  - Fine-tuning on raw outcome labels works (51.4% to 77.0%), but fine-tuning on the model's own filtered chain-of-thought does not (47.8-48.0%), and RL-trained forecasters drifted toward degenerate justifications.
  - In the wild on ICLR 2026, AI review scores correlate with human scores at only r=0.15 while AI reviewers agree with each other at r=0.49 (a 'hivemind'). A carefully engineered GPT-5.4-mini pipeline reaches AUROC 0.82, but the discrimination comes from the base model, with the pipeline mainly cutting variance (SD 2.8 to 0.7).
  - Implementation variance is 5-10x larger than rerun noise, so 'one run is not an idea'. The outcome labels we would train critics on are themselves about ICC 0.5-0.6 reliable.

## creativity_science
- Alien Space of Science: sample idea-atom combinations that score high on coherence and low on community 'availability' (Artiles, Weiss, Brinkmann, Rahwan, Schölkopf, Pal, Larochelle, Goyal, Rahaman 2026 (arXiv v1 1 Mar, v2 15 May 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Break papers into fine-grained concept units and cluster them into a shared vocabulary of 'idea atoms'. Learn two models over it. (1) A coherence model scores whether a set of atoms makes a viable research direction. (2) An availability model scores whether any existing author community is placed to produce that set; this operationalizes Sourati & Evans. Then rank all k=3 atom sets by F_beta: maximize coherence, minimize availability. Claude Opus 4.7 turns the chosen atoms back into natural language. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: do the combinatorial choice outside the LLM, in an explicit space that has an anti-crowd term, and use the LLM only to turn chosen atoms into text and to repair them. | Result: Corpus: 16,068 LLM papers. Claude Opus 4.7 and Gemini 3.1 Pro were each prompted 300 times to pick novel yet feasible atom triples. Share of combinations that contain one of the method's 10 most-used atoms: Claude 95.7%, Gemini 76.7%, Alien 34.3%. Effective atom coverage: Claude 10.5%, Gemini 20.6%, Alien 72.5% (random 86.6%). Coherence-only ranking puts 23 held-out 2025 atom triples in its top 1000, against 0.739 expected at random (31.1x). GPT-5.5 judge win rate: Alien 55.6% on the full pool, 72.2% on the top half, 69.4% on the top ten; Claude 48.9%, 27.8% and 30.6%. Human raters (40 blind reviews, 10 raters): overall quality ties Alien = Claude = 4.03; Alien scores highest on novelty (3.90) and feasibility (3.90). Autoresearch execution on A100s: 40 ideas, 224 blind LLM-judged pairwise comparisons. Rank percentile: Alien 0.633, Claude 0.574, Gemini 0.495, Random 0.295. During the refinement loop, Claude's reports drift toward interpretability, from 2/10 to 8/10; Alien's stay at 0/10. | Limits: Preprint with a single domain (LLM research) and small execution n (10 ideas per arm). The execution reports are judged by an LLM, not by task metrics, so this is weaker than realized outcomes. The availability model depends on author data. Baselines received the whole atom vocabulary in one prompt, which may understate what LLMs can do with better prompting (for example, noisy prompts). Human ratings are few (40). | Relevance: This is the closest published analogue to OptiLex's generator layer. It uses a frontier Claude model as the baseline and reproduces OptiLex's pilot collapse, in which 30 prompts fell into 3 families. It also shows that the collapse gets worse inside an iterative refine/execute loop, which is exactly where OptiLex's critics and fixers sit. Direct design implications: atomize the data/compute/energy-efficiency literature, build an author-availability model, sample combinations with the anti-crowd term, and measure effective atom coverage as a first-class diversity metric. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01092
- Human-aware AI / 'alien' hypotheses: model the distribution of scientists, then tune toward or away from the crowd (Jamshid Sourati & James Evans (Nature Human Behaviour) 2023) [retrospective_prediction]: Build a hypergraph whose nodes are materials or drugs, properties or diseases, and authors. Random walks over it simulate inferences that experts can reach cognitively. For forecasting, author-aware walks predict both what humans will discover and who will discover it. For generation, a mixing coefficient beta combines plausibility (embedding similarity) with 'alienness' (hypergraph shortest-path distance from human experts). beta>0 avoids the crowd. Merit of the avoided region is checked with first-principles or data-driven scores, such as DFT power factor for thermoelectrics. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: score candidates on two separate axes, scientific plausibility and human availability, and sample in a moderate anti-crowd band rather than at the extreme. | Result: Human-aware models forecast future discoveries up to 400% more precisely than content-only models. COVID-19 therapies: 36-42% of predictions entered clinical trials within 12-19 months, against 10-12% for content-only, which is 350-400% higher. Energy materials: about 100% higher precision. Drug repurposing: 43% higher. As beta rises past 0, discoverability by humans collapses. Average first-principles merit does not collapse until about beta=0.4. The most consistently promising beta interval is 0.2-0.3. For some targets, such as thermoelectrics, theoretical merit rises as the model avoids the crowd. | Limits: Tested on literature-based discovery of materials and drugs with link-prediction targets, not free-text ideas. The 'alien' merit rests on simulations or theoretical scores, not lab validation. It needs author-level data. The optimal beta varies by property. | Relevance: It gives OptiLex a tunable exploration knob with evidence behind the setting: a moderate anti-crowd band keeps soundness. It also gives a way to test alien ideas cheaply before execution: simulation or theory scores, the analogue of small-scale training runs or scaling-law fits. It is the foundation the Alien Space system builds on. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.01495
- AI tools expand individual impact but contract science's collective focus (Qianyue Hao, Fengli Xu, Yong Li, James Evans (Nature 649:1237-1243) 2026 (arXiv v1 Dec 2024; Nature Jan 2026)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A pretrained language model classifier (F1=0.875 against expert labels) flags AI-augmented papers among 41.3M natural-science papers. The study compares careers, the extent of topics covered, and follow-on engagement. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: without a counterweight, AI assistance drifts toward data-rich, already-crowded areas, so an idea engine needs explicit counter-steering and collective-diversity metrics, not only per-idea quality. | Result: Scientists doing AI-augmented work publish 3.02x more papers, receive 4.84x more citations, and become project leaders 1.37 years earlier. AI adoption shrinks the collective volume of topics studied by 4.63% and cuts scientists' engagement with one another by 22.00%. Corroborating experiment: Doshi & Hauser 2024 (Science Advances) found that LLM ideas made individual stories more creative but the stories more similar to each other. | Limits: Correlational: AI adoption is endogenous to data-rich fields. 'AI tools' here are mostly ML used as a research method across eras, not LLM ideation engines. Citations measure attention, not truth. | Relevance: This is a realized-impact warning at population scale. OptiLex's own test domain (LLM training efficiency) is data-rich and crowded, which is exactly where the pull is strongest. It argues for tracking portfolio-level topic coverage in the evaluation layer. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07727
- Surprise via hypergraph improbability, and 'knowledge expeditions' by outsiders (Feng Shi & James Evans (Nature Communications 14:1641) 2023) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A hypergraph embedding model predicts which combinations of contents (MeSH/PACS/USPC codes) and contexts (cited journals) should appear. A paper's surprise is how improbable its combination is under the model; content and context surprise are scored separately. Source analysis: expedition novelty is the average embedding distance between the authors' publishing backgrounds and the venue of the focal paper. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: build 'expedition' generators. Take a problem as the target field frames it, attack it with a toolkit from a distant field, and write it for the target audience. Score content surprise and context surprise separately. | Result: Corpora: MEDLINE (19.9M papers), APS (541K) and US patents (6.5M). Hit = top 10% cited in the same year. In MEDLINE, the most surprising context combinations are about 4x more likely to be hits than random, content about 2x, and joint content+context about 5x. Nearly 50% of papers in the highest joint-novelty group are hits. The most surprising expeditions are 3.5x more likely than random to be hits, and expedition novelty beats career or team novelty as a predictor. Patents: the most surprising are 2x more likely to be hits, but expedition novelty is not significant. | Limits: Correlational, with citations as the outcome. Surprise is scored after the fact against a model of the literature. The authors call for causal tests. The expedition effect vanishes in patents, where distance-crossing is common. | Relevance: This gives a concrete generator operator (the expedition) and a concrete critic signal (hypergraph improbability) for OptiLex. The patent null suggests distance-crossing loses value where it is routine, and ML already borrows heavily from physics and neuroscience. | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10039062/
- Atypical combinations: high median conventionality plus a tail of atypicality (Uzzi, Mukherjee, Stringer, Jones (Science 342:468-472) 2013) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Each pair of journals co-cited in a paper's references gets a z-score: observed co-citation frequency against randomized citation networks. The randomization uses Monte Carlo link-switching that keeps each paper's citation counts and timing. Each paper gets two numbers. Median conventionality: is the paper's median z in the upper half of all papers' medians? Tail novelty: is its 10th-percentile z below 0? Hit = top 5% of citations over 8 years. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: '90/10 composition'. Build each idea mostly from highly conventional, in-domain components and inject one or a few atypical pairings. Critics should check both the conventional core and the atypical tail rather than maximize novelty everywhere. | Result: Corpus: 17.9M WoS papers. For 1990-2000 papers, hits per 100 papers: high conventionality + high tail novelty 9.11, against a background of 5. High conventionality only: 5.82. High novelty only: 5.33. Low on both: 2.05. Peak impact sits at the 85th-95th percentile of median conventionality. Tail novelty appears in 36.1% of solo, 39.8% of two-author and 49.7% of 3+-author papers (+37.7% for teams over solo). Only about 41% of papers have any tail z<0. | Limits: Citation-based and measured at journal level. Critics show confounding by disciplinary effects (Boyack & Klavans 2014). Convergent validity is modest: Bornmann et al. 2019 found only +7.47% 'new finding' F1000 tags per SD of the Uzzi-type score (https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.03233). The conventionality premium may partly reflect how easily readers absorb the work (citations), not effectiveness. | Relevance: This is the canonical quantitative target for a composition constraint. It is testable on OptiLex ideas by mapping each idea's components to literature co-occurrence z-scores. The key finding is that pure novelty (low conventionality) barely beats baseline. | https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1240474
- Filling holes in concept space (persistent homology): higher-order gaps explode but pay off only in empirical fields (Jiajie Luo & James Evans 2026 (arXiv 28 Aug 2026)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Apply persistent homology to high-dimensional concept embeddings to find holes of dimension 0 (disconnected ideas), 1 and 2 (higher-order cavities), and the works that fill them. Then compare the citations and surprise of hole-filling works with baseline works. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: pick the order of combination (pairwise vs higher-order) per domain rather than using one global novelty knob. | Result: The number of holes scales with the number of concepts at power-law exponents of 1, 1.37 and 1.87 for dimensions 0, 1 and 2, and the share filled collapses toward 0 for large dimension-1 and dimension-2 holes. Top-quintile citation enrichment of hole-filling works: in empirical/natural sciences, about 1.5 (dim 0), 1.9 (dim 1) and 2.6 (dim 2); in formal/design fields, 1.2, 1.1 and 0.9. Computer science and engineering fall as order rises. Psychology reaches about 3.2x at dim 2; in mathematics, fewer than 20% of dim-1 and dim-2 fillers reach the top 20%. | Limits: Fresh preprint, not peer-reviewed. Citations as outcome. The results depend on the embedding and the concept extraction. | Relevance: This is a direct warning for OptiLex's ML test domain. In CS and engineering, higher-order atypical combinations are not rewarded and may be penalized, so a general-purpose engine should condition its combination depth on the domain. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28822
- Bias against novelty: high-variance, delayed, 'foreign-field' payoff, and expert evaluators penalizing novel proposals (Wang, Veugelers & Stephan (Research Policy 46:1416; NBER WP 22180); Boudreau, Guinan, Lakhani & Riedl (Management Science 62:2765) 2017 / 2016) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Wang et al.: novelty is the count of first-ever cited-journal pairs, weighted by distance (1 − cosine similarity of co-citation profiles); highly novel = top 1% in the subject category; the sample is all 773,311 WoS articles from 2001. Boudreau et al.: evaluators were randomly assigned to single-author grant proposals (2,130 evaluator-proposal pairs; 142 evaluators; 150 proposals), and novelty was measured as new MeSH-term combinations. EVALUATION-LAYER PRINCIPLE: treat novelty as a variance bet (a portfolio, not a threshold), use judges from outside the home field, and never use short-horizon proxies. | Result: Wang: highly novel papers have 39.82% higher odds of being top-1% cited over 14 years. Their odds of top 10% are 16.80% higher and of bottom 10% 16.76% higher, so both tails grow. Odds of top-cited in foreign fields are 62.37% higher; the home-field effect is not significant. They are less likely to be top-cited in citation windows shorter than 3 years. Boudreau: proposals in the right tail of novelty get systematically lower scores. Intellectual proximity alone moves scores by 1.1 points on a 10-point scale (residual SD 1.7). | Limits: Correlational (Wang) and citation-based. Bornmann et al. 2019 found Wang's W score has weak convergent validity with F1000 expert tags. The Boudreau setting is one medical school. The NBER numbers may differ slightly from the published Research Policy version. | Relevance: LLM judges trained on human reviews probably inherit the home-field novelty penalty, which fits OptiLex's 53% self-agreement. The evaluation layer should include out-of-domain judge personas and portfolio allocation for high-variance ideas. | https://www.nber.org/papers/w22180
- HindSight: time-split evaluation of LLM ideas against future publications (Bo Jiang 2026 (arXiv Mar 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: Restrict the generator to literature before a cutoff T. Match its generated ideas to papers published in the following 30 months and score the matches by citation impact and venue acceptance. EVALUATION PRINCIPLE: use realized future research as a label in place of LLM-judge novelty. | Result: Across 10 AI/ML topics, the LLM-as-judge finds no difference between retrieval-augmented and vanilla generation (p=0.584). HindSight finds retrieval-augmented ideas score 2.5x higher (p<0.001). HindSight scores correlate negatively with LLM-judged novelty (rho=−0.29, p<0.01). | Limits: Single author, 10 topics, preprint. By construction it rewards ideas humans later pursued ('inevitable' ideas) and cannot credit valuable alien ideas nobody pursued. That directly conflicts with the Sourati/Alien objective. | Relevance: This is cheap retrospective validation for OptiLex's evaluation layer, and more evidence that LLM novelty judgments are anti-correlated with realized value. It should be paired with, not replace, execution on the efficiency test domain. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15164
- Analogical search engines on purpose/mechanism representations (Kittur lab), extended to LLM transfer (BioSpark) (Kang, Qian, Hope, Shahaf, Chan, Kittur (ACM TOCHI); Kang, Lin, Chen, Hong, Martelaro, Kittur (CHI 2025) 2022 / 2025) [expert_human_rating]: Represent papers by purpose and mechanism, and retrieve papers that match the target's purpose at an abstract level but differ in details (a 'partial purpose match'). BioSpark adds LLM cards for mechanism extraction, transfer ('Sparks'), trade-offs and Q&A, plus tree-of-life diversification. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: retrieve inspirations at an intermediate level of abstraction, then run an explicit map-and-adapt step that transfers the mechanism, not the surface. | Result: TOCHI 2022 (6 scientists, 250 think-aloud transcripts): participants rated analogy results more novel (2.7 vs 2.3, p=1.3e-7). Creative-adaptation ideas: 53 from analogy results vs 10 from keyword results (5.3x). Partial-purpose matches produced creative adaptation 47% of the time (analogy) vs 21% (keyword). 38% of analogy partial mismatches yielded nothing useful. Purpose-match was the only significant mediator. BioSpark (12 designers, against AskNature+ChatGPT): 10.3 vs 5.5 ideas (p=.04). Expert-judged creative quality, the geometric mean of novelty, value and feasibility: 6.5 vs 4.5 (ICC 0.94/0.74/0.90). Biological inspirations used: 8.2 vs 4.6. | Limits: Very small n, and humans do the ideating. Outcomes are ideas and ratings, not executed results. The baseline in BioSpark is weak-ish. Benefits to human ideators may not carry over to an LLM-only loop. | Relevance: This supports a 'partial-match analogy retriever' module for OptiLex's knowledge base, and a distance sweet spot rather than maximum distance. The 38% dud rate argues for a cheap filter after transfer. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.15476
- Scideator: facet recombination (purpose/mechanism/evaluation) with distance-controlled retrieval and a facet-grounded novelty checker (Radensky, Shahid, Fok, Siangliulue, Hope, Weld (ACM CAIS '26) 2024-2026 (arXiv v7 May 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Extract purpose, mechanism and evaluation facets from input papers and from papers retrieved at graded distances (very near, near, far, very far). An LLM proposes analogies across the chosen facets. A novelty checker ranks retrieved literature by facet (retrieve, then rerank) and classifies novelty. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: recombine at facet level with explicit distance quotas, because selectors, human or LLM, drift toward near facets. | Result: 22 CS researchers, within-subjects: Creativity Support Index median 70.5 vs 61.0 for the same-LLM baseline (Wilcoxon p<.01). Gains were mainly in exploration and expressiveness. Participants used input and near facets substantially more than far ones, citing lower relevance of far facets. The novelty checker did not improve overall confidence (n=17, n.s.) but shifted ratings when it labeled an idea 'not novel'. In ablations, facet-grounded novelty classification beat unstructured classifiers. | Limits: Outcomes are self-reported creativity support, not idea quality or execution. Small n. Only CS/NLP. | Relevance: Facets map cleanly onto OptiLex's efficiency ideas: purpose (data, compute or energy target), mechanism, evaluation. The near-facet preference is a human analogue of LLM mode collapse and argues for enforced far-facet quotas in the generator. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14634
- Typed recombination knowledge bases: CHIMERA (blend vs inspiration edges), RATIO (ideation moves), MUSE (problem-solution-rationale) (Sternlicht & Hope (ACL 2026); Sharon & Hope (arXiv Aug 2026); Cohen & Hope (arXiv Aug 2026) 2025-2026) [retrospective_prediction]: LLM extractors mine 'recombination in action' from abstracts as typed edges. A 'blend' fuses two concepts (symmetric); an 'inspiration' runs from a source domain to a target (directed). A model trained on the knowledge base predicts, for a new problem, which blend or inspiration will occur. RATIO defines retrieval by ideation move: Address, Broaden, Specify. MUSE extracts 37K problem-solution-rationale triplets. KB PRINCIPLE: store human research as typed operations, not only as documents or entities, so generators can query 'what inspired solutions to problems like X'. | Result: CHIMERA has 28K+ recombination examples from AI papers, with extraction accuracy estimated at 80.55% by a GPT-4.1 judge on 2,000 examples. Recombination prediction is trained on a pre-2024 split and tested on 2024 papers. In a human evaluation (3 volunteers, 100 examples), model suggestions ranked similar to the gold recombinations and were preferred over GPT-4o and other baselines. RATIO: operation-specific fine-tuning 'substantially boosts retrievers' but leaves 'much room' for improvement. | Limits: Abstract-level extraction. The human evaluation is tiny. Correctness was estimated with an LLM judge. No execution outcomes. Mostly AI-domain. | Relevance: This is a concrete schema for OptiLex's 'knowledge base of human research' that directly supports expedition- and analogy-style generators, beyond GraphRAG-style entity graphs. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20779
- Structured recombination (conceptual blending made operational): DishCOVER (Mizrahi, Shani, Stanovsky, Jurafsky, Shahaf (TACL 2026) 2025-2026) [expert_human_rating]: An LLM translates ideas (recipes) into structured trees. Recombination happens in structured space through edit-distance merging of two source structures. Candidates are ranked by element-level rarity (novelty) under a value constraint, then decoded back to text. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: make the creative leap by symbolic operations on structured representations. The LLM only translates and repairs, which avoids token-level mode collapse. | Result: Culinary-expert novelty ratings of recipes rated valuable: 3.53 vs 3.146 for GPT-4o (p=0.0009) in Experiment 1, and 3.612 vs 3.141 (p=9.2e-08) in Experiment 2. Share rated valuable: 80% vs 82% (Exp. 1) and 85% vs 98% (Exp. 2). Mean nearest-neighbour similarity to Recipe1M+ is 0.774 vs 0.851. 38% of DishCOVER outputs fall below 0.75 similarity; none of GPT-4o's do. | Limits: Recipe domain. The baseline is GPT-4o, a 2024 model. The value cost is visible in Experiment 2. No executed outcomes beyond expert ratings. The mechanism it fixes, distributional collapse, is still present in frontier models per the Alien Space result. | Relevance: This is the strongest current evidence that conceptual blending works as an explicit operator. A structured schema for training-efficiency ideas (data, objective, architecture, optimizer, schedule, hardware) could serve as the recombination substrate. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20643
- Declining disruptiveness, its contested measurement, and which novelty types disrupt (Park, Leahey & Funk (Nature 2023); Leahey, Lee & Funk (ASR 88:562); Fang et al. (arXiv 2510.03240) 2023-2026) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: CD_5 index: do later papers cite the focal work without also citing its references (disruptive, near +1) or together with them (consolidating)? Park et al. link the decline to narrower use of prior knowledge. Leahey et al. classify novelty as new methods, new theories or new results using text from Citation Classics essays. GENERATOR PRINCIPLE: if the target is disruption, bias the generator toward new methods and tools and toward broad, diverse prior-knowledge inputs. | Result: Park et al. (arXiv version): across 45M papers, average CD_5 fell 91.9-100% from 1945 to 2010 depending on field. Across patents, it fell 78.7-91.5% from 1980 to 2010. The decline comes with falling diversity of work cited. Leahey et al.: new methods tend to be more disruptive, new theories less disruptive, and new results have no robust effect. | Limits: The decline is contested. Holst et al. (arXiv 2402.14583) attribute it to zero-reference database artefacts. Nature published their comment in 2026 as 'Dataset artefacts can partially drive the measured decline' with a reply from Park et al. saying declines remain significant (P<0.01); I did not read that exchange in the primary source (secondary: phys.org and EurekAlert). Fang et al. argue innovation moved toward cross-field 'generalizations' rather than declined. Leahey et al.'s sample is Citation Classics, which introduces selection bias. | Relevance: Do not build OptiLex's rationale on 'science is slowing'. The robust lesson is to target method novelty for disruption and to feed generators diverse prior knowledge. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.11184
- Knowledge-sourcing breadth: citation 'hotspot' (recent plus old sources) and disciplinary or team distance effects (Mukherjee, Romero, Jones & Uzzi (Science Advances 2017); Yegros-Yegros, Rafols & D'Este (PLOS ONE 2015); Lin, Evans & Wu (J. Informetrics 2022); Leibel & Bornmann (QSS 2026) 2015-2026) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Hotspot: sources with low mean age but high age variance, meaning fresh work mixed with old classics. Distance: the variety, balance and disparity of cited disciplines, and embedding-based atypicality against disruption. Team size against disruption. RETRIEVAL PRINCIPLE: the knowledge-base retriever should mix frontier and decades-old sources on purpose. Distance should be a portfolio dial: moderate for near-term impact, far for disruptive long shots with a long lag. | Result: Hotspot works are twice as likely to be in the top 5% or better by citations. Basis: 28,426,345 WoS papers (1945-2013) and 5,382,833 US patents (1950-2010). Coauthoring raises the chance of hotspot work. Yegros-Yegros (62,408 articles): an inverted U, where highly cited papers draw on many proximal disciplines but little that is distal. Lin/Evans/Wu: atypical papers are nearly 2x more likely to disrupt, but convergence takes 10+ years. Leibel & Bornmann's multiverse reanalysis of 'small teams disrupt': the negative team-size effect is robust, but its size depends heavily on model specification. | Limits: All correlational and citation-based. Lin, Frey & Wu 2023 (Nature: remote teams produce fewer breakthroughs) is cited from secondary summaries only. Mapping human team findings onto LLM agent teams is speculative. | Relevance: These are cheap, untested retrieval and portfolio policies for OptiLex. Examples: pair 2025-2026 efficiency papers with 1990s optimization or information theory, and keep separate near-distance and far-distance arms instead of one blended objective. | https://europepmc.org/article/PMC/PMC5397134
- Classical creativity operators with LLMs: TRIZ, structure-mapping, combinatorial axes (Llull), and associative 'bisociation' (Jiang, Li, Qian, Zhang, Luo (AutoTRIZ, Adv. Eng. Informatics 2025); Szczepanik & Chudziak (TRIZ Agents, ICAART 2025); Zhao et al. (Llull machine, 2025); Khojasteh et al. (YARN, 2026); Lewis & Mitchell (TMLR 2025); Wadhwa et al. (CREATE, 2026) 2024-2026) [expert_human_rating]: AutoTRIZ: an LLM detects technical contradictions, looks up inventive principles in the deterministic contradiction matrix, and generates solutions. Llull: sample Theme x Domain x Method combinations and prompt an LLM with each. YARN: LLM-made abstractions feed a structure-mapping engine. CREATE: scores associative paths between concepts on specificity and diversity, a measurable proxy for bisociation. OPERATOR PRINCIPLE: use these as cheap, interpretable diversification operators in the generator pool, not as validated sources of quality. | Result: AutoTRIZ (GPT-4, 100 runs per case): in 7 of 10 textbook cases, the top-3 contradiction detections match or half-match the expert textbook analysis. Its other evidence is one battery-thermal case study. TRIZ Agents: one engineering case, scored by an LLM judge. YARN: abstractions consistently improve analogical mapping in narratives over end-to-end LLMs. Lewis & Mitchell: GPT-family models lose accuracy on counterfactual variants of analogy tasks that humans solve robustly. CREATE: the strongest frontier models score best, but thinking models are not always better, and creative-prompting methods add only limited gains. No bisociation- or conceptual-blending system for science ideation with quantitative outcome evidence was found. | Limits: Mostly case studies, LLM judges or outdated models (GPT-3.5/4). The GPT-era analogy brittleness may not carry over to current frontier models, though the mechanism, reliance on surface similarity, plausibly persists. No executed outcomes. | Relevance: These are low-cost ways to add operator diversity to OptiLex's generator pool, for example TRIZ contradictions such as 'more data efficiency without more compute'. The evidence does not support prioritizing them over availability-based or expedition generators. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.13002
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Uzzi et al. 2013: papers with high median conventionality (median journal-pair z in the upper half) and high tail novelty (10th-percentile z<0) are hits (top 5% cited) at 9.11 per 100, against a background of 5. High conventionality only: 5.82. High novelty only: 5.33. Low on both: 2.05. Peak impact sits at the 85th-95th percentile of median conventionality. (caveat: 1) Scope: the 2x2 numbers in Fig. 2 cover only Web of Science papers from 1990-2000. The full dataset is 17.9M papers from 1950-2000, and the SOM says other decades give similar results. 2) A "hit" is a paper in the top 5% of citations across the whole dataset, counted over the 8 years after publication. The background rate of 5 per 100 follows from that definition. The SOM repeats the test with top-1% and top-10% cutoffs. 3) Novelty and conventionality are measured from pairs of cited journals, compared against a null model that randomly rewires citations. They describe the reference list, not the ideas themselves, and no humans or LLMs rated anything. The results are correlational. 4) The groups are very different in size. Per the Fig. 2 caption the bars cover 6.7%, 23%, 26% and 44% of papers, and I did not confirm which bar is which category. Most papers (59.3% in the 1990s) have no tail novelty. 5) The 85-95th percentile peak does not come from the 2x2 split. It comes from separate regressions on 11 equal-sized bins of median conventionality, with field fixed effects and a split by team size. The SOM puts the peak at "approximately the 85th percentile", and impact falls again above it. 6) Later work showed these journal-pair novelty scores change with the null model and the level of analysis (for example Boyack & Klavans 2014; Bornmann et al. 2019). Treat the exact numbers as specific to this measure, not as a general law. 7) Teams (3 or more authors) have high tail novelty 37.7% more often than solo authors, which is a confound when the 2x2 is read causally.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Artiles et al. 2026 (Alien Space of Science): when Claude Opus 4.7 picks 'novel yet feasible' atom triples 300 times, 95.7% of combinations contain one of its 10 most-used atoms, and effective atom coverage is 10.5%. The coherence-high, availability-low sampler reaches 34.3% and 72.5%, ties Claude on human-rated quality (4.03), and ranks best after autoresearch execution (rank percentile 0.633 vs 0.574). Claude's reports drift toward interpretability during the refinement loop, from 2/10 to 8/10. (caveat: 1) The samples are small. The human rating was 40 blinded reviews from 10 raters with ML/CS postgraduate degrees. Only 40 ideas were executed, 10 per source. The paper reports no significance tests, so the 4.03 tie and the 0.633 vs 0.574 gap are directional only. 2) Humans did not rank the executed reports. A blind LLM judge (Gemini 3.1 Pro) ranked them with a six-round Swiss-style pairwise schedule. 3) The corpus is narrow: 16,068 ML conference papers, so it covers ML research only. 4) Claude Opus 4.7 also turned the Alien sampler's atom triples into written ideas. The comparison therefore tests which atoms get picked, not Alien against Claude end to end. 5) The ideas were run on A100 40GB GPUs with bounded budgets, and the paper does not name the autoresearch agent it used. 6) The metrics are defined as follows. "Effective atom coverage" is exp(entropy of atom frequencies) divided by vocabulary size. The 95.7% / 34.3% figures are "top-10 concentration": the share of combinations that contain one of that method's ten most-used atoms. 7) Alien is best on average rank percentile. It also placed 3 of the top 5 and 4 of the top 10 reports.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Shi & Evans 2023: in MEDLINE, papers with the most surprising joint content+context combinations are about 5x more likely than random to be top-10% cited; context surprise alone about 4x, content surprise alone about 2x. Nearly 50% of the highest joint-novelty group are hits. The most surprising 'expeditions' (authors publishing far from their background venues) are 3.5x more likely to be hits, while career and team novelty matter less. (caveat: All the numbers match the primary source. Things the claim leaves out:
(1) A "hit" is a top-10%-cited paper among papers from the same publication year. So "random" means a 10% base rate, and "nearly 50%" is the same fact as "about 5x".
(2) Content is measured by combinations of MeSH keywords. Context is measured by combinations of the journals a paper cites (its references), not the journal it appears in.
(3) Surprise is the improbability of a combination under a hypergraph embedding model trained on earlier papers. It is a statistical score, not a human or LLM judgment.
(4) The results are observational correlations. They come from binned hit rates and regressions across novelty percentiles. They show association, not that surprise causes impact.
(5) Expedition novelty is the average distance between the team members' past publishing venues and the venue of the paper in question. Career and team novelty still show gradual increases in hit rate, so "matter less" means a weaker effect, not no effect.
(6) The MEDLINE data covers about 19.9M papers; the paper says the analysis focuses on 1966 onward. The patterns are reported to replicate in US patents (6.5M, 1979-2017) and APS physics papers (541K, 1893-2013).)
Gaps:
  - Nobody has measured the Uzzi conventionality/tail-novelty profile, or Shi-Evans hypergraph surprise, on LLM-generated ideas and linked it to executed outcomes. For example: does the high-conventionality, atypical-tail profile predict which ideas survive the Ideation-Execution Gap?
  - No generator implements Shi & Evans 'expeditions' explicitly: a distant-field persona solving a target-field problem framed for the target audience, compared head-to-head with generic interdisciplinary prompting on execution.
  - Availability/alienness models exist for materials and drugs (Sourati & Evans) and for LLM-research papers (Alien Space), but not for data, compute or energy efficiency of model training. No study sweeps the anti-crowd parameter against real training-run outcomes. Theory suggests beta around 0.2-0.3, but that comes from DFT and disease scores.
  - Motif collapse during refinement (Alien Space: Claude reports went from 2/10 to 8/10 on interpretability) has not been studied in soundness critic/fixer loops. Nobody has built an 'availability-preserving fixer', one that must repair soundness without raising the idea's availability or crowd score.
  - No knowledge base combines typed recombination edges (CHIMERA), problem-solution-rationale triplets (MUSE), typed ideation-move retrieval (RATIO), and an author-availability hypergraph. All current resources are limited to AI or bio abstracts.
  - Distance calibration has not been done for LLM-only engines. Human studies show a partial-match sweet spot (Kang 2022: 47% vs 21% creative adaptation, but 38% duds) and that humans avoid far facets (Scideator). No one has optimized retrieval distance against executed outcomes, or checked whether the Luo & Evans CS/engineering reversal (higher-order combinations pay less) holds for machine-generated ideas.
  - Retrieval policies taken from science-of-science have not been tested as LLM ideation inputs: mixing source ages (Mukherjee hotspot: 2x hit rate), and targeting new methods over new theories (Leahey et al.).
  - No evaluation metric reconciles 'humans will do this' (HindSight-style future matching, which by construction penalizes alien ideas) with 'valuable but nobody will do it' (Sourati/Alien). Only execution, or cheap simulation proxies such as scaling-law fits, can arbitrate. Nobody has built such a proxy ladder for ML-efficiency ideas.
  - It is untested whether citation-derived regularities transfer to machine-generated ideas at all. Part of the conventionality premium may be about readers absorbing the work, not effectiveness. When the evaluator is an execution harness, the conventional core may matter instead for implementability.
  - TRIZ, conceptual-blending, bisociation and structure-mapping operators have never been compared in a controlled way against plain or noisy prompting with frontier models and executed outcomes. The evidence is case studies, GPT-4-era models or LLM judges.
Surprises:
  - In Alien Space of Science, a Claude frontier model (Opus 4.7) was the most collapsed baseline, less diverse than Gemini 3.1 Pro: its top 10 atoms appear in 95.7% of combinations, against 76.7% for Gemini. Its high-upside tail also shrank under a GPT-5.5 judge, with win rate falling from 48.9% to 27.8% in the top-half pool. The collapse got worse during autoresearch refinement, not only at generation.
  - In computer science and engineering, OptiLex's test domain, the citation premium for filling higher-order conceptual holes falls as order increases (Luo & Evans 2026), the opposite of empirical sciences, where enrichment rises from about 1.5 to 2.6. More atypical multi-way combinations may be the wrong knob for ML.
  - Shi & Evans: interdisciplinary careers and multidisciplinary teams predict impact much less than 'expeditions', one-directional moves of a field's toolkit into a distant field's problem. The expedition effect disappears in patents, where boundary-crossing is routine.
  - Sourati & Evans: human discoverability collapses as soon as the model starts avoiding the crowd (beta>0), but first-principles merit holds until about beta=0.4. For some targets it even rises, which suggests crowded fields are over-harvested.
  - Uzzi 2013: novelty without a conventional core barely beats baseline (5.33 vs 5 hits per 100). Peak impact sits at an extreme 85th-95th percentile of conventionality, not at high novelty.
  - Wang/Veugelers/Stephan: novel papers pay off in foreign fields (+62.37% odds of top-cited) but not in their home field (not significant), and they are penalized in citation windows shorter than 3 years. A single in-domain judge, human or LLM persona, would systematically miss them.
  - The famous 'science is becoming less disruptive' result is actively contested. Holst et al. attribute it to zero-reference database artefacts; Nature published their 2026 comment titled 'can partially drive'. Fang et al. argue innovation moved to cross-field generalizations instead of declining.
  - In the Kittur-lab analogy study, the distance that drove creative adaptation also produced 38% duds. In Scideator, human researchers mostly ignored far facets. Humans show the same pull toward the near and familiar that LLMs do.

## harness_patterns
- Execution-grounded evolutionary idea search (automated executor + evolutionary selection) (Chenglei Si, Zitong Yang, Yejin Choi, Emmanuel Candès, Diyi Yang, Tatsunori Hashimoto 2026 (arXiv 2601.14525, 20 Jan 2026)) [executed_computationally]: An LLM ideator proposes batches of ideas (50 per epoch for GRPO, 80 for nanoGPT). An LLM executor turns each idea into code and launches large parallel GPU runs. The measured results drive an evolutionary loop: exploitation starts at 50% and rises over epochs, and new variants either combine the strengths of above-baseline ideas or are completely new. It was compared with best-of-N at identical sampling budgets and with RL on the same execution reward. | Result: Within 10 search epochs it found a GRPO post-training recipe scoring 69.4% vs 48.0% for the baseline (1.5B model, math), and a nanoGPT pre-training recipe reaching the target validation loss in 19.7 min vs 35.9 min. It beat best-of-N at the same budget (N=80-240) from epoch 1. Claude-4.5-Opus and Sonnet as executors implemented >90% of nanoGPT ideas; with GPT-5 as executor the rate was 42-78%. Claude-4.5-Opus kept scaling while Claude-4.5-Sonnet and GPT-5 'tend to saturate early'. RL mode-collapsed: at epoch 68, 119 of 128 sampled ideas were one of two tricks (RMSNorm->LayerNorm or an EMA), vs 51 at epoch 0. RL raised average reward but not the maximum. | Limits: Only two environments, both in ML training. The best recipes were combinations of known tricks (architecture changes, hyperparameter tuning, EMA of checkpoints); novelty was explicitly out of scope. No per-idea GPU cost is reported (256-1024 GPUs per RL rollout batch). No correlation is reported between pre-execution LLM judgments and executed outcomes. | Relevance: The closest template for OptiLex's test domain: nanoGPT time-to-target-loss is itself a compute-efficiency objective. It supports making execution the evaluation layer, with LLM judges only as a cheap pre-filter. It shows the choice of generator model (Opus-class) decides whether search keeps scaling. It also warns against RL-tuning the ideator on execution reward, because that collapses diversity; the search should stay in the harness. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14525
- Controlled scaling laws for agent architectures (single vs independent / centralized / decentralized / hybrid) (Yubin Kim, Ken Gu et al. (19 authors) 2025 (v1 9 Dec 2025; v2 8 Apr 2026)) [executed_computationally]: 260 configurations: 5 architectures x 3 LLM families (GPT-5 nano/mini/full; Gemini 2.0 Flash, 2.5 Flash, 2.5 Pro; Claude Sonnet 3.7/4/4.5) x 6 agentic benchmarks (BrowseComp-Plus, Finance-Agent, PlanCraft, WorkBench, SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench). Total reasoning tokens were matched at a mean of about 4,800 per trial. A predictive model is fitted from coordination and task properties. | Result: The effect of architecture ranged from +80.8% (decomposable financial reasoning, centralized) to -70.0% (sequential planning). Tasks where a single agent already exceeds ~45% accuracy get negative returns from adding agents. Trace-level error amplification was 17.2x for independent agents vs 4.4x for centralized. Coordination overhead relative to a single agent: independent 58%, decentralized 263%, centralized 285%, hybrid 515%. The fitted model picks the best architecture for 87% of held-out configurations (cross-validated R^2 = 0.373). | Limits: Per-trial budgets (~4.8k reasoning tokens) are far below those of long-horizon research harnesses. The benchmarks have ground-truth answers; none is open-ended ideation. R^2 is modest. The newest frontier models (Opus 4.5+, GPT-5.2+) are not included. | Relevance: Producing many ideas is decomposable, which favours parallel workers. Vetting and fix chains are sequential, which is where multi-agent setups hurt. Implication: parallelize generation, keep one central verifier to limit error propagation, and do not add agents to stages a single Opus agent already handles well. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296
- Budget-matched single-agent vs multi-agent controls (and debate vs vote) (Dat Tran & Douwe Kiela (2026); Hyeong Kyu Choi, Xiaojin Zhu, Yixuan Li (2025) 2026 (arXiv 2604.02460, 2 Apr 2026); Debate-or-Vote arXiv 2508.17536 (Aug 2025, NeurIPS 2025 spotlight)) [executed_computationally]: Tran & Kiela hold the thinking-token budget fixed (100 to 10k) and compare a single agent with sequential and parallel multi-agent systems on multi-hop QA (FRAMES, MuSiQue 4-hop), using Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama and Gemini 2.5, with an information-theoretic argument based on the data-processing inequality. Debate-or-Vote separates multi-agent debate into its voting and debate parts across 7 NLP benchmarks and proves that debate induces a martingale over the agents' beliefs. | Result: At matched thinking budgets the single agent matched or beat multi-agent systems. Example, MuSiQue 4-hop at 1k tokens: DeepSeek-R1-70B 0.407 single vs 0.320 sequential multi-agent; Gemini 2.5 Flash 0.331 vs 0.287. Multi-agent systems became competitive only when the single agent's use of its context degraded, or when more compute was spent. Debate-or-Vote: majority voting alone accounts for most of the gains attributed to debate. | Limits: Short multi-hop QA, not open-ended generation. Open or mid-tier models. The authors acknowledge budget-control artifacts in the Gemini API. The diversity benefit of multi-agent systems is not measured. | Relevance: Any OptiLex claim that the harness beats plain Opus must be token-matched; otherwise the gain may just be extra compute. Given OptiLex's compute/energy goal, the harness itself should be scored in validated ideas per token. Multi-agent is justified by context isolation and decorrelation, not by 'collaboration' as such. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02460
- Diversity over headcount in agent scaling (Yingxuan Yang, Shangding Gu et al. (8 authors); contrast: Wenzhe Li, Yong Lin, Mengzhou Xia, Chi Jin (Self-MoA) 2026 (arXiv 2602.03794, 3 Feb 2026); Self-MoA arXiv 2502.00674 (Feb 2025)) [executed_computationally]: An information-theoretic framework in which performance is bounded by the number of effective, uncorrelated information channels (K*), estimated without labels. Homogeneous agent scaling is compared with heterogeneous agents that differ in model, prompt or tools. | Result: '2 diverse agents can match or exceed the performance of 16 homogeneous agents'. Homogeneous agents saturate early because their outputs are strongly correlated. Contrast, Self-MoA: aggregating samples from one top model beat mixing different LLMs by 6.6% on AlpacaEval 2.0 and 3.8% on average across MMLU, CRUX and MATH, because mixing lowers average quality. | Limits: Reasoning and QA benchmarks, not ideation. Diversity helps only when members are of comparable quality (Self-MoA). Numbers taken from the abstract. | Relevance: Matches the OptiLex pilot: 30 clean Opus prompts gave 3 families, while noisy or novelty prompts gave 3-5x more. The harness budget should go into decorrelating generators of similar quality (prompt noise, personas, different retrieval seeds, peer frontier models) rather than more copies of the same one. K* is a candidate label-free monitor of generator redundancy. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03794
- Soundness critics as first-gate filters (SoundnessBench) and style bias in idea judges (Sy-Tuyen Ho, Minghui Liu, Huy Nghiem, Furong Huang; SciStyleBench (11 authors) 2026 (arXiv 2605.30329, 28 May 2026; SciStyleBench arXiv 2608.01666, Aug 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: 1,099 ML research proposals reconstructed from ICLR submissions and labelled with the reviewers' soundness sub-scores. 12 frontier LLMs act as binary soundness gatekeepers under a standard prompt and an aggressive (sceptical) prompt. SciStyleBench keeps the content of 600 ideas fixed and varies their style 15 ways (9,000 evaluations per setting). | Result: Standard prompt: Claude-Opus-4.6 passed 71.8% of low-soundness proposals (low-soundness recall 28.2%, macro-F1 60.5%). Mean false-positive rate across the 12 models was 74.0%. Best model: GPT-5.4 at macro-F1 69.7%. The aggressive prompt flips the error type: mean high-soundness recall falls to 36.1%, and GPT-5.4 rejects everything (0.0% high-soundness recall; Opus 4.6 reaches macro-F1 68.6%). SciStyleBench: direct judges score Substance Recognition 0.504 (near chance) and Style Bias Index 0.566 before mitigation (0.759 and 0.501 after). | Limits: Labels are ICLR reviewer sub-scores (noisy expert ratings, not executed outcomes). Proposals are reconstructed from papers. No ensemble or retrieval-augmented critic condition was tested. | Relevance: This undermines a pipeline that uses critics as gates: an Opus soundness critic would let about 7 in 10 unsound ideas through, and making it harsh trades that for killing good ideas. Together with our 53% judge self-agreement, critics should triage and flag rather than gate. Thresholds need calibrating against executed outcomes, and ideas should be normalised to a fixed format before judging. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30329
- Verification-gated shared context (DeLM) and blackboard coordination (Yuzhen Mao & Azalia Mirhoseini (DeLM); Alireza Salemi et al. (blackboard) 2026 (arXiv 2606.10662, 9 Jun 2026); blackboard arXiv 2510.01285 (Sep 2025, rev. Jan 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Parallel agents, a task queue and a shared context replace the central orchestrator. Each contribution is compressed into a 'gist' and admitted only after an LLM verifier confirms it is grounded: first a verbatim check of the reference-grounded summary, then a faithfulness check of the gist. Failed updates are rejected or regenerated with feedback. In the blackboard variant, a central agent posts requests and subordinate agents volunteer. | Result: SWE-bench Verified: 77.4% pass@4 vs 71.8% for the centralized AOrchestra-Parallel, at about $0.12 per task (~50% cheaper). Up to +10.5 points over the strongest baseline; up to +5.7 points on LongBench-v2. Removing verification cut accuracy from 60.1% to 55.2%. Blackboard: 13-57% relative gain in end-to-end success over RAG and master-slave baselines (KramaBench, DSBench, DA-Code). | Limits: Coding and QA with checkable facts. The LLM verifier checks faithfulness to sources, not whether a novel claim is true. A single recent paper, not replicated. | Relevance: A blueprint for OptiLex's knowledge-base ledger: admit only verified, evidence-linked entries (results, failures, constraints), so parallel generators do not spread unsupported claims. This is consistent with the 17.2x vs 4.4x error-amplification finding. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10662
- Month-scale hypothesize-verify-analyze funnel (DeepScientist) (Yixuan Weng, Minjun Zhu, Qiujie Xie, Qiyao Sun, Zhen Lin, Sifan Liu, Yue Zhang 2025 (arXiv 2509.26603, 30 Sep 2025)) [executed_computationally]: Discovery is framed as Bayesian optimization: many hypotheses get a cheap LLM valuation (a surrogate), a subset is selected for implementation, and the results are analysed. A cumulative findings memory balances exploration and exploitation. Gemini-2.5-Pro handles core logic and Claude-4-Opus writes code. | Result: >20,000 GPU-hours. About 5,000 unique ideas led to about 1,100 experimentally validated ones and 21 progress findings (0.42% of ideas, ~1.9% of those implemented). It beat 2025 human SOTA on three tasks by 183.7%, 1.9% and 7.9%. About 60% of failed trials ended early on implementation errors; most of the rest gave no gain or a regression. The authors say selection raises the success rate to about 1-3%, from 'effectively zero'. | Limits: Gains on 2 of the 3 tasks are small. The authors chose the tasks. 2025-era models. The claim of ICLR-comparable paper quality rests on limited review. | Relevance: A realistic funnel prior for OptiLex: expect about 1-2% of implemented ideas to be real wins, and the executor to be the largest source of loss. Invest in the fixer/executor and cheap kill experiments. The compute per win (~1,000 GPU-hours per finding) must be charged against OptiLex's efficiency frontier. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26603
- Orchestrator-worker deep research (Anthropic Research feature) (Anthropic (engineering blog) 2025 (13 Jun 2025)) [llm_judge_only]: A lead agent (Claude Opus 4) plans and saves the plan to external memory, then spawns parallel Sonnet 4 subagents, each with its own context window and explicit task boundaries. Effort-scaling rules are in the prompt: 1 agent with 3-10 tool calls for simple queries, more than 10 subagents for complex research. A citation agent follows. Evaluation used an LLM-judge rubric (factual accuracy, citation accuracy, completeness, source quality, tool efficiency) plus human testing. New versions roll out via rainbow deployments. | Result: The multi-agent system beat single-agent Opus 4 by 90.2% on an internal research eval. On BrowseComp, token usage alone explained 80% of performance variance, and 95% together with tool calls and model choice. Agents use about 4x the tokens of chat and multi-agent systems about 15x. Parallel tool calling cut research time by up to 90%, and rewriting tool descriptions cut task time by 40%. 'Upgrading to Claude Sonnet 4 is a larger performance gain than doubling the token budget on Claude Sonnet 3.7.' | Limits: The 90.2% figure is from an internal eval, not token-matched against a single agent, and vendor-reported. The tasks are breadth-first search. The authors call it a poor fit for tasks with shared context or dependencies, such as most coding. Early failure modes included spawning 50 subagents for simple queries. | Relevance: A pattern to reuse for OptiLex's literature, knowledge-base and prior-art layer, which is breadth-first and parallelizable. The 80%-of-variance-by-tokens result means multi-agent is mainly a way to spend more tokens usefully through context isolation. The ~15x token cost has to be counted in OptiLex's efficiency accounting. | https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system
- Hybrid parallel -> distill -> refine test-time scaling for long-horizon agents (Joongwon Kim, ..., Hannaneh Hajishirzi, Ruslan Salakhutdinov (16 authors); Xiangming Gu, Soham De, Larisa Markeeva, Petar Veličković, Razvan Pascanu; Aman Sharma & Paras Chopra 2026 (arXiv 2604.16529, 16 Apr 2026; 2604.05868, 7 Apr 2026); Sequential Edge arXiv 2511.02309 (Nov 2025)) [executed_computationally]: Each long rollout is compressed into a compact summary that keeps its hypotheses, progress and failure modes. Parallel scaling uses Recursive Tournament Voting (comparisons within small groups). Sequential scaling uses Parallel-Distill-Refine: new attempts are conditioned on distilled summaries of earlier runs. | Result: Claude-4.5-Opus on SWE-bench Verified: 70.9% -> 77.6%. Terminus 1 on Terminal-Bench v2.0: 46.9% -> 59.1%. Gu et al. find parallel sampling beats sequential mainly because sequential sampling explores less, not because of aggregation or context length. Contested by Sharma & Chopra: sequential refinement beat parallel self-consistency in 95.6% of configurations at matched compute (5 open models, 3 benchmarks, up to +46.7%). | Limits: Checkable coding and math tasks. The parallel-vs-sequential evidence conflicts and depends on task and model. Not tested on ideation. | Relevance: Suggested OptiLex flow: run diverse parallel generators first for exploration, distil each lineage into a compact entry in the ledger, then run sequential fixer rounds conditioned on the distilled failures. Rank with small-group tournaments rather than global pointwise scores. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16529
- Tournament-evolution hypothesis generation (Google AI co-scientist) (Juraj Gottweis et al. (51 authors, Google) 2025 (arXiv 2502.18864, 26 Feb 2025; published in Nature 2026)) [lab_validated]: A supervisor sends asynchronous tasks to Generation, Reflection, Ranking (pairwise debate tournament with Elo), Evolution, Proximity (clustering and dedup) and Meta-review agents. More test-time compute means running the tournament longer. | Result: Picking the highest-Elo answer gave 78.4% top-1 accuracy on GPQA diamond (Elo tracked correctness). Across 203 research goals, top-10 and maximum Elo rose from the earliest to the latest 10% of results. Experts on 11 goals rated novelty 3.64/5 and impact 3.09/5, preferring it to Gemini 2.0, o1, o3-mini-high and DeepSeek R1. Its AML drug-repurposing candidates inhibited tumour cells in vitro at clinically applicable concentrations in several AML cell lines. | Limits: The Elo rise is self-referential (same judge), and the Elo-correctness link was shown on GPQA, not on open-ended ideas. Expert ratings have small n. The lab validations are curated, and the success rate across all hypotheses is not reported. Gemini 2.0-era models. | Relevance: The reference architecture OptiLex most resembles. Elo rises with compute, but only as measured by its own judge, so OptiLex should anchor its tournament in executed results (as Si et al. 2026 do) and use a proximity agent for mechanism-family dedup. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18864
- Structured world model as shared memory across hundreds of rollouts (Kosmos) (Ludovico Mitchener, Angela Yiu et al. (37 authors, Edison Scientific / FutureHouse) 2025 (arXiv 2511.02824, 4 Nov 2025)) [expert_human_rating]: Each cycle runs up to 10 parallel data-analysis and literature-search tasks. Their outputs are summarised into a structured world model, which agents query to propose the next tasks. A run lasts up to 12 hours: ~200 agent rollouts, ~42,000 lines of code, ~1,500 papers. | Result: Experts judged 79.4% of 102 report statements accurate: data-analysis 85.5%, literature 82.1%, synthesis/interpretation 57.9%. Valuable findings scaled linearly with cycles, up to 20 tested. Of 7 highlighted discoveries, 3 reproduced preprint or unpublished findings not seen at runtime (retrospective) and 4 were new. | Limits: Only 102 statements from 3 reports. The 'six months of work' figure is collaborators' self-report. The world-model schema and cost are not disclosed. | Relevance: Supports OptiLex's knowledge-base/ledger layer. The least reliable class, synthesis at 57.9%, is exactly where ideas are formed, so synthesised claims should be tagged and sent to verification rather than stored as facts. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02824
- Long-horizon parallel agent teams with file/git ledgers and hierarchical planners (Anthropic engineering (C compiler, 5 Feb 2026; long-running harnesses, 26 Nov 2025; harness design, 24 Mar 2026); Cursor (14 Jan 2026) 2025-2026) [executed_computationally]: Anthropic: 16 Claude agents in infinite loops, each in a Docker container, claiming tasks by writing lock files to current_tasks/ and syncing through git, with no orchestrator. Roles were specialised (dedup, performance, critique, docs). GCC served as an oracle to split one monolithic task (compiling the kernel) into parallel subtasks. Related Anthropic posts add an initializer agent, a JSON feature list, a progress file, and a planner/generator/evaluator design with a separate sceptical evaluator. Cursor: flat agents with locks failed, and optimistic concurrency made agents risk-averse; recursive planners plus workers plus a judge worked. | Result: About 2,000 Claude Code sessions, $20,000, and 2B input / 140M output tokens produced a 100,000-line Rust C compiler that builds Linux 6.9 on x86, ARM and RISC-V. Cursor: locking reduced 20 agents to the effective throughput of 2-3. The planner/worker design ran hundreds of concurrent agents for about a week and wrote a browser of over 1M lines of code; 'many of our improvements came from removing complexity'. Anthropic harness: solo agent 20 min / $9 vs full harness 6 h / $200 (quality judged qualitatively). The sprint construct was dropped with Opus 4.6. | Limits: Software engineering with near-perfect test oracles. Case studies of single runs, with no controlled ablations; vendor-reported. Model rankings shift: Cursor found GPT-5.2 better than Opus 4.5 at long autonomy. | Relevance: Engineering patterns for OptiLex's long-running engine: file ledgers with git as the source of truth, lock-file task claiming, oracles that split monolithic goals, hierarchical planners over flat swarms, a separate sceptical evaluator, and pruning scaffolding as models improve. The core lesson transfers directly: the task verifier must be 'nearly perfect, otherwise Claude will solve the wrong problem'. | https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler
- RL-trained orchestrator swarm (Kimi K2.5 Agent Swarm / PARL) (Moonshot AI (Kimi Team) 2026 (blog 27 Jan 2026; arXiv 2602.02276)) [executed_computationally]: Parallel-Agent RL trains only the orchestrator, which spawns frozen sub-agents on demand (up to 100 sub-agents and 1,500 tool calls). Reward = an instantiation reward (against serial collapse) + the sub-agent finish rate (against spurious parallelism) + task outcome; the first two terms are annealed to zero. Latency is measured in critical steps. | Result: Single agent vs swarm: BrowseComp 60.6% -> 78.4%; WideSearch item-F1 72.7% -> 79.0%; in-house swarm benchmark 41.6% -> 58.3%. 3-4.5x faster wall-clock on WideSearch. | Limits: Not token-matched. Wide-search tasks only. Vendor-reported. Requires RL infrastructure. | Relevance: Swarms pay off on breadth, and without an incentive models fall back to serial work. A prompted Opus orchestrator may similarly under-parallelise, so OptiLex needs explicit fan-out rules in the harness to substitute for PARL. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02276
- Quality-diversity lineage search for ideation (IDEAgent) (Varun Gumma, Navonil Majumder, Soumitra Sinhahajari, Soujanya Poria 2026 (arXiv 2607.22375, 24 Jul 2026)) [llm_judge_only]: Multiple agents evolve lineages of ideas. Quality comes from multi-objective repair feedback, with 5 independent soundness scores aggregated. Diversity comes from sequential memory and explicit comparison against completed, ancestor and rejected ideas along 8 mechanism axes (failure mode, causal diagnosis, intervention, signal source, locus, objective, evaluation regime, assumption class). The population metric 'Yield' is the largest mutually diverse set of ideas above a quality threshold. | Result: 3.89x the Yield of the best baseline; non-zero Yield on 8x more topics (32 topics across 8 CS domains); about $1.31-6.72 per qualifying idea. | Limits: LLM judges only, with no human or executed validation. Baselines are internal variants, not other systems such as AI Scientist. | Relevance: The 8-axis mechanism comparison and the Yield metric are a ready-made version of OptiLex's mechanism-family count. They need validating first, since our pilot's judge agreed with itself only 53% of the time. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22375
- Market/auction allocation by agent self-assessment (MarketBench) (Andrey Fradkin & Rohit Krishnan 2026 (arXiv 2604.23897, 26 Apr 2026)) [executed_computationally]: Agents bid on tasks with self-reported success probability and token cost. The auction's allocation is compared with the full-information allocation on 93 SWE-bench Lite tasks across six recent LLMs. | Result: The LLMs 'are miscalibrated on both success probability and token usage', and auctions built from their self-reports diverge from the full-information allocation. Adding context on past performance improved calibration only modestly. | Limits: Coding tasks only. The exact size of the efficiency gap is not captured here. | Relevance: Argues against letting generator agents bid for compute on their own confidence in an idea. Allocate compute through external signals instead: cheap executed pilots or calibrated judges. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23897
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Across 260 configurations with matched reasoning-token budgets (~4,800 tokens per trial), adding agents gives negative returns once single-agent accuracy exceeds ~45%. Independent agents with no central verification amplify errors 17.2x vs 4.4x for centralized coordination. Architecture effects range from +80.8% (decomposable financial reasoning) to -70.0% (sequential planning). => CORRECTED: Kim et al., "Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems" (arXiv 2512.08296v3, Apr 2026; Google/MIT/UW et al.) covers 260 configurations: 6 benchmarks (Finance-Agent, PlanCraft, Workbench, BrowseComp-Plus, SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench), 5 architectures and 9 models from 3 families (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic). Total reasoning tokens were matched on average (mean 4,800 per trial), not capped at a fixed budget. The ~45% figure is a decision boundary from a fitted regression. The fit has a negative P_SA x log(1+n_agents) interaction (beta = -0.236), and the boundary is 0.17 in standardized units, about 0.45 after denormalization. It is not a hard observed cutoff. The 17.2x (independent) vs 4.4x (centralized) figures are trace-level error-amplification factors estimated from execution-trace token analysis, not ratios of final-answer error rates. The +80.8% (Centralized on Finance-Agent, 0.631 vs single-agent 0.349) and -70.0% (Independent on PlanCraft, 0.170) are relative changes vs the single-agent baseline, averaged across model families. (caveat: 1) The numbers are version-sensitive. Only v3 (8 Apr 2026) was checked, and it states 260 configurations across 6 benchmarks. Older versions and secondary write-ups may cite different counts, such as 180 configurations on 4 benchmarks. Cite v3.
2) The 45% threshold is model-derived: it depends on a regression with modest predictive power (cross-validated R²=0.373, or 0.413 with task-grounded metrics). It is a statistical tendency, not a rule.
3) "Amplify errors 17.2x" is easy to misread as 17x more wrong answers. It is a trace-level factor estimated from execution-trace tokens: roughly, how much extra work coordination failures cause.
4) The +80.8% and -70.0% are the extreme single cells: one architecture on one benchmark each, averaged across models. They are not typical effect sizes.
5) Scoring is benchmark-native task success: factual correctness for Finance-Agent, goal satisfaction for PlanCraft, task completion for Workbench. The paper reports validator agreement of Cohen's kappa 0.87-0.91. From the text alone I could not confirm whether any benchmark uses an LLM grader.
6) Every task is a tool-using agentic benchmark (finance, planning, web browsing, software engineering, terminal). None is an open-ended creative or research-ideation task, so applying these results to creative or research harnesses is an extrapolation.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: Execution-guided evolutionary search beat best-of-N at identical sampling budgets from epoch 1. It reached nanoGPT target loss in 19.7 min vs 35.9 min and GRPO accuracy of 69.4% vs 48.0% within 10 epochs. Claude-4.5-Opus kept scaling while Claude-4.5-Sonnet and GPT-5 saturated. RL on the same signal mode-collapsed (119 of 128 ideas were two tricks). => CORRECTED: Si, Yang, Choi, Candès, Yang and Hashimoto (arXiv 2601.14525, "Towards Execution-Grounded Automated AI Research") compared execution-guided evolutionary search with best-of-N at matched budgets in only one setup: GPT-5 on nanoGPT, first 3 epochs versus N in {80, 160, 240}. There it beat best-of-N from epoch 1 onward. The headline numbers are measured against the original hand-written baselines, not against best-of-N. Within 10 search epochs, the best GRPO accuracy went from 48.0% (baseline) to 69.4% (Claude-4.5-Sonnet backbone), and nanoGPT time to target validation loss 3.28 went from 35.9 to 19.7 min (Claude-4.5-Opus backbone). Only Claude-4.5-Opus kept scaling on both tasks; Sonnet and GPT-5 saturated early. Separately, RL on Qwen3-30B with the executor as reward raised average reward but not max reward, and mode-collapsed on nanoGPT: at epoch 68, 119 of 128 sampled ideas were one of two ideas (RMSNorm to LayerNorm, or EMA). (caveat: 1) The claim puts best-of-N next to 19.7 vs 35.9 and 69.4 vs 48.0, which reads as a head-to-head result. It is not: those numbers compare the search against the untouched baseline code, and the matched-budget best-of-N test covers only GPT-5 on nanoGPT for 3 epochs. 2) The two headline numbers come from different backbones (Sonnet for GRPO, Opus for nanoGPT), not from a single run or model. 3) Results are judged by real GPU runs: 256 GPUs for GRPO and 1024 for nanoGPT, with 50 and 80 ideas per epoch respectively. No LLM judge is involved, but the tasks are narrow, single-metric benchmark environments. 4) Human baselines put this in context. On nanoGPT the human speedrun leaderboard is at about 2.1 min, far below 19.7 min. On GRPO, 69.4% is only slightly above the Stanford CS336 class leaderboard best of 68.8%. 5) The RL mode collapse is for Qwen3-30B finetuned with the executor reward, a different model and method from the frontier-LLM search. It is shown on nanoGPT at epoch 68. 6) I verified the finer details through WebFetch summaries of the arXiv HTML, not by reading the PDF directly; the individual figures look internally consistent.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Under standard prompting, Claude-Opus-4.6 rated 71.8% of low-soundness ML proposals as sound (12-model mean 74.0%). Sceptical prompting lowered mean high-soundness recall to 36.1%. The best macro-F1 was 69.7% (GPT-5.4). (caveat: 1) The 71.8% figure is not stated in the paper. It is derived as 100 minus Opus's low-soundness recall of 28.2%. 2) The paper calls the sceptical condition the "aggressive prompt": it defaults to low soundness unless rigor is clearly shown. Mean high-soundness recall fell from 91.8% to 36.1%, and mean macro-F1 fell from 54.9 to 49.3. The errors moved from false positives to false negatives rather than going away. 3) Opus-4.6 was the most balanced model under the aggressive prompt (low recall 72.2, high recall 66.4, macro-F1 68.6), close to GPT-5.4's best of 69.7. GPT-5.4 itself fell apart under that prompt (high recall 0.0, F1 29.5), so "best model" depends on the prompt. 4) The 12-model mean is unweighted and includes older or weaker models (LLaMA-3.3-70B, GPT-4o, Kimi), whose low recall of 2 to 14% drags it down. Frontier models alone look less optimistic (GPT-5.4 low recall 64.6, Sonnet-4.6 37.2). 5) Ground truth is human: ICLR reviewer soundness scores, with a mean of 3 or more labelled high and 2 or less labelled low. Scores in between appear to be left out. Proposals were reconstructed from 1,099 ICLR submissions (458 low, 641 high). So the task is binary classification against peer-review labels. There was no execution or lab validation. The authors frame it as proposal-stage soundness, not prediction of full review outcomes. 6) The domain is ML proposals only, and the result is the models acting as standalone judges, not inside an agent harness.)
Gaps:
  - No study compares a multi-agent ideation harness with a single agent at matched token budgets on executed outcomes. All budget-matched studies (Tran & Kiela 2026; Kim et al. 2025/26) use QA, reasoning or coding with ground truth.
  - No one has measured soundness-critic precision and recall against executed outcomes for ML-efficiency ideas. SoundnessBench labels are ICLR reviewer sub-scores, and Si et al. 2026 report no correlation between pre-execution judgments and executed results.
  - No ablation shows how generator diversity (prompt noise, personas, different models) changes the cost per validated win. DeepScientist reports a funnel (5,000 -> 1,100 -> 21) but does not vary diversity; heterogeneity-scaling papers use QA.
  - Population-level mechanism-family metrics (IDEAgent's Yield and 8 axes, the K* effective-channel count) have not been validated against human experts or executed outcomes; judge self-agreement on novelty (our 53%) makes this a blocking gap.
  - Verification-gated shared memory (DeLM, Kosmos world model, blackboard) has been tested on coding, QA and data analysis, not on whether it improves the novelty or hit rate of generated ideas. How to verify synthesised claims (Kosmos synthesis accuracy 57.9%) before storing them is unsolved.
  - No harness reports compute or energy per validated discovery (e.g., GPU-hours or joules per finding that beats SOTA) in a way that fits OptiLex's combined data+compute+energy frontier. DeepScientist implies ~1,000 GPU-hours per finding, but nobody has optimised this.
  - Tournament/Elo ranking of open-ended ideas has not been shown to track executed success; the co-scientist's Elo-correctness link is on GPQA, and its Elo growth with compute is measured by its own judge.
  - No execution-grounded ideation harness exists beyond ML and code. For lab domains, realised validation is small-n and curated (co-scientist AML, Virtual Lab nanobodies).
  - Market or auction allocation of compute using external (not self-reported) value estimates, such as cheap pilot runs, is untested. MarketBench shows self-reports fail.
  - Semantic and mode collapse over long runs is documented (RL collapse in Si et al. 2026; 30,076 agents on Moltbook, arXiv 2609.16051), but no research harness has tested anti-collapse interventions against executed outcomes over month-long runs.
Surprises:
  - Learning from execution feedback in the weights (RL) did worse than learning in the harness (evolutionary search). By epoch 68 RL had collapsed to two tricks (119 of 128 ideas: RMSNorm->LayerNorm or EMA) and raised average reward but not the maximum.
  - Generator model choice beat orchestration: in execution-grounded search Claude-4.5-Opus kept scaling while Claude-4.5-Sonnet and GPT-5 saturated early, and Anthropic reports that upgrading the model beat doubling the token budget. Yet Cursor found GPT-5.2 better than Opus 4.5 at week-long autonomy ('stop earlier and take shortcuts'), so the best model depends on the role.
  - The winning executed ideas for nanoGPT and GRPO were combinations of known tricks, not new mechanisms. Realised efficiency gains may not require the novelty that judges reward.
  - Sceptical prompting does not fix a critic; it flips the error type. GPT-5.4 went from macro-F1 69.7% to rejecting every proposal (0.0% high-soundness recall).
  - In DeepScientist, about 60% of failed trials died from implementation errors, so the executor, not the ideator, is the dominant bottleneck; only 21 of ~1,100 implemented ideas were real advances.
  - Removing harness complexity repeatedly helped. Cursor's integrator role created bottlenecks, and Anthropic dropped its sprint construct with Opus 4.6: 'every component in a harness encodes an assumption about what the model can't do on its own.'
  - Coordination overhead at matched tokens is large (centralized 285%, hybrid 515% vs single agent), and multi-agent setups lost up to 70% on sequential planning. Vetting and fixing chains are the sequential part of an idea engine.
  - Two findings pull in opposite directions: diverse agents beat many homogeneous ones (2 ≈ 16), but mixing different LLMs lowered quality vs one strong model (Self-MoA +6.6%). They reconcile only if diversity comes from members of comparable quality, e.g. varying Opus prompts and seeds rather than adding weaker models.
  - Kosmos statement accuracy was 85.5% for data analysis but only 57.9% for synthesis and interpretation: the layer that forms ideas is the least accurate.

## cross_domain_outcome_forecasting
- LLM-simulated survey experiments predict treatment effects (Ashokkumar, Hewitt, Ghezae & Willer) (Stanford (Willer lab) / Stanford GSB 2026 (Nature, online 8 Jul 2026; preprint 2024)) [retrospective_prediction]: GPT-4 is prompted many times to role-play demographically representative US respondents under each experimental condition. The simulated responses are pooled into condition means, and differences between them become predicted treatment effects, which are then correlated with realized effects from preregistered experiments. The test is time-split: studies not public by GPT-4's Sept 2021 cutoff are compared with published ones, with an extra check on whether GPT-4 can guess each study's author. | Result: Preprint (476 effects, 70 studies, 105,165 participants): r=0.85 (disattenuated 0.91). Direction correct on 90% of significant contrasts. Unpublished/post-cutoff studies (N=33, 273 effects): r=0.90 (adj 0.94). Published studies (N=37, 203 effects): r=0.74. Studies whose author GPT-4 could not guess (56%): r=0.69. Lay forecasters (N=2,659): r=0.79. An unweighted human+LLM average gave r=0.88. Raw GPT-4 effects are too large: RMSE 10.9pp vs 8.4pp for forecasters; multiplying by 0.56 cuts it to 5.3pp (forecasters 6.0pp, combined 4.7pp). Megastudies: survey r=0.47, field r=0.27, non-text treatments r=0.24. In the 6 megastudies that have expert forecasts, LLM r=0.37 vs experts r=0.25. Nature version: 469 effects, 119,330 participants, 15 megastudies with 606 effects ('lower but comparable' to experts). The authors' demo site gives megastudy r=.34 vs experts .26. | Limits: The main archive is text-based US survey experiments, the easiest regime. When the treatments are all plausible and all aim the same way (megastudies), r falls to about 0.3-0.47, and field or non-text treatments are worse. The higher r for unpublished studies may reflect subset composition, such as a wider spread of effects. The author-unrecognized subset has lower r (0.69), so recognition may still help somewhat. GPT-4 only, with no frontier-2026 models. I could not read the Nature full text, so the Nature-version r values were not checked. Li & Ji (2026) show that matching human response distributions is not evidence of treatment-effect accuracy. | Relevance: This is the strongest positive evidence that a cheap LLM simulator can rank ideas before execution in a field where execution is costly. It works for ranking, not for absolute magnitudes, and it is weakest at discriminating among many plausible interventions, which is exactly the idea-engine regime. Design rule: use forecasters for ordinal triage, shrink predicted magnitudes, and blend with human forecasts where available. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-10742-x
- SciPredict: natural-science outcome prediction with calibration audit (Sehwag, Lau, ... Furong Huang, Bing Liu (Scale AI / UMD et al.) 2026 (arXiv 12 Apr 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: 405 expert-built tasks from papers published after 31 Mar 2025, covering 33 subfields of physics, biology and chemistry.
