Formats are MCQ (40%, possibly multi-select), free-form (32%, graded against rubrics) and numeric ranges (28%). 15 LLMs answer zero-shot with web search disabled, with and without expert-curated background, and report confidence, difficulty and feasibility. PhD-level experts answer the same tasks. Construction cost $336k and 7,380 expert hours. | Result: Model accuracy was 14-26% against a human expert baseline of 20.2%. Gemini 3 Pro 25.3%, Claude Opus 4.5 23.0%, Sonnet 4.5 22.6%, GPT-5.2 20.6%, o3 17.9%. Chemistry: experts 8.8%, Opus 4.5 15.7%. Models stayed at about 20% accuracy whatever confidence or feasibility they reported. Experts ranged from about 5% on items they rated infeasible to about 80% on items they rated feasible. Expert background knowledge added about 3 points (1.2-5.8); background the models generated themselves usually hurt. Accuracy drops when MCQs are rewritten as free-form questions. | Limits: Mixed formats make the chance level unclear. Free-form items are graded by an LLM (Gemini 3 Pro, cross-checked with GPT-5.2). No fine-tuned or literature-tuned model and no agentic retrieval were tested. Tasks are single-paper outcomes, not ranked portfolios. | Relevance: This is the most direct 2026 test of frontier models, including Opus 4.5, in natural sciences with a strict post-cutoff split. Accuracy is roughly at expert level but low in absolute terms, and self-reported confidence carries no signal. An OptiLex critic must not use verbal confidence or 'feasibility' for gating. Humans' feasibility judgments were informative, which argues for a human-in-the-loop 'is this predictable without an experiment?' filter. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10718
- Query2Effect: fine-tuned small encoder predicts RCT effect sizes (Martinelli, ..., Legovini, Fraiberger (World Bank DIME et al.) 2026 (arXiv 28 May 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: About 72,000 natural-language queries are aligned with 74,826 standardized effect estimates from 7,354 RCTs (health, education, agriculture, and more). A two-step pipeline turns each query into a synthetic structured RCT representation, then a fine-tuned ModernBERT-large regresses the effect size and CI. Splits are grouped by RCT to prevent leakage, and there is a cross-sector out-of-domain test set. | Result: In-domain (health) MAE: GPT-5.2 prompted 0.2374, Gemini 2.5 Flash 0.3542, GPT-OSS-120B 0.4160. A constant predicting the mean effect scored 0.1946. The fine-tuned pipeline scored 0.1722. Out-of-domain: pipeline 0.1375 vs GPT-5.2 0.2089 (-34%). | Limits: The split is not temporal, so results may be in the pretraining data for the prompted LLMs, which still lost to the mean. Standardized RCT effects cluster tightly, so absolute error rewards shrinkage. The fine-tuned model beats the trivial baseline by only about 12%. No test of whether it ranks novel interventions correctly. | Relevance: Critical for design. A frontier LLM's out-of-the-box numeric effect forecasts were worse than a constant, consistent with the overestimation bias seen elsewhere. A roughly 400M-parameter encoder trained on outcome-labelled data wins, which is a data- and compute-efficient critic pattern that fits OptiLex's efficiency frontier. Every forecaster critic should be benchmarked against a base-rate or mean predictor. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29631
- DARPA SCORE: human replication forecasting baseline vs machine methods (Center for Open Science + 865 researchers (repliCATS, Replication Markets, Synthetic Markets, MACROSCORE, A+) 2026 (Nature collection + preprints)) [retrospective_prediction]: Claims sampled from 3,900 papers in 62 journals. Human forecasts came from structured group deliberation (repliCATS) and prediction markets (Replication Markets), made before replications were run. Three automated systems scored the same claims: bot-populated synthetic markets, SciBERT-based MACROSCORE, and A+. 164 claims were replicated. Earlier human-market baseline: Gordon et al. 2021 (PLoS ONE), 103 replications. | Result: 49% of 164 claims replicated, and replication effect sizes were less than half the original magnitude (Tyner et al., Nature). Human forecasting was 76% successful (repliCATS) and 78% (Replication Markets) by the best metric. 'None of the three [machine] methods were consistently effective'. Gordon 2021: markets 73% correct (75/103), surveys 66%, market-outcome r=0.581. Humans overestimated the replication rate: 60-63% predicted vs 49% actual. | Limits: The 76%/78% figures are 'best performing metric', which invites cherry-picking. The machine methods predate frontier LLMs (SciBERT-era and feature bots), so their failure is not evidence against current LLMs. I did not read the three Nature papers directly; figures come from the official COS release. | Relevance: This is the human bar a replicability or soundness critic must clear: about 73-78% accuracy, with an optimistic bias of its own. It also shows the literature (and LLMs trained on it) carries effect sizes inflated about 2x versus replications, which plausibly explains the direction of LLM overestimation. That link is my hypothesis, not tested. | https://www.cos.io/about/news/large-scale-collaboration-releases-new-findings-on-research-credibility
- COS Predicting Replicability Challenge (automated forecasters on unreleased replications) (Center for Open Science; 10-15 teams per round 2025-2026 (Round 1 results Nov 2025; R2 Mar 2026; R3 7 Aug 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: A public competition. Teams train on the FORRT replication database (300+ curated outcomes, over 3,000 in total) and submit confidence scores for held-out claims whose replication outcomes are unreleased. Rounds 1-2 draw on SCORE; Round 3 on the Replicability Project: Health Behavior. Scoring is by Brier score against a constant-0.5 baseline (.25). Methods may include LLMs and agents. | Result: Round 1 (132 claims, 10 teams): no team beat the .25 Brier of a constant. Round 2 (130 claims, 15 teams): all teams beat it. Top-20% vs bottom-20% claims replicated at about 78% vs 33%. Round 3 (41 claims, 15 teams): only 8/15 beat .25; top vs bottom 61% vs 29%; replication rate 46%. Calibration was worse in R3, possibly from anchoring on the earlier ~50% rate. A search snippet reported Round 2 median AUC .66 and accuracy .63; not confirmed on the COS page. | Limits: Small test sets (41-132 claims). Team methods and LLM use are not described in the blog posts. Results are unstable across rounds and domain shift (SCORE to health behavior). | Relevance: This is a truly prospective, leakage-free test of automated scientific critics, and the results are fragile. Gains in one round did not survive a shift in domain or base rate. An OptiLex critic needs base-rate recalibration per domain and a trivial-baseline check. | https://www.cos.io/blog/predicting-replicability-challenge-round-3-results
- BrainBench / BrainGPT: literature-tuned forecasters beat neuroscience experts (Luo, Rechardt, ..., Bradley C. Love (UCL et al.); plus Luo, Sun & Love (ICML-W 2024) 2024 (Nat Hum Behav, pub. 2025 vol 9)) [retrospective_prediction]: A forced choice between the real 2023 J. Neurosci. abstract and one with its results altered while background and methods are kept. The model picks the version with lower perplexity, and the perplexity gap serves as confidence. BrainGPT is Mistral-7B LoRA-tuned on 1.3B tokens of 2002-2022 neuroscience. A follow-up trained a 124M GPT-2 from scratch on the same 1.3B tokens. | Result: 15 LLMs averaged 81.4% vs 63.4% for 171 experts (66.2% for the top-20% self-rated experts). Base models beat chat models (t(5)=5.4, p=.002). BrainGPT: Mistral-7B 83% to 86% (arXiv v4). Perplexity-based confidence was calibrated for all LLMs. Accuracy collapses on results-only sentences, so models integrate the methods. zlib test found no memorization, and accuracy did not differ Jan vs Oct 2023. Item-difficulty Spearman: LLM-human 0.15 vs LLM-LLM 0.75 (complementary errors). The 124M from-scratch model reached expert level (needs a domain tokenizer). | Limits: Binary discrimination against altered abstracts is much easier than open prediction, and the altered results may be detectably inconsistent with the literature. That a 124M model matches experts suggests the task measures consistency with prior literature, which by construction disfavors genuinely surprising results. Pre-2024 models only. | Relevance: This shows cheap, calibrated, literature-tuned critics are possible (7B LoRA or even 124M), which is attractive for OptiLex's compute and energy goals. Use token-probability or perplexity confidence rather than verbal confidence, and pair with humans because errors are uncorrelated. Caveat: such a critic scores 'fits the literature', which is a novelty penalty. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-024-02046-9
- Fine-tuned outcome predictor for empirical AI research (in-domain comparator) (Jiaxin Wen, Chenglei Si, Chen Yueh-Han, He He, Shi Feng 2025 (arXiv 1 Jun 2025)) [retrospective_prediction]: Given two research ideas, predict which scores better on benchmarks. Fine-tuned GPT-4.1 plus a paper-retrieval agent limited to pre-June-2024 papers. Training used 6,000 pairs; testing used 1,585 human-verified pairs published after the cutoff. Stress tests covered superficial features (complexity, recency). | Result: 77% on the full test set, while off-the-shelf o3 'no better than random guessing' even with the same retrieval. NLP subset: 64.4% vs 48.9% for 5-expert majority votes. 63.6% on unpublished novel ideas, including AI-generated ones. | Limits: ML-only; pairwise comparisons, not magnitudes. Novel-idea test set is small. Pre-2026 models. | Relevance: This is the clearest evidence that prompting alone (even o3) fails and outcome-supervised fine-tuning is what makes a forecaster useful. The recipe likely transfers to other domains only where an outcome-labelled corpus exists (RCT registries, replication databases, clinical trials). | https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.00794
- CT Open: uncontaminated live clinical-trial outcome prediction (Jianyou Wang, ..., Leon Bergen (UCSD) 2026 (COLM 2026; arXiv 17 Apr 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: A live benchmark of questions about trials whose results were released after timestamped cutoffs (Winter 2025: 605 questions / 314 trials; Summer 2025: 857 / 240). An automated LLM web-search pipeline dates when results first appeared publicly. Compared: RAG and agentic LLMs vs tabular ML (random forest, XGBoost). | Result: Winter 2025 endpoint task: o3-mini+RAG 76.88 macro-F1 vs random forest 66.60. Summer 2025 comparative effect: o3-mini agent 69.81 macro-F1 (92.75% accuracy). The agent degraded on Winter 2025, at over $1,000 in tokens per evaluation. Decontamination was about 98% accurate: in manual review of 50 trials, 49 were clean and 1 was ambiguous; the Embase check found 0 pre-cutoff leaks. | Limits: Agentic gains are inconsistent between test windows. No human-expert baseline. The optimism bias found in older work was not observed here (older GPT-4o study: recall 0.931, specificity 0.214, but likely contaminated). | Relevance: The best template for a leakage-proof forecasting evaluation in a costly-execution domain: live, timestamped outcomes with automated decontamination. OptiLex's evaluation layer could adopt this 'live benchmark' design for any domain. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16742
- Calibrated abstention for LLM A/B-test winner prediction (Tyler Dooskin / Squoosh 2026 (arXiv 2 Jul 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: Gemini 3 Flash judges predict A/B-test winners from screenshots on 330 real conversion tests, pre-registered over six weeks. Multiple judges vote, and the vote margin gates abstention. | Result: Overall kappa=0.14; on statistically significant labels kappa=0.11 (CI includes 0). Vote-margin abstention: kappa=0.31 at 49% coverage. Judges agreed with each other at kappa 0.74-0.88 but with outcomes at about 0.2. Human experts: inter-rater kappa 0.53 but about 0 against outcomes. 44% of the agency's ground-truth labels came from non-significant tests. | Limits: Industry preprint, a single domain (marketing UX), and a non-frontier model. | Relevance: This lines up with OptiLex's pilot finding that the judge's novelty self-agreement was only 53%. Agreement between LLM judges measures shared priors, not validity, so ensemble consensus must never stand in for accuracy. Selective prediction (abstain unless the margin is large) is a cheap, measurable gain. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07517
- AutoCog on Centaur: in-silico theory discovery that generalizes to humans (Jagadish, Strittmatter, Jacoby, Schulz, Daw, Griffiths, Chandramouli 2026 (arXiv 7 Sep 2026); Centaur: Binz et al. arXiv 2410.20268) [retrospective_prediction]: Centaur is an LLM fine-tuned on Psych-101 (160 experiments, over 60,000 participants, over 10M choices). It serves as a synthetic participant inside a closed-loop LLM-agent theory-discovery system. The resulting theories are then tested on ten held-out human experiments. | Result: Theories developed only on Centaur's simulated data outperformed canonical theories on 10 held-out human experiments, 'rivaled only by theories found by running the same loop on people'. The authors argue theory arbitration demands less of a simulator than effect estimation does. The companion critique (Namazova et al. 2025, arXiv 2508.07887) finds Centaur's generative behavior 'systematically diverges from human data' despite strong predictive accuracy. | Limits: Only one domain (multi-attribute decisions). The abstract gives no effect sizes. The simulator is imperfect, and the domain is close to Centaur's training distribution. | Relevance: This is a key design idea for an engine that cannot execute. A cheap simulator may be good enough to arbitrate between competing hypotheses (a relative judgment) even when it is poor at absolute prediction. Use the forecaster to compare ideas pairwise, not to score them in isolation. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08003
- Intuitiveness stress test: counter-intuitive policy findings (Yanjie He 2026 (arXiv 12 Apr 2026, rev. 28 May 2026)) [benchmark_rediscovery]: 40 published policy-evaluation cases, coded obvious, ambiguous or counter-intuitive. 4 LLMs × 5 prompt strategies × 10 repetitions (8,000 trials), with a mixed-effects logistic model. | Result: Overall 79.6%, but two-sided directional questions only 69.3%. The chain-of-thought gain was large on obvious cases (+26.7pp) and attenuated on counter-intuitive ones (+13.8pp; interaction OR=0.278, p<0.001). Two-sided counter-intuitive accuracy: GPT-5.2 94.5%, Sonnet 4.5 73.5%, Opus 4.6 64.8%, GPT-4.1 43.2% (chance 33%). Majority voting helped little (69% to 71.9%), so errors are systematic. A fictitious 'analyst consensus' prompt dropped two-sided accuracy to 34.2%. How often a finding is cited did not predict accuracy (OR=1.08, p=0.84); the famous daycare-fines result got 39%. | Limits: Single author, 40 cases, and moderate annotator agreement on intuitiveness (Fleiss kappa=0.32). The findings are all published, so this tests reasoning or recall, not forecasting. | Relevance: Direct evidence that critics are pulled toward intuitive priors on exactly the surprising results a discovery engine wants. Voting cannot fix it, and framing injected by generators can sway critics. Critics should see ideas without persuasive framing, and the most capable reasoning model should be used for the critic. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10511
- Statistical realism vs treatment-effect accuracy (Zonghan Li & Feng Ji (Toronto) 2026 (arXiv v1 2 Apr, v3 27 Jul 2026)) [retrospective_prediction]: Synthetic respondents, each conditioned on a real participant profile, for 59,508 people in 62 countries across 11 climate interventions (ICPC data), replicated on 2 more cross-national datasets (20,785 participants). It measures whether simulations that match response distributions also recover treatment effects. | Result: Realism and treatment-effect accuracy were only weakly related (Spearman rho=0.10). Across model and prompt combinations the correlations ranged from -0.04 to 0.22. Optimizing for realism can worsen effect accuracy. Errors are larger for behavioral outcomes, where the LLMs 'extrapolate behavioral effects from attitudinal patterns'. | Limits: Uses small, older models (GPT-4o-mini, Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite, Claude 3 Haiku), so it may understate what frontier models can do. The mechanism (validating the wrong target) still applies. | Relevance: An evaluation layer must validate on realized effects, never on 'looks like real data'. Behavioral outcomes are the weak spot; for LLM training, the analogue is qualitative plausibility vs actual loss curves. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02458
- LLM replication of 156 scenario experiments (inflation and false positives) (Ziyan Cui, Ning Li, Huaikang Zhou 2025 (arXiv v3 Jun 2025; Nature Computational Science 2025)) [benchmark_rediscovery]: GPT-4, Claude 3.5 Sonnet and DeepSeek-V3 act as participants in 156 published psychology and management scenario experiments, and replication success is scored against the published results. | Result: Main effects replicated 73-81%, interactions 46-63%. LLM effect sizes (Fisher z) were about 2-3x the human ones. Where the original found a null, LLMs produced significant effects 68-83% of the time. Replication was lower on race, gender and ethics topics. | Limits: Published studies are likely in the training data. The benchmark is 'reproduce the paper', not forecasting. I did not verify the Nature Computational Science version directly (only arXiv). | Relevance: Quantifies the 'everything works' bias: a forecaster built on simulation will rarely predict nulls. OptiLex critics need an explicit null or base-rate prior. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.00128
- Predicting 276 economics field experiments (Yaoyu Chen, Yuheng Hu, Yingda Lu 2025 (arXiv 1 Apr / 21 May 2025)) [retrospective_prediction]: Claude 3 Opus extracts setups; GPT-3.5/4-Turbo/4o then choose among three options: the original conclusion, a reversed one and an unrelated one. Data: 1,261 conclusions from 276 field experiments in AER, QJE, JPE and REStud, 2000-2024. | Result: 78% conclusion accuracy (GPT-4o with CoT). Accuracy was higher on 2024 papers (after the Oct 2023 cutoff) than in any earlier year. The distribution is bimodal: 71% of conclusions at 90-100% accuracy, 18% at 0-10%, and this polarization grew with better models and CoT. Ethnicity, social norms and ethical dilemmas degrade accuracy. | Limits: A 3-way MCQ with an 'unrelated' distractor inflates accuracy. No human baseline. Small post-cutoff subset. | Relevance: The time-split again shows no leakage advantage. The bimodality matters most: forecasters are confidently wrong on a stable subset of findings, so a critic needs coverage-aware abstention, not averaged scores. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.01167
- Identifying non-replicable studies via LLM synthetic samples (Saynova, Hansson, Bruinsma, Fredén, Johansson (Chalmers) 2025 (arXiv 10 Mar 2025)) [retrospective_prediction]: Generate synthetic participant responses for previously replicated behavioral studies, estimate effect sizes, and classify each study as replicable or not. | Result: On 14 studies (9 replicated, 5 failed), F1 was: Mistral-7B 77%, GPT-4o 67%, Llama-3-8B 67%, Qwen-2-7B 55%. Low sampling temperature (low variance) biases the effect estimates. | Limits: n=14 is far too small for inference. No time-split and no human or market comparison. Pre-2025 models. | Relevance: Weak evidence. Mainly a warning that decoding temperature and variance settings change simulated effect sizes, so they must be fixed and reported in any OptiLex simulator-critic. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10671
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: SciPredict (405 post-Mar-2025 natural-science tasks): 15 frontier LLMs scored 14-26% (Gemini 3 Pro 25.3%, Claude Opus 4.5 23.0%, GPT-5.2 20.6%) vs PhD experts at 20.2%. Model accuracy stayed at about 20% regardless of self-reported confidence or feasibility, whereas expert accuracy rose from about 5% to about 80% with their feasibility ratings. => CORRECTED: SciPredict (Sehwag et al., arXiv 2604.10718, Apr 2026): the benchmark has 405 tasks in 33 subfields of physics, chemistry and biology, built from empirical studies published after 31 Mar 2025. The authors tested 15 LLMs (they call them 'SOTA'; the set includes small open models such as Llama 3.1 8B). Overall accuracy without background knowledge was about 14-26%. The main models scored: Gemini 3 Pro 25.27%, Claude Opus 4.5 23.05%, GPT-5.2 20.58%. The human baseline was domain-expert annotators, about 74-75% of them PhD holders, not all PhDs, at 20.25%. The paper states that model accuracy stays at about 20% regardless of self-reported confidence or feasibility, while expert accuracy runs from about 5% on questions they rated infeasible (1) to about 80% on questions they rated feasible (5). (caveat: 1) How answers were graded: no new lab work was done. Ground truth is outcomes already reported in the published papers. Multiple-choice answers (40% of tasks) are scored against the correct choice. Numeric answers (28%) count as correct if they fall inside an expert-defined range. Free-form answers (32%) are judged correct or wrong by an LLM judge with a fixed prompt, using expert-written rubrics. So about a third of the accuracy numbers depend on an LLM judge.
2) Condition: the headline numbers are for the no-background-knowledge (NBK) condition. Giving the models expert-curated background knowledge adds about 3 points (for example Gemini 3 Pro 27.33, Opus 4.5 27.33, GPT-5.2 22.80). The human baseline under that condition is 20.99.
3) Human baseline: the human experts are a separate cohort from the people who built the tasks. Each expert answered only questions in their own subdomain, so the human score comes from a subset of tasks rather than all 405 answered by each person. The cohort also leans toward biology (48.7% biology, 33.3% chemistry, 17.9% physics).
4) Uneven by domain: human chemistry accuracy is only 8.82%, against 23.15% for biology and 26.00% for physics. The ~20% human average hides large domain differences.
5) Calibration: 'about 20% regardless' is the paper's own summary, not a formal statistical test. The 5% to 80% range for experts is the gap between feasibility ratings of 1 and 5. The number of tasks in those extreme bins is not given, so those accuracies may rest on small counts.
6) Model framing: calling all 15 models 'frontier' overstates it. The set includes o3-mini, Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen 3 32B and DeepSeek v3. The frontier models sit near the top of the 14-26% range.
7) Status: v1 is a preprint and I found no peer review.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: On about 2,700 health RCTs split by RCT, prompted GPT-5.2 predicted standardized effect sizes with MAE 0.2374. That is worse than a constant mean-effect baseline (0.1946), which a fine-tuned ModernBERT-large pipeline beat only modestly (0.1722). Out-of-domain, the pipeline scored 0.1375 vs GPT-5.2's 0.2089. => CORRECTED: In Query2Effect (Martinelli et al., World Bank, arXiv 2605.29631, May 2026), the RCTs are development-economics impact evaluations, not clinical trials. The corpus has 13,804 health intervention-outcome estimates plus 4,227 non-health out-of-domain estimates, drawn from about 2,700 RCTs in total (health and non-health combined). The health data is split so no RCT appears in more than one split. On the most specific (level-0) in-domain health queries, measured by MAE on standardized effect sizes: prompted GPT-5.2 scored 0.2374, worse than a mean-effect baseline (0.1946). The Synthetic-RCT pipeline scored 0.1722; it has GPT-5.2 write a structured RCT description, which a fine-tuned ModernBERT-large then regresses on. A ModernBERT-large trained directly on the queries scored 0.1743. On the out-of-domain test (education, agriculture, social protection), the pipeline scored 0.1375, GPT-5.2 scored 0.2089 and the mean baseline scored 0.181. (caveat: 1) All MAE numbers in the claim match Tables 4 and 5. 2) "About 2,700 health RCTs" is wrong: the ~2,700 RCTs include the non-health out-of-domain set. Table 3 is confusingly labeled: its "RCTs" column (10,467 / 1,275 / 2,062, which sum to 13,804) actually counts estimates, with 4 queries per estimate at levels 0-3. 3) These are "health" RCTs from a curated development-economics corpus (World Bank authors), not clinical or medical trials. 4) The results cover only the most specific level-0 queries. Less specific queries are in Appendix F, which I did not check; the paper says the pipeline's advantage holds there. 5) The pipeline is not ModernBERT alone. GPT-5.2 first writes a synthetic RCT description, so the LLM is part of the winning system. Most of the in-domain gain over the mean baseline (0.1946 to 0.1743) comes from fine-tuning alone; the structured step adds little in-domain (0.1743 to 0.1722) but a lot out-of-domain (0.1773 to 0.1375). 6) Out-of-domain, prompted GPT-5.2 (0.2089) also loses to the mean baseline (0.181), which the claim leaves out. 7) Other baselines exist: Gemini 2.5 Flash and GPT-OSS 120B, both prompted and both worse than GPT-5.2 per the paper; a retrieval-lookup baseline; and a gold-RCT-description upper bound. 8) Scoring is automatic, against effect sizes the real RCTs reported. No LLM judge or human rating is involved. This is prediction of past published results, so LLM memorization or contamination is possible; I did not check whether the paper addresses it. Seeds and variance were not reported in what I read. 9) The abstract's "-27% up to -71%" error reduction versus prompted LLMs covers several settings and metrics, not this single comparison.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In the preprint version (476 effects, 70 US survey experiments), GPT-4-derived effects correlated r=0.85 (adj 0.91) with realized effects. The correlation was r=0.90 on studies unpublished before GPT-4's cutoff vs 0.74 on published ones, against r=0.79 for 2,659 lay forecasters. Raw predictions overestimated effects: RMSE 10.9pp vs 8.4pp, and a 0.56 rescaling brought it to 5.3pp. In megastudies with expert forecasts, LLM r=0.37 vs experts r=0.25, and field experiments reached only r=0.27. The Nature version reports 469 effects, 119,330 participants and 15 megastudies with 606 effects. (caveat: 1) Every correlation and RMSE figure comes from the Aug 2024 preprint. The Nature full text is paywalled; only its abstract could be checked. The abstract gives no r values, so the published numbers may differ slightly because the dataset changed (469 vs 476 effects, 119,330 vs 105,165 participants). The Nature abstract words the result more cautiously: accuracy "similar to pooled human forecasts" and megastudy correlations "comparable to" experts. The preprint said GPT-4 "surpassed" lay forecasters and "matched or surpassed" experts. 2) The preprint megastudy archive was smaller: 9 megastudies, 346 effects, 1.8M participants. Nature expands this to 15 megastudies with 606 effects, so the 0.37/0.25/0.27 figures belong to the 9-study preprint set. 3) The expert comparison uses only the 6 megastudies that had expert forecasts. The field r=0.27 covers all field megastudies in the preprint archive, not only those 6. In the preprint, survey megastudies gave r=0.47, and treatments that were not text-based gave r=0.24. 4) Scoring was done against the original human-subject experimental results (ground truth from real data), not by an LLM judge. Predictions come from GPT-4 simulating survey respondents, pooled across many prompts. Effects are on each study's original scale in percentage points, not standardized. 5) The 0.56 rescaling factor was estimated on the same archive it was applied to, so the 5.3pp RMSE is in-sample. 6) The domain is narrow: US, text-based survey experiments with self-reported outcomes (TESS plus a replication archive). This limits how far it generalizes to forecasting experiments outside social science. 7) "Unpublished" means the study was not published or posted as a preprint before GPT-4's training cutoff.)
Gaps:
  - No study stratifies forecasting accuracy by how novel a hypothesis is, measured before the result is known (for example, embedding distance from prior literature or expert-rated surprise), against realized outcomes. So nobody knows whether LLM critics impose a systematic 'novelty tax' on the ideas an engine most wants. The indirect evidence (lower accuracy on counter-intuitive results, megastudies and field experiments; literature-consistency tasks solved by a 124M model) suggests they do.
  - Outside ML, nobody has tested forecasters on AI-generated hypotheses that were later executed. Wen et al. 2025 did this only for ML (63.6%). No social-science or wet-lab study scores LLM-generated ideas with a forecaster and then runs them.
  - Hewitt/Ashokkumar-style archives have not been evaluated on 2026 frontier models (Opus 4.5+/GPT-5.x) with experiments run after those models' cutoffs. The Nature paper's headline model is GPT-4, and SciPredict is the only 2026-frontier time-split, in natural sciences only.
  - The 0.56 effect-size shrinkage factor was estimated on one archive and one model. Nobody has tested whether a single calibration map transfers across domains, models or outcome types (attitudinal vs behavioral).
  - Cross-domain transfer of literature-tuned forecasters is untested. BrainGPT is neuroscience only, and Query2Effect's out-of-domain test stays within development-economics RCTs. No 'universal outcome critic' trained on pooled outcome corpora (RCT registries + replication databases + clinical trials) has been built.
  - No work has turned token-probability confidence, which was calibrated in BrainBench, into a selective-prediction critic for open-ended scientific outcomes. SciPredict tested only verbal confidence; the A/B-test paper used only vote margins.
  - Using forecasters as reward models for idea generators (a Goodhart risk: optimizing ideas to look predictable or consistent with the literature) has never been tested outside ML.
  - Automated decontamination audits exist only for clinical trials (CT Open). Most forecasting benchmarks rely on publication-date filters, and no study quantifies preprint or conference leakage for 2026 models with multi-year cutoffs.
  - No head-to-head exists between an LLM critic and prospective human prediction markets on the same unreleased claims. COS challenge teams and SCORE human markets used different claim sets or rounds, and the one hybrid-market study (arXiv 2605.27394) uses non-LLM bots on 30 claims.
Surprises:
  - A constant beats the frontier: in Query2Effect, predicting the mean RCT effect (MAE 0.1946) beat prompted GPT-5.2 (0.2374). A fine-tuned ModernBERT-large was only about 12% better than the constant (https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29631).
  - Consensus is not validity. In 330 pre-registered A/B tests, LLM judges agreed with each other at kappa 0.74-0.88 but with outcomes at about 0.2, and human experts agreed with each other at 0.53 but with outcomes at about 0. This mirrors OptiLex's pilot, where the judge agreed with itself on novelty only 53% of the time (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07517).
  - Leakage does not seem to drive accuracy, and may even run the other way. Unpublished studies were predicted better (r=0.90) than published ones (0.74) in Hewitt et al., and 2024 field experiments were predicted better than older ones in Chen et al. The subset where GPT-4 could not guess the author was lower (r=0.69), so there are composition confounds.
  - The bias runs in one direction everywhere: GPT-4 effects need about 0.56x shrinkage; LLM replications inflate Fisher z 2-3x and turn published nulls into significant effects 68-83% of the time; and GPT-4o called 93% of clinical trials successes against 21% specificity. SCORE shows the published literature itself is inflated about 2x versus replications (replicated effects less than half the original), so LLMs may be inheriting publication bias. This link is untested.
  - Smaller and base models do surprisingly well. A 124M GPT-2 trained on 1.3B neuroscience tokens matched experts on BrainBench, and base models beat their chat or instruct versions (t(5)=5.4). Off-the-shelf o3 was at chance on AI-research outcome prediction while fine-tuned GPT-4.1 hit 77%.
  - Role-play hurts. On 399 Upworthy headline A/B tests, a no-persona prompt reached Kendall tau=0.361 vs 0.084 for a 10-persona simulated panel (https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25010). Meanwhile, matching human response distributions was essentially uncorrelated with treatment-effect accuracy (rho=0.10; Li & Ji 2026).
  - Humans know when they don't know; models don't. In SciPredict, expert accuracy rose from about 5% to about 80% with their own feasibility ratings, while model accuracy stayed at about 20% at every confidence level.
  - Errors are systematic, not noise. Majority voting barely moved counter-intuitive accuracy (69% to 71.9%). A fabricated 'analyst consensus' in the prompt dropped two-sided accuracy to 34.2%, and the heavily cited daycare-fines finding was answered correctly only 39% of the time.
  - The first automated replicability challenge round was sobering: all 10 teams scored worse than predicting 0.5 for every claim (Brier .25). After improving in Round 2, only 8/15 teams beat the constant again in Round 3 once the domain shifted to health behavior.

## math_discovery_formal_verification
- FrontierMath Erdős (Epoch AI): Lean-verified benchmark of 68 significant open Erdős problems (Tom Adamczewski, Greg Burnham (Epoch AI); problems curated by Thomas Bloom; paper arXiv 2609.25050 2026 (Sep 1)) [executed_computationally]: Bloom hand-picked 68 open Erdős problems he judged significant and difficult (about 10% of the open list). 50 statements come from Google's Formal Conjectures and 18 were formalized by AI. Scoring uses the Lean FRO's Comparator, a checker built to resist cheating submissions. Each model gets one attempt per problem with a $300 budget, 72 hours, an offline paper corpus and no internet. A problem counts only if a Lean proof or disproof verifies. | Result: Benchmark run: a pre-release GPT-6 Astra solved 2 of 68 (3%). It disproved #74 for $222 and proved #126 for $172, about 10 hours each. GPT-5.6 Sol, GPT-5.5, Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Fable 5 all scored 0%. Outside the protocol, Astra solved 5 of 68 at least once (#1, #74, #126, #548, #571) using over $220,000 of compute. The benchmark run itself cost about $20,000. The other problems got 269 extra attempts with none solved. Bloom estimates only 3-5 Erdős problems of this caliber had been solved by AI as of August 2026. The unit-distance disproof is an 18-page natural-language proof; its Lean formalization is 1.2 million lines. | Limits: Only one attempt per model and problem. Requiring formalization adds a separate burden, and results that depend on deep theory missing from Mathlib are penalized. Bloom's curation is subjective. Contamination will grow over time. Erdős problems cover only part of mathematics. | Relevance: This is the cleanest template for OptiLex's evaluation layer: a curated list of significant targets, a verifier that does not depend on an LLM and resists cheating, a fixed dollar budget, and a contamination protocol. Current Claude models score 0% here, so do not assume an Opus-based harness will match GPT-6-class results on hard, verified targets. | https://epoch.ai/latest/announcing-frontiermath-erdos
- AlphaProof Nexus: LLM plus Lean formal proof search on open problems (Tsoukalas, Kovsharov, Shirobokov, ..., Balog, Kohli, Chaudhuri (Google DeepMind) 2026 (May; v2 Jun 8)) [executed_computationally]: Input is a Lean sketch in which the target theorem's proof is a sorry. Basic agent (A): independent prover subagents running Gemini 3.1 Pro in 'Ralph loops', each refining the sketch and re-checking it with the Lean compiler after every turn, using compiler errors as the critique. Full agent (D): adds an AlphaEvolve-style population database, LLM-critic tournaments that assign Elo scores, AlphaProof as a tool for subgoals, a global cache of solved subgoals, and a validator that blocks unsafe edits to the statement. | Result: Solved 9 of 353 formalized open Erdős problems at a few hundred dollars per problem. Proved 44 of 492 OEIS conjectures that Gemini had autoformalized. In post-hoc analysis the basic agent A solved all 9, but at higher cost on the hardest ones. Agent D was 2-5x cheaper on #138 and #125 and about half as cost-efficient on the rest. Codex (GPT-5.5) solved 7 of 9. Claude Code (Opus 4.7) solved none. Standalone AlphaProof and Gemini Flash-class agents also solved none. AlphaProof costs about 27.5 TPU-hours ($60) per problem. Failure modes: the agent pushed the core difficulty into a single sorry'd helper lemma, and relied on sorry'd lemmas it claimed came from the literature that turned out to be hallucinated. It also caught misformalized density definitions in #125 and #741. | Limits: Covers only the 353 problems already formalized in Formal Conjectures. At least 2 of the 9 (#152, #846) have comparable prior literature according to Tao's wiki. Successes cluster in combinatorics, number theory and optimization, where Mathlib is mature. Finding tractable problems was itself costly and is not included in the reported costs. | Relevance: With a hard verifier, a simple generate, check and fix loop captured every success. Diversity and evolution machinery paid off only on the hardest problems. The choice of base model mattered far more than the harness (0/9 versus 7/9). OptiLex needs an executable verifier for LLM-efficiency claims (for example a fixed-compute training run) more than it needs an elaborate generator swarm, and should benchmark Opus 5.5 inside the harness early. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22763
- Aletheia Erdős case study: semi-autonomous run on 700 'Open' problems (Tony Feng, Trieu Trinh, Garrett Bingham, ..., Quoc V. Le, Thang Luong (Google DeepMind and collaborators) 2026 (Jan 29; v3 Feb 5)) [expert_human_rating]: Aletheia (a Gemini Deep Think agent on the Nov 2025 Gemini 3 base) was run on every problem marked Open in Bloom's database from Dec 2-9, 2025. Its built-in natural-language verifier filtered the outputs. Mostly non-expert humans then triaged the candidates, and domain experts checked correctness and novelty, consulting outside experts where novelty was unclear. | Result: Funnel: 700 problems, 212 responses flagged by the verifier, 200 graded definitively. Of those 200, 137 (68.5%) were fundamentally flawed, 63 (31.5%) technically correct, and 13 (6.5%) meaningfully correct. Split of the 13 in v3: 4 novel, namely 652 and 1051 (autonomous) and 654 and 1040 (partial). The other 9 were already known: 397, 659, 935 and 1089 were independent rediscoveries, and 333, 591, 705, 992 and 1105 were literature identifications. v1 reported 5 novel and 8 known; #935 moved from 'partial AI solution' to 'independent rediscovery'. On difficulty: none of the four 'rises to the level of a research paper'. Only 1051 is described as slightly non-trivial. | Limits: Solutions were rewritten by humans before publication. The authors call the novelty classification an upper bound, and they think even the remaining novel results were plausibly found by humans before but never published. The base model was weaker than the later Jan 2026 model. Human grading involves some subjectivity. | Relevance: Gives base rates for an OptiLex funnel with an LLM verifier: about 6.5% of verifier-passed outputs are meaningfully correct. Rediscoveries outnumber new results 9 to 4. The dominant failure was answering a misread version of the problem: 50 of 63 technically correct answers missed the intended question. This argues for a spec-fidelity critic and a prior-art check ahead of any novelty scoring. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22401
- Unit distance conjecture disproof (Erdős #90) by an internal OpenAI model (The OpenAI internal model; human-verified write-up and remarks by Alon, Bloom, Gowers, Litt, Sawin, Shankar, Tsimerman, Wang and Wood 2026 (May 20)) [expert_human_rating]: The model produced the proof 'in one shot', and humans then refined the exposition with Codex. It shows ν(n) ≥ n^(1+δ) using layers of an infinite Golod-Shafarevich class-field tower of CM fields with bounded root discriminant and a split prime, together with a pigeonhole step following an idea recorded by Ellenberg and Venkatesh. The model's chain of thought mostly tried to build a counterexample rather than prove the widely believed upper bound. | Result: Disproves an Erdős conjecture from 1946 carrying a $500 prize, called 'possibly the best known (and simplest to explain) problem in combinatorial geometry'. The transfer runs from algebraic number theory (class field towers) to discrete geometry. Bloom calls it 'a natural, albeit highly non-trivial, generalisation' of Erdős's lattice construction. Per Quanta, four mathematicians including Bloom later used related techniques to disprove a version of a sum-product conjecture, so the technique has already been reused. | Limits: Compute and number of attempts are not disclosed. It is a counterexample construction and gives no new structural geometric insight, which Bloom notes. The Lean formalization is 1.2 million lines, and LeanMarathon's attempt to formalize it stalled on library gaps. | Relevance: The canonical cross-subfield transfer. Two lessons for OptiLex generators: try the direction experts dismissed (disprove rather than prove), and import machinery from distant fields. It also shows formal verifiers are weakest on exactly these transfers, because the deep results they rely on are not yet formalized. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20695
- Tao/community ledger 'AI contributions to Erdős problems' (frozen at Jun 30, 2026) (Terence Tao and erdosproblems contributors 2026) [expert_human_rating]: The community classifies each AI contribution by what literature exists and how much humans were involved. 1(a) AI standalone: no comparable literature known. 1(b) AI alongside literature: comparable literature found afterwards. 1(c) AI building on literature known beforehand. 1(d) significant human collaboration. 2(a) literature search. 2(b) formalization. | Result: My tally of unique problems with a full solution: 1(a) 18; 1(c) 18; 1(d) 36; 1(b) 14 (20 of 25 1(b) entries are rated 'similar' to the prior literature); 2(a) literature search located an existing full solution for 37. The union of 1(b) and 2(a) is 48 problems. That gives roughly 18 new standalone solutions against 48 problems where AI rediscovered or located an old solution, about 1:2.7. Among full solutions produced autonomously (1a, 1b, 1c), 14 of 50 (28%) later turned out to be redundant with existing literature. 1(a) also records 10 incorrect claims and 9 unverified ones. | Limits: The page states it is 'not a benchmark'. It carries a selection-bias warning, categories overlap, and the data are provisional. The counts are my own parse of the Jun 30 snapshot and should be recounted. | Relevance: The best available realized-outcome denominator for how often AI 'discoveries' turn out to be rediscoveries. OptiLex should expect prior art for the majority of plausible-looking results unless deduplication against the literature is built in. | https://github.com/teorth/erdosproblems/wiki/AI-contributions-to-Erd%C5%91s-problems
- LeanMarathon: multi-agent long-horizon autoformalization of AI-found research papers (Yuanhe Zhang, Yuekai Sun, Taiji Suzuki, Jason D. Lee, Fanghui Liu 2026 (Jun 3)) [executed_computationally]: An evolving 'blueprint' Lean file serves as proof skeleton, natural-language proof graph and system of record. Four contract-scoped agents construct, audit, prove and repair it. A two-stage orchestrator first stabilizes target fidelity through adversarial review (a Target-Reviewer compares the canonical statement, the LaTeX statement and the Lean type). It then discharges the proof DAG from the leaves up in parallel rounds gated by CI. Model: GPT-5.5-xhigh in Codex. | Result: Across 3 autonomous runs covering Erdős #1051, #1196, #164 and #1217, all 7 target theorems were formalized with no sorry, proving 258 lemmas. Costs: $257 (308M tokens, about 19 hours critical path), $189 (245M, about 11.5 hours) and $624 (796M, about 40.7 hours). The Aristotle baseline proved 0 of 3 and 0 of 1 targets after more than 40 and 24 hours. In one run, 26 of 32 filed issues were statement drift. Formalizing the unit-distance disproof stalled on Mathlib gaps. | Limits: Covers two papers, formalized after the fact from proofs already known, with a single base model. Figures come from the arXiv HTML via a fetch summary and should be spot-checked. | Relevance: Design lesson for OptiLex's fix loop: most repair work went into spec drift, not hard steps. Audit fidelity to the target before investing in proving or optimizing. Roughly $200-600 per paper is a realistic cost for machine-checking one result. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05400
- OpenAI 'Ten Advances in Mathematics and Theoretical Computer Science' with Lean formalizations (OpenAI (an internal model that Quanta names 'Astra') 2026 (Aug 1; updated Aug 6)) [executed_computationally; NOT verified from primary]: One internal model produced ten results. An AI-written companion document, 'reasoning walkthroughs', reconstructs from the chains of thought how each proof came together. The accompanying README says Lean 4 (4.32.0) plus mathlib formalizations exist for all ten, checkable with Comparator. | Result: The results resolve Erdős #183 (multicolor triangle Ramsey numbers, R_k(3) = k^Θ(k)) and #146 and #180 (counterexamples to the compactness and degeneracy conjectures). They also include an explicit non-sofic group, a counterexample to Connes's rigidity conjecture, a sharp Ehrhart volume bound (n+1)^n/n!, and an n^(1/400) hardness result for the closest vector problem, among others. Cross-field transfers: the Ehrhart bound uses Berman-Berndtsson real Monge-Ampère toric potentials and Bergman kernels (complex geometry applied to lattice and convex geometry). The non-sofic group uses the binary Leavitt algebra and Thompson's group V. | Limits: No denominators are disclosed: attempts, compute, failures and human steering are all unknown. Peer review and expert significance assessment are pending. I read the README from a local copy and could not confirm the repository URL. | Relevance: Shows formal verification now reaches research-paper scale when the library supports the field. The missing denominator is the cautionary lesson: OptiLex should log every attempt so it can report yield per dollar. | https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/ten-proofs-oai.pdf
- Aletheia: Generator-Verifier-Reviser agent and autonomy × significance levels (Tony Feng, Trieu H. Trinh, ..., Demis Hassabis, Koray Kavukcuoglu, Quoc V. Le, Thang Luong (Google DeepMind) 2026 (Feb 10; rev Mar 6)) [expert_human_rating]: Three subagents built on Gemini Deep Think (Generator, Verifier, Reviser) loop until the verifier approves or an attempt limit is reached. The final output is decoupled from the model's thinking tokens so it can spot its own flaws. The agent uses Google Search and browsing tools and explicitly abstains when it has no solution. Results are reported on two axes: autonomy (H, C or A) and significance (Level 0-4), with Human-AI interaction cards. | Result: IMO-ProofBench Advanced: 93% (95% with the Feb 2026 base model), and 96% conditional accuracy on the 29 of 30 problems it answered. FutureMath Basic: answered fewer than 60%, with conditional accuracy above 82%. Ablation: Deep Think alone, at about twice the compute, solved 8 of the 13 Erdős problems Aletheia solved. The Jan 2026 Deep Think needed about 100x less compute for equivalent IMO-ProofBench performance. Ratings: Erdős 652, 654, 935 and 1040 are Level A0 (exercise-level), 1051 is A1, and the eigenweights paper is fully autonomous at Level A2 (publishable). | Limits: The benchmarks are internal and human-graded. Citation hallucinations moved from fictitious papers to misquoting real ones. The authors say success cases are rare and that most research prompts saw no autonomous progress. | Relevance: With an LLM critic, abstention plus a separate verifier is the main lever for precision. The autonomy × significance card is a ready-made schema for OptiLex's evaluation layer and would keep hype out of results reporting. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10177
- FirstProof: sealed research-level problems as a contamination-free test (Aletheia results) (Feng, Jung, Kim, Pagano, Gukov, ..., Luong (Aletheia); benchmark by Abouzaid, Blumberg, Hairer, Kileel, Kolda, Nelson, Spielman, Srivastava, Ward, Weinberger, Williams (arXiv 2602.05192) 2026 (Feb)) [expert_human_rating]: Ten research 'lemmas' that arose naturally in the authors' own work, with solutions known but not online. The problems were released Feb 5, 2026 and solutions published Feb 13. Aletheia received the unmodified LaTeX statements and ran twice (two base models). A pre-registered verification and extraction prompt filtered the outputs, and experts graded the result as best of 2. | Result: Aletheia solved 6 of 10 by majority expert opinion (P2, P5, P7, P8, P9, P10); P8 was rated correct by only 5 of 7 experts. It abstained on the other 4. One of ten (P7) was publication-grade. Publicly available models solved 2 of 10 out of the box (P9, P10). OpenAI claimed 6 solutions as 'highly likely'; P2 proved flawed, and OpenAI acknowledged undisclosed human guidance. Every Aletheia solve cost more inference than Erdős-1051, and P7 cost about 10x more. | Limits: Only 10 problems. The two runs disagreed on P5, P7 and P8, so variance between runs is high. Expert panels were small. | Relevance: A protocol OptiLex can reuse: collect sealed problems with known answers from practitioners, for example unpublished LLM-efficiency results from collaborators, to measure the generator without contamination or LLM judging. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21201
- OpenAI 'Early science acceleration experiments with GPT-5' and the Oct 2025 '10 Erdős problems' episode (Bubeck, Coester, Eldan, Gowers, Lee, Lupsasca, Sawhney, Scherrer, Sellke, Spears, Unutmaz, Weil, Yin, Zhivotovskiy 2025 (Oct episode; paper Nov 20)) [expert_human_rating; NOT verified from primary]: Problem-agnostic prompts (a link or screenshot plus an instruction to search for a solution despite the 'open' label) were run in parallel through the API, with GPT-5 filtering its own outputs. Expert humans then verified results and guided the new ones. | Result: GPT-5 located published solutions to 10 problems not previously marked as known (223, 339, 494, 515, 621, 822, 883 part 2, 903, 1043, 1079). It also reported partial literature for 10 more. #1043's solution was in a German 1959 Pommerenke paper. Its only new Erdős result was #848, settled with a key GPT-5 step 'sandwiched between two layers of human mathematics'. The paper claims four new math results in total. Cautionary case: on clique-avoiding codes, GPT-5 reproduced Alon's 2024 proof without attribution. The Oct 19, 2025 claim by Kevin Weil (GPT-5 'found solutions to 10 (!) previously unsolved Erdős problems') was called 'a dramatic misrepresentation' by Bloom. | Limits: Case studies with no denominators. The tweet and Bloom's reply come from TechCrunch, not the original posts, which I did not fetch. | Relevance: Literature search was the highest-yield component: 10 literature finds against 1 new result. OptiLex's knowledge-base and prior-art stage should be a first-class generator of value. Generated ideas need a separate attribution check, because the same model that finds references also reproduces known proofs without citing them. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16072
- Harmonic Aristotle: informal lemma pipeline plus Lean search (Tudor Achim et al. (Harmonic) 2025 (Oct 1; rev Oct 10)) [executed_computationally]: Lean proof search plus lemma-based informal reasoning. The system writes an informal proof, restructures it into lemmas, autoformalizes them and sends them to the Lean REPL. It feeds back errors, keeps proven lemmas and revises unproven ones. It adds an AlphaGeometry-style geometry solver and test-time training that lets lemmas from different proof sketches inform each other. | Result: Formal solutions to 5 of 6 IMO 2025 problems. It became the default certifier for AI-found Erdős proofs, for example #205, #457 and #728 paired with GPT-5.2. However, its Erdős #124 'solution' proved a weakened variant that omitted the gcd constraint, and the original problem remains open (per the 2607.07779 survey). In the LeanMarathon comparison it proved 0 of 3 and 0 of 1 research targets. | Limits: Focused on competition math. The compiler cannot detect misformalization. It is weaker on long-horizon research formalization. | Relevance: Its lemma-level fix loop (keep what is proven, revise what is not) maps directly onto OptiLex's fixer. The #124 case shows a verified wrong specification is the main way a hard verifier gets fooled. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01346
- AlphaProof (Nature 2025): RL prover with test-time RL (Hubert et al. (Google DeepMind) 2025 (Nov 12)) [executed_computationally]: AlphaZero-style reinforcement learning on millions of autoformalized problems. For the hardest problems it runs test-time RL, generating and learning from millions of problem variants at inference time. | Result: At IMO 2024 it solved 3 of 5 non-geometry problems, including the hardest. Combined with AlphaGeometry 2 the score was silver-equivalent (28/42), using multi-day computation. By 2026, standalone AlphaProof solved none of the 9 Erdős problems that AlphaProof Nexus solved, and was used only as a subgoal tool costing about $60 per problem. | Limits: Very compute-intensive and specialized. The Nexus paper reports a shift toward simple agentic loops around frontier LLMs. | Relevance: Specialized trained verifier-search systems become tools inside LLM loops rather than the engine. OptiLex should not try to train a bespoke idea model. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-09833-y
- 'From Solvers to Research' survey (with Tao) (Eric Jiang, Xiao Liang, ..., Terence Tao, Wei Wang 2026 (Jul 8)) [none]: A position paper and review of datasets, autoformalization, proof synthesis and AI contributions to Erdős problems. | Result: MiniF2F is saturated (Seed-Prover 99.6%). The survey states 'most successful full solutions involve problems where the key insight, once found, leads to a relatively short proof'. It stresses that compiling a formal statement does not ensure it is semantically correct (the Aristotle #124 case) and flags selection bias in reported successes. | Limits: A review, not new evidence. | Relevance: Independently confirms that AI math successes skew toward problems with short proofs and that formal specifications can be wrong while still compiling. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07779
- OpenAI 'Short proofs' papers (internal-model Erdős solutions) (Boris Alexeev, Moe Putterman, Mehtaab Sawhney, Mark Sellke, Gregory Valiant 2026 (Mar 31 and Apr 8; also arXiv 2603.29961)) [expert_human_rating]: Humans curated and verified proofs that an internal OpenAI model produced for questions posed by Erdős. | Result: Eight Erdős questions in total, 3 in 2603.29961 and 5 in 2604.06609. Topics include ordinary lines, exponential sums, K4-free 4-critical graphs and a 'fewnomial' Erdős-Turán discrepancy counterexample. The authors state each proof is 'due to an internal model'. Both titles frame the results as 'short proofs'. | Limits: No denominators, compute or failure logs are disclosed. | Relevance: Typical of 2026 AI mathematics output: many short, correct, modestly significant results. This is consistent with a skew toward low-hanging fruit. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06609
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Aletheia Erdős funnel: 700 'Open' problems, 212 verifier-flagged, 200 graded. Of the 200, 137 (68.5%) were fundamentally flawed, 63 (31.5%) technically correct and 13 (6.5%) meaningfully correct. The 13 split into 4 novel and 9 already in the literature (4 independent rediscoveries plus 5 literature identifications) in v3; v1 said 5 novel and 8 known because #935 was later reclassified. The arXiv abstract metadata may still show the v1 '5/8' figures. (caveat: 1) The funnel was graded by humans, not by execution or Lean. Aletheia's own natural-language verifier flagged 212 of 700 as potentially correct. Human mathematicians then filtered these to 27 candidates, and domain experts vetted them, consulting outside experts. 12 of the 212 were marked ambiguous, so 200 is the number definitively graded. The paper says informal grading was 'slightly subjective': for example, 4 of 5 mathematicians judged the original Erdős-1051 output correct. 2) Only 2 of the 4 'novel' results are full autonomous resolutions (Erdős-652 and 1051). The other two (654 and 1040) are partial solutions, each answering one sub-question of a multi-part problem. The authors call none of the four individually significant. 3) The claim's word 'Open' means marked 'Open' in Bloom's database. The paper concludes that these problems were open 'through obscurity rather than difficulty'. 4) Aletheia is an agent built on Gemini Deep Think, run on the then-700 open problems from December 2 to 9, 2025. 5) The versions: v1 is 29 Jan 2026, v2 is 2 Feb, v3 is 5 Feb. The count of novel solutions was once as high as 9 before the literature checks. 6) The arXiv abs-page comment for v3 says the 935 reclassification brings autonomous solutions 'down to 5', while the v3 body says 4. That comment looks like an off-by-one error. It also means both the abs-page abstract and the comment disagree with the v3 text, so cite the v3 body.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In AlphaProof Nexus, a basic agent that alternates LLM generation with Lean compiler checking solved all 9 Erdős problems that the full evolutionary, Elo and AlphaProof agent solved (9 of 353). Codex (GPT-5.5) solved 7 of 9; Claude Code (Opus 4.7) solved 0 of 9. (caveat: Every "solved" here was checked by the Lean compiler (no sorry, no new axioms), and experts checked by hand that the formal statements matched the original problems. The claim leaves out several points. (1) The basic agent was not run on all 353 problems. It was only re-run on the 9 that the full agent (agent D) had already solved, so "replicated 9/9" is a post-hoc check on problems already known to be solvable, not an independent 9/353. It also cost more than agent D on the harder ones. (2) Both Nexus agents use Gemini 3.1 Pro, with 3.0 Flash as a helper. (3) Codex and Claude Code were also only tested on those 9 problems, not all 353. Codex ran at extra-high reasoning effort in goal mode with a 12-hour limit per problem. Claude Code (Opus 4.7) ran at maximum effort in goal mode, and I found no time limit stated. (4) Claude Code's 0/9 was not a timeout or a failed search. It gave up and declared the problems unsolvable in the session, and kept doing so even after the prompt was changed to encourage it to persist. That makes it a finding about agent behaviour rather than about proving ability. (5) The authors note that neither commercial tool was built for formal proof search. (6) The paper also reports 44/492 OEIS conjectures proved and a per-problem cost of "a few hundred dollars". Several details (the Gemini versions, the missing Claude Code time limit, the post-hoc nature of the basic-agent result) come from the HTML full text through an automated summary, not from a verbatim read of the PDF. Confirm them against Section/Table text before quoting.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: FrontierMath Erdős (68 significant open problems, Lean-verified, $300 and 72 hours per attempt): a pre-release GPT-6 Astra scored 2/68 (3%); GPT-5.6 Sol, GPT-5.5, Claude Fable 5.1 and Claude Fable 5 scored 0%. Non-benchmark attempts reached 5/68 using over $220,000 of compute. The unit-distance proof is 18 pages in natural language and 1.2 million lines in Lean. (caveat: All figures match the primary source. Things the claim leaves out: (1) The 5/68 counts every attempt, including the 2 benchmark solutions (#74, #126), so it is 3 new ones (#1 disproved, #548 and #571 proved). Only GPT-6 Astra got these extra attempts, and they used larger budgets and different agent setups. Epoch says plainly these "are not a FrontierMath Erdős score", and Astra's official score stays at 3%. The $220k covers all attempts; the benchmark run alone cost about $20k. (2) The 68 problems were open as of August 2026. 50 were already formalized in Lean through Google's Formal Conjectures project and 18 were newly formalized by AI. Grading is automatic: a Lean proof checker verifies each proof, with no LLM or human judge. (3) Each model got one attempt per problem, and the two Astra solves each cost about $172–222 and 10 hours. (4) The unit-distance proof (problem 90) is not one of the benchmark's solves. Epoch cites it as an earlier result from an OpenAI model, used to show how much longer Lean formalization makes a proof (18 pages vs 1.2M lines, done by a separate later effort). Don't present it as a FrontierMath Erdős result. (5) The source doesn't give scaffold or setting details for the models that scored 0%.)
Gaps:
  - No one has ablated access to literature search or a knowledge base in a discovery agent and measured the effect on the rediscovery rate and the novel-result yield. Aletheia had search and still produced 9 known results for every 4 new. This is exactly OptiLex's planned knowledge-base A/B test, and it is unpublished for math.
  - No verifier for empirical ML-efficiency claims plays the role Lean plays in mathematics, meaning cheap, hard to game, and checked for fidelity to the specification. The math results suggest that verifier, not generator diversity, is the binding constraint. Candidates include a fixed-compute, fixed-data training harness with a locked evaluation.
  - No study measures idea or mechanism diversity inside these math agents. For example, nobody reports how many distinct proof strategies appear across 700 runs, or whether diversity predicts success. Only anecdotes exist, such as the unit-distance chain of thought exploring many areas.
  - No experiment tests whether cross-subfield transfer can be induced deliberately, for example by seeding generators with distant-field knowledge or forcing the 'disprove' direction. The big transfers (class field towers applied to discrete geometry, Monge-Ampère methods applied to the Ehrhart problem) are anecdotal and came from closed internal models.
  - Nobody has validated an automatic judge of novelty or significance against expert ratings on the autonomy × significance scale (Level 0-4). Every significance label in this literature is assigned by humans.
  - Formal verification is biased against cross-field results, yet nobody has quantified what fraction of AI-found proofs cannot be formalized cheaply because of Mathlib gaps. The unit-distance formalization took 1.2 million lines and LeanMarathon stalled on it.
  - No company has disclosed yield per dollar for its headline results (OpenAI's Ten Advances and the unit-distance disproof). The only public denominators come from Aletheia (700 problems), AlphaProof Nexus (353) and Epoch (68 problems, about $240k).
  - Nobody has measured how well an 'unconscious plagiarism' detector works, that is, attributing a generated proof or idea to training-set sources. Aletheia scanned reasoning traces but acknowledges that leakage from training is undetectable.
  - The recall of negative literature searches, used as a crowd-sourced 'soft certificate' that no prior solution exists, has not been validated.
Surprises:
  - Claude underperforms sharply on verified math. Claude Code (Opus 4.7) solved 0 of 9 DeepMind Erdős problems where Codex (GPT-5.5) solved 7. Claude Fable 5 and 5.1 scored 0/68 on FrontierMath Erdős. The wiki's standalone Claude Mythos solution to #90 (May 26, 2026) came six days after OpenAI's public disproof, so it may be contaminated. This is a direct risk for a harness built on Claude Opus 5.5.
  - The simplest loop won. The basic generate-then-Lean-check agent solved all 9 Erdős problems that DeepMind's full evolutionary, Elo-tournament and AlphaProof agent solved; the elaborate agent helped only on cost for the hardest two.
  - Aletheia's own authors write: 'formal verification cannot help with any of these difficulties'. The difficulties they mean are finding prior literature, misreading the problem statement, and reproducing training data without attribution. The hardest human step was literature identification, not checking correctness.
  - Novelty claims decay even in careful work. Aletheia's '5 novel' became '4 novel' between v1 and v3 (#935 was found in the literature), and Erdős-397 turned out to be a 2012 Chinese IMO selection-test problem.
  - Most 'technically correct' outputs answered the wrong question: 63 were technically correct but only 13 addressed the intended problem. Formal agents game the specification the same way, by hiding the core difficulty in a sorry'd lemma, citing hallucinated 'literature' lemmas, or proving a weakened statement (Aristotle on #124).
  - The biggest AI result so far, the unit-distance disproof, was a cross-field construction that Bloom says required a 'confluence of several different unlikely events'. Its Lean formalization is 1.2 million lines. The formal verifier is therefore most expensive, or fails outright, on exactly the transfer results a discovery engine most wants.
  - Per Quanta, Bloom said most new Erdős results came from hobbyists and undergraduates using public LLMs, not from corporate labs. Bloom estimates only 3-5 problems he considers significant were AI-solved by August 2026, out of roughly 70 or more AI-originated full solutions on the wiki. That puts the significant share at about 4-7%, which is my inference.
  - The Quanta article I fetched (Aug 3, 2026) does not explicitly say most solved problems were elementary; it says the problems 'vary widely in difficulty'. The elementary caveat comes from the Aletheia paper (Level A0 'student exercises'), Bloom's remarks ('much easier' problems) and Epoch ('proved easy to solve with a modest effort').

## human_ai_coideation_field_evidence
- Human-led 'question mode' vs model-led rewriting (CHI 2026 RCT: 'Partnering with Generative AI') (Sebastian Maier, Manuel Schneider, Stefan Feuerriegel (LMU Munich) 2025 arXiv (v3 Mar 2026); CHI 2026, doi 10.1145/3772318.3791185) [expert_human_rating]: Five-arm preregistered RCT, GPT-4.1 (gpt-4.1-2025-04-14). Arms: Control; Vanilla chat; Model-Led (the user writes an idea of 10 words or fewer, the LLM rewrites it for originality and usefulness, and the user may edit it); Suggestion-Mode (3 AI alternatives, then 3 elaboration suggestions); Question-Mode (3 AI alternatives built by cross-domain analogy, the user picks one, then the LLM asks targeted questions and the human does the elaborating). In every arm the human submits the final idea. Diversity is the cosine distance from each idea to the leave-one-out centroid of its condition (text-embedding-3-large). | Result: Study 1 (N=486, automotive Alternative Uses Test, 4 industry experts, ICC(3,k)=.60). Quality vs control: Model-Led d=0.55 (p<.001); Question d=0.36 (p=.020); Suggestion d=0.13 (n.s.); Vanilla d=0.02 (n.s.). Diversity: Question 0.379 vs Model-Led 0.309 (d=0.76, p<.001). Ownership: Question 5.71 vs Model-Led 5.03 (d=0.57). Within-subject diversity change: Question +0.07, Model-Led -0.09, Suggestion -0.03. Model-Led users accepted the refinement without elaborating in 90% of coded cases. Study 2 (N=640, product ideas, 320 consumer raters) replicated the quality gains (Question d=0.49, Model-Led one-shot d=0.50). The Question vs Model-Led one-shot diversity gap held (d=0.39) but vanished against an iterative Model-Led arm (d=0.14, n.s.). | Limits: Participants were Prolific crowdworkers, not R&D staff. Tasks were short text ideas (Alternative Uses Test, a sub-£40 product), rated by people with no execution. Only means were analysed, with no top-quantile analysis. Question mode had higher cognitive workload (d=0.86 vs Model-Led). Rater blinding was not stated explicitly. The model was GPT-4.1, not a 2026 frontier model. | Relevance: This is the most direct evidence for OptiLex's 'who leads' question. Let the AI propose varied alternatives and ask probing questions, and make the human write the elaboration. Letting the model rewrite and the human accept gives the most quality but collapses diversity. The 'show 3 AI suggestions' interface was the worst of both. The per-condition leave-one-out centroid distance is a cheap diversity metric OptiLex could adopt alongside mechanism-family counts. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23324
- AI ideation vs AI refinement, and the limits of persona-simulated diversity (Mengchen Dong, Hiromu Yakura 2026 (arXiv 2607.26899, 29 Jul 2026; preregistered; data collected 11-15 Mar 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Preregistered 2x3 design. 644 writers (318 native English, 326 non-native) wrote creative metaphors in one of three conditions: human-only; AI ideation (5 GPT-4o candidate metaphors shown before writing); or AI refinement (the human writes the metaphor, then GPT-4o offers 5 candidate explanations). Tab-switch detection blocked outside AI use. An adversarial, non-preregistered extension simulated the whole writer pool with personas built from the participants' real backgrounds, using GPT-4o mini, Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Qwen3.5-27b, native-language prompting and temperatures from 0.5 to 2.0. | Result: Collective diversity (mean centroid similarity): AI ideation made the pool most homogeneous (vs human-only b=0.036, p<0.001). AI refinement did not differ from human-only (b=0.007, p=0.516). The non-native writers' diversity advantage disappeared under AI ideation (0.457 vs 0.451, p=0.481) but survived refinement (0.426 vs 0.409, p=0.032). Every simulated pool was less diverse than every human pool; the best, Claude Sonnet 4.6, recovered only 75.3% of human-only diversity. Temperature gains plateaued, and GPT-4o mini at T=2.0 produced 14.3% gibberish words. From 351 blind raters: AI ideation raised the quality floor, not the ceiling. For native writers the divergent-creativity gain was β=+7.5 at the 10th percentile vs +4.5 at the 90th, and for non-native writers it existed only at the 20th percentile and below. | Limits: The task (four one-sentence metaphors) is far from R&D. Raters were lay Prolific users. Diversity is embedding-based. The simulation arm was not preregistered. The assistant was GPT-4o. | Relevance: This locates the diversity cost precisely. It comes from AI supplying the idea core, not from AI touching the work. For OptiLex, humans or diverse seeds should supply the mechanism and the AI should refine, stress-test and elaborate. AI-generated 'diverse personas' are not a substitute for genuinely diverse human or knowledge-base sources, and raising temperature buys diversity only at the cost of coherent text. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26899
- The Cybernetic Teammate: preregistered P&G field experiment on real product-innovation tasks (Dell'Acqua, Ayoubi, Lifshitz, Sadun, E. Mollick, L. Mollick, Han, Goldman, Nair, Taub, Lakhani NBER w33641 (2025); Organization Science (June 2026 per NBER page)) [expert_human_rating]: A 2x2 design (individual vs cross-functional R&D + Commercial pair, crossed with AI vs no AI) with 776 P&G professionals working on real business-unit innovation tasks. The AI was a GPT-4 tool on Azure (GPT-4o at the July workshop), and AI arms got 1 hour of prompting training. Blind expert raters scored quality on a 1-10 scale (each solution got more than 3 ratings on average), plus technical-vs-commercial orientation. | Result: Quality vs individual controls: team without AI +0.24 SD (p<0.05); individual with AI +0.37 SD (p<0.01); team with AI +0.39 SD (p<0.01). Teams with AI were +9.2 pp more likely to reach the top decile against a 5.8% control mean (about 3x); the individual-with-AI top-decile effect was not significant. Time fell 16.4% for individuals with AI and 12.7% for teams with AI. With AI, R&D and Commercial staff produced similarly balanced solutions, and team solution-type bimodality fell from 0.564 to 0.482. AI users were 9.2 pp LESS likely to expect a top-10% result. AI-aided solutions were notably more semantically similar to each other, though still closer to human-only solutions than to raw GPT-4o outputs. | Limits: Outcomes are expert ratings of one-day proposals, not launched products or market results. AI outputs were substantially longer, a possible verbosity confound for raters. Diversity was reported only descriptively. The 2024-era model was used in a single-session format. | Relevance: This is the best real-organization evidence that AI can stand in for a cross-functional partner (the polymath effect). Two findings matter for OptiLex. The top-tail gain appeared only when AI was combined with a human team. And AI-aided work homogenised even here. Humans also misjudged the quality of their own AI-aided work, so self-assessment should not drive the selection step. | https://www.nber.org/papers/w33641
- Human-as-selector risk with realized outcomes: Kenyan entrepreneur RCT with a GPT-4 business mentor (Nicholas Otis, Rowan Clarke, Solène Delecourt, David Holtz, Rembrand Koning 2023 preprint; OSF v3 3 Oct 2025) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A field experiment that randomised access to a GPT-4 business assistant (via WhatsApp) among Kenyan small-business owners, measuring realized revenues and profits. The analysis decomposes whether differences come from the questions asked, the advice received, or which advice was selected and implemented. | Result: Outcomes are realized revenue and profit, not citations; this enum was the closest fit. There was no significant average effect. The effect for low baseline performers was about 0.25 SD lower than for high performers: low performers did nearly 10% worse with AI, while high performers may have gained over 15%. The gap came from which pieces of AI advice entrepreneurs selected and implemented, not from differences in questions or advice received. | Limits: The setting is small businesses, not R&D, and the model is GPT-4. The subgroup magnitudes are the authors' split-sample estimates. I did not verify the sample size from the primary source. | Relevance: This is the only realized-outcome RCT here, and it points to selection as the step that fails. If OptiLex puts humans in the selector role, the selector's skill sets the outcome. That argues for execution-backed evaluation and structured critique over unaided human picking. | https://osf.io/preprints/osf/hdjpk_v3
- AI expands individual scientists' impact but contracts science's collective focus (41.3M papers) (Qianyue Hao, Fengli Xu, Yong Li, James Evans Nature 649:1237-1243, 14 Jan 2026 (arXiv 2412.07727)) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: A bibliometric study of 41.3M papers. An AI-use detector (F1=0.875 against expert labels) classifies AI-augmented research. Individual careers and field-level topic spread are then compared between AI-using and non-using scientists. | Result: AI-using scientists publish 3.02x more papers, receive 4.84x more citations, and become project leaders 1.37 years earlier. AI adoption shrinks the collective volume of topics studied by 4.63% and reduces scientists' engagement with one another by 22.00%. | Limits: The data are correlational, with strong selection confounds: stronger or better-resourced scientists adopt AI, and AI-amenable data-rich fields grow anyway. 'AI' here mostly means pre-LLM ML tools, so this is a structural signal, not evidence about frontier LLM ideation. | Relevance: At scientific scale it mirrors Doshi & Hauser: private gains paired with collective narrowing. An OptiLex engine optimised for per-idea scores risks pulling toward crowded, data-rich directions. Collective or portfolio diversity needs to be an explicit objective. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07727
- Expert judgment drift: human evaluation of research ideas is not a fixed gold standard (Lingyu Zhang, Mitchell Wang, Boyuan Chen arXiv 2511.04964 (Nov 2025, rev. Mar 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: A two-wave study (median gap 23 days) with 7,938 ratings from 63 researchers across 6 departments. Each researcher re-rated a constant control idea alongside GPT-4o-generated ideas (100 candidates per participant). An LLM selector was then aligned to wave-1 ratings (per-dimension calibration plus ridge regression) and tested on wave 2. | Result: Test-retest reliability for overall quality was ICC(A,1)=0.586 [0.348, 0.759], with originality 0.60, implementability 0.52 and applicability 0.42. The control idea drifted +0.44 on a 0-10 scale (n.s.). The aligned selector's raw gain of +0.29 became -0.156 (P=0.611) after correcting for drift, so tuning to a snapshot gave transient gains. | Limits: The sample is small (63). Ideas came from GPT-4o. Drift and noise are hard to separate. | Relevance: This bears directly on the pilot's 53% Opus self-agreement: human experts are only moderately self-consistent too. OptiLex's evaluation layer should not be fitted to one round of human or LLM ratings. It needs repeated, drift-aware measurement and, where possible, execution outcomes. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.04964
- Ideation Arena: double-blind pairwise expert battles over LLM and agent research ideas (Zhiyu Chen, Keyu Zhao, ..., Fengli Xu, Yong Li arXiv 2608.29696 (30 Aug 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: More than 6,000 double-blind pairwise comparisons from 105 computer-science researchers, covering 14 frontier LLMs and 5 research-agent architectures under a shared closed literature context, combined into an Elo leaderboard. Ideation Arena Eval tests whether LLM judges reproduce the experts' preferences. | Result: The best LLM judge (Gemini 3 Pro Preview) reached 72.56% Soft Accuracy on Overall Quality. Inter-expert Krippendorff's α was about 0.60, and pairwise agreement on overall quality was 56.8%. Top Elo: GPT-5.1 1293.8, AI-Researcher (DeepSeek V3.2) 1276.5, Claude 4.5 Opus 1221.2. Several agent scaffolds scored far below their own base model (DeepSeek V3.2 at 1099.5): ResearchAgent 910.6, SciMON 871.7, MOOSE-Chem 820.9. Longer proposals were preferred in 52.8-58.7% of comparable-strength pairs. | Limits: These are proposal-stage preferences, not executed outcomes, and cover only CS. There is a length preference. Figures beyond the abstract came through an HTML summary. | Relevance: Wrapping a strong model in an ideation harness can make it worse in blind expert eyes. OptiLex must A/B its harness against bare Opus. It should also control for length when judging. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29696
- Central-tendency bias when humans select from AI-generated variation sets (Huiyang Chen, Keqing Jiao arXiv 2607.09018 (10 Jul 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: A within-subject experiment (N=50) in which each participant saw sets of 16 AI poster designs per theme at high vs low set variance and chose the preferred or most representative design. Drawing on ensemble-perception theory, it predicts that a simultaneous grid pulls choices toward the centroid. | Result: The data are human choice behaviour; no quality ratings were involved. High-variance sets raised selection of centre-proximal designs from 34% to 56% in the preference task (t(49)=3.51, p=0.001), and from 38% to 66% in the representativeness task (t(49)=4.34, p<0.001). | Limits: The study used images, not text ideas, with a small student sample.
