It measures centrality of choice, not the downstream quality of what was chosen. | Relevance: Generating more diverse candidates and showing them together in a grid can make human selection less diverse. OptiLex's selection interface should use pairwise or sequential presentation, a critique-before-choice step, or cluster-then-pick with a quota per cluster, rather than a single 'pick the best' grid. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09018
- AI as antagonist or critic: constructive-conflict agent (plus a contrarian-teammate counterpoint) (Howard Ziyu Han, Nikolas Martelaro (HAI 2026, conditionally accepted); counterpoint: Jin, Martinez-Maldonado, Gašević et al. 2026 (arXiv 2608.04166); counterpoint arXiv 2512.18239 (Dec 2025, rev. Jul 2026)) [expert_human_rating]: Han & Martelaro ran a between-subjects study (N=45 design students) with three conditions: Self-Reflection; Stepwise Guidance (a written constructive-conflict framework); and an Interactive agent that synthesises stakeholder pushback on the student's own design proposal. The counterpoint (Jin et al., https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18239) placed 224 students in 97 online triads alongside an undisclosed supportive or contrarian AI teammate on a creative movie-plot task. | Result: Both the Stepwise Guidance and Interactive conditions gave significantly higher self-reconsideration and more proposal improvements than Self-Reflection. The antagonistic agent introduced more conflicting perspectives, and its users generated and discarded more ideas. In the triads, the contrarian AI produced challenge- and reflection-rich discourse but lowered teamwork satisfaction and psychological safety, with no gain in creative performance. | Limits: Samples are small student groups with mostly self-report outcomes. The abstracts give no effect sizes, and I did not extract any. | Relevance: Critique aimed at an individual's own artefact during revision helps. Contrarian pressure inside group ideation does not raise output. For OptiLex this supports AI critics or soundness-checkers aimed at human-owned or AI-drafted ideas during refinement, rather than contrarian agents present during divergent generation. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04166
- AgentPanel: asynchronous forum of heterogeneous agents with lightweight human votes (Zhiyao Cui, ..., Lei Bai, Shuyue Hu (Shanghai AI Lab et al.) arXiv 2608.03283 (4 Aug 2026)) [llm_judge_only]: Heterogeneous LLM agents (DeepSeek-V4-Flash, Qwen3.5-397B, GLM-5.2, MiniMax-M3) decide for themselves whether to post in a persistent, Reddit-like forum, triggered by forum events or a schedule. Humans submit questions, browse threads, give upvotes, downvotes and stars, and request summaries, so the human acts as a lightweight selector and steerer. | Result: Against a centralized multi-agent-debate baseline: LiveIdeaBench (GPT-4o judge) feasibility 5.08 vs 4.08 and Avg5 6.14 vs 5.99. IdeaBench feasibility, for example, 0.31 vs 0.04 (Qwen3.5-397B). In the human study (N=20), 65% preferred AgentPanel over common LLM tools for breadth and early-stage suitability, and 55% for novel perspectives. | Limits: Offline gains are judged by LLMs, and some are small (Avg5 +0.15). The human study is only preference data from 20 people, with no execution or blinded expert rating. The authors say the ideas 'remain speculative candidates that require expert validation'. | Relevance: This is an architecture pattern OptiLex could copy: decentralised, heterogeneous generators with human voting instead of one central debate. The evidence is too weak to justify it, and it has not been tested for the centroid-selection bias. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03283
- Aftereffects of LLM assistance on later unassisted creativity (coach vs direct answer) (Harsh Kumar, Jonathan Vincentius, Ewan Jordan, Ashton Anderson CHI 2025 (doi 10.1145/3706598.3714198; arXiv 2410.03703)) [executed_computationally]: Two preregistered experiments: divergent thinking (Alternative Uses Test, N=460) and convergent thinking (Remote Associates Test, N=640). Participants had three exposure rounds with GPT-4o giving either direct answers or 'coach' strategy frameworks, or had no LLM (control), then one unassisted test round. | Result: Closest enum: the outcomes are objective Remote Associates Test accuracy and automated originality and diversity scores. Remote Associates Test accuracy in the unassisted round was 62% for control, 57% for direct-answer and 50% for coach (coach vs control -10.6 pp, 95% CI [-18.6, -2.7], p=0.005), even though direct-answer reached 80% during exposure. On the Alternative Uses Test, coach-exposed participants were less original than control in the test round (p=0.009). Homogenisation persisted after the LLM was removed: coach pairwise distance fell from 38.0 to 37.3 while control rose from 39.4 to 39.7. | Limits: The tasks are psychometric, and the 'coach' is a generic strategy list, not questions about the user's own idea. It is a single-session study. | Relevance: Socratic or coaching modes are not automatically safe. Generic frameworks can homogenise how people think and weaken their unaided skill. This reconciles with Maier et al. only if the AI's questions target the human's own specific idea. OptiLex should measure what the humans can do unaided over time, not just the output they produce with the tool. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.03703
- Meta-analysis: GenAI and creativity (28 studies, 8,214 participants) (Niklas Holzner, Sebastian Maier, Stefan Feuerriegel arXiv 2505.17241 (May 2025)) [expert_human_rating]: A systematic review and random-effects meta-analysis comparing AI alone vs humans, humans with AI vs humans alone, and idea diversity in human-AI collaboration. | Result: AI alone vs humans: g=-0.05 (no difference). Humans with AI vs humans alone: g=0.27. Diversity of ideas in human-AI collaboration: g=-0.86. | Limits: It pools heterogeneous, mostly lab and crowd tasks from the 2023-2024 model era, with mixed rater types (human and automated). No realized outcomes are included. | Relevance: This is the base rate the design has to beat. Typical co-ideation buys a small-to-moderate quality gain at a large diversity cost, so diversity preservation must be designed in from the start. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17241
- AI ideas injected into evolving human idea chains increase collective diversity (Joshua Ashkinaze, Julia Mendelsohn, Li Qiwei, Ceren Budak, Eric Gilbert ACM Collective Intelligence 2025 (doi 10.1145/3715928.3737481; arXiv 2401.13481 v3 Jul 2025)) [llm_judge_only]: A dynamic experiment in which each participant saw 6 example ideas drawn from earlier participants in the chain, with 0, 2 or 4 of them from ChatGPT-3.5, labelled as AI or not. N=844 across 48 countries, 3,414 responses and 175 chains. | Result: Creativity was scored by a fine-tuned GPT-3 classifier (r≈0.88-0.91); diversity by embeddings. Global collective diversity rose with high exposure (δ=0.31 disclosed, 0.26 undisclosed, both p=0.001). Individual creativity was unchanged (F=0.12, p=0.97). Human-only chains converged over time (slope β=-0.39, p=0.03), while high-exposure chains diverged (slope difference +0.53 to +0.57). | Limits: It used ChatGPT-3.5 and automated creativity scoring on an Alternative Uses Test task. The sign is opposite to Doshi & Hauser and Dong & Yakura; the mechanism difference is mixed human and AI examples feeding a converging pool. | Relevance: This shows diversity loss is not an unavoidable property of AI exposure. It happens when every participant is anchored on the same model's ideas. For OptiLex, AI ideas that differ from the current pool's centroid can counter human convergence. This is testable as 'diversified allocation' of AI seeds. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.13481
- Shared-AI-anchor homogenisation and design fixation (Doshi & Hauser 2024; Wadinambiarachchi et al. CHI 2024; Jansson & Smith 1991) (Anil Doshi & Oliver Hauser; Samangi Wadinambiarachchi, Ryan Kelly, Saumya Pareek, Qiushi Zhou, Eduardo Velloso; David Jansson & Steven Smith Science Advances 10(28), 12 Jul 2024; CHI 2024 (arXiv 2403.11164); Design Studies 12:3-11 (1991)) [expert_human_rating]: The classic finding (Jansson & Smith, doi 10.1016/0142-694X(91)90003-F): designers copy features of an example they are shown, including its flaws. With GenAI, Doshi & Hauser had 293 writers see 0, 1 or 5 GPT-4 story ideas, rated by 600 evaluators. Wadinambiarachchi et al. had 60 designers use or not use an AI image generator for visual ideation. | Result: Doshi & Hauser: novelty +5.4% (1 idea) and +8.1% (5 ideas); usefulness +3.7% and +9.0%. For the least creative writers, novelty rose up to +10.7% and usefulness +11.5%. AI-aided stories were 10.7% and 8.9% more similar to each other and about 5% closer to the AI ideas. Evaluators applied an ownership penalty of at least 25% when told AI ideas were used. Wadinambiarachchi: AI users showed 'higher fixation on an initial example' and 'produced fewer ideas, with less variety and lower originality'. | Limits: Lay raters and short creative tasks with 2024-era models; the fixation study is small (N=60) and image-based. I read Jansson & Smith only at the metadata level. The anchoring mechanism is structural, so it plausibly still applies with frontier models. | Relevance: This is the mechanism behind every negative-diversity result: a shared, early, concrete AI example. OptiLex's ordering rule follows. Elicit independent human or knowledge-base ideas first, and bring AI ideas in late, abstracted to mechanism level, and varied per person. | https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11244532/
- Integrity caveat: MIT-disavowed Toner-Rodgers materials-R&D study (arXiv 2412.17866) (Aidan Toner-Rodgers (paper); statement by MIT, with Daron Acemoglu and David Autor Paper Dec 2024; MIT statement 16 May 2025; arXiv withdrawal (v2) 20 May 2025) [none]: The paper claimed a large lab RCT of AI-assisted materials discovery (about +44% materials and +39% patents, per the task brief). It was the most-cited 'real R&D' evidence that AI boosts discovery and reshapes scientists' work toward judging candidates. | Result: MIT stated it has 'no confidence in the provenance, reliability or validity of the data'. arXiv administrators withdrew the paper 'due to concerns about the validity of the data and incomplete Institutional Review Board requirements'. MIT also contacted the Quarterly Journal of Economics, where the paper had been submitted. | Limits: I did not re-verify the +44% and +39% figures in the withdrawn text. They must not be cited as evidence. | Relevance: Removing it leaves no credible RCT with realized R&D outcomes (patents, validated materials) for human-AI co-ideation. OptiLex's claims should rest on its own executed experiments, and any design or claim that relied on this paper should be stripped. | https://economics.mit.edu/news/assuring-accurate-research-record
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: In a field experiment with 776 P&G professionals, individuals with AI gained +0.37 SD in quality, matching teams without AI (+0.24 SD). Teams with AI gained +0.39 SD and were +9.2 pp more likely to reach the top decile (control 5.8%). AI users were 9.2 pp less likely to expect top-10% results, and AI-aided solutions were more semantically similar to each other. (caveat: 1) How it was judged: quality is a 1-10 score from 22 blinded human experts (1,595 ratings, about 2.9 per solution). Scores are standardized to the individual-without-AI control. Agreement between raters is modest: ICC2 0.452, Pearson r 0.198. Nothing was checked by execution, lab work or the market. An LLM-judge rerun is only a robustness check in the appendix. 2) Unit of analysis: regressions use 550 solutions, not 776 people. 3) Setting: a one-day virtual workshop on real business-unit problems. The AI was GPT-4 via Azure (GPT-4o at the July session), and AI arms got 1 hour of prompt training. 4) Top decile: only Team+AI is significant. Individual+AI is +1.9 pp and not significant, and Team+AI vs. Team No AI gives p=0.19. "About 3x" is the authors' framing. 5) Team+AI (0.39) is not significantly different from Team No AI (0.24), p=0.24. So AI's added value on top of teamwork is not established. "Matching" in the individual-vs-team comparison is the authors' own word ("comparable"). 6) The -9.2 pp expectation figure pools all AI-enabled participants against the control and is self-reported. 7) Semantic similarity comes from sentence-embedding cosine distance. AI-aided solutions were still less clustered than GPT-4o-only outputs, so the authors read this as humans shaping the AI's output, not simple homogenization. 8) This is an NBER working paper and not peer reviewed as of this version.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: With 644 writers in a preregistered study, AI-supplied ideas compressed collective diversity (b=0.036, p<0.001) while AI refinement of human ideas did not (b=0.007, p=0.516). No persona-simulated pool (GPT-4o mini, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Qwen3.5-27b, T up to 2.0) matched any human pool; the best recovered 75.3% of human-only diversity. AI ideation raised the quality floor, not the top. => CORRECTED: Dong & Yakura, arXiv:2607.26899 (July 2026). In a preregistered creative-metaphor experiment on Prolific, 644 writers (318 native, 326 non-native English) each wrote 4 metaphors. Getting GPT-4o-generated ideas made the pool more homogeneous than writing with no AI (b=0.036, p<0.001; higher embedding centroid similarity means less diversity). AI refinement of the writers' own ideas did not (b=0.007, p=0.516). In a simulation that was not preregistered, persona pools from GPT-4o mini, Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Qwen3.5-27b were all less diverse than every human pool. That held at the default temperature of 1.0, with native-language prompting, and across a temperature sweep from 0.5 to 2.0. Only GPT-4o mini and Qwen went up to 2.0; Claude stopped at 1.0. The one exception was GPT-4o mini at T=2.0, which got past the plateau only through degenerate output: 14.3% gibberish words and under 75% format adherence. The strongest simulation (Claude Sonnet 4.6, default settings) recovered 75.3% of the human-only pool's diversity. Ratings came from 351 blind human evaluators. On divergent creativity (novelty and surprise), AI ideation's gains were concentrated at the bottom of the distribution. On convergent creativity (aptness and explanation quality), it raised ratings across the whole distribution. (caveat: (1) Only the human experiment was preregistered. The authors say the LLM persona simulation "was not preregistered" and call it an adversarial extension. (2) "T up to 2.0" wrongly implies all three models were tested that high. Claude only went to 1.0, and the 75.3% figure is at the default T=1.0 with English output. (3) "No simulated pool matched any human pool" is only true for fluent text. GPT-4o mini at T=2.0 went past the diversity plateau by producing gibberish. The authors call this "counterfeit" diversity, and their Fig. 3D frames it as a diversity-versus-fluency frontier. (4) Diversity was measured automatically as cosine similarity to the pool centroid using all-MiniLM-L6-v2 embeddings. Humans did not judge it, and it is not a quality measure. (5) Quality was judged by 351 native-English human evaluators on 0-100 scales. LLM judges appear only in a supplementary calibration, and two of the three over-rewarded some content. (6) "Floor, not top" is the authors' own summary, but it overstates their data. It applies to divergent creativity only. For native writers, the divergent gain was still significant at the 90th percentile (β=+4.5, p=0.003 vs. +7.5 at the 10th). Convergent creativity rose across the whole distribution for both groups. (7) The setting is Prolific crowdworkers writing short creative metaphors in about 20 minutes, with GPT-4o (2024-08-06) as the assistant. It is not real R&D ideation, and no ideas were executed or tested in a lab, so carrying it over to R&D co-ideation is an extrapolation. (8) The finding the paper leads with is omitted from the claim. Non-native writers, especially those working in their native language, added the most diversity, and AI ideation made that advantage undetectable.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: In a preregistered RCT (N=486, GPT-4.1, 4 blind automotive experts), model-led rewriting raised idea quality most (d=0.55 vs control) but cut diversity relative to the human-led question mode (0.309 vs 0.379 centroid distance, d=0.76) and lowered ownership (5.03 vs 5.71). Question mode still raised quality (d=0.36). Showing 3 AI suggestions did not raise quality (d=0.13, n.s.). Within subjects, diversity rose +0.07 in question mode and fell -0.09 in model-led mode. => CORRECTED: In a preregistered online RCT (AsPredicted; N=486 UK Prolific crowdworkers, not R&D staff; GPT-4.1) on a car-feature Alternative Uses Test variant, rated by 4 automotive-innovation experts via the Consensual Assessment Technique (blinding is not reported; ICC(3,k)=.60), model-led rewriting raised idea quality most vs a no-AI control (d=0.55, p<.001). Its idea diversity was lower than question mode (0.309 vs 0.379 cosine distance to leave-one-out centroid, text-embedding-3-large; d=0.76). Ownership was also lower (5.03 vs 5.71 on a 7-point scale; d=0.57), though the no-AI control scored highest at 6.11. Question mode raised quality (d=0.36, p=.020). Suggestion mode (3 alternative ideas plus 3 elaboration suggestions) did not raise quality (d=0.13, p=.366), and neither did a vanilla chatbot (d=0.02). Within subjects, diversity went +0.07 in question mode, -0.03 in suggestion mode and -0.09 in model-led mode (all p<.01). A second study (N=640, product ideas judged by 320 student raters) replicated the quality pattern: question d=0.49, model-led d=0.50, suggestion d=0.04 n.s. (caveat: All the numbers in the claim match the paper. The errors are in how the claim frames it. (1) The paper never says the raters were blind. It says four experts "rated all ideas independently", so "blind" is unsupported. (2) This is not real R&D. Participants were UK Prolific crowdworkers doing a creativity task in the style of the Alternative Uses Test (repurposing car parts). Only the raters had industry experience, at least 5 months in an automaker's innovation department, which is a modest bar. Inter-rater reliability was moderate (ICC .60). (3) Quality was judged by humans (expert ratings averaging originality and usefulness), not by execution or lab work. Diversity comes from an LLM embedding (text-embedding-3-large), not from human judgment. (4) Every AI arm lowered ownership compared with no AI (control 6.11), question mode included. Question mode only kept ownership higher than the other AI arms. (5) Question mode also opened with 3 AI-generated alternative ideas, so "3 AI suggestions" does not describe the suggestion arm alone. The suggestion arm added 3 elaboration suggestions. (6) Model-led was the least effortful arm (workload 40.9 vs 60.0 for question mode), and in the qualitative sample 90% of model-led participants simply accepted the rewrite. (7) Study 2 (N=640, student raters) found question and model-led quality effects about equal (d=0.49 vs 0.50). The diversity gap against the iterative model-led variant was not significant (d=0.14). So "model-led raised quality most" does not hold across both studies.)
Gaps:
  - No RCT compares humans as critics of AI ideas with humans as selectors of AI ideas using realized, executed outcomes. Every interaction-mode study (Maier 2026, Dong 2026, Doshi 2024) uses human or embedding ratings of text. The only realized-outcome RCT (Otis 2025) shows selection is where outcomes diverge.
  - No field experiment in real R&D varies WHEN AI ideas are shown (before vs after independent human ideation) or at what abstraction level (a mechanism sketch vs a full solution) with 2026 frontier models. Timing evidence comes from GPT-4 through GPT-4.1 on Alternative Uses Test, metaphor or story tasks.
  - No one has tested 'diversified allocation': giving each human different AI seeds far from the pool centroid, versus the same model's top-k ideas. Ashkinaze (diversity up) and Doshi/Dong (diversity down) suggest the shared anchor is the causal lever, but this has not been tested directly.
  - No co-ideation study in ML or LLM-training-efficiency research has executed the ideas. OptiLex's domain makes small-scale execution cheap enough to close the ideation-execution gap for human-AI modes, which no one has done.
  - Selection interfaces for text research ideas are untested against central-tendency bias. No study compares a grid, pairwise, sequential or cluster-quota selection interface on the diversity of what gets selected and on executed quality.
  - No drift-aware, longitudinal evaluation protocol exists for co-ideation systems. No one has calibrated LLM judges or human raters against executed outcomes over repeated waves.
  - Top-tail reporting is rare. Most RCTs report means only (for example, Maier et al. has no quantile analysis). Only P&G (top decile) and Dong & Yakura (quantile regression) look at the ceiling, which is what matters for discovery.
  - Long-run skill effects in real R&D teams are unmeasured. Kumar et al. show unassisted performance falls after only 3 rounds, but there is no months-long study of researchers using frontier co-ideation tools.
  - After the Toner-Rodgers withdrawal, there is no credible preregistered, independently audited RCT of human-AI co-ideation with realized R&D outputs (patents, validated materials, shipped products).
  - No human study has evaluated multi-agent forum or panel designs (AgentPanel) beyond a 20-person preference survey and LLM-judge scores, and none has been checked for selection-side homogenisation.
Surprises:
  - The common 'show the user 3 AI suggestions' interface was the worst option in the CHI 2026 RCT. It gave no quality gain in Study 1 (d=0.13, n.s.) and reduced within-subject diversity (-0.03), whereas the question mode raised both quality and diversity.
  - Coaching was not safe. A GPT-4o 'coach' giving generic strategies hurt later unassisted Remote Associates Test accuracy more than direct answers (50% vs 62% for control) and left lasting homogenisation (Kumar et al. CHI 2025). Reflective questions help only when they target the user's own idea.
  - Showing people MORE varied AI options increased the choice of the central, most typical option: from 34% to 56% for preference and from 38% to 66% for representativeness (Chen & Jiao 2026). More diversity generated can mean less diversity selected.
  - Published research-ideation scaffolds (ResearchAgent, SciMON, MOOSE-Chem on DeepSeek V3.2) scored 189-279 Elo points below their own bare base model in blind expert battles (Ideation Arena 2026). A harness can destroy value.
  - Human experts agree with each other only 56.8% pairwise on overall idea quality (Ideation Arena) and re-rate identical ideas with ICC 0.586 (Zhang et al.). Our Opus judge's 53% self-agreement is therefore near the human noise floor, not a unique LLM failure.
  - P&G participants retained over 75% of AI text in many solutions, yet those solutions stayed semantically closer to human-only work than to raw GPT-4o outputs. The same AI users were 9.2 pp less confident while scoring higher.
  - AI ideas mixed into evolving human idea chains INCREASED collective diversity (Ashkinaze et al., CI 2025), the opposite sign to Doshi & Hauser and Dong & Yakura. Human-only chains themselves converge over time (β=-0.39).
  - Even Claude Sonnet 4.6 personas built from real participants' backgrounds, with native-language prompting and high temperature, recovered only 75.3% of human pool diversity. Pushing temperature to 2.0 bought diversity only through gibberish (14.3% non-words for GPT-4o mini).
  - Contrarian AI teammates in groups increased reflective talk but lowered psychological safety with no gain in creative performance (Jin et al.). Antagonistic critique of an individual's own design did produce more improvements (Han & Martelaro). Where critique is placed matters more than whether it is present.
  - Model output diversity itself has been decreasing across three years of LLM releases (Patel et al., arXiv 2608.19437, Aug 2026), so the shared-anchor problem may grow rather than shrink with newer models.

## physical_science_discovery_realized_outcomes
- MatterGen's flagship synthesis: generated 'novel' TaCr2O6 shown to be a 1971 compound already in its training set (Zeni et al. (Microsoft Research AI4Science), Nature 641 (online 22 Jan 2025); audit by Mikkel Juelsholt, Materials Horizons, DOI 10.1039/d6mh00268d (Europe PMC first publication 12 June 2026, PMID 42007796) 2025-2026) [lab_validated]: Joint diffusion over atom types, coordinates and lattice, pretrained on Alex-MP-20 (607,683 stable structures), fine-tuned on bulk modulus; 8,192 candidates generated per target bulk-modulus value (50/100/150/200 GPa); filtered on (1) uniqueness+novelty against an extended Alex-MP-ICSD reference of 850,384 ordered + 117,652 disordered ICSD structures using a purpose-built ordered-disordered structure matcher, (2) MatterSim/DFT energy above hull, (3) phonon stability, (4) contains oxygen; 75 survivors, 4 picked by expert inspection, 1 synthesized. | Result: Funnel: 8,192 generated per target -> 75 filtered -> 4 chosen -> 1 synthesized (TaCr2O6, targeted 200 GPa). Measured bulk modulus up to 169 GPa (158 +/- 11 GPa by nanoindentation) vs 222 GPa DFT for the ordered structure, i.e. 'within 20% of target' only using the max of four measurements. Juelsholt's crystallographic re-analysis: the product is not novel but identical to previously reported Ta1/2Cr1/2O2, 'first described in 1971 and included in MatterGen's training dataset' (paper's own words; note the brief's '1972' does not match the primary abstract). MatterGen also rediscovered 101 ICSD compounds absent from training (95 with DFT bulk modulus, MAE 23 GPa, RMSE 32 GPa vs target). | Limits: n=1 synthesis; the novelty failure was found by one author's manual crystallography, not by any automated check; no published Microsoft response found in this pass; the 'within 20%' property claim depends on selecting the maximum of four nanoindentation measurements on a likely non-compact powder. | Relevance: The single cleanest demonstration that a novelty screen can be database-rich, disorder-aware and purpose-built and still pass a compound the model was trained on. For OptiLex this says the binding constraint is the equivalence relation used to compare an idea to prior art (and to the training corpus), not the size of the knowledge base — exactly the failure mode our 30-prompt mechanism-family collapse hints at. | https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlehtml/2026/mh/d6mh00268d
- Substitution-based structural novelty audit of five generative crystal models (Masahiro Negishi, Aron Walsh (Imperial College London) 2026) [executed_computationally]: Classifies each generated crystal into (1) exact training duplicate, (2) reproducible from a known structure type by elemental substitution, (3) unmatched; run on 10,000 chemically valid, metastable crystals per model trained on MP-20. | Result: 81-92% of chemically valid, metastable generated crystals are training duplicates or substitution-derived. Per model (duplicate / substituted / unmatched): MatterGen 20.5 / 60.6 / 18.9; DiffCSP++ 28.7 / 63.0 / 8.3; WyckoffTransformer 26.6 / 59.7 / 13.7; Crystalite 29.6 / 60.5 / 9.9; Chemeleon2 8.3 / 80.3 / 11.4. The standard StructureMatcher metric counts both substituted and unmatched structures as 'novel', hiding this. High-symmetry outputs are the most prototype-bound; memorization dominates in training-sparse regions. | Limits: Submitted 22 June 2026, preprint (not yet peer reviewed); MP-20 only, up to 20 atoms; 'unmatched' is a residual category, not a positive demonstration of usefulness; no experimental follow-up. | Relevance: A direct template for OptiLex's novelty metric: define novelty relative to an explicit generative closure of prior art (here, substitution), not to string/structure identity. Our 3-vs-15 mechanism-family count is the same measurement done by hand; this paper shows the audited number is 3-5x worse than the headline metric. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23166
- A-Lab autonomous synthesis, the PRX Energy rebuttal, and Nature's 2026 Author Correction (Szymanski et al. (Ceder/Persson, LBNL+Berkeley+Google DeepMind), Nature 624:86-91 (29 Nov 2023); rebuttal Leeman, Liu, Stiles, Lee, Bhatt, Schoop, Palgrave, PRX Energy 3:011002 (7 Mar 2024); Author Correction Nature 650:E1 (19 Jan 2026) 2023-2026) [lab_validated]: Targets from Materials Project + GNoME stability data; synthesis recipes proposed by NLP models trained on the literature; robotic powder synthesis; automated Rietveld refinement of PXRD decides success; active learning proposes improved recipes. | Result: Corrected article: 353 experiments in 17 days, 36 of 57 targets realized (63%). Leeman et al. re-examined all 43 claimed synthetic products and concluded 'no new materials have been discovered in that work', with two thirds 'likely to be known compositionally disordered versions of the predicted ordered compounds'. Nature's Author Correction (19 Jan 2026) concedes the novelty claims 'were subject to misinterpretation - their intention was to indicate that the materials were new to the prediction platform, not necessarily new to science', drops 4 targets as inconclusive from XRD alone (36 of 40 reported successes confirmed on manual, post-publication peer-reviewed re-analysis) and removes one compound (Zn2Cr3FeO8) that was mistakenly in the training data. | Limits: The correction's re-analysis was done by the original authors (peer-reviewed post hoc); Leeman et al. worked from published patterns, not samples; 'novel' was never operationally defined in the original paper. | Relevance: The cleanest separation of the two axes OptiLex must score independently: the making/execution engine was largely right (36/40 structural conclusions confirmed) while the novelty claim went to zero. An ideas factory needs separate, differently-evaluated verdicts for 'correct/works' and 'new', and the novelty verdict needs an adversarial human-grade checker. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-09992-y
- GNoME's 2.2M structures and the Cheetham-Seshadri novelty/utility audit (Merchant, Batzner, Schoenholz, Aykol, Cheon, Cubuk (Google DeepMind), Nature 624:80-90 (29 Nov 2023); audit by Anthony K. Cheetham & Ram Seshadri, Chem. Mater. 36:3490-3495 (8 Apr 2024, PMC11044265) 2023-2024) [retrospective_prediction]: Graph-network energy models in an active-learning flywheel over substitution and random-structure candidate generation, filtered by GNN then DFT; outputs a 384,870-entry Stable Structure database plus GNoME Explorer. | Result: GNoME claims: 2.2M structures below the convex hull, 381,000 new stable entries (421,000 total), 736 'already independently experimentally realized', hit rate >80% with structure and 33% per 100 trials composition-only vs 1% previously, energies to 11 meV/atom, 45,500 novel prototypes by XtalFinder. Audit: all 10 randomly sampled Stable Structure entries were found in the ICSD, usually at higher symmetry, with only one space-group match; the database's top four space groups are all non-centrosymmetric and account for ~34% of entries (Pm 12.7%, P1 10.2%) whereas in the ICSD Pnma+P21/c are ~16% and non-centrosymmetric groups ~1%; 18,138 entries contain Pm/Ac/Pa and a further 23,529 contain Tc/Np/Pu; the authors find 'scant evidence for compounds that fulfill the trifecta of novelty, credibility, and utility'. | Limits: Audit sample is 10 random entries plus a selective scan, not a census; the 736 realizations are concurrent literature matches, not results GNoME caused; as of 25 Sep 2026 the Nature page carries no correction or Matters Arising (checked directly), unlike A-Lab. | Relevance: Shows what happens when an ideas factory optimises a proxy (DFT hull distance at 0 K) that ignores entropy/disorder and practicality: the yield of 2.2M ideas is a space-group distribution no experimentalist recognises. For OptiLex: an evaluator that is cheap and scalable will be gamed by the generator, and the audit cost per idea stays human-expert-sized. | https://doi.org/10.1021/acs.chemmater.4c00643
- Rentosertib (ISM001-055): generative-AI target plus generative-AI molecule, Phase IIa positive, Phase III now dosing (Xu, Ren, ... Zhavoronkov (Insilico Medicine + 15 Chinese centres), Nat Med 31:2602-2610 (online 3 June 2025, PMID 40461817); discovery paper Ren et al., Nat Biotechnol (8 Mar 2024, PMC11738990); Phase III NCT07687459 2024-2026) [lab_validated]: PandaOmics (multi-engine omics+literature target discovery) nominated TNIK as an anti-fibrotic target; Chemistry42 ran 30 generative models in parallel with shared scores and structure-based scoring against the TNIK kinase domain; candidates filtered on synthetic accessibility, novelty and med-chem properties, then synthesized and assayed. | Result: Phase IIa (n=71, 12 weeks, 4 arms): FVC change +98.4 mL (95% CI 10.9 to 185.9) at 60 mg QD vs -20.3 mL (95% CI -116.1 to 75.6) placebo; TEAE rates 72.2/83.3/83.3% vs 70.6% placebo; discontinuations mainly liver toxicity or diarrhoea. Discovery took 'roughly 18 months from target discovery to preclinical candidate nomination'. Phase III NCT07687459 (52 weeks, 320 patients estimated, primary endpoint annual FVC decline, 47 sites, all in China) has actual start 9 Sep 2026, first posted 7 Jul 2026, primary completion estimated 30 Oct 2029. | Limits: FVC was a secondary endpoint in a 71-patient safety trial; the CI's lower bound is 10.9 mL; single-country Phase III; the discovery paper never states how many molecules were synthesized or the compute spent, so no cost-per-validated-result can be computed; sponsor-authored. | Relevance: The one end-to-end realized outcome for a generative hypothesis outside ML: a first-in-class target AND molecule surviving to Phase III. The timeline (18 months idea->candidate, 4+ years and counting for evidence) sets the honest latency for any OptiLex claim of 'validated' ideas outside cheap-to-execute domains. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41591-025-03743-2
- Phase-transition base rates for AI-discovered molecules (Madura K. P. Jayatunga, Margaret Ayers, Lotte Bruens, Dhruv Jayanth, Christoph Meier (BCG X), Drug Discovery Today 29(6) (online 30 Apr 2024, PMID 38692505) 2024) [realized_impact_bibliometric]: Census of the clinical pipelines of AI-native biotech companies, classifying each AI-discovered/AI-designed molecule by phase outcome and comparing with historical industry base rates. | Result: Phase I success 80-90% for AI-discovered molecules, 'substantially higher than historic industry averages'; Phase II ~40%, 'albeit on a limited sample size, comparable to historic industry averages'. | Limits: Small Phase II sample; company self-reported pipelines; 'AI-discovered' is self-declared and spans everything from virtual screening to de novo generation; survivorship bias (discontinued preclinical work is invisible); not a randomized comparison. | Relevance: Localises the bottleneck precisely where OptiLex's design choice sits: AI is already good at generating artefacts that behave (chemistry, Phase I) and no better than humans at picking hypotheses that are true (biology, Phase II). Spend on critics/evaluation against reality, not on more generator diversity. | https://doi.org/10.1016/j.drudis.2024.104009
- Discontinued AI-derived clinical candidates (Recursion, Exscientia) (Recursion Pharmaceuticals 8-K exhibit 99.1, Q1 2025 (filed 5 May 2025); ClinicalTrials.gov records NCT05130866, NCT05085561, NCT06536465, NCT05920408 2025) [lab_validated]: Phenotypic-screening/ML platform (Recursion OS) nominating targets and molecules; outcomes read out as pre-specified futility thresholds and efficacy trends in Phase 2. | Result: On 5 May 2025 Recursion discontinued REC-2282 (NF2) - 'Phase 2 passed the futility threshold primarily driven by the 40mg cohort, however the 60mg and combined dose arms did not pass the futility criteria', limited tumour shrinkage - and REC-994 (cerebral cavernous malformation) - long-term extension showed 'no promising trends in MRI or functional outcomes', the 400mg arm 'indistinguishable from natural history'; REC-3964 (C. difficile) moved to out-licensing on market grounds. CT.gov confirms NCT05130866 and NCT06536465 TERMINATED 'due to sponsor decision... not related to safety'. Exscientia's EXS21546 combination trial NCT05920408 was terminated with 'Emerging data demonstrates challenge for EXS21546 to reach suitable therapeutic index' (last update 8 Nov 2023). | Limits: Company-framed disclosures; REC-994/REC-2282 originated from phenotypic screening rather than generative design, so they test the target-hypothesis layer, not molecule generation; Exscientia's DSP-1181 (the first 'AI-designed' molecule into Phase I) discontinuation could not be confirmed in a primary source in this pass - only DSP-0038 appears in Exscientia's 2021 20-F. | Relevance: The negative tail that novelty-and-plausibility scoring never sees: ideas that pass every in-silico and Phase I filter and die on efficacy. Any OptiLex evaluation layer that reports only its hit rate on executed ideas will overstate value unless discontinuations are counted in the same denominator. | https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1601830/000160183025000074/exhibit991-q0125.htm
- Google Co-Scientist: tournament-evolved hypotheses with a search-grounded novelty critic, validated in vitro (Gottweis, Weng, ... Kohli, Karthikesalingam, Natarajan et al. (Google), arXiv 2502.18864 (26 Feb 2025, updated 29 June 2026) 2025-2026) [lab_validated]: Multi-agent Gemini system: Generation, Reflection (review + novelty, with an external literature-search tool), Ranking via Elo tournament, Evolution, Proximity, Meta-review, with asynchronous test-time compute scaling; domain experts then select the top-k hypotheses for wet-lab test. | Result: AML repurposing: from a ranked list restricted to candidates with no prior preclinical evidence, experts picked 3 (Nanvuranlat, KIRA6, Leflunomide); KIRA6 gave IC50 10 nM in KG-1a vs 180 nM in non-malignant TK6 (18-fold window) and 144 nM in NOMO-1, while Leflunomide and Nanvuranlat 'showed little to no effect' in MOLM-13 - 1 of 3 novel picks worked. Earlier evidence-backed picks: Binimetinib IC50 as low as 2 nM. Liver fibrosis: 3 expert-selected epigenetic targets -> 2 of 3 drugs anti-fibrotic in human hepatic organoids without toxicity (one, Vorinostat, already FDA-approved). cf-PICI: independently recapitulated an unpublished mechanism. Critic ablation: on a dataset of published (therefore non-novel) ideas the Reflection agent without search scored novelty 6.14/10, with search 2.38/10; correctness 7.4 -> 8.46/10; GPQA AUC 0.643 -> 0.651. Expert ratings across 11 goals: mean preference rank 2.36, novelty 3.64/5, impact 3.09/5; Elo rises with test-time compute across 203 goals. | Limits: Preprint; every wet-lab result passed a human expert filter, and the counterfactual (experts choosing from a non-AI list) is untested; in vitro only; small n per experiment (n=3 replicates); expert ratings are subjective, as the paper states; the paper-QA benchmark measures rediscovery of published hypotheses, not novelty. | Relevance: The closest existing build to the OptiLex harness, and its ablation is the most actionable number in this whole dimension: an ungrounded novelty critic rates known-published ideas 6.14/10 and a retrieval-grounded one 2.38/10. Our 53% self-agreement judge is what the ungrounded variant looks like from the outside; grounding the critic in retrieved prior art is the first thing to build. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18864
- Robin: lab-in-the-loop agent that proposed and validated ripasudil for dry AMD (Ghareeb, Chang, Mitchener et al. (FutureHouse), arXiv 2505.13400 (19 May 2025) 2025) [lab_validated]: Literature agents (Crow/Falcon) generate and rank therapeutic hypotheses via an LLM-judged tournament; humans run the assay; a data-analysis agent (Finch) analyses flow cytometry and RNA-seq and proposes the next round. | Result: Round 1: top 5 candidates tested (Exendin-4, Fingolimod, MFGE8, Y-27632, AICAR+TUDCA); ROCK inhibition validated. Round 2: 10 candidates tested; ripasudil increased RPE phagocytosis '7.5-fold compared to DMSO controls' per the agent's analysis, while the human analysis of the same data showed a 1.75-fold increase. RNA-seq found ABCA1 upregulated 3-fold (adjusted p = 2.13e-83). | Limits: Preprint, no peer review; 15 compounds tested in total, single assay, single cell type (ARPE-19/human primary), no animal model; ripasudil is an approved drug (repurposing, not new chemistry); the headline effect size depends on the agent's own analysis pipeline; humans chose the substrate and ran the wet work. | Relevance: Evidence that an ideas-plus-analysis loop can reach a real, validated result at ~15 executions - and a warning that the automated analysis layer can report a 4x larger effect than a human analysing the same data. If OptiLex scores its own ideas with its own analysis agent, the evaluation layer needs a human-analysed control arm. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.13400
- Kosmos: long-horizon AI scientist with statement-level expert audit (Mitchener et al. (FutureHouse + Edison Scientific), arXiv 2511.02824 (4 Nov 2025) 2025) [expert_human_rating]: Structured world model shared between a data-analysis agent and a literature-search agent; up to 12 hours and ~200 rollouts per run; every statement in the output report cites either code or a primary paper. | Result: Independent scientists audited 102 statements from three reports: 79.4% accurate overall, but 85.5% of data-analysis statements reproducible, 82.1% of literature statements validated against primary sources, and only 57.9% of synthesis/interpretation statements accurate. Average run: 42,000 lines of code and 1,500 papers; collaborators judged a 20-cycle run equal to ~6 months of their own research time, with valuable findings scaling linearly up to 20 cycles. Of 7 highlighted discoveries, 3 reproduce preprinted/unpublished findings not accessible at runtime and 4 are claimed novel. | Limits: Preprint; 102 statements from 3 of 7 runs; 'equivalent researcher time' and 'valuable findings' are collaborator self-reports from people who co-ran the system; the 4 novel contributions have no independent experimental validation; the linear-scaling claim rests on the same self-reports. | Relevance: Quantifies where an ideas factory actually breaks: retrieval and code are ~85% reliable, synthesis - the step that produces the idea - is ~58%. That is the stage OptiLex's critics must attack, and it argues for critics specialised on cross-source synthesis rather than on fact-checking individual claims. | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02824
- Generative de novo antibiotics with in vivo validation (NG1, DN1) (Krishnan, Wong et al. (Collins lab, MIT/Broad/Wyss), Cell (online 14 Aug 2025, PMC12790861) 2025) [lab_validated]: GNN (Chemprop) activity and cytotoxicity predictors screen >45M chemical fragments; fragments expanded by a chemically reasonable genetic algorithm (CReM) and a VAE, or generated unconstrained; filters on prediction score, PAINS/Brenk substructures, and structural novelty enforced as Tanimoto similarity <0.5 to any of 559 curated known antibacterials; medicinal chemists shortlist for synthesis. | Result: 1,156,945 unique fragments passed filters; >36M previously unenumerated compounds generated; after down-selection 24 compounds were synthesized and 7 (29%) were selectively antibacterial. NG1 reached MIC 0.5 ug/mL against N. gonorrhoeae ATCC 49226; DN1 MIC 4 ug/mL against MSSA and MRSA with therapeutic index 32; both reduced bacterial burden in mouse models with mechanisms distinct from clinical antibiotics. Synthesizability failure recorded explicitly: for one shortlist 'none of these compounds were synthetically accessible within typical synthesis timeframes and costs'. | Limits: Novelty is defined only as a fingerprint-similarity threshold (Tanimoto <0.5) against 559 compounds, not as a prior-art or mechanism check; human medicinal chemists performed the final selection at every stage (4,831 -> 90 -> 24); mouse infection models, not clinical evidence; 36M-to-24 conversion means the effective denominator is set by human capacity, not by the generator. | Relevance: A working template for 'generate at 10^7 scale, validate at 10^1', and a caution on both ends: the novelty metric is a similarity threshold that nothing audits, and the real gate is synthesizability/human review. OptiLex's funnel should record how many ideas are discarded for executability rather than for quality. | https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2025.07.033
- Virtual Lab: agent team designs 92 SARS-CoV-2 nanobodies (Kyle Swanson, Wesley Wu, Nash L. Bulaong, John E. Pak, James Zou (Stanford/CZ Biohub), Nature (online 29 July 2025, PMID 40730228; preprint bioRxiv 2024.11.11.623004, 12 Nov 2024) 2024-2025) [lab_validated]: LLM PI agent runs team and individual meetings with specialist agents plus a critic agent, with a human giving high-level feedback; the agents authored a design pipeline combining ESM, AlphaFold-Multimer and Rosetta. | Result: 92 nanobodies designed; experimental validation found a range of functional binders, with 2 showing improved binding to JN.1 or KP.3 while retaining ancestral spike binding - a ~2% rate for the headline claim. | Limits: Binding assays only - no neutralization, potency or developability claims; designs were mutations of four known parent nanobodies, so novelty is incremental; no non-agent baseline pipeline was run on the same problem; the human researcher steered the agenda. | Relevance: Shows the agent-team pattern's realistic yield (2 of 92) in a domain with a cheap, objective wet-lab readout, and that the contribution was pipeline composition from existing tools rather than a new mechanism. Useful calibration for what an OptiLex 'idea' is worth when the evaluator is an assay rather than a judge. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-09442-9
- SCIGEN: geometry-constrained generation where the designed property did not materialize (Okabe, Cheng, Chotrattanapituk et al. (MIT), Nature Materials (online 22 Sep 2025) 2025) [lab_validated]: Wraps a diffusion generator with hard geometric constraints (Archimedean/Lieb, honeycomb, kagome lattices) so sampling stays in the original distribution while enforcing motifs; multistage stability screening, then high-throughput DFT, then a GNN magnetic-ordering classifier. | Result: 10 million compounds generated; >10% pass multistage stability screening; DFT on 26,000 candidates gives >95% convergence and 53% structural stability; a GNN detects magnetic ordering in 41% of relaxed structures; two predicted materials were synthesized and characterized - TiPd0.22Bi0.88 and Ti0.5Pd1.5Sb - which 'display paramagnetic and diamagnetic behaviour, respectively'. | Limits: The two realized materials do not show the magnetic quantum behaviour the constrained search was designed for; novelty of the two compounds is not audited against ICSD in the abstract; the 41% magnetic figure is a classifier output, not measurement. | Relevance: A property-conditioned generator whose in-silico evaluator (GNN magnetism) and whose experimental outcome disagree. For OptiLex: conditioning on a target property is only as good as the property predictor, and the 10^7 -> 2 funnel again ends at human synthesis capacity. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41563-025-02355-y
- Matbench Discovery: how the evaluator itself fails in high-throughput discovery (Riebesell, Goodall, Benner, Chiang, Deng, Ceder, Asta, Lee, Jain, Persson, Bartel et al., Nature Machine Intelligence (23 June 2025) 2025) [executed_computationally]: Benchmark framework for ML energy models used as pre-filters to DFT, scoring prospective discovery (classification of stability) rather than retrospective regression; covers random forests, GNNs, one-shot predictors, Bayesian optimizers and universal interatomic potentials, with a live leaderboard and user-weighted metrics. | Result: Documents a misalignment between regression metrics (MAE) and task-relevant classification metrics: accurate regressors can carry unexpectedly high false-positive rates when predictions sit near the 0 eV/atom decision boundary; universal interatomic potentials are now good enough to pre-screen stable hypothetical materials cheaply. | Limits: Computational benchmark only; stability is a proxy for synthesizability, which is a proxy for usefulness; says nothing about novelty; leaderboard results shift over time. | Relevance: Directly transferable lesson for OptiLex's evaluation layer: the metric that looks best for a scorer (average error) is not the metric that governs downstream yield (false positives near the accept/reject boundary). Design the idea-ranking metric as a classification problem at the decision threshold, with calibrated false-positive cost. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s42256-025-01055-1
- Coscientist and the 2024-2026 closed-loop self-driving labs (Boiko, MacKnight, Kline, Gomes (CMU), Nature 624:570-578 (20 Dec 2023); Strieth-Kalthoff et al., Science 384:eadk9227 (17 May 2024); Dai, Cooper et al. (Cronin/Cooper), Nature (6 Nov 2024) 2023-2026) [lab_validated]: Coscientist: GPT-4 agent with internet/documentation search, code execution and direct control of liquid handlers, designing and running experiments. Successors: a cloud AI planner orchestrating design-make-test-analyze cycles across five laboratories worldwide with in-line characterization; and mobile robots that share human lab equipment, combining LC-MS and benchtop NMR with a heuristic decision-maker that auto-checks reproducibility of screening hits. | Result: Coscientist executed six task families including autonomous optimization of palladium-catalysed cross-couplings. The delocalized asynchronous loop discovered 21 new state-of-the-art organic solid-state laser gain materials, with gram-scale synthesis confirming best-in-class stimulated emission in a thin-film device. The mobile-robot workflow ran structural diversification, supramolecular host-guest and photochemical synthesis campaigns with orthogonal characterization deciding which reactions advance. | Limits: Optimization within known reaction/material classes rather than new mechanisms; no prior-art novelty audit of the 21 emitters in the abstract; Coscientist's chemistry was small-scale and curated; decision-makers are heuristic, so the 'idea' contribution is narrow. | Relevance: Defines the domain condition under which an ideas factory pays off: a cheap, objective, orthogonal, automated readout closes the loop and makes generator quality measurable. OptiLex's ML-training test domain has exactly this property (runs are executable), which is the strongest argument for choosing it as the first proving ground - and for treating any domain without such a readout as a places where only human experts can score. | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-023-06792-0
Independent claim checks:
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: 81-92% of chemically valid, metastable crystals generated by five current models are training duplicates or reproducible by elemental substitution from a known structure type; for MatterGen the split is 20.5% duplicate / 60.6% substituted / 18.9% unmatched, and the standard StructureMatcher novelty metric counts the substituted ones as novel. (caveat: 1) Everything is measured on MP-20 (Materials Project crystals with at most 20 atoms per primitive cell). The models were trained or evaluated on MP-20 and sampled unconditionally, 10k samples each. That makes "current models" narrower than it sounds: MatterGen here is the MP-20 setting, not the production model trained on Alex-MP-20. 2) "Metastable" means within 0.1 eV/atom of the convex hull as computed with the MACE-MPA-0 machine-learned potential. It is not DFT, and there was no synthesis or lab validation; all results come from computational structure matching. 3) "Unmatched" (8-19%) is not the same as truly novel or useful. It only means no duplicate or substitution match was found. 4) The paper notes the effect is strongest in high-symmetry crystal systems. 5) The range is the spread across the five models, not a pooled figure. MatterGen is the low end (81%) and DiffCSP++ the high end (~92%). 6) This is a June 2026 preprint and has not been peer-reviewed.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [confirmed]: Google's Co-Scientist Reflection agent, asked to rate the novelty of ideas already published (hence non-novel), scored them 6.14/10 without an external search tool and 2.38/10 with it; correctness scoring rose from 7.4 to 8.46/10. (caveat: 1) The result appears only in v2 (June 2026, the Nature version), not in the Feb 2025 v1, so cite v2 or the Nature paper. 2) All scores are the model's own LLM auto-evaluations (1-10). No humans judged them. The only ground truth is that the ideas were already published. 3) The dataset is Google's in-house "paper QA" rediscovery benchmark: 200 examples from bioRxiv papers published after the model's knowledge cutoff. The benchmark is not publicly released. 4) The comparison is an "initial review" without search against a "full review" whose search had no date cutoff, so search could find the source paper itself. The drop to 2.38 therefore shows retrieval finding the paper, not intrinsic novelty judgment. The paper says the unaided model "hallucinates" novelty on post-cutoff work. 5) The base model is Gemini 2.0-era: the main text links the ablation run to Gemini 2.0 Flash, while other ablations in the supplement use Gemini Flash 2.5. The same ablation raised GPQA correctness AUC only from 0.643 to 0.651, so the correctness improvement is modest. 6) This is an internal component ablation. It is not a lab or clinical outcome.)
  * CLAIM CHECK [corrected]: MatterGen generated 8,192 candidates per bulk-modulus target, filtered to 75, had experts pick 4, synthesized 1 - and that one, TaCr2O6, was shown to be the known disordered Ta1/2Cr1/2O2, first described in 1971 and present in MatterGen's own training set, despite a novelty screen against 850,384 ordered plus 117,652 disordered ICSD structures using a purpose-built ordered-disordered matcher. => CORRECTED: MatterGen (Zeni et al., Nature 2025) generated 8,192 candidates for each of four bulk-modulus targets (50/100/150/200 GPa; 32,768 in total). Filtering across all targets left 75. Experts picked 4 of those for synthesis, and 1 synthesis succeeded: a compositionally disordered TaCr2O6, from the 200 GPa target. The novelty screen compared candidates with 850,384 ordered structures from Alex-MP-ICSD (recomputed from MP, Alexandria and ICSD, so not ICSD alone) plus 117,652 disordered ICSD structures, using a newly proposed ordered-disordered structure matcher. Juelsholt (ChemRxiv 2025-26; Materials Horizons 2026) argues from crystallographic analysis that the synthesized phase, reported as Ta1/3Cr2/3O2, is identical to the known compound Ta1/2Cr1/2O2. He dates that compound to 1972, not 1971, and says it was in MatterGen's training data. (caveat: 1) Year: the author's own abstract (ChemRxiv v2 and v3) says 1972. I found no 1971 anywhere I could access, so '1971' looks wrong or unsupported. 2) The RSC HTML (the given URL) and the ChemRxiv PDF are behind Cloudflare bot checks, so I could not read the critique's body text. The training-set claim and the Ta1/2Cr1/2O2 identification come from Juelsholt's abstract only; I did not check them against MatterGen's training data (Alex-MP-20) myself. 3) '8,192 per target, filtered to 75' can be misread: 75 is the total left after filtering 32,768 candidates across four targets, not 75 per target. 'Synthesized 1' means 4 syntheses were attempted and 1 succeeded. 4) The 850,384 ordered structures are the Alex-MP-ICSD hull reference (MP + Alexandria + ICSD, recomputed with DFT), not 850,384 ICSD entries; only the 117,652 disordered structures are ICSD-specific. 5) How each side reached its result: MatterGen's novelty was decided by an automated structure matcher, and its synthesis was checked in the lab (XRD with Rietveld refinement; bulk modulus estimated from nanoindentation at up to 169 GPa, 158 ± 11 GPa, against a 200 GPa target and 222 GPa by DFT). The rebuttal is a human expert's crystallographic re-analysis, not new lab work as far as the abstract shows. 6) Nature itself describes the product as 'a compositionally disordered version of the ordered structure predicted by MatterGen', so the disorder was acknowledged. What is disputed is novelty. The composition also differs: Nature reports TaCr2O6 (Ta:Cr 1:2), Juelsholt Ta1/2Cr1/2O2 (1:1). 7) Side effect: while I tried to open the ChemRxiv PDF in the Claude desktop browser pane, a file-save dialog appeared for the user. Nothing was retried or downloaded on purpose.)
Gaps:
  - No cross-domain, date-cutoff prior-art benchmark for novelty critics. Co-Scientist's ablation (6.14 vs 2.38 out of 10) is a one-off internal test on published ideas; Negishi & Walsh's substitution audit is crystals-only. Nobody publishes a standing benchmark where an idea-generation system is scored on prior-art hit rate against a corpus frozen before a cutoff date, with an equivalence relation richer than string/fingerprint similarity. This is a buildable, cheap, and currently unowned artefact - and it is the exact thing OptiLex's critic layer needs to be measured on.
  - No published training-corpus leakage check for generated ideas. MatterGen's flagship result was in its own training set; A-Lab had to remove Zn2Cr3FeO8 for the same reason after publication. No system reports 'fraction of proposed ideas traceable to training data' as a standard metric, even though for OptiLex (whose knowledge base is human research) this is the primary failure mode.
  - No study reports the full funnel with honest denominators and cost. The antibiotics paper is the only one that prints the whole chain (45M fragments -> 1.16M -> 36M generated -> 4,831 -> 90 -> 24 synthesized -> 7 active); Insilico's TNIK papers never state how many molecules were synthesized; nobody reports compute or energy per validated result. Cost-per-validated-idea - the quantity OptiLex's core goal is about - is unpublished everywhere in this literature.
  - The human-expert filter's contribution is never ablated. Every lab-validated case runs top-k expert selection (Co-Scientist 3 from a ranked list, MatterGen 4 of 75, antibiotics 24 of millions, Robin 5 then 10). No one has run the control where the same experts pick from a random or heuristic candidate list, so the generator's marginal value over 'experts plus a cheap enumerator' is unmeasured.
  - No blinded, pre-registered head-to-head of AI-generated versus human-generated hypotheses executed in the same lab with pre-specified success criteria. All the 2025-2026 lab wins (Robin, Co-Scientist, Virtual Lab, Kosmos) are unblinded, single-arm, and partly self-evaluated - the non-ML analogue of the Ideation-Execution Gap result we already have.
  - No disorder/degeneracy analogue for non-materials ideas. Crystallography has a named failure mode (ordered prediction vs disordered reality) and now has ordered-disordered matchers. There is no equivalent tool for deciding whether two ML-training ideas are the same mechanism in different notation - the precise problem behind our 30-prompts-to-3-families collapse.
  - No audit of the analysis layer in lab-in-the-loop systems. Robin's own analysis reported 7.5-fold where humans reported 1.75-fold on identical data, and Kosmos's synthesis statements were 57.9% accurate. Nobody has published a systematic study of automated-analysis effect-size inflation, although every closed-loop ideas factory depends on it.
  - Post-publication novelty auditing has no infrastructure and no incentives. The two decisive audits here came from single domain experts working manually (Cheetham & Seshadri on 10 random entries; Juelsholt on one compound) and took 4 months to 17 months to appear. There is no automated, continuously running prior-art auditor for generated-discovery databases - a natural product for the OptiLex harness and a strong external validation target.
Surprises:
  - The A-Lab Author Correction (Nature 650:E1, 19 January 2026) separates the two axes cleanly: on manual, post-publication peer-reviewed re-analysis the platform's structural conclusions held in 36 of 40 reported successes, while the novelty claim was redefined out of existence - the materials were 'new to the prediction platform, not necessarily new to science'. The execution engine worked; only the novelty verdict failed.
  - MatterGen's novelty screen was not naive - it compared against 850,384 ordered plus 117,652 disordered ICSD structures with a purpose-built ordered-disordered matcher - and still passed a 1971 compound that sat in its own training data. Corpus size and disorder-awareness were not the binding constraint; the equivalence relation was.
  - Robin's own analysis agent reported ripasudil increasing phagocytosis 7.5-fold while the human analysis of the same data gave 1.75-fold. A 4x effect-size discrepancy inside the flagship result of a lab-in-the-loop system, disclosed in the paper itself.
  - Kosmos's accuracy degrades exactly at the idea-forming step: 85.5% for data-analysis statements, 82.1% for literature statements, 57.9% for synthesis/interpretation. The retrieval and code layers are reliable; the synthesis that constitutes 'the idea' is barely better than a coin flip at the margin.
  - SCIGEN generated 10 million geometry-constrained candidates to find magnetic quantum materials, had a GNN predict magnetic ordering in 41% of relaxed structures, and the two compounds actually synthesized came out paramagnetic and diamagnetic - the designed property did not survive contact with the lab.
  - GNoME's 384,870-entry stable-structure database has a space-group distribution no experimentalist would recognise: the top four space groups are all non-centrosymmetric and cover ~34% of entries (Pm alone 12.7%), whereas non-centrosymmetric groups account for ~1% of the ICSD. A cheap 0 K evaluator that ignores entropy was gamed at scale by its own generator.
  - Phase I success for AI-discovered molecules is 80-90% while Phase II is ~40%, i.e. at the historical base rate. The generative layer has solved the part that was already tractable and left the hypothesis-truth bottleneck untouched - and Recursion's 5 May 2025 discontinuation of both REC-994 and REC-2282 was for futility and efficacy, explicitly not safety.
  - The decisive novelty audits were done by hand, by two or three people, and took a year or more to land, while the systems they audited generate millions of candidates per run. The asymmetry between generation throughput and audit throughput is the real rate limit on 'AI discovery', and it is the gap an ideas factory with a strong critic layer could actually close.
